## P: perform–pop-top trailer

### perform
 
 <i>v.</i> put on a scene [ca1950s Upper Hutt]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>perform</i> to throw a tantrum; eg `If that kid doesn't get his own way, does he perform! Needs a tickle up, I'd say.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [PBRF](p-per.md#pbrf))

>  ..he is also something of a poster child for the Performance-Based Research Fund, which was unveiled a year ago this month. <i>(Jun 11, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### perilla
 
 <i>n.</i> a herb of the mint family

>  Perilla, a member of the mint family with a unique flavour, which is commonly grown in Japan, Korea and Vietnam, has such pretty leaves that it was grown as a border plant in Victorian gardens, often alternating witrh begonias. <i>(Nov 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### perino
 
 <i>adj.</i> a blend of merino, wool and possum fur



 
 <i>adj.</i> a blend of merino, wool and possum fur

>  Woolyarns has developed a superior blend of merino, wool and possum fur which is aptly named perino yarn. <i>(Sep 30, 2003,Dominion Post,A13</i>)

### periodic detention
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also PD](p-per.md#pd))

>  `You get guys driving to PD (periodic detention) while disqualified,' he said. <i>(Jun 5, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A9</i>)

>  It is the 25th birthday ... of ...periodic detention. Known [sic] universally among its followers as PD. ... Periodic detention was spawned in 1962 by ... John Robson and Justice Minister Ralph Hannan. Instead of giving soft criminals a prison term they could instead lose their freedom at the weekends and do good work for the community. <i>(Oct 23, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,27</i>)

### peripatus
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  His slug is not a slug, but aperipatus... The peripatus is like a slugin general appearance. <i>(Dec 11, 1912,Otago Witness,68</i>)

>  "We would find wetas and peripatus. Do you know peripatus?... It's a very pretty creature with a crushed velvet body, greyish green - its whole body undulates beautifully - it's the missing link between the worms and the anthropods [sic] like crayfish or woodlice. It's segmented but each segment has a pair of small stumpy legs with two tiny claws." <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  It is most commonly called peripatus - from the same Greek root as <i>peripatetic</i>, meaning itinerant or wandering... The animal is also called the velvet worm or walking worm - references to two of its more obvious traits. Peripatus are found largely in the southern hemisphere - in Australasia, south-east Asia, Africa and Central and South America. <i>Ibid.</i> 66 I have to roll my peripatus onto its back again to view the circular, lobe-lipped mouth with double pair of sickle-shaped mandibles, and the two lumpy "gun turrets" - oral papillae - used to such effect in the capture and consumption of prey. Both the jaws and oral papillae, as well as the antennae, are pairs of modified limbs. Peripatus are opportunistic and will scavenge in preference to expending energy on hunting. <i>Ibid.</i> 74 Peripatus may boast a lineage more ancient than that of New Zealand's other, more famous, "living fossil," the tuatara. Early marine animals, such as this 500-million-year-old fossil from the Burgess Shale in British Columbia, appear remarkably similar to living onycophorans... <i>P. novaezealandiae</i> females.. have been found to carry embryos at all stages of development at all times of year, and to give birth regardless of the season, albiet more abundantly in summer. <i>(Apr 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 46/60</i>)

### periperi
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a potato thought to be grown in New Zealand in pre-European times. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  She grows a potato known as the periperi, which is probably the only potato that has been cultivated in New Zealand since pre-European times. <i>(Apr 29, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

### Perishable
 
 <i>n.</i> 'The Perishable', a Canterbury-West Coast train taking perishable goods from Christchurch to Greymouth

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... the <i>Perishable</i>, the train taking perishable goods from Christchurch to Greymouth ... <i>(1966,Australian & NZ English [TS],157</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> I catch the perishable to Christchurch, convinced I have more chance ... in the city than in a small town like Greymouth ... <i>(1974,Loner, The,124</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> When Ron discovered I was going back to the Coast on the night train he offered to drive me to Springfield station. ... The station was deserted except for a porter who seemed amazed that a woman should be travelling alone on the `perishable' as this train was called. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,89</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The Perishable, introduced some time during the mid-twenties, was an express goods train that ran between Christchurch and Greymouth - in both directions - on six nights of the week. As well as its usual duties, the train provided a convenient weekend service for Canterbury Mountaineering Club members travelling to Arthur's Pass. <i>(1987,Rail,128</i>)

>  David and I started off on the overnight Perishables Train from Christchurch bound for Arthur's Pass. <i>(2008,NZ Memories,74/34</i>)

>  The South Island had its equivalents, including the quaintly named Cabbage Train from Blenheim to Christchurch and the Perishable from Christchurch to Greymouth. <i>(Oct 30, 2004,Dominion Post,A1</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> 'The Perishable', a fruit and vegetable night-train (Hawkes Bay)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> After calling into Te Aute, ... we caught the perishable to Wellington. <i>(1974,Loner, The,112</i>)

### periwinkle
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Parsonsia</i> spp. also native, New Zealand periwinkle

>  <b>KOHERE</b> [Hdg] The Love-sick Periwinkle. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,20</i>)

>  Blue-eyed periwinkle, / Ivy wild and free, / Lupin and geranium, / Bloom of elder tree ... <i>(Dec 17, 1896,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  While passing through this nursery we were surprised to note the large quantity of American aloe, and also of the variegated periwinkle. <i>(Feb 7, 1876,North Otago Times,2</i>)

It has endless trailing and twining stems sprawling over hedges and banks, glossy green leaves and beautiful pale blue silver-shaped flowers an inch or more across. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,135,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> periwinkle</i> [s] <i>Vinca major</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,57</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Two species of <i>Parsonsia [heterophylla</i> and (<i>Capsularis</i>) have a similar [climbing] habit though lacking in prickles. They are variously termed KAIKU, NATIVE PERIWINKLE, or NEW ZEALAND PERIWINKLE. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,1929,144 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> . [various medium sized snailshaped shellfish See also periwinkle, <i>Amphibola crenata</i>, the edible marine mud snail, and titiko]

>  This is the kind of piffle that the herring-gutted, periwinkle-eating cockney gulps down with his bread and margarine and bad beer. <i>(Nov 25, 1911,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  They speedily spread themselves on arrival along sea beaches and feed greedily on a small periwinkle. <i>(Dec 18, 1907,Otago Witness,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the edible marine snail, <i>Amphibola crenata</i>, titiko

>  ... there are not wanting Several Sorts [of fish] that are well known; such as Mullet, Cavallas, Guard-Fish, Horse-Mackeral. Amongsyt the crustaceous Tribes we found Craw-fish, Muscles. [sic] Cockles, Scollops, Whelks, Periwinkles, ... <i>(1773,Cook's Journals,2/787</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in Cook Journal (1961) 2/787]

>  Of other shellfish there are ten or twelve sorts such as periwinkles, wilks, limpets, and some very beautiful sea Ears and another sort that sticks to the weeds , with some other things as sea eggs, starfish ... ed. fn Periwinkles, <i>Melarhapa </i>spp. (2), and probably Zediloma <i>and</i> Melagraphia <i>spp. Whelks,</i> Cominella <i>sp. Limpets, Cellana spp. The `very beautiful sea Ears' were certainly the Paua, <i>Haliotis iris; ... </i> <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Then follows a series of shell beds, consisting of the remains of the following species, now still inhabiting the estuary;- <i>Chione stutchburyi</i> (Cockle); <i>Huai</i> or Pipi, <i>Mesodesma chemnitzii</i>, Pipi; <i>Amphibola avellana </i>(Periwinkle) Hetikutiku, and <i>Mytilus smaragdinus</i> (Mussel) Kuku; <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,416</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ngaeti</i>, n. <i>Periwinkle</i>; a species of univalve mollusc. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,263</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Pupu - the shellfish periwinkle (turbo granosus). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> PERIWINKLE (<i>Melarhaphe oliveri</i>). A most abundant shell on high tidal rocks throughout New Zealand. It seldom grows larger than three eighths of an inch and is bluish white with a broad spiral band of bright blue. ... This is a true periwinkle, but that name is frequently applied locally to other shellfish, some of the top-shells and the cat's-eye. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Periwinkle or mud snail .. .. takarepo, titiko, whetiko .. .. <i>Amphibola crenata </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> The sea also brought them snapper, mullet, parore and flounder; whitebait in season; and oysters, pipi and karahu (periwinkles). <i>(1983,Whina,38</i>)

### perk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The few who will talk about some of thetruly wondrous perks available, do sowith extreme care... Those really"working the smorgs". as the jargon hasit, are positively purse-lipped. (Workingthe smorgs is where the employer offers,over and above a salary, a "smorgasbord"of perks). <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Pity about the searchers, eh giant? Might have been a few good perks otherwise. <i>(1964,Watersiders,87</i>)

>  All the perks! Coats! Boots! What other coach has ever got a free bloody car? <i>(Aug 4, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  There is nothing fair and reasonable about a travel perk that allows MPs and their spouses to fly free to Queenstown for a skiing holiday... Most of the perks are administered by the Parliamentary Services Commission, a group of senior MPs who gather together to bilk the taxpayer. <i>(Oct 26, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  She didn't join Mana Motuhake in Opotiki for the perks, pay and glory, because there weren't any. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  His most controversial role was overseeing and paying MPs' allowances and entitlements. They are know to the public as perks, but it is a term Mr O'Sullivan does not like. <i>(Mar 29, 2003,Dominion Post,A15</i>)

>  There is, of course, plenty of research evidence from North America and Germany to show that penal rates are more than a mere perk... <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  She fails to mention the "perks" solo mums are also eligible for. <i>(Jun 12, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to get by a perk or lucky gift

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Then he ... wheeled out the old push-bike he'd perked off a neighbour. <i>(1962,One of Us,78</i>)

 <i>n.</i> of a rubbish truck

### perk-buster
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics) Originally applied to MP Rodney Hide after he began a campaign to restrict MP's perks

>  Having "perk-buster" MP Rodney Hyde lend weight to that interpretation did nothing to dispel that view. <i>(Mar 28, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A17</i>)

>  [Hdg] Perkbuster MP travels business class... Parliament perkbuster Rodney Hide prefers to travel business class when he flies from his Auckland home to Wellington. <i>(Apr 3, 1997,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Perkbuster MP travels business class... Parliament perkbuster Rodney Hide prefers to travel business class when he flies from his Auckland home to Wellington. <i>(Apr 4, 1997,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Say what you like about his brute populism and wide-eyed bounciness, the Perks-Buster-General is growing in political stature. <i>(Apr 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### perk-busting
 
 <i>n.</i> [also perks-busting]

>  He [Rodney Hide] was elected on a platform of perk-busting and has achieved his position by false pretences. <i>(Nov 14, 2009,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Mr Mallard said account were being scrutinised in more details after ACT NZ's perk-busting campaign. <i>(Apr 25, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>94</b> His perks-busting fervour stimulated copy-cat behaviour from Labour on ministerial houses for Wellington-based ministers. <i>(Apr 19, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> There is a whole raft of people who only come to light in some perk-busting campaign - names like Ronald Bailey, Gordon Christie, Leo Schultz and Bruce Barclay who served three terms as parliamentarians. <i>(1997,Dark Art of Politics, The,11</i>)

>  ASK PERK-BUSTING Act MP Rodney Hide, and he'll tell you the government is inquiry obsessed. <i>(Jul 10, 2005,Sunday Star Times,13</i>)

>  He was elected on a platform of perk-busting and has achieved his position by false pretences. He has fallen from grace and must resign or be sacked. <i>(Nov 14, 2009,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### perking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  PERKING is the name for fringe benefits that are taken by employees and should not be confused with the `perk' that is an authorised addition to a salary. The word originated on the floor of the factory but the practice is not confined to that area. Those who make a habit of perking don't consider it dishonest but factory owners disagree and waste time and effort trying to stamp it out. <i>(Aug 22, 1983,National Business Review,36</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> R.A. told Lew and Harry the pilfering has reached an `Unacceptable level.' `Pilfering' is their word. In the plant it's `perking'. <i>(1985,Secret Diary of a Telephonist,105</i>)

### pero

>  <b>WALTON</b> The dog, according to a tradition still existing among the natives, was bestowed on the island, at a very remote period, by a number of divinities, who made a friendly descent on its shores. The name of a dog formerly was <i>pero</i>, which countenances the supposition that the country had been visited by Juan Fernandez, <i>pero</i> in the Spanish language, signifying a dog. <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

### perpetual lease
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The features of the system were, originally, the possession of a small farm, not exceeding 50 acres in extent, held under a perpetual lease for terms of thirty years, with recurring valuations at the end of each terms. ...Residence and improvement of the soil were compulsory. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,202</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Perpetual Lease,</i> though a misnomer, is a statutory expression in New Zealand. Under the former Land Acts, the grantee of a perpetual lease took a term of thirty years, with a right of renewal at a revalued rent, subject to conditions as to improvement and cultivation, with a right to purchase the freehold after six years' occupation. <i>(1898,Austral English,347</i>)

>  No. of Area in Holders. Acres. 3. Lands held on perpetual lease .. .. 120 13,008 4. Lands held as leaseholds in perpetuity .. 1,000 191,150 <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,24</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In 1882 he introduced legislation to stop the further sale of public lands and to establish a `perpetual lease' at a low rental, though with periodic revaluations; but the Legislative Council, ... frustrated his purpose by giving lessees a right of purchase at a cheap rate. [He= William Rolleston]. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,161</i>)

>  The operation of what is called the perpetual-lease system with right of purchase, which became part of the land-law of the colony [93] in 1882, has had the effect of lessening the demand for land under deferred payments, ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,92</i>)

>  Since all lands held under the Crown `by lease in perpetuity' are subject to land-tax, the necessity for the periodical revaluations under the perpetual-lease system is done away with ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,201</i>)

 <i>n.</i> redshank

### perspidrip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The critics will no doubt note that <i>to oozle</i> and <i>to trickle</i>, which denoted much slower modes of locomotion, were comparatively little [30] used, and will not be surprised that Wellington trampers are sometimes afflicted with<i> perspidrip,</i> or describe themselves as<i> jagged. Jagged</i> (one syllable), worn out by exertion, exhausted, no doubt has some relation to the English dialect <i>on the jag</i>, a drunken spree, and the American <i>jagged</i>, tipsy, and also owes something to<i> jiggered. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,29</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail; also Tramper 15(4)/3]

### perv
 
 <i>n.</i> (also pervo) a person who watches another or others with erotic or sexual interest (AOD/NZOD distinguishes from a sexual pervert as such - valid?); the act of so watching; an illicit or lustful look; (more neutrally) a look (around) [also perve]

>  [Hdg] Ogler's 'perv' costs $300. A Wellington man caught ogling a woman showering in a camping ground toilet.. was fined $300 in Whangarei District Court yesterday... [He] said he was "just having a perv", before driving off in his vehicle. <i>(Jan 11, 2001,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>McLauchlan ed.</b> Clarry grunted and crouched and looked into the oven - like some old perv looking in a window, Simon thought. <i>(2000,Morrieson's Motel,131</i>)

>  We'll have a perve around here, I said. <i>(Mar 26, 2002,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  <i>give us a perv</i>: let's see <i>to perv:</i> to admire sexually <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### perve
 
 <i>v.</i> (perve on or at) look (at) with erotic or sexual interest. [abbreviation of (sexual) pervert] [also perv (see also perv n.)]

>  So there we were perving at the protesters when I actually began to listen to what the guy with the megaphone was saying. <i>(Sep 15, 1999,Dominion,20</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> DI: You never do get used to the screws pervin' at you, do you, Ma? <i>Ibid.</i> 110 <i>Perving</i>: watching (as a peeping Tom does) with prurient interest. <i>(1984,Outside In,63</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Oh no. My privates being perved at by some poxy person from Papakura, Papatoetoe or Pukekohe and his pals, misery. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,111</i>)

>  " We come here to perve, hence the dark glasses," he said. <i>(Nov 10, 2004,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> To be, to act as, a sexual pervert; esp. of a male: Australian: since ca. 1920. Baker, 1942. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,505,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>perv. (v.)</i> to look at with lust <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,98</i>)

### pest plant
 
 <i>n.</i> formerly <i>noxious weed</i> - a plant declared to be harmful to the environment or to animals whose sale and planting is prohibited and which in some cases it is a duty to eradicate. [Jim Cameron writes: Not in OED, NSOD or Collins. DNZE has an entry for noxious weed.]

### pet
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep used to lead others into a dip or into the freezing works pens. (See [decoy; Judas sheep](../D#decoy;-judas-sheep),[yard sheep](y-yc.md#yard-sheep))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pets: see under Judas, which name is obvious from their grisly task of leading other sheep to a fate from which they themselves escape. But age will catch them up. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,60</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> The sheep were led by a Judas sheep or pet - I have heard them called both. All pets had a wide leather collar round their necks. <i>(1990,Among the High Hills of Canterbury,29</i>)

### petako
 
 <i>n.</i> fern with roughly sickle-shaped leaflets

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Asplenium polyodon</i> SICKLE SPLEENWORT ... Petako <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,438</i>)

### peter
 
 <i>n.</i> a cash register; esp. in tickle a/the peter: of a person in charge, to regularly steal money from a till (cf. milk v) [ca1950 R. Byrne, a cash box, esp. to <i>rat, tickle the peter</i> = help oneself to the cash (-box)]

>  <i>(1943,Penguin New Writing,17/66</i>) [NOTES: (Sargeson)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He pushed me over one more double gin which he only pretended to ring up on the peter. <i>(1946,That Summer,65</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tickle</i>. 2. To steal from, to rob, as in<i> tickle a peter</i>, to rob the till: New Zealand c. (- 1932). Perhaps ex<i> to tickle trout.</i> see <i>sere</i> and <i>tail. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,883</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> To steal from cash-box ot till: New Zealand and Australia: since ca. 1920: 1932, Nelson Baylis (private letter); 1942, Baker. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,507</i>)

>  ... tickle the peter. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> peter: cash-register. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `I'm just on the way up to a branch office,' Fred said. `We've only just discovered that our senior salesman there has helped himself to a vacation in Australia.' `Did he tickle the peter?' Hubert asked. `To the tune of two thousand quid. ...' <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,143</i>)

>  <i>tickle the peter</i> v<i>. steal from common fund or petty cash. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tickle</i> to steal or rob, often in phr. <i>to tickle the peter</i>, to rob the till; NZ c.1932, Australia later. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>tickle the peter</i>: To tickle the peter is to REMOVE MONEY FROM THE CASH REGISTER without recording the transaction. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,103</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a safe, esp. in phrase to crack a peter

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `I've got a job on, mate. I need some help.' ... `What job? Scrub-cutting?' `Don't be wet behind the ears. I mean a real job. Cracking a peter.' `A peter?' `Can't you talk English? A safe. A peter. It's the same thing.' <i>(1985,Horse,67</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Breaking and Entering) 67]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> (Ex. sense 2.) `A parcel or bundle, whether large or small'; in C. 20, usually a tramps' pack: 1812, J.H. Vaux; 1859, H; Oct. 1879, Macmillian's Magazine; 1890, B and L; 1901, F and H; 1932, Rev. Frank Jennings; 1932, Nelson Baylis (New Zealand); 1932 Matt Marshall, The Travels of Tramp-Royal, `Peter, tramp's pack'; 1935, Hippo Neville (ditto); 1937, John worby (ditto): 1942, Baker (Australia); extant. ... `A cash-box': ?U.S.A.: 1859, Matsell; 1862, Mayhew (see peter-cutter); June 1863, Sessions Papers; 1864, H, 3rd ed.; 1869, A Merchant, Six Years (a safe); Oct. 1879, Macmillan's Magazine; 1889, Clarkson and Richardson; 1890, B and L; app. obsolete by 1910, except in N.Z. and Australia. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,506,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a prison cell [ca1941-5 Prison and Detention (R.C.)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> I'd always give my drink a little jerk outside Whippy's peter so a bit of tea would splash out on the floor to him to clean up in the morning. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,77</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> For a while he had charge of the pound and he was a fair soandso down there, doing over your peter for tobacco two or three times a day. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,125</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `So that was that. I spend what's left of the night in the peter and when I come out I'm out of work'. <i>(1964,Watersiders,110</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> PETER (n) Cell. Probably from peter, an iron chest or box, British, early 19th century. <i>Ibid.</i> 77 And I'd always give my drink a little jerk outside Whippy's peter so a bit of tea would splash out on to the floor <i>Ibid.</i> 180A tealeaf is just a cunt in my eyes, and you'd be about the lowest ... You're a peter thief and you're a dog. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,252</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> A punishment cell: Australian convicts': 1890 B and L; 1901, F and H; in C. 20 Britain, any prison cell, as in Jim Phelan, Lifer, 1938; 1942, Baker ('Prison, a cell') records it use in Australia in 1891 and declares its obsolescence there; again in Baker, 1945. C. 20, current in S. Africa for any prison cell: J.B.Fisher, letter of May 22, 1946. -->cf P: 4. a punishment cell: Austr. cant from ca1880; obsolesc. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,506</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> orig. a half-gallon or gallon demijohn used to hold draught beer; thence a half-gallon, flagon of draught beer. =half-gallon jar, =flagon, =jar, =half-g cf Blue Peter. [ca1930's Canterbury (J. Columbus) originally blue peter, a chemist's blue jar of about three-quarter gallon ca1940 R.Byrne (1951) `Can also refer to half-gallon DYC vinegar flagons popularly used for collecting draught beer']

>  Sir, - I am also a South Islander, and the statement made by P.W. were covered in wicker covering to protect them. Some half-gallon jars were known as `Blue-Peters' on account of the colour. In Christchurch I can go further back than P.W. Twenty to 25 years ago they started selling and filling these jars. I think half-gallons were1/3. gallons 2/6. <i>(Jan 19, 1955,Dictionary of the Underworld</i>) [NOTES: [1930][?p. corresp. col.]]

>  Sir, - I am also a South Islander, and the statement made by P.W. were covered in wicker covering to protect them. Some half-gallon jars were known as `Blue-Peters' on account of the colour. In Christchurch I can go further back than P.W. Twenty to 25 years ago they started selling and filling these jars. I think half-gallons were1/3. gallons 2/6. <i>(Jan 19, 1955,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: [1930][?p. corresp. col.]]

>  The fact is that 15 years ago in Dunedin we regularly had our half and one-gallon jars filled in the bar. So well established was the custom that the `gang' I was with kept special wooden cases to carry the `peters,' as the jars were called. <i>(Jan 17, 1955,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: [1940] [?p. Corresp. col.]]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> half and one-gallon jars filled in the bar. So well established was ... all the blokes brought along a peter and sat around telling yarns. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,150</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> <i>The Jar</i> [title]...I'd bought a peter of wine/ From the owner of a Dalmatian vineyard, <i>(1979,Collected Poems,392,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A cell or `peter' at Mt. Crawford is ... somewhere about nine feet long by seven wide and ten or eleven in height. <i>(1945,In Prison,38</i>)

>  <b>SLIGO</b> Then because it was hot, someone fetched a peter of home-brew ... <i>(1987,Final Things,36</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... spurs coming down and petering out in a swamp. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,108</i>)

>  <i>peter 2 n. NZ & Austral</i>. a half-gallon jar for beer or wine. [name] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,562</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Sitting up all night over a peter to listen to the 1949 series. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,173</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>half-G</i>: Less than 50 per cent of a full-G. Also known in Dunedin as a peter, after the first missionary to land in that city, St Peter Speight. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  New Zealand's traditional take-away beer container - the flagon, also known as a `jar', `half-G' or `peter' - was yesterday defended by its manufacturer.... <i>(Oct 6, 1982,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Depending on regional expressions, [draught-beer containers] are variously known as a `jar', `peter', `half-gee' (half-gallon) or the more modern `pub pet' two-litre plastic bottle. <i>(Jul 1988,Pacific Way,50</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83 ed.</i>)

### peter out
 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> The man who is out of candles or matches must 'peter' out his nimble sixpence or go to bed in the dark. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,87</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The pasture grasses, mostly ryegrass, were petering out, and other worthless grasses were eradicating the once prolific better grazing areas. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,156</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> There's plenty of men back in the hills [of Central Otago] yet, combing for gold. They reckon one day they might strike the Empress Reef. That reef never petered out, you know. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,54</i>)

>  The track will "peter out" temporarily atCape Foulwind. <i>(1932,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,I/42</i>)

### peter school
 
 <i>n.</i> a gambling den

>  They attacked the totalisator and "Peter" schools; complained against the administration of the licensing laws, and urged the formation of a strong Church vigilance committee to spur the authorities to do their duty. <i>(Oct 27, 1893,Mataura Ensign,6</i>)

>  [In local criminal slang]... a `peter school' is a gambling den, ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Underworld slang ... <i>peter school</i>, a gambling den. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> a gambling den: New Zealand and Australian: 1932, Nelson Baylis (private letter; N.Z.); 1941, Sidney J.Baker, New Zealand Slang; 1942 Baker,Australian Slang; 1944, M and B (Aus.); extant. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,507</i>)

>  <i> peter school n</i>. gambling den. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

### petrel
 
 <i>n.</i> members of the <i>Procellaria</i> and allied bird genera

>  It is certainly not a petrel, but, on the contrary, its bill is that of the albatross, the nostrils opening by tubes on either side. On the other hand, its foot is not that of the albatross, having a rudimentary thumb-claw, which the petrel tribe possess. <i>(Aug 31, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,104</i>)

>  Their beautiful wings and general appearance will have pointed them out as belonging to the family of petrels. <i>(Oct 19, 1892,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  This little petrel is not unlike several others which we saw in milder climates, but from all of them it can be distinguished by the length and slenderness of its legs, and the bright orange webs of its feet. <i>(Apr 14, 1904,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw [in the Indian Ocean] <i>Procellaria glacialis</i> or Fulmar & the next day the <i>Procellaria aequinoctialis</i>, which is [189] very improperly thus called, as it is never seen near the Equinoxial line ... They are all Petrels ... [ed. fn] Respectively the Antarctic Fulmar and White-chinned Petrel (or Cape Hen). <i>(Dec 3, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/1888,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We also got four kinds of Petrel in their burrows,- the Mutton Bird, Parara, Titi Wainui, and the Kuaka. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,16</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Among these may be noticed the albatross, the molimawk, the Cape pigeon, and several varieties of tern and petrel. The Cape pigeon, which is a beautifully marked black and white bird about the size of a wood pigeon, will follow the vessel to the end of the voyage. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,8</i>)

>  Most of the eighteen or so smaller petrels belong to species that are widely distributed in the Southern Ocean... Most of the petrels return to the islands after dark. <i>(1991,New Zealand's Subantarctic Islands,42</i>)

>  On predator-free 'petrel islands', [warblers'] nests were low to the ground. <i>(1984,Notornis,31/97</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Here they found an immense number of. petrels of the bluish species, common over the whole southern ocean, some being on the wing, and others in the woods, in holes under ground. formed between the roots of trees and in the crevices of rocks, in places not easily accessible, where they probably had their nests and young. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,I/153</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  In skeletons the collection is not very strong, the only ones articulated being a large petrel and a hedgehog. <i>(Dec 29, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Mottled Petrel Pterodroma inexpectata </i>(FORSTER, 1844) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Scaled petrel, Peale's petrel, rainbird, korure, titi.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,76</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw a great Variety of bird this day viz. A white & sooty Albatross (<i>Diomedea exulans & palpebrata</i>). The black & sooty Petrel, (<i>Procellaria aequinoctialis & fuliginosa</i>) the black banded Petrel (<i>Procellaria vittata</i>); the small white rumped Petrel (<i>Proc. pelagica</i>) & we saw likewise three times a <i>Port Egmont Hen</i>, which is commonly thought a mark of the vicinity of Land. [ed. fn] The birds listed here are, respectively, the Wandering Albatross; Light-mantled Sooty Albatross; White-chinned Petrel; Great-winged Petrel; <i>Pachyptila</i> spp; Wilson's Storm Petrel and Antarctic Skua. . <i>(Jan 28, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/220,1982 ed.</i>)

### phalanger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>phalanger n</i>. (in Australia and New Zealand) tree-dwelling marsupial with webbed hind feet, e.g. the opossum. [F f. Gk <i>phalaggion </i>spider's web] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,563</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 848]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  `I believe that the member for Tarawera is referring to the possum (Trichosurus vulpecula), not opossums (genus Didelphis) which are American marsupials,' [Agriculture Minister Moyle] said in his written reply. ... And for the name, you can be forgiven if you call our imported possum (the `brushtail possum,' to be precise) an opossum. The Aussie possum - there are about six types across the Tasman - looked so much like the American counterpart when first discovered by Europeans that it was called an opossum. As recently as 1981, scientists who turned up for the first New Zealand symposium on marsupials ... were sagely talking about possums in 12 of the papers presented while their colleagues talked about opossums in six papers. All were referring to the same nocturnal nuisance. One of the great achievements of that symposium was to finish up talking the same language after taking a vote that favoured for possum. ... The fur trade is pushing for `NZ phalanger' (a generic name for a group of marsupials) while possum stew exporters are trying to win acceptance for `Kiwi bear' in Oriental restaurants. <i>(Jun 28, 1988,Auckland  Star,1</i>)

 <i>n.</i> New South Wales

### philo-Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> one who sympathises with Maori causes

>  <b>BOYD</b> A genuine philo-Maori, who objected to settler arrogance and injustice, he was nevertheless interested in the Heretaunga himself though he refused to cooperate with Tanner and the other lessees <i>(1984,City of the Plains,10</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> pro-Maori

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> ... I have elicited a correct view of an action on which many aspersions have been cast in this country, by the Philo-Maori party. <i>(1864,Memoranda,77</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Certain of your Philo-Maori men in England, certain of our Philo-Maori men in the Colony - ... believe ... that this <i>latter</i> cause and motive for his Rebeldom, professed by the Maori, was the <i>true</i> cause. <i>(1865,England's NZ War,39</i>)

>  Exclaimed the philo-Maori chaps with admiration, then,/ of the noble Maori savage .../ <i>(Aug 5, 1865,Punch in Canterbury,82</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Those who knew the natives could say that war and ruin were convertible terms; but they were denounced as `philo-Maories'. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,416</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Often the facts and events it records are brutal, and there is nothing philo-Maori about the book. <i>(1948,Taina,ix</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> But `philo-Maori' writers such as Octavius Hadfield and William Martin expended a great deal of ingenuity in doing just that. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,79</i>)

>  Bishop Hadfield was absent from Wellington.. the mere mention of whose name, and commendation of whose Philo-Maori sentiments.. was quite sufficient to make the "vigorous prosecution of the war" men clear out of the sanctuary. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  Bishop Hadfield was absent from Wellington.. the mere mention of whose name, and commendation of whose Philo-Maori sentiments.. was quite sufficient to make the "vigorous prosecution of the war" men clear out of the sanctuary. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

### PHO
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Primary Health Organisation [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  To get the lower fees, your doctor must belong to a PHO. <i>(Oct 2, 2002,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The main aim of the PHOs - through which the Government's primary healthcare strategy is to be delivered - was to prevent disease by getting to patients early... <i>(Oct 2, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  PHOs with more of the needy would have heavier workloads for the same amount of public funding. <i>(Mar 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  Wairarapa Community PHO [heading]. .. Wairarapa Community PHO is a Charitable Trust set up from 1st January 2004 under the Government's Primary Health Care Strategy to coordinate and develop the delivery of Primary Health Care and Health Promotion to the communities of the Wairarapa. <i>(Nov 8, 2005,Dominion Post,39</i>)

>  For a kick-off, it's another acronym. We once had NPHs, then CHEs, then DHBs and now PHOs. These things all roughly mean "hospital", but they mean it in different ways. <i>(Oct 12, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Announcing the minimum requirements for primary health organisations (PHOs) this week, Mrs King said there wpould be "new money" to back up the strategy and she hoped to announce that soon... PHOs will receive a set amount of funding for each person enrolled with them. <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The bulk of the money will be directed to new primary health organisations (PHOs), which will be funded according to the number of patients they have on their books rather than per visit. <i>(Mar 18, 2002,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Ms Ritchie is particularly concerned about the inclusion of primary mental health care when the Public Health Organisations (PHO)s are established... The board is currently in the process of producing a document defining how PHOs will work. <i>(Dec 10, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

### phoner-upper
 
 <i>n.</i> someone who rings in to a talkback radio session

>  Famous people polarise the views of the common herd - who are the phoner-uppers - because we all feel we own them when they die. <i>(Nov 11, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

### phoney
 
 <i>n.</i> a cellphone user

>  Out of vanity, I had peeled off the strip of sellotape across the phone that kept the batteries in place... And yet I refused to accept I was a phoney. <i>(May 13, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### phonics
 
 <i>n.</i> [not NZ]

>  At last the government is promoting a change to the method of teaching reading from the "whole word cancer" which has been in vogue for many years, to the phonics method. <i>(Aug 19, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  It is true that some phonics is taught in whole-language programmes,but it is taught as phonetic clues to be used in conjunction with a holistic reflex. <i>(Feb 5, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### phormium
 
 <i>n.</i> . [from scientific name Phormium tenax] -New Zealand flax (See [flax](fin-fly.md#flax))

>  Variations of phormium will be fully investigated, also the action of different soils upon fibre production and the like. <i>(May 13, 1908,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The phormium, or flax-plant, grows in wild luxuriance throughout the three islands of New Zealand: it is indigenous to the country, and perennial; the leaves averaging from six to ten feet in length. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,18,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> Mr. Nicholas brought some of the seeds of the New Zealand phormium with him to England ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,179</i>)

>  Phormium .. Tons .. 11,992 <i>Ibid.</i> 116 Phormium (New Zealand hemp) .. 219,375 <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,115</i>)

>  <i>phormium n</i>. a genus of plants including New Zealand flax, P. tenax. [G. phormion, species of plant] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  Your general conclusions in `The Evolution of Maori Clothing' of local development based on phormium as opposed to `aute' and the necessities of a colder climate, may have to be reviewed if it is found that a Western Pacific wave had established the art of working flax in Pre-Toi times. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/19,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  [paging system changes] The Phormium industry in New Zealand ... was built largely on an export trade to cordage fibre markets overseas. <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,31/4/1</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  There was extensive sealing and whaling, trade in <i>Phormium</i> and timber, as well as in potatoes and pork. <i>(Apr 1952,NZ Geographer,4</i>) [NOTES: (Murphy)]

>  <i>phormium n</i>. any of a genus of plants of the lily family with tough leathery evergreen leaves. Also called: <i>New Zealand flax, flax lily.</i> [C19: NL < Gk <i>phormos</i> basket] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,851</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  The refuse accumulates as a waste product in the process of producing the fibre variously known as New Zealand flax, New Zealand phormium, or New Zealand hemp. <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)

### phormium tenax
 
 <i>n.</i> used as a popular or proper rather than scientic name for harakeke or native flax

>  It is intended by some merchants of this place, to form a depot at Kapiti, for the obtainment of <i>Phormium tenax</i>, and the cutter <i>Royal William</i> sailed last week with the requisite material for such a purpose. <i>(Nov 4, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  The following further Exhibition awards have been announced to-day .. Second order of merit: Colonial Museum of New Zealand, phormium tenax .. Fourth order of merit .. James Seed, Southbrook, Canterbury, manufactures from phormium tenax. <i>(Mar 2, 1881,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  "It is unfortunate that farmers have not yet realised what an extremely valuable plant we have in our New Zealand flax (Phormium Tenax) ..." <i>(May 5, 1927,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The herbaceous productions indigenous to the country are, wild celery, canary grass, wild parsley, plantain grass, a species of ray-grass, the <i>ensata</i> or flag, the phormium tenax or flax-plant, and a species of the fern. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/249</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> A considerable quantity of Phormium tenex [sic] grows here ... but it is reported here that it is a bastard kind called by them the wood flax and not fit to be manufactured into flax. <i>(Mar 18, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 18 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 361]

>  The only exports from New Zealand are wood, phormium tenax, or New Zealand flax, pork, potatoes, maize, sulphur and oil; ... <i>(1842,GBPP</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Dec 4 Hobson Despatch in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 179]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... an open spot ... now grown over with the highest Phormium tenax I ever saw. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/143</i>)

>  [The Waikato] runs through ... deep fern fringed with raupo (a bullrush) ...[sic] and the magnificent toi-toi, a gigantic grass whose flower stalk is from 8 to 12 feet and has an exquisite feather of two feet long playing in every air. This grass is ... with the phormium tenax the most remarkable of the open country vegetable products. ... You see many native dwellings & many canoes for they are a most locomotive people, few as ignorant of all but their own kainga as our British Hodge. There are troops of wild ducks & shags, some hawks of large size, bitterns & smaller waterfowl unknown to me. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/80</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The distinctive marks which characterise it as not English are the occasional Ti palms, which have a very tropical appearance, and the luxuriance [34] of the Phormium tenax. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iii/33-34</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year iii]

>  <i>phormium tenax [=] New Zealand flax</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,58</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The<i> phormium tenax</i>, or New Zealand flax, is the most useful plant that country affords. ... Of the leaves of the <i>phormium </i>... the natives make all their common garments ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,46</i>)

>  <i>PHORMIUM TENAX </i>(New Zealand Hemp). The preparation of Phormium for export is an industry of New Zealand which has not made vast progress ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,153</i>)

>  A new mode of treating the <i>Phormium tenax</i> by steam had been discovered, by which flax can be made for sale for GBP15 a ton, at a large profit. <i>(Jun 12, 1858,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

### phosphorised oats
 
 <i>n.</i> rabbit poison

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Phosphorised oats, toxa, pollard baits, strychnine and jam combined, gradually laid down the `bunny' population ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,39</i>)

### photographic fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Mecodium sanguinolentum</i>. [[JD notes obs. but a curious item.]]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Probably the most abundant filmy ferns are three species commonly associated. The first is the PHOTOGRAPHIC FERN (<i>M[ecodium] sanguinolentum</i>) so named because when dried and pressed it leaves a brown stain on the paper accurately depicting its own form, even though light is not an agent in the process. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,100,1929 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i> phragmites</i> [s] <i>Phragmites australis</i> (Cav.) Trin. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,58</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a play on PT, physical training

 <i>n.</i> manuka manna

>  <b>HOOKER</b> piamanuka, <i>Col[enso]</i>. Manna exudation of <i>Leptospermum scoparium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> piamanuka, <i>Col[enso]</i>. Manna exudation of <i>Leptospermum scoparium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> A sort of manna, which exudes from the [manuka] plant in all stages, is called by [the Maori] Piamanuka. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/196</i>)

### picaninny
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was generally remarked by the ladies present that the picaninny and the ladies of the Court looked very much better and better clothed than on the occasion of the former visit. <i>(Aug 9, 1881,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>a little child</i>. The word is certainly not Australian. It comes from the West Indies (Cuban <i>piquinini</i>, little, which is from the Spanish <i>pequeno </i>small, and <i>nino</i>, child). The English who came to Australia, having heard the word applied to negro children of the aborigines. After a while English people thought the word was aboriginal Australian, while the aborigines thought it was correct English. It is pigeon-English. [1696: Aust 1830] <i>[351] used as adj. and figuratively, to mean little</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,350</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> This fellow [a native servant] ... met me ... telling me that Mrs. King had got a <i>pickeeninnee</i>, (a child) he began to describe her groans ... while ... under the pains of labour; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/280 All the inhabitants flocked around us ... and the mothers, pointing to the children they carried in their arms, called out to us repeatedly, <i>homi pickeninnee wow,</i> which means, give the child a nail; ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/171</i>)

>  <b>LEIGH</b> They [Maoris] observed they could not let me have it, for if they did all their pickaninnies (their children) would die, ... . <i>(Aug 4, 1830,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1830 Aug 4 Rev. S. Leigh in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/709]

>  <b>MARSHAL</b> [A Maori says] `Mitter King ... teach picaniny so high' <i>(1834,Narrative of Two Visits,6</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the `pickers up' were busy gathering up the fleeces as they fell from the bereft sheep and carrying them to the sorting table, where they [173] were stripped of the `pieces', which were thrown aside, while the best of the fleece was quickly classified, rolled together, and deposited in a kind of bin, according to its quality, whether `clothing' or `combing.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> The shearing and picking and culling - picking out the fat sheep to send to the freezing works, and culling the old ewes from the young ones. <i>(1924,Heather of the South,183</i>)

>  [Maori] carry their picaninees on their backs <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/92</i>) [NOTES: (John Pierce)]

>  Richards and Mould returned yesterday with some traps, his woman, and the Picaninnies. <i>(May 18, 1842,Piraki Log,129,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> [The natives] are very fond of horses ... They will buy nothing but mares, having a great desire to have `pickaninny' horses of their own. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),145</i>)

>  Most of the picaninnies have been `down with the measles' as the people here phrase it. <i>(Nov 24, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/159,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> Arriving at the Purapura landing-place, we found three large canoes filled with natives - men, women, and picaninnies - all covered with green leaves, the mourning costume of the Maoris. <i>(1867,Robert Maunsell,187,1938 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COTTLE</b> ... little Maori pickaninny ... <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,97</i>)

>  <b>OWEN</b> ... me the ole man's pickaninny [here a half-cast] [fn] Quite common, meaning, in first instance, little child; and subsequently, offspring of any age. <i>(1909,Philip Loveluck,28</i>)

>  On being hauled out [of the lilipi gruel] the [Maori] child was handed over to the women ... very little stuff from the picannini was wasted. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,116</i>)

>  Incidentally, the wahines and `Picanninis' invariably joined in the free-for-all Donnybrook with which [the Maori football match] ended. ho;1925 Mander Allen Adair (1971) 85 He loved to carry her about in a shawl on his back as the Maoris did their picaninnies, ... <i>(Jun 10, 1920,Quick March,41</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> `The kids at them school he the cheeky brat ...', volunteered a lady with a piccaninny fastened to her back ... <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,43,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>piccaninny ... n</i>. small abo. child [Adopted from W. Indies]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Johnny said yes, and did Dave know what Rangi and Eileen and the pickaninnies would do when they got to town? <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,229,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> Fancy these <i>tamariki</i>, piccaninnies or brats of one or two years old, indulging ... in the herb nicotiana. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,9</i>)

>  Another aged chief, who might easily be mistaken for him, sat beside him, tattooed almost exactly the same. The latter had the head of a piccaninny sticking out from under the mat. <i>(Feb 9, 1878,Otago Witness,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Most of the picanninies have been `down with the measles' as the people here phrase it <i>(Nov 24, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/156,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

### picarel
 
 <i>n.</i> red baitfish

>  <b>WILSON</b> BAITFISH, RED <i>Emmelichthys nitidus:</i> A less common species in New Zealand waters this fish turns up unpredictably in vast schools that give the water a red appearance - and the fish its name. Grow up to about 16 inches and known also as picarel, pearl fish and red herring. Colours light pink to dark red. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,35</i>)

### Piccadilly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> At about 11.15 every morning we go through a piece of agony known as `Piccadilly'. All the New Zealanders in [? Sling] camp, three to four thousand strong, march along a certain section of the road in column of fours. On either side are about sixteen Sergeant Instructors, beautiful fellows, their superb figures almost bursting through their London tailor-made tunics. Their duty and pleasure it is to blast us up in a loud voice for any little irregularity of deportment they may notice or think they notice ...... The camp Commandant and half a dozen of the sherangs dressed in their best ... take the salute. <i>(Mar 31, 1917,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,49</i>) [NOTES: nf OED. P5.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(Is this the same usage as in 1; or an independently developed sense of `hyper-smart, formal marching'. ? )]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> [The Pongo sentry] was in full gear with blancoed web and anklets and rifle sling and he did the full Piccadilly up and down outside the door slapping salutes right left and centre. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,185</i>)

### Piccadilly stunt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Of course we still get plenty of"Picadilly Stunts." <i>(Aug 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/51 : 57</i>)

### pick
 
 <i>v.</i> to guess rightly

>  When one comes to think of it, instead of saying `Rightly, sir,' Connelly would have remarked, `They picked it, my oath,' or words to that effect, and instead of saying, `I alone killed Bourke,' he would probably observe, `I was on my lonesome, boss.' [Explanatory sentence for `They picked it, my oath' `Have you been rightly or wrongly convicted?' asked the discredited 'Tec. `Rightly,sir,' said Connelly.] <i>(May 29, 1909,NZ Truth,205/5/2</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The boss ... exclaimed, `why blow me down if here isn't His Excellency and party now!' Clarrie glanced up. `Well; by crikey old chappie; how quickly you picked us,' he responded. [1900's] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,14</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) reckon, predict (esp. as I'm picking)

>  <b>GORDON & DEVERSON</b> <i>pick</i> (for example, <i>I'm picking</i> - I reckon <i>the All Blacks will win</i>) <i>(1985,NZ English (Gordon & Deverson),48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a cattle snack

>  <b>STEWART</b> We drove around ... and had a good leisurely look at the stock while tossing a bit of hay about - just to `provide a pick'. <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,65</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to peck [ca1935 Havelock <i>Pick</i> and <i>peck</i> used apparently indiscriminately]

>  Roosters pick you sometimes when you try to catch, they'll turn around and pick you. <i>(1965,Speech and Idiom of Maori Children,Appendix B. xix,1963 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Speech and Idiom Maori Children Appendix B. xix [Standard III-IV Children]]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [drafting] to select stock for sale or slaughter.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> I remember .. watching the well-known dealer, Andrew Grant, picking 'fats.' <i>(1921,Tutira,362</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Brought sheep in, picked out the tops, then drafted up some odds and ends. <i>(Feb 26, 1931,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>PATOKA REUNION COMMITTEE</b> Mr Mason Waterworth who was one of the first to pick fat lambs in this area ... <i>(1985,Podocarp to Pasture,26</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a selection or draft of stock for sale or slaughter.

>  The first pick of lambs can be taken and the remainder spread as lightly as possible. <i>(Mar 1955,NZ Journal of Agriculture,299</i>)

>  <b>PATOKA REUNION COMMITTEE</b> Of the three thousand, eight hundred lambs, six hundred and fifty went off their mothers at the first pick ... <i>(1985,Podocarp to Pasture,29</i>)

### pick at
 
 <i>v.</i> to nag (at); to pick on [cf P: Chaff, annoy: Aust coll: -1916 (from dial. <i>pick on</i>) ca1940 (Havelock) eg Stop picking at me. I'm not going home yet. ca1951 Wellington The landlady's always picking at me - won't get off my back.]

 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to separate wool from dags.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Picked dags for the rest of the afternoon. <i>(Oct 11, 1939,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

### pick handle
 
 <i>n.</i> as a measure of width

>  She's a big woman, the boss's wife, two pick handles across the backside and an arm that can fell a bullock, ... <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,36</i>) [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]

### pick the eyes out of
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  [Reference to the transferring of parts of the country to the city] They pick the eyes out of the country. The populous areas are taken and we are left with the rural districts. <i>(May 31, 1958,Christchurch Star-Sun,9</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> With free selection of land at [pounds]2 per acre everyone had the same opportunity to buy, and a leaseholder ran the risk of waking any morning to find the eyes picked out of His run by some outsider ... <i>(1930,Sketch of His Life,18</i>)

### pick up
 
 <i>v.</i> sc. fleeces (See [cf. picker-up](per-pop.md#picker-up))

>  <b>WALKER</b> My job at first was picking up fleeces <i>(Nov 10, 1862,Journal</i>)

>  I was picking up and rowling the fleeces <i>(Jan 30, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,75,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> His work was to pick up fleeces. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,46</i>)

>  I then muster for Brown and Engst shears last. I put Manuel and Lyne on to shear to. ... I press. The general hand Hobbs picks up and rolls fleeces. <i>(Dec 9, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,331,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> I'm engaged in the baling and pressing of wool, / And when picking up fleeces my hands are quite full. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>) [NOTES: [`A New Chum's Letter Home']]

>  <b>PERRY</b> A boy or man to five shearers is sufficient for picking up the fleeces, but the operations at the wool table will be described in the chapter on wool-classing. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,83</i>)

>  When a shearer finishes a sheep, the <i>f</i>. is left lying on the <i>board</i> (q.v.). It is <i>picked up</i> by a boy (the <i>f.-p.</i>), who carries it to a table throws it out flat to be skirted, rolled and classed. The verb, to<i> f.-pick</i>, means `to be a fleece-picker'. To <i>p. up</i> is to do the actual <i>job. </i> <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> There was nothing new to me in picking up fleeces. ... I had often given a hand in the smaller woolsheds at shearing time, either penning up with the shepherds or picking up and helping the presser. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,167</i>) [NOTES: -->1964 Middleton Walk on the Beach 167 (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> picking up fleeces: the rouseabout (shearer's hand) picks up the newly-shorn fleece, throws it so that it lands spread out on the table, pulls off the short and dirty wool which had been under the belly (<i>bellies</i>), rolls the fleece and puts it into the press. Nowadays the shearer usually shears the belly separately before starting on the fleece. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... I was allowed to miss school to help in the shed by picking up the bellywool and removing the pizzle stains from the wether bellywool. I was also `tar-boy', which meant putting dabs of Stockholm tar on any cuts inflicted by the shearers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to collect wool from around a shearer's stand on the woolshed board.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The fleecies whose job was to pick up the fleeces ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,30</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> The fleecie picks up the fleece in such a way ... <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> It was always my job to turn the grindstone for them (shearers); to shed up, pick up, pen up, press up and be there pronto with the tar pot ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,93</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The naked feet of the brown women `picking up' from the shining greasy floor. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,11</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> `I don't need a shearer, but I need shed hands. Can you pick up?' <i>(1965,Skippers Road,89</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Jack Frost ... was `picking up'. and I was the rouseabout - penning up the sheep, sweeping the shearing board and bailing [sic] the wool. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,72</i>)

### pick up the ball and run with it
 
 <i>phr.</i> to take a forward moving initiative (from Ellis's supposed initiative in starting rugby union)

>  Wellington Chamber of Commerce members, criticising Economic Summit Meeting, said that now they `should pick up the ball and run with it' if things were to get done. <i>(Oct 3, 1984,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 2YA Wellington Local News Bull 8.05 am]

>  When I withdrew my services on October 31 Maori Affairs secretary Tamati Reedy picked it (the loan proposal) up and ran with it. Then it became a government negotiation. <i>(Dec 27, 1986,Dominion,2</i>)

### pick-and-go
 
 <i>n.</i> a rugby term for short runs from the scrum

>  The All Blacks were intent on going up the middle primarily using the pick 'n' go. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  But mostly, they slavishly stuck to one-off running from second phase and predictable pick-and-gos in the misguided belief Mealamu and Collins would crack the ironclad Australian defence. <i>(Nov 17, 2003,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  They were tight in the maul carving off good yardage at times; they used the pick' n' go up the middle; and they gave the ball width at other times. <i>(May 16, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

### pick-and-shovel work
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  GBP2 an acre would be paid which was more than a man could make at pick-and-shovel work. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

### pick-up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [rodeo riding] the person who plucks a fallen rider out of danger from his mount or retrieves a rider from the back of a beast, when his time is completed.

>  The whistle indicated that a rider had ridden the specified time. If he was still aboard, the pick-up man came forward on his quick, obedient horse and plucked him expertly from his turbulent mount. A rider could find himself down across the saddle...or with back hard against the shoulder of the pick-up's horse. <i>(Jan 30, 1963,Weekly News,40</i>)

### pick-up boat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i>Pick-up boat </i>- The boat used for picking-up whales killed by whale-chasers. The pick-up boat would then tow the whales to the whaling station for processing. Sometimes, if the mother ship was not available for pick-up work, a spare chaser or the one with the least work on hand would take on the task of picking up. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,228</i>)

### Pick6
 
 <i>n.</i> A TAB racing selection

>  [Hdg] Friday Pick6 still hot to trot... Today we have the Pick6 which still requires you to pick six winners, but you can invest at the TAB. <i>(Jul 17, 1997,Evening Post,21</i>)

### pickaninny
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [picaninny](per-pop.md#picaninny))

### pickapo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pikopo](per-pop.md#pikopo))

### picker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Fleece-picking. - As soon as a shearer has taken the fleece off and let the sheep go through the port-hole to the counting-out pen, the picker gathers the fleece up in such a manner that she or he can on getting to the rolling table, throw it out with the breech part at the right end. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed to pick foreign matter out of scoured wool.

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Picker - hand who picked residual foreign matter out of scoured wool before packing. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [shearing] the picker-up of fleeces from the board.

>  WANTED - 16 SHEARERS and 2 Fleece Pickers, at the Orari Gorge Station. <i>(Nov 13, 1869,Timaru Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> .. the picker gathers the fleece up in such a manner that he or she can on getting to the rolling table, throw it ... <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fat stock buyer.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Pickers of stock for the frozen meat trade ,too, are suspicious of blacks. <i>(1921,Tutira,379</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Miller took horse to ferry for Borthwick's picker. <i>(Apr 1, 1932,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  When a fat stock picker comes into a flock of lambs he may pick 30 per cent to 70 per cent of the mob. If..the picker has come in a little too late, then a high proportion of the lambs will be overfat. <i>(1966,NZ Journal of Agriculture,58</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHINSON</b> He had been employed as a picker for many years and many thousands of sheep and lambs had been felt and appraised by his experienced hands. <i>(1981,Hutchinsons of New Zealand, The,105</i>)

>  <b>FARAM</b> He used to buy in young cattle to fatten, and in 1916, one Brown, a picker, got 86 bullocks and 20 more a month later, so that Dad promptly bought another 90 weaners at the fair. <i>(1987,Jonathon Hutchinson of Parihohonu,32</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> I knew Jack didn't want to lose a fat-lamb picker and understood ... <i>(1990,Wild Horses & Me,148</i>)

### picker-up
 
 <i>n.</i> farming: a person who clears fleeces from the shearing board. plural:pickers-up

>  First, clean all the points, the crutchand the belly wool, and let this be sweptaside and taken away at once by the"picker-up". <i>(Sep 18, 1864,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the `pickers up' were busy gathering up the fleeces as they fell from the bereft sheep and carrying them to the sorting table, where they [173] were stripped of the `pieces', which were thrown aside, while the best of the fleece was quickly classified, rolled together, and deposited in a kind of bin, according to its quality, whether `clothing' or `combing.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  Evidence was given for the [shearers'] Union by Messrs. J. Ormiston (picker-up and woolroller), Charles Pearce (woolroller), C. Armstrong (woolpresser), W. Redyard (cook), and F. Waddell (Secretary of Canterbury Union). <i>(Dec 15, 1910,Maoriland Worker,4/3/1</i>)

>  Why shouldn't the `picker up' get as much as the wool-roller? Surely running up and down the board all day is as hard work as standing at a wool table. <i>(Mar 20, 1911,Maoriland Worker,7/15/</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `Them yella dogs ain't no good anyway,' said a `picker up'. <i>Ibid.</i> 57 The pickers-up gathered the fleeces as they fell intact from the shears and bore them to the sorting table where they were quickly `skirted' and `classed'. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,50</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> There are also baskets for the bellies and locks ... The `picker up' is the name given to the young man or boy whose duty it is to [75] carry the wool from the shearer to the skirting table ... <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,74</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> At shearing time [at Benmore, 1892] about 55 men were employed in the shed: 28 to 30 shearers, 2 penners-up, 4 pickers-up, 2 broomies, 6 rollers, 1 classer, 1 fleece carrier, 4 piece sorters, 3 pressers, 1 bale brander, 1 butcher and brander. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,208,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> PICKER-UP: One who picks up the fleece, and carries it to the wool-table. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111</i>)

>  <i>picker-up n. NZ & Austral</i>. person who gathers fleece when it is shorn from sheep. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,567</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  The picker-up, commonly called the "fleece-o," collected the shorn fleece, carried it to the rolling table, where the wool roller, after pulling off and tossing the skirts on one side, rolled it and passed it in his turn to the wool classer. <i>(Sep 23, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a shearing team who picks up fleeces from shearing board.

>  15s to GBP1 is the general wages paid to pickers up, and a boy usually has to pick up, sweep and tar, for eight shearers. <i>(May 12, 1893,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  In a shearing shed a number of boys are required as 'pickers'-up' [sic] of the wool as it is shorn, and appear to be as necessary to the shearer as his shears. <i>(Jan 21, 1903,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> eight shearers, one packer, one fleece roller and one picker-up. <i>(1865,Chudleigh Diary,23</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the 'pickers up' were busy gathering up the fleeces as they fell from the bereft sheep ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  <b>BISLEY</b> By retaining hold at the points originally gripped and throwing the rest of the fleece gently away from him, the picker-up should be able to spread it neatly, tip uppermost, on the wool tables, without causing any break in it. <i>(1897,Practical Treatise on Wool and Sheep Breeding,36</i>)

>  The picker-up must be shown how to lift the fleece from the board and how to deliver it upon the rolling-table. <i>(Oct 1902,NZ Farmer,567</i>)

>  The picker-up should keep his broom going sweeping to one side of the boards ... <i>(Nov 1917,NZ Farmer,1238</i>)

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> As soon as the fleece is shorn off, the 'picker up' or 'fleece O' should be ready to take it up immediately. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,16</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> Joe was shearing in the woolshed on his pat. As shearer, roller, picker-up and broomie all in one. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,21</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> The picker-up was raring to go. [picker up] <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,93</i>)

### picker-upper
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [docking] the person who picks up and holds lambs for castrating, tailing or marking.

>  <b>Stapylton-Smith, M</b> As 'picker-upper,' I was at least able to ensure that the youngest, smallest lambs were tailed first. <i>(1990,Other End of the Harbour, The,137</i>)

### Picket-Fence
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> WAITAHANUI About 11 kilometres South of Taupo, the highway crosses this famous stream alongside the Lake. The `rip', where the stream enters the lake, attracts dozens of anglers who are content to stand waist-deep and shoulder-to-shoulder. Collectively, these enthusiasts are known as `The Picket-Fence.' <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,281</i>)

### pickililli
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  His Worship:What's that? Dly [sic] lily. What sort of lily? - Small flower; it made the grow up flower. His Worship: What the Maoris used to call pickililli, I suppose. <i>(Jun 8, 1912,NZ Truth,363/6/1</i>)

### picking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As the time between the shearing and picking and culling - picking out the fat sheep to send to the freezing works and culling the old ewes from the young ones - was not a particularly busy one..Heather was free to do more in the house. <i>(Oct 14, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) skirting of locks from a fleece.

>  <b>HASTIE</b> I sent Edwin and Thomas to the picking of locks of wool in the shed. <i>(Jan 22, 1885,Diary</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) selection or drafting of stock, usually lambs for slaughter.

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> For the first practice in backing live sheep, the race used for fat lamb picking should be used. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,41</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> Hang on until after shearing and the lamb picking is finished, in about two months' time. <i>(1990,Wild Horses & Me,144</i>)

### picking bag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fruit-picker's bag [usually slung from the shoulders, and fastened around the waist].

>  The fruit should be carefully picked into a suitable picking-bag, and from thence equally carefully into the orchard picking-box. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,210</i>)

>  Picking bags should be repaired if there are any holes whcih allow fruit to fall out and be damaged. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,117</i>)

>  What about the picking bag? Do you empty it frequently, or is it full of "buttons" and small twigs? <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,59</i>)

>  Each handling, from the placing of the fruit in the picking bag or box until it finally packed, should be carried out with care. <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,41</i>)

### picking gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of fruit or vegetable harvesters.

>  During the period the picking is light an opportunity should be taken to overhaul the equipment of the picking gangs. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,117</i>)

### picking pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or cattle pen where selections are made by a stock buyer or picker.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Manu and I waded through the massed sheep; we had already filled the 'crown' and 'picking' pens from the first mob in. The buyer was an old friend who had picked the lambs for me since I had started farming. The crown pen was designed to hold enough sheep to fill the two picking pens twice ... <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,155</i>)

### picking up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> fleece-picking [1988 citation belongs with the verb to pick up]

>  <b>HARPER</b> They both helped too with the drafting and dipping, and picking up in the woolshed - usually barefoot. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,30</i>)

>  A rouseabout - also variously known as a rousie, woolhandler, shedhand, fleeco, the person `picking up' or a blue tongue - is responsible for the passage of wool after being shorn from the sheep's back to the wool press where it is baled. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCOTT</b> <i>Picking up</i> The process by which a planing machine produces planed timber which has a hairy surface. This may be the result of blunted or wrongly angled planing knives or reverse grain or tension wood in the timber, or a combination of these. Such hairiness is very hard to eradicate. <i>(1987,Imported Timbers,71</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> `Picking up' on a frosty morning is an unpleasant job and, with a big `kill' takes a long time. Rideable country is the best because a an carrying twenty-five rabbits had quite a good load, and a horse can take many more. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,61</i>)

### picking-box
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [orcharding] a field case into which the contents of picking-bags are placed.

>  The fruit should be carefully picked into a suitable picking-bag, and from thence equally carefully into the orchard picking-box, which should be well made and perfectly smooth on the inside. Picking-boxes when full should be handled as carefully as possible. <i>(Apr 20, 1920,NZ Journal of Agriculture,210</i>)

### picking-up team
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [haymaking] the team picking up bales for stacking or storage.

>  It was gruelling work for the senior college lads who made up part of the contractor's picking-up team. <i>(Jan 16, 1963,Weekly News,43</i>)

### pickings
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  If Agents-General are to be at liberty to engage in private speculation for which their position gives them facilities, then these must be considered as "pickings" and taken into account in fixing the salary. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> trimmings

>  <b>GORDON</b> And at last it was finished. Old Tar's house, painted white with green `pickings', reared up ready at the top of the hill. <i>(1974,Undiscovered Country,301</i>) [NOTES: 1913 Mansfield Old Tar in 1974 Gordon Undiscovered Country 301]

>  West's and Royal Pictures. <i>(Mar 16, 1910,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: (1910 Mar 16) 1952 Oct 1 Evening Post 14 [King's Theatre Programme of 16 March 1910]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> ?feed for sheep etc.

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `How about I get half a dozen [sheep] down into the pen this evening ... There's enough pickings for them there.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,60</i>)

### pickle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The pelt curer took the density of his pickle with a potato, and judged the acidity by tasting it. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in New Zealand, preserves including separate pieces of fruit or vegetable

>  Pickle can usually be used in a broad sense to encompass any food that is preserved in brine or vinegar, which explains the use of the word by supermarkets to mark the relevant aisle. However, in traditional New Zealand preserving, the word pickle is usually reserved for preserves in which the pieces of fruit or vegetable remain separate, most obviously in onions and gherkins, but also in chow chow and picalilli in which the vegetables are preserved in a thickened mustard sauce. <i>(May 15, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRACE</b> The goldsmith threw the ring in the `pickle', a green, deadly looking chemical in an earthenware pot on the floor. <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,12</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> in various exclamatory expressions: pickle my ...

>  <b>HELMER</b> `...I gets down to the stag. Wull, pickle me bloody agates, would you believe it? The bullet has caught the old boy clean in the family jewels.' <i>(1964,Stag Party,82</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `Well pickle my tit', he says, if it isn't young Blue. <i>(1964,Watersiders,109</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pickle my daisies! </i>exclamation of doubt; eg `Pickle my daisies if that's true.' <i>[Shepard] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

### pickled
 
 <i>adj.</i>

We now have laboratory tests for every stage from the sheep's back to the pickled skin stored in its cask. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  Got remarkably pickled. <i>(Sep 7, 1942,NZEF Times,II/63 : 5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ? NZ =Salted pork or 'corned' pork or beef

 <i>n.</i>

### pickthank
 
 <i>adj.</i>
