### pickup baler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early haypress or baler.

>  The farm is highly mechanised, the implements being as follows:- 1 18 h.p. pickup baler, which can press up to 900 bales per day. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,63</i>)

>  Where a pickup baler is not available, alternative machinery reduces harvesting costs. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,175</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> During World War Two a second tractor was purchased and a pick-up baler. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,173</i>)

### picnic
 
 <i>n.</i> awkward adventure, unpleasant experience, troublesome job.

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Picnic, n.</i> Besides the ordinary meaning of this word, there is a slang Australian use denoting an awkward adventure, an unpleasant experience, a troublesome job. In America the slang `an easy or agreeable thing.' (`Standard.') The Australasian use is an ironical inversion of this. -->1896. Modern: -->`If a man's horse is awkward and gives him trouble, he will say, `I had a picnic with that horse,' and so of any misadventure or disagreeable experience in travelling. So also of a troublesome business or other affair; a nursemaid, for instance, will say, `I had a nice picnic with Miss Nora's hair.'' [Check: omit ?] <i>(1898,Austral English,351</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The expressions `too true,' `too right,' in which<i> too</i> has its old intensive value, are in common use; so are `that'll be a picnic', `what a picnic' (where <i>picnic</i> = `trouble, bother') <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i> sl. a troublesome job; an awkward expirience; a difficult adventure. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <i>picnic n</i>. a wild confusion, a messup, a difficult task, unpleasant experience. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>picnic n. (Coll.)</i> wild confusion, messup, difficult task, unpleasant experience. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>picnic ... NZ & Austral. colloq</i>. awkward task or situation, a fuss (<i>we're in for a picnic, and no mistake, if the river floods). </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,567</i>) [NOTES: [contrast Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 856, sense 3]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>picnic</i> problem; ironic, reversal of what is usually a pleasnt occasion; eg `If you take the boat out in that weather, mate, it's your picnic.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

>  When he saw our predicament, he came to the rescue with some precious petrol. Even then it was a picnic pouring it in the tank without the rain pouring in too. <i>(2009,NZ Memories,77/9</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a racing club

These meetings.. are often conducted in a "picnic atmosphere".<i>Ibid.</i> 172 Wairoa.. is one of the North Island "picnic" clubs... It relies on the picnic atmosphere as its main attraction. <i>(1970,Horse Racing, Trotting & Dog Racing in NZ,163</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### picnic meeting
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Pictonian

>  <b>BARRER</b> The early history of Picton and Blenheim is marked by bitter struggles between the two towns for the honour of being Marlborough's capital and seat of Government. There are many old Pictonians who deserve a place in these pages. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,242</i>)

>  The Representation Act is supposed to give every place "representation according to population" (?) and so we can only conclude that a Wellingtonian is only half as good as a Pictonian, or two-thirds as good as an Ashburtonian. <i>(Oct 19, 1881,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  .."Marlborough, however, carried its point, and Picton became a capital among New Zealand capitals, till further jealousy removed its honours to Blenheim, a thoroughly Pictonian version of the reason why.'[sic] <i>(Jul 19, 1873,Marlborough Express,7</i>)

>  "I suppose you have encountered worde gales than this?" asked an inquisitive Pictonian of a sailorman during a rough trip across the Strait ... <i>(Oct 10, 1904,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Young Pictionian Rihari Watson says Cactus inspired him to consider a career in the air force. <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.dia.govt.nz/Pubforms.nsf/URL/COMMUNITYMATTERS_Spring2008.pdf]

### picture bus
 
 <i>n.</i>

### picture crowd
 
 <i>n.</i>

### picture hall
 
 <i>n.</i> picture theatre in a country district: ?town-hall as picture theatre

>  Wests' Proprietary have secured sites for picture halls in Christchurch, Dunedin, and Auckland. <i>(Jul 30, 1909,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Nothing much happened to break the days or evenings, except young 'prentices in the picture-hall banging their seats and chanting: `Siddown, siddown', if a Homie rose ceremoniously ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,138</i>)

>  St. Thomas's Hall, Newtown. The People's Popular Picture Hall. <i>(Nov 15, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  advertiser wishes to meet Man with little capital, for fisrt-class investment in new picture hall. <i>(Mar 25, 1911,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  It has been known for some time that Messrs. John Fuller and Sons have contemplated the rebuilding of the popular picture house, His Majesty's Theatre, in Courtenay-place.... In the first place, it is to be a theatre proper, not simply a picture hall. <i>(Jul 18, 1911,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The Opera House is to be again opened as a permanent picture show.... This will again make three permanent moving picture establishments [in Palmerston North], and it is understood that there is a probability of a fourth picture hall being opened at an early date. <i>(Oct 18, 1911,Evening Post,4</i>)

### picture house
 
 <i>n.</i> cinema, picture theatre (See [cf. picture hall](per-pop.md#picture-hall))

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> The milk bars, the rendezvous of the `Land of the Long White Cloud', are interspersed and barnacled to the Picture House. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,12</i>)

>  I first heard him speak at the littleLondon theatre - Auckland's one and onlysixpenny picture house. <i>(1934,Journalese,57</i>)

>  It has been known for some time that Messrs. John Fuller and Sons have contemplated the rebuilding of the popular picture house, His Majesty's Theatre, in Courtenay-place.... In the first place, it is to be a theatre proper, not simply a picture hall. <i>(Jul 18, 1911,Evening Post,7</i>)

### picture night
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

M'Mahon and Donelly have secured an extended lease of the building and propose to run a continuous all-day picture show. <i>(Mar 13, 1911,Evening Post,8</i>)

This will again make three permanent moving picture establishments [in Palmerston North], and it is understood that there is a probability of a fourth picture hall being opened at an early date. <i>(Oct 18, 1911,Evening Post,4</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

Most of these are most luxuriously appointed and compare more than favourably with those in other countries which have to cater for much bigger and, in certain cases, more highly discriminating audiences. <i>(Aug 7, 1930,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <i>picture theatre NZ & Austral</i>. cinema. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,567</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Workmen are busily engaged at present in the old Federal Billiard Saloon in Manners-street, renovating the historic hall and putting it in order for a picture theatre. Messrs. M'Mahon and Donelly have secured an extended lease of the building and propose to run a continuous all-day picture show. <i>(Mar 13, 1911,Evening Post,8</i>)

### picture-card
 
 <i>n.</i> a cigarette card [ca1933 G.T. Diary (Dannevirke) `Picture-cards' was the usual name given by the kids to `cigarette cards'.]

### pictures
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The pictures are the latest from the best English and Continental makers, and are described as of great interest and to have been specially selected to suit all tastes. <i>(Sep 17, 1909,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Pictures at Lower Hutt. At the Kings George Theatre, Lower Hutt, tomorrow night, the principal attraction will be a detective drama entitled "The Diamond Makers". <i>(Jun 9, 1914,Evening Post,2</i>)

### piddle out
 
 <i>v.</i> peter out

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The road, never one to be deterred by the terrain, obliged [15] by following each of its little, tortuous curves and contours until it piddled out, predictably twisting across a sizeable plateau towards the last homestead and property along its route. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,14</i>)

### pidgin-Maori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Unaware that his new and distinguished dusky friend had received, and profited by, some of the benefits of pakeha civilization, the descendant, by name, of the great Roman conqueror carried on the conversation in a mixture of languages which can be well described as "pidgin Maori." <i>(May 2, 1914,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  We did expect that Mr. Bryce would have declined to have his plain English speech converted into pigeon.Maori [sic]; but sad to relate, even he has succumbed to the evil influences of his surroundings, and has produced in his letter to Wahanui a document that would have delighted the heart of Sir Donald McLean. <i>(Dec 8, 1882,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  They wanted to marry him to one of the pretty girls of the settlement, but it wouldn't go down with Broady, who earnestly assured them in his best pigeon Maori that he was aready a much-married man. <i>(Nov 4, 1893,Observer (Auckland),7</i>)

>  A Maori abroad would use Aotearoa in general reference to his homeland, unless he used the pidgin-Maori "Niu-Tireno". <i>(Nov 2, 1907,Evening Post,13</i>)

### pie
 
 <i>adj.</i> [ca1938 The timber was not set pie (on the stud) The brick landed pie on his head.] (See [cf. pie on](per-pop.md#pie-on); [half-pie](h-hea.md#half-pie))



 
 <i>n.</i> the collected remnants of sheepskin from which the wool is plucked [ca1940 at least] (See [pie-picker](per-pop.md#pie-picker))

>  <b>CARR</b> ... there is only one job that the most fastidious could refuse, and that is `picking pie'. Tuis [?=This] consist [sic] in pulling the wool off the trimmings that have been taken off by the `wool pullers' at the beam. These trimmings are ... allowed to rot until the wool comes away easily. [Pie pickers] live and eat by themselves. <i>(1913,Country Work,19</i>)

### PIE
 
 <i>abbr.</i> (Commerce) portfolio investment entity, a diversified investment scheme. [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Although the PIE rules only come into effect in October this year, there is already a large amount of activity in the funds management industry as people gear up to become PIEs. <i>(Apr 18, 2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: http://www.bellgully.com/resources/resource.01203.asp]

>  The tax paid by a PIE (in most cases currently taxed at 33%) on the sale of shares of New Zealand companies and listed Australian companies will be exempt. <i>(Apr 2006,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: http://www.bellgully.com/newletters/12financial/legislation_Taxing_offshore_investment]

>  PIE is changing the way that individuals are taxed within managed funds. <i>(Aug 1, 2006,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: http://scoop.co.nz/stories/BU0608/S00011.htm]

>  PIEs are normally diversified investment schemes. It remained to be seen whether the extra 3c tax break would encourage more depositors to switch to PIEs. It [the Reserve bank] expected PIEs "to become a popular way to earn interest". <i>(Jul 31, 2007,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### pie house
 
 <i>n.</i> the place where pie is kept

>  <b>GORDON</b> [The <i>chopper </i>also <i>scalps</i> them which gets taken off as the <i>ear-piece</i>, and goes down to the <i>pie-house</i>, on to the <i>pie-heap</i> where it rots and is plucked by the <i>pie-pickers. </i> <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... you were on hourly rates shoving the heavy-laden trolleys of sheepskins from the painters to the pullers or working in the piehouse where it got into you and you could never go to the pictures. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67</i>)

### pie on
 
 <i>phr.</i> very good, all right [from Maori e pai ana] [also as exclamation (she's right, ok)] (See [cf. kapai](k-kea.md#kapai))

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> very good: New Zealanders: 20c. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pie</i> good, <i>pie on</i> expert or efficient, good at, from Maori `pai', good, `pai ana', suitable; eg `That paint job was pie on, ta muchly.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pai - good, kind, well; e pai ano - please, good <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/376</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pie on.</i> Very good: New Zealanders': C. 20. Prob. ex Maori <i>pai ana. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,626</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> When we gets the old gig properly worked over everything should be pie-on. <i>Ibid.</i> 124 `Well,' thinks Jake, `this is a nice sort of hitch. Just as we were getting everything pie-on as Uncle Ted calls it.' <i>Ibid.</i> 151 `Surely the gig's properly set up now, Uncle Ted,' he says. `Everything's what you call pie-on.' <i>Ibid.</i> 166 When we gets the old gig properly worked over everything should be pie-on. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,119,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Northland)]

>  <b>HULME</b> [e pai ana] `I didn't see you come back.' `O, I been here', the old [Maori] lady grins. `Listening. E pai ana.' <i>(1983,Bone People, The,301</i>)

### pie on/at
 
 <i>phr.</i> expert at, very good at. [dubious; cf. DNZE]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>To be pie at</i> (or <i>on</i>), to be expert or efficient at something, is another phrase of wide use in this country ... from the Maori <i>pai</i>, good <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> `to be pie on' (=to be very good at) seems to be derived from the Maori <i>pai ana. </i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

### pie wool
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. pie n](per-pop.md#pie))

>  Note ? : [Passenger vessel] does not accept first slipe, 2nd slipe, pie wool class 1, pie wool class 2, Hides, Clippings. <i>(Dec 16, 1953,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: 1953 Dec 16 Shaw, Savill and Albion Coy Timetable.]

>  ... some 7 per cent of this freezing works wool is produced from the skin ..... In the past these skin pieces were rotted and the wool recovered, since skin rots faster than wool. The process was unpleasant and the wool (known as `pie' wool) often suffered fibre damage.' <i>(Jun 13, 1957,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Some mention should be made of `pie-wool'. This is the wool from pieces which are trimmed off the woolly skin, and are not `painted' with depilatory. Pie-wool is loosened by allowing the skin to putrefy, a repulsive and laborious job, and after separation the wool is washed and dried. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)

>  <i>Pie Wool</i> All the head, ear , cod , shank, and pullers' skin pieces ... are taken to the pie house, where under normal atmospheric conditions they are allowed to sweat in heaps ... <i>(Sep 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,261</i>)

### pie-cart
 
 <i>n.</i> a mobile food shop for mainly takeaway meals. [aka pie cart]

>  And at 2 p.m. - Coffee Stand Plant, consisting of 1 Coffee Stall on wheels, and 1 Hot Pie Cart (without reserve) ... <i>(Mar 4, 1887,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Letitia Berkley was well known to the Dunedin public, peelers and beaks as the Fountain and its two pie carts. <i>(Nov 7, 1914,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Over the road now I see the pie-cart halted by the kerb ... The same old pie-cart we used to wander down to from the hostel ... We strolled down late at night for pea pie and spud steaming hot in the white wrapping paper through the hatch ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,163</i>)

>  `They didn't need it,' said the Grim Dig. `They were all issued with piecarts, I think.' `Makes me hungry, all this matter [sic, =? natter] about food,' I said. `Me too, said the Grim Dig. `Lets go and have some savs and mash.' <i>(Jun 4, 1959,Upper Hutt Leader</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> The rest of the staff ... spent the hour dining or having a snack at the pie-cart, .. <i>(1960,Word for Word,201</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> The <i>sine qua non</i> are all here - the closely-shorn air-strip, the public park and the pie cart, our wayside coffee stall, up to date, roomy, fully equipped. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,12</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,243</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1969,Joy of the Worm,243</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> Lacking [42] main centre status [the thirteen smaller centres] clamour for the outward and visible signs of city stature - a university, a new airport, a Government Life building, a railway, a piecart. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,41</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> As a resident, John was virtually a regular, treated to fare markedly superior to the `pea, pie, and pud' he had at the pie cart on the way back from university on week nights. <i>(1978,It Was So Late,xii</i>) [NOTES: 1978 ed. note in 1949 Cole It Was So Late (1978) xii]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>pie-cart</i>: a house trailer fixed up as a TRAVELLING KITCHEN dispensing meat pies (and often fish and chips, dim sims and anything else that can be fried) through a hatch running the entire length of one side. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,76</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Nevertheless, going out to dinner for many Kiwis [in the 1960's] still meant the fish and chip shop or the pie cart. The smartest pie carts were (and still are) converted buses with canvas awnings. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,33</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> ...going out with boys or loitering ... outside the pie cart after the pictures. <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,107,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pie cart n. NZ </i>van etc. from which hot takeaway food is sold. [PIE 1] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,567</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 365]]

>  Anyone under 40 probably knows the name Elvis Presley, says Marcus... Someone said they saw him at the Taumarunui pie cart last week. Or maybe it was Te Kuiti. <i>(Aug 17, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66</i>)

>  Super Liquor owners ... lodged a complaint with the ... Council about the smell, noise and drunkenness associated with the piecart, which has been sited outside their shop. <i>(May 14, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>) [NOTES: Original from <i>Motueka-Golden Bay News </i> 8/3/94]

>  There are those who would no doubtbelieve that in this age of fancyrestaurants, trendy bars and home-delivery pizza joints there is no placefor that grand old New Zealand tradition,the all-night pie cart. <i>(Jan 15, 1993,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> 'Where d'you want to go?' asked Hal, of everyone. And they answered ... pie cart ... bed ... home ... Los Angeles. <i>(1990,Bad Music,94</i>)

>  The "bush express", which consists of one carriage known as the "pie-cart" and a light locomotive, leaves the cookshop daily at 7.15 a.m. <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> A chance remark about pie carts led me, in a weak moment, to defend those aromatic and enticing vehicles... "But Anne! No lady would go to a pie cart at midnight - and alone!!!" <i>(1939,Farmer's Wife, The,220</i>)

>  We need a pie-cart, or perhaps two pie-carts. <i>(Aug 16, 1943,NZEF Times,III/12 : 6</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The smartest pie carts were (and still are) converted buses with canvas awnings. <i>(1982,200 Years of NZ Food & Cookery,33</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Eager crowds no longer gathered round the piecart ordering great helpings of "pea, pie and spud" or "steaka da bif". <i>(1971,Our Open Country,244</i>)

>  After feeding hungry Wellingtonians burgers and pies 24 hours a day for six years, Norman Donald has put his landmark pie cart up for sale... His first pie cart was on the Rotorua lakefront in 1991. <i>(Aug 16, 1999,Dominion,11</i>)

>  From inside, on this morning soon after midnight, came the sound of a trumpet, a melancholy wailing of night shifts, dislocated camaraderie and pie-cart feeds. <i>(Jan 31, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  They [sc. the tourists] seem to overlook Dunedin's superlative, de luxe, too too utterly devastating pie-cart. <i>(May 12, 1938,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  We won't recommend his pie-cart license to be renewed. <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,IV/132</i>)

>  Plates stamped "pie-cart de luxe," glass bowls of toothpicks.. there were a few points which appealed to the aesthetic tastes of the pie-cart frequenter. <i>(Dec 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 33</i>)

>  A thoughtful host had annexed a pie-cart from town. <i>(Jun 12, 1935,NZ Free Lance,12</i>)

>  Here [sc. at Port Craig] we had a ride on the sawmill train, great high unwieldy object, known locally as the pie-cart. <i>(Jan 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,35</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> You've got the pie-cart, known as the kai-cart, on the left. <i>(1957,Tavern in the Town,150</i>) [NOTES: 1984 edition]

>  <b>MILLER</b> Jones made the mistake of involving himself in an altercation with a constable at the piecart. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,35</i>)

>  Piecarts are central components of Kiwi urban identity (they say a town never really qualifies for city status till it has an all-night piecart) and Wellington's was no exception. <i>(Mar 13, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  'Pie Cart Rock'n'Roll', a Johnny Cooper original, stands as his finest performance of the genre and is a strong contender for the title of New Zealand's first rock'n'roll classic. <i>(Apr 3, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  He is Southland raised.. has been married twice and cites the Gore piecart as his favourite eatery - only half in jest. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  The pie cart is feeding drunks and the Criterion Hotel is playing glam rock. <i>(Sep 28, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),20</i>)

>  The pie cart is feeding drunks and the Criterion Hotel is playing glam rock. <i>(Sep 28, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),20</i>)

>  I once thought that if ever I was moved to write a third book on old Wellington days, I hoped ... to devote a separate chapter to the famous pie-cart men of the city. ... It was a treat ... to push yourself up under the surrounding canvas, right into the bright lights and warmth of stove, and order a `pea-piepud' for sixpence. <i>(Oct 8, 1977,Dominion,2</i>) [NOTES: (1904, May 11) 1977 Oct 8 Dominion 2]

>  <b>TURNER</b> We [NZders in England] drifted down to the pie-cart (coffee-stall in your speech) <i>(1922,Happy Wanderer,37</i>)

>  [Cartoon caption satirising upgrading of name] From Pie Cart to Coffee Stall. <i>(1934,Otago Capping Book,28</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Two cadgers whined for the price of a meal. Timothy felt his panache come back. ... `Come along to the pie-cart, boys.' <i>Ibid.</i> 244 You'll admire the old sport in the pie-cart, Mac. ... Bede heard him say: `Come along to the pie-cart, I can hear my belt-buckle click against my spine.' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,125,1970 ed.</i>)

>  I found myself outside a grill-room, ... a poky, cheap-looking, ... kind of joint, just one degree better than a pie-cart. <i>(Nov 1954,Numbers,2/23</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Sturm)]

### piece
 
 <i>n.</i> a piece (of bread and butter), snack [ca1920 G.L. Gabites, South Canterbury Applied also to a piece of apple etc ca1930 J.L.Kelly, West Coast. `Gis a piece, mum', the usual request for something to eat on return from school.]

>  <b>FRAME</b> We banged on the bedroom wall, `Mum, bring us a piece!' She brought us a piece of bread and jam. <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,105,1966 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (`Royal Icing')]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> After breakfast, taking what Renwick called a `piece' in their pockets, Gilbert and Leslie [?] started on foot for Waterfall Gully. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,87</i>) [NOTES: [?bread and butter; ?snack]]

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [Reference Porirua 1850s] I do not seem to remember breakfast that morning - we had `pieces' in the boat, ... <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,30</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 30]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Run along and brush yourself, do. Eliza, dearie, wouldn't you like a piece before you go? <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,7,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> My chief recollection of that visit is that Mrs McKellar gave me a big piece. [?c1870s] <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,39,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KINROSS</b> She would always stop ... to cut me a `piece', or give me an apple or a slab of cheese, and she would let me lick out the mixing bowl when she did the baking. <i>(1963,Please to Remember,69</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> Peter stared insolently at her but only because he wanted something to eat. `I want a piece', he said. / `You pick up your schoolbag ... or their won't be any piece!' <i>(1963,Coal Flat,120</i>)

>  Then it was time to pack up. Last snacks were handed round to the children (`pieces' we called them). <i>(Oct 5, 1963,Dominion,9</i>) [NOTES: [R. Orr writing about Organised Picnics, 50 years ago]]



 
 <i>n.</i> farming: (plural) oddments of fleece wool

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The big stations may make classing a very simple business, ... and may even divide up the skirtings or pieces from the fleece into different classes, besides keeping the bellies, locks, and stained wool separate. <i>Ibid.</i> 148 The belly wool and the pieces or locks are kept separate from the fleece when rolling it, ... <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,144</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The skirtings of the neck and shoulder can be called first pieces, and the britch ends made into second pieces. The `bellies' can be placed in a pack hung on the shearing-board, and when two of these are filled they can be pressed. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,121</i>)

>  `Pieces' are mainly skirtings other than necks or bellies and consist of heavy-conditioned, stained or inferior wool removed from the fleece. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> I take the next [bale of wool] and weigh it. `Bellies and pieces,' they say. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,2,1940 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `You Can't Go Three Days']

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1951,Early Country Runholders,378</i>)

>  Baskets or skeps on wheels are handy for holding pieces and can be wheeled to the press from the table ... <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,311</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They also swept up the bellies and pieces, and so kept the shearing board clean and tidy. A young Maori helped in the sheep-yards drafting out the strangers, as it is taboo to shear sheep belonging to another station. <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,32,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Erina had gathered up Mutu's last fleece and thrown it expertly on the table before the classer. A few random wrist movements removed the necks and edging pieces. <i>Ibid.</i> 166 `What's 2nd Pcs H?' `Grade 2, pieces - that's skirtings - hogget wool.' `And Bls E?' `That's belly wool. E grade bellies.' `Lox?' `Oh, that's floor sweepings.' `What about Lbs Wigs?' `Lamb top-knots - off the head.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,3</i>)

>  The shorn fleeces are picked up from the board floor and thrown out on to a slatted `skirting' table, where they are trimmed of all inferior pieces. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,66</i>)

>  Body wool trimmings removed from the fleece when it is skirted after shearing. The longer pieces are termed first or bulky pieces while the shorter pieces are termed second pieces. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,57</i>)

>  <i>pieces</i> pl. <i>n. N.Z. </i>fragments of fleece wool. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,856</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 568; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 365]]

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Pieces</i> see skirtings. <i>Ibid.</i> 17 ... colonial woolscourers who removed grease, sticks and thistles from the locks and pieces - the dirty, inferior quality skirtings trimmed from the fleeces. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Fleece</i>. The main part of the sheep's wool when shorn. It is picked up in one piece and carried to the wool table, where it is skirted and classed. The small bits and pieces that are cut off separately by the shearer are the locks, bellies and pieces. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  ... the latter is always skirted and classed, whereas the black fleece was rolled up just as taken from the sheep's back, the belly-pieces being also left in. ... For example, white wool divides, when skirted by the roller at the wool-table, thus: Belly pieces, say, 6d. per pound; first pieces, 8d. per pound; ... fragments or locks, 4d. per pound. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/`196</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> NEW ZEALAND. - Scoured fleece, 1s. 8-1/2d. ...; average flocks, 1s. 5d. ...; pieces and locks, 1s. 1d. ... unwashed, 10-1/2d. ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),234</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the `pickers up' were busy gathering up the fleeces as they fell from the bereft sheep and carrying them to the sorting table, where they [173] were stripped of the `pieces', which were thrown aside, while the best of the fleece was quickly classified, rolled together, and deposited in a kind of bin, according to its quality, whether `clothing' or `combing.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The waste pieces [sc wool trimmed from fleeces by the fleece-picker] are thrown into a heap, where they are again picked over before being sent on to the scouring works. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,244</i>)

>  <b>STRACHAN</b> We had [in 1858] an old tent pitched inside and had about half a bale of pieces as a field bed. ... after wading through the Ahuriri we had to take off our pants before making a camp bed of shingle and snowgrass. <i>(1917,Experiences</i>) [NOTES: 1917 James Strachan Experiences (Hocken L.) in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 65]



 
 <i>n.</i> an event, sp. WWI

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>piece, (right) through the</i>. For the duration of the War : military coll., mostly New Zealand : 1915; ob. B.& P. Ex sitting through a play. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,626</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a loose, often soiled, outer skirting removed from a fleece before it is rolled.

>  Wool sale. Murray Lot 3 1 bag, X locks and pieces 2d. <i>(Dec 5, 1868,Weekly News,9</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the 'pickers up' were busy gathering up .. where they were stripped of the pieces ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  If the flockmaster decide [sic] to shear his sheep in the grease, like care ought to be taken to separate carefully the locks and pieces from the fleeces. <i>(Sep 1882,Northern New Zealand Settler,31</i>)

>  .. last year my clip, including pieces, bellies and locks, averaged gross in Timaru ... <i>(1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,9</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Put up two temporary wool bins in the woolshed for the bellies and pieces ... <i>(Oct 14, 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>BLACK & DYSON</b> Remove discoloured pieces, bellies, locks ... <i>(1978,Carpetmakers: Specialty Carpet Wool breeds of NZ,14</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> When you're rolling up the fleeces, skirting off the daggy pieces ... <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,82</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> .. you can divide the bellies and pieces into crutchings (from the back end) bellies, leg pieces and top knots (the short hairy pieces from the top of the head and cheeks.) <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,84</i>)

>  Bulky skirtings, second shear bellies and pieces and first crutching were unchanged on Nov 8. <i>(Nov 24, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard,9</i>)

>  Auckland Wools on the London Market .. scoured half-bred piece, U with I under, 3 bales, 1s 2 1/2d... <i>(Sep 28, 1876,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### piece of piss
 
 <i>phr.</i> something easy

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> I've got it all worked out and your part is a piece of piss. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,84</i>)

>  <i>a piece of piss:</i> easy <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### pieces bin
 
 <i>n.</i> (in a woolshed)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The wool bins are hinged, and can thus be swung against the wall when shearing is finished. The pieces bins are collapsible, and therefore able to be removed when not in use. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,20</i>)

### piecey
 
 <i>n.</i> a piece (of bread and butter)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Would anybody here fancy a piecey?' asked Mrs Kelly. ... I followed her to the pantry, hoped she would put some of her great plum jam on the pieces of bread. <i>(1968,Sydney Bridge,13</i>)

### pied fantail
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) piwakawaka, piwahawaha, tiwakawaka, tiwai-waka

>  Pied fantail are insect eaters but may live and breed in suitable gardens. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,25</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 38. RHIPIDURA FLABELLIFERA. Gml. Pied Fantail. Piwahawaha <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,14</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Pied and Black Fantail. <i>Ibid.</i> 143 {1888 2nd edn 1/69} RHIPIDURA FLABILLIFERA. (PIED -FANTAIL.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Piwaiwaka, Tiwai-waka, Piwakawaka, [Tirairaka, Pirairaka] and Tiwakawaka [and Pitakataka]. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,LIX</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> ... we find the flycatcher, of which there are here two species, namely, the pied fantail, <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, called by the Maoris piwakawaka ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,152</i>)

>  35. <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, Gml. Pied Fantail, Pied Flycatcher, Ti-waka-waka <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,374</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i> Gm. Pied Fantail. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/90</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i> Gm. Pied Fantail. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/90</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>pi[i]wakawaka</i>, a bird (Rhipidura flabellifera), pied fantail: the so-called black fantail, which is chocolate-colour (R. fuliginosa) is ti[i]wakawaka. The former is common throughout Newzealand; the latter is common in the South Island but rather rare in the North and is thought to be a mutant of the former. Both names were applied to both birds by the Maori, but for distinction pi[i]wakawaka for the pied and ti[i]wakawaka for the black have been adopted by the Pakeha. The bird is noted for the disaster it caused to Maui in his struggle with the mother of night, Hine-nui-te-po, Death personified. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FANTAIL <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i> Maori name: Piwakawaka. ... Birds of normal colour pattern (`Pied Fantail') make up most of the population in North Island, but the `Black Fantail' (i.e. dark phase) accounts for perhaps a quarter of the population of the South Island subpecies; ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  Fantail <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) North Island Fantail <i>R.f. placabilis </i>BANGS, 1921. South Island Fantail <i>R.f. fulginosa</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787). Chatham Island Fantail <i>R.f. penitus</i> BANGS, 1911. OTHER NAMES: <i>Piwakawaka, pied fantail, black fantail. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,280</i>)

>  The pied fantail and the ground lark, or pipit, favour most parts of the forest. <i>(Jun 1, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### pied oystercatcher
 
 <i>n.</i> red-bill

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 68. HAEMATOPUS LONGIROSTRIS. Vieill. Pied Oyster-catcher. Red-bill. Torea. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,26</i>)

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Platycercus auriceps</i>, yellow-top paroquet; <i>Nestor meridionalis</i>, brown parrot; <i>Carpophaga Novae Zealandiae</i>, wood pigeon; <i>Haematopus unicolor</i>, black oyster catcher, or red bill; <i>Haematopus longirostris</i>, pied oyster catcher; <i>Ocydromus australis</i>, Weka, or wood hen; <i>Casarca variegata</i>, Paradise duck; <i>(1872,Proceedings Otago Provincial Council,39</i>) [NOTES: AppendixBlack Stewart Island]

>  The Pied and Black Oyster-catchers, and Pied and Black Stilts, all occur on the main river beds; ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/209</i>) [NOTES: Travers NZ Birds]

>  <b>BULLER</b> HAEMOTOPUS LONGIROSTRIS. (PIED OYSTER-CATCHER.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Torea. ... This fine species ... is generally dispersed round the southern coast of Australia ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,273,1888 2nd edn 2/16 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Haematopus longirostris</i>, Vieill. Pied Oyster-catcher, Red-bill, Torea. <i>(1885,NZ Journal of Science,510</i>) [NOTES: T.H. Potts]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Torea,</i> n. 1 <i>Haematopus ostralegus and H. longirostris, pied oyster catchers; H. unicolor, black oyster catcher</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,438</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [T]here are two species [of oystercatcher], the pied (<i>Haematopus ostralegus finschi,</i> ...) common on South Island estuaries and shingle flats, and the black (<i>H. unicolor</i>, ...), common on Stewart Island and breeding also as far north as Cook Strait and up the East Coast of the North Island. Northern Hemisphere pied oyster-catchers are known as sea pies.) <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,260</i>)

>  <b>Mc.LINTOCK</b> Pied Oystercatcher .. .. torea .. .. <i>Haematopus ostralegus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  The pied oystercatcher may also be ranked first for independence and intelligence. <i>(Nov 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

>  Sturdy pied oystercatchers probing into the silt pulled out pipis. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,E 16</i>)

### pied shag
 
 <i>n.</i> karuhiruhi

>  Pied shag are one of several cormorant species which use urban lakes. Some establish breeding colonies in trees over water. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,26</i>)

>  The pied shag goes under / for a long count as if she has something to prove. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  <b>FALLA</b> PIED SHAG <i>Phalacrocorax varius Maori name:</i> Karuhiruhi. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,66</i>)

>  Six kinds of shag fish in Wellington harbour: the big black shag, or common cormorant, which is about the size of a goose, the little black shag, the pied shag, the spotted shag and, recently, a long shag was seen here. <i>(Aug 18, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  A few minutes brisk paddling placed us upwind and up current of the pied shags. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  <i>80. Phalacrocorax varius varius (Gmelin, 1789) Pied Shag (Kahuriruhi) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,32,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pied Cormorant Phalacrocorax varius</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karuhiruhi, pied shag, yellow-faced cormorant. ... Common around mainland Australia ... </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,116</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> When the launch passed by, about twenty-five adult pied shags exploded from that one little tree ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,18</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> GRACULUS VARIUS. Gml. Pied Shag. Kawau. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,51</i>)

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Querquedula gibberifrons</i>, little teal; Anas superciliosa</i>, grey duck; ... <i>Graculus carbo</i>, cormorant or black shag; <i>Graculus varius</i>, pied shag; <i>Graculus brevirostris</i>, white throated shag; also another species of shag, locally called the `blue shag' ... pretty highly esteemed by the sailors as an article of food; <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  PHALACROCORAX VARIUS. (PIED SHAG.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Karuhiruhi. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,328,1888 2nd edn 2/149 ed.</i>)

>  ... I observed the nests of the common pied shag (<i>Graculus varius</i>). <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/151</i>)

>  There are thirteen species [of shag] in New Zealand <i>Phalacrocorax novae-hollandiae</i> (Black shag), <i>P. melanoleucus</i> (Frilled Shag), <i>P. brevirostris </i>(White-throated Shag), <i>P. varius Pied shag, and <i>P. punctatus</i> (Spotted Shag), which I have often found in the inland bays, rivers, and lakes. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/100</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>6/75</b> <i>Hypoleucus varius</i> Gm. Pied Shag. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

### pied stilt
 
 <i>n.</i> poaka

>  <b>POWELL</b> WHITE-HEADED OR PIED STILT (<i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i>), Poaka of the Maoris, is widely distributed in both the North and South Islands of New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PIED STILT <i>Himantopus leucocephalus Other names</i>: Stilt, Barker, Poaka, White-headed Stilt, Daddy Long-legs, Torea (Rotorua), Pip (Kaipara). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,147</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The pied stilts (<i>Himantopus leucocephalus, </i>... ) are seen a long marshy shores, river flats and swamps. ... Their relationship with the much rarer black stilt (<i>H. ovaezealandiae</i>) is uncertain. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,264</i>)

>  <i>183. Himantopus himantopus leucocephalus </i>Gould, 1837 <i>Pied Stilt (Poaka)</i> Australia and New Zealand ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,51,1980 ed.</i>)

Poaka. Pied Stilt. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 78. HIMANTOPUS LEUCOCEPHALUS. Gould. Pied Stilt. Poaka. 79. HIMANTOPUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gould. Pied Stilt. Poaka. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,29</i>)

>  <i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i>, Gould. Pied Stilt, Poaka. - Makes a slight nest of grass, often by the edge of lagoons or meres, in swamps ... <i>(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,558</i>)

>  HIMANTOPUS LEUCOCEPHALUS, <i>Gould.</i> - Pied Stilt. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/126</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Poaka</i>, n. <i>1. Himantopus leucocephalus, stilt,</i> and <i>H. picatus, pied stilt;</i> shore birds. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,332</i>)

### pied tit
 
 <i>n.</i> miro-miro [incl pied-tit]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 30. PETROICA TOI-TOI. Less. Pied-tit. Miro-miro. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,12</i>)
