### pieing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> A great deal of research work has been done in Australia recently in the use of `enzyme' preparations to get quick sanitary `pieing', but unfortunately so far costs have proved too high. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)

### Pierce's Disease
 
 <i>n.</i> [viticulture]

>  Pierce's Disease, the latest outbreak of agricultural pestilence to rattle the West, is relentlessly tearing through Californian vineyards, worrying winemakers around the world, not least in Australia and New Zealand, for whom California's wine culture has long been a source of inspiration and grapevines... Pierce's Disease is caused by <i>Xylella fastidiosa</i>, a bacteria that lives in the xylem of plants, the thin layer beneath the surface that transports water and nutrients from the roots. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  It begins by stating that the threat of Pierce's Disease to vineyards and the wine industry was enough for Australia to ban all imports of Californian table grapes. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  Just when the Californian wine industry is on the verge of defeating its old-world masters of the international wine trade, it is attacked by an apparently invulnerable enemy, Pierce's Disease... Beavering away assiduously in their University of Florida laboratories in search of a masterstroke with which to defeat the evil Pierce's.. these heroes have devised a brilliantly tricky plan. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### piere
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NAX `PIERE' (NZ Robin) <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

### pig
 
 <i>v.</i> to sleep double; pigging later meant generally `to rough it'

>  Since I slept with my own brother as a boy, this is the first time that it has been my misery to <i>pig </i>with a man ... The custom of<i> pigging</i> is so barbarous ... <i>Ibid </i>34 The whole male population habitually <i>pigging</i> is no small matter <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,33</i>) [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]



 
 <i>n.</i> a demi-john, flagon (espec. of draught beer); a vessel, a five-gallon ?stone jar for beer [also peter; cf. salt pig ca1951 (see quot. PETER] (See [also peter; cf. salt pig](../A#also-peter;--salt-pig))

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie slipped down the fire-escape, and came back with a pig of beer. Word went round the wards. `Starkie's got a pig.' ... All went well, until, as the pig passed from bed to bed, one man dropped the mighty bottle. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pig</i> flagon of beer. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a girl

>  <b>I DAVEY</b> <i>Pig,</i> lately current slang for girl, is straight Danish <i>Pige</i> girl. But I'd like to know how it came to be adopted here. <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1966 July 29 Letter From I. Davey (Whangarei)]



 
 <i>n.</i> the anchor [?pig-iron]

>  In Buddie Willa's Viking: ... Into Horomamae and dropped the 'pig' <i>(Apr 20, 1952,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 diary in 1982 Sansom In the Grip of an Island 153]

### pig and potatoes
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MATHEW</b> To witness a parcel of beastly savages - ... men, women and children devouring pig and potatoes is not a very interesting spectacle. <i>(1840,Journal,52</i>) [NOTES: 1840 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 52]

### pig bucket
 
 <i>n.</i> formerly, receptacle for kitchen waste to be fed to pigs; a (domestic) slop-tin for organic or food wastes, originally to be fed to pigs. [aka pig-bucket, pigs' bucket, pig's bucket] (See [pig tub](per-pop.md#pig-tub),[pig barrel](per-pop.md#pig-barrel))

>  <i>Thursday Morning, Sep. 4.</i> .. again we have biscuits .. [the shipboard authorities] have opened a new bag and in consequence [we] have not the disagreeable fusty taste the old dusty scraps (which are now swimming in the pigs' bucket) used to have. <i>(1851,Biscuit and Butter,58,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Biscuit and Butter (Kennaway Journal) (1973) 58]

>  <b>GLEN</b> The bottom of the pig-bucket. <i>(1926,Uncles Three,27</i>)

>  <b>McDONALD</b> If you were for giving Uncle William coffee he'd just be pouring it into the pig's bucket, and there's [check: there'd] be your white stuff wasted! <i>(1954,Stinson's Bush,83</i>)

>  <b>HOSKEN</b> At that time Mrs T. was helping me and as she cleaned up some spilt sugar and flour, said, `What shall I do with this?' I looked at it and said, `Oh put it in the pig bucket.' Next she showed me some dried bread, etc., and said, `What shall I do with this?' Again I said `Put it in the pig bucket.' <i>(1964,Life on a Five Pound Note,28 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> The pig bucket was half full ... <i>(1975,Outback,17</i>)

>  <b>PRESTON</b> Leaving the cookhouse, they scraped their plates into the pig bucket, then filed past three basins of hot water ... <i>(1978,Woolgatherers,41</i>)

>  There was a pig-bucket in her own yard, in which the refuse of her house was kept, but the smell could not have proceeded from it. <i>(Oct 28, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

### pig country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) unimproved land with scrub and bush, likely to be inhabited by wild pigs.

>  .. from that point to the top of the mountain saddle was famous pig country, and many a good hunt for tuskers and their progeny was held there ... <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,43</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> All was pig country in fact except for 100 acres, more or less, along the top of a ridge, beginning at the far north-west end of the airstrip and running south-east from there to the neighbour's boundary. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,64</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Wally had told me that he had no experience in pig country, so when I saw a big pig up a side gully, I thought it would be a good chance to initiate him into the noble art of wrestling razorbacks. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,111</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> We all peered at the vista of bush gullies, fern patches, stands of kanuka and devil-weed - real pig country. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,14</i>)

### pig dog
 
 <i>n.</i> one of several breeds of dog used for hunting wild pigs. [specifically may be a bailer, finder, or holder]

>  When we were under way again, finding our cut path through the dwarf bush and the fog, Rob, the pig-dog, provided us with a sensation. <i>(Mar 5, 1896,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  Wanted to Sell, Bull Terrier, 30s; splendid pig-dog. <i>(Feb 26, 1902,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The party should provide themselves with a couple of good Maori guides; with a supply of salt pork, biscuits, tea, a little brandy, tobacco (for their own use and for presents to the Natives), pepper and salt, a couple of good pig dogs, a gun or two, and, at this season, stout blankets sewn into the shape of a sack for bedding. <i>(Aug 16, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  LOST - Yellow Pig Dog, small scar on front leg; answers to name "King." <i>(Jan 10, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  Unfortunately, birds caught by pig-dogs are generally torn and useless. <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/145</i>) [NOTES: (Pycroft)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> We had a couple of pig dogs with us, Maori kuris, which Hursthouse had borrowed from an old wahine at Te Kuiti ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,19</i>)

>  Mildred and Hongi, after an exciting day with the pig-dogs in the bush behind the homestead, were wandering down ... under the scented wattles. <i>(Sep 10, 1921,Quick March,23</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i>-pig'dog n. a dog used in hunting wild pigs.-pig'-face n. a kind of cactus. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> `When I was a boy [c1840s] ... there was a saw which said `A New Zealand squire had to have a pig dog, a musket and a Maori wife'.' <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,174</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> He went on to say what a great pig-dog she was ... <i>(1959,Stone, The,46</i>)

>  <b>GROVER</b> The best pig dog for miles. Look at his scars. <i>(1967,Another Man's Role,12</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> He was a very good pig dog, but when he ws with us he only hunted for kiwis. Joe would bail up a kiwi while we grabbed it. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,30</i>)

>  <i>pig-dog NZ & Austral.</i> dog used in hunting wild pigs. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,568</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 365]]

>  I won't be going out in a blaze of publicity. I'll be like a pig dog - I'll just go into the bush and lie down. <i>(Apr 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B9</i>)

>  Heading east, I'm lured down a side road by an intriguing sign, 'Pig Dog Training School'. I meet Joshua Kauta and his son Calib, who are encouraging nine pig dogs into a cage on the back of the trailer. <i>(Jun 2003,AA Directions,22</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> "You need a pig dog for the porkers." <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,145</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> There are many stories of pig dogs that ended up in a gory state and had to be placed in a sack and carried home to be operated on. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,123</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Bailer: a pig dog trained to - or does so naturally - force a wild pig to stand at bay until the hunter arrives. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Truly a pig-dog's lot in life is no easy one. [pig-dog] <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,143</i>)

>  The KZ7 team.. could improve its status with the locals by using a doggie mascot which is more authentically kiwi - a huntaway, perhaps, or a pig dog. <i>(Dec 15, 1991,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> On the threshold [of the whaler's house] an old mongrel pig-dog, scarred all over the head and neck ... <i>(1845,Adventures by Sea and Land,331</i>) [NOTES: [quoted from McIntosh Marlborough 43]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> `The pig-dogs are of rather a mongrel breed, partaking largely of the bull-dog, but mixed with the cross of mastiff and greyhound, which forms the New South Wales kangaroo-dog' [q.v.]. <i>(1845,Adventures by Sea and Land,2/6</i>)

>  ... a wild dog happened to be ... a pig dog of the bull-terrier breed gone wild from the Maori or whaling settlements. <i>(1877,TrNZI,10/321</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Pig-dogs are of rather a mongrel breed, partaking largely of the bull-dog, but mixed with the cross of mastiff and greyhound which forms the New South Wales Kangaroo dog. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/6</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Many kinds of dogs have been introduced by the Colonists; including <i>Bull-dogs</i>, by the whalers, <i>Kangaroo-dogs</i>, (a mixture of greyhound and mastiff) from New South Wales, and various crosses between these and the natives' dogs, which go under the general name of <i>pig-dogs,</i> and combine courage with speed, being used chiefly to hunt the wild hog, and taught to hold him, when caught, by ear or jowl, until the hunter comes up. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,166</i>)

>  TO BE SOLD, two very handsome and Powerful DOG PUPPIES, excellent Watch-dogs, and now ready for breaking as Pig-dogs, or Retrievers, or both. <i>(Sep 20, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> Pig-hunting is a most ignoble and stupid sport; there are in the country plenty of so-called pig dogs. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),266</i>)

>  [We] started with tent blankets, knives, spears, stockwhips, pig dogs and a large amount of provisions <i>(Sep 15, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,102,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> We could seldom procure the loan of a good pig-dog. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,49</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> We had a breed of dogs on the station called pig-dogs, a cross between a mastiff, and kangaroo dog. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,37</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Good pig-dogs are not easily met with, and in the bush they are esteemed a prize. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/321</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Three pig dogs - a breed ... between the bull and the collie - followed at our heels ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,82</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pig-Dog, n</i>. a dog used in hunting wild pigs. <i>(1898,Austral English,351</i>)

>  Dad breeds and trains pig dogs, and we always had the best. <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

### pig face
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of vegetation

>  Before we came here, where the hills down to the sea are covered with pig face, and a foghorn sends its lonesome boom across the mornings, my mother and I lived everywhere. <i>(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  <b>2028</b> <i>Pig&#769;.face&#769;</i>. (p&ibreve.g&#769;.f&amacr.s&#769;.), <i>n.; pl. PIGFACES.</i> } (Bot.) <i>The Pig's&#769;. face&#769;.</i> (p&ibreve.gz&#769;.-)<i>; pl.</i> PIGS' FACES. } fruit of the Australian ice plant (<i>Mesembryanthemum sequilaterale</i>) : - called also<i> canajong</i>. In form it resembles a pig's head and face. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Disphyma australe</i> (Sol.) J.M. Black Common names: <i>New Zealand ice plant, round-leaved pigface</i> Maori names: <i>Horokaka, ruerueke </i>... This plant, generally known as <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i>, is a common seashore ice plant in New Zealand, Australia, and Norfolk and Lord Howe Islands <i>(1981,NZ Medical Journal,54</i>)

### pig fern
 
 <i>n.</i> = HARD FERN (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>), called also ring fern, silver fern [[nf dicts] ca1953 Oct R. Mason notes (p.c.) that it has been known by that name in Wellington since at least the mid-1930's to M.White and Margaret Fyfe and that M.M. Small, Wildlife branch, states that it is general in country districts, and usually the only name used. Pigs root for the rhizomes. ?d Tramper 26 (1) 4]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> HARD FERN (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>), called also pig fern [ring fern], and silver fern, is abundant in both islands, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,19,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 1953,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The SILVER BRACKEN or LACE FERN, also known in parts of Westland as the pig fern (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>) is a slender and graceful plant ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,110,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pig fern [=] ring fern [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,58</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> Further investigation brought to light many pieces of totara posts in amongst the pig-fern! <i>(1973,Listen...!,36</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> And the summer-dried gorse and pigfern had caught fire. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,72</i>)

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> ... sweating mangrove swamps,/ cabbage tree prairies inland, an oceanic/ upcountry of pig fern. .../ <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,27</i>) [NOTES: (Smithyman `Resort')]

>  Leaning back in a bed of pig fern the author was characteristically laconic. <i>(Nov 16, 1986,Sunday News,5</i>)

>  The best known examples [of specialised habitats] are bracken (<i>Pteridium esculentum</i>), which occurs in areas that have been burnt or cleared, and pig fern (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>) which forms extensive colonies on reverting pasture <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

>  Byron picked a path amongst the pig fern and flax, through manuka and foliage where they wouldn't leave tracks. <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,16</i>)

>  Christchurch appointed itself cultural capital and, pretty soon, those snooty southerners took to calling the north Pig Island, because of its introduced wildlife and the prevalence of pigfern, which sprang up after bushfires and felling. <i>(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) Paesia scaberula, known also as bracken, ring fern, carpet fern, scented fern or hard fern, common in areas which have been cleared of bush.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> Hard Fern (Paesia scaberula) also called pig fern, and silver fern is abundant in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,19</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The Silver Bracken or Lace Fern, also known as the pig fern..is a slender and graceful plant <i>(1961,Flora NZ,110</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> As soon as Harvey had fired we hurried down through high pigfern and a jungle of logs. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,14</i>)

>  <b>BLAKE</b> We weren't finding much sign of good bush country either, just lots of waste land covered in pigfern and manuka and occasional motley patches of regenerating second growth hanging off the papa cliffs and poking out of the gullies. [pig-fern; pig fern] <i>(1999,Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer,213</i>)

### Pig Island
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> New Zealand. [aka Pig Islands.]

>  We kicked up our heels and resolved to cross over to New Zealand (Pig Island it was called where we hang out); and share in the good things provided by Vogel and Co. to all comers. <i>(Mar 24, 1892,Otago Witness,43</i>)

>  There is some feeling of patriotism to be found in every grade of New Zealand society .. First there is the liberal type, chiefly notable for a magnificent ignorance of all things outside that infinitesimal speck of land which he fondly and poetically describes as "Pig Island". <i>(Jan 17, 1895,Otago Witness,40</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> of New Zealand

>  The New Zealand wild pigs supposedly descended from farm animals brought here by James Cook gave rise.. to the coloquial but unflattering <i>Pig Islands</i> (or less logically <i>Pig Island</i>), which is found from 1906, and is well known.. from James K. Baxter's.. poem sequence <i>Pig Island Letters</i> (1966). A <i>DNZE</i> citation from 1946 reports <i>Pig Island</i> as an Australian usage, but there is no entry for it in <i>AND</i>. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  "I think Parbury could beat the two in the one ring, so you can inform Sydney boxers who are thinking of crossing the Tasman to keep as far away from Pig Island as they can." <i>(Nov 12, 1906,Star, The,1</i>)

>  Whilst in Sydney Ned Perry ran into a lot of the boys well known in New Zealand. Amongst those he met and who wish to be kindly remembered to friends in Pig Island were Gene Volaire, Havilah and Tommy Uren, Ern Baxter and Ike Kutner. <i>(Jul 11, 1925,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  He had been let in for it by a "Pig Island mug." <i>(Jul 27, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  Seventy years later James K Baxter added: Love is not valued much in Pig Island / Though we admire its walking parody. <i>(Jan 9, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  ...if [small towns] see you're not some pig island wanker ... they're willing to share. <i>(Sep 28, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),21</i>)

>  I don't think the States offer betterliving conditions than old Pig Island. <i>(May 2, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/17 : 111</i>)

>  I think I will wait and give some PigIsland girl the chance of turning medown. <i>(May 10, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/43 : 154</i>)

>  He thought he was back on the old farm inPig Island. <i>(Jun 7, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/45 : 199</i>)

>  Christchurch appointed itself cultural capital and, pretty soon, those snooty southerners took to calling the north Pig Island, because of its introduced wildlife and the prevalence of pigfern, which sprang up after bushfires and felling. <i>(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

### Pig Islander
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a New Zealander

>  One morning he sang, or hummed, the song again, and one of the hands said sneeringly, 'I didn't know you were a Pig Islander, Hermann!' <i>(Oct 23, 1913,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Peregrinating Pig-Islander Pulls Leg of Patriotic Pressman. <i>(Jan 31, 1920,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  Every night will seem novel to New Zealanders, or, as the W.A. men style us, "Pig Islanders". [sic] <i>(Jan 24, 1895,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  A soldier writing in a recent issue of the "Chronicles of the N.Z.E.F.," edited by Clutha MacKenzie, waxes enthusiastic over the service which the Y.M.C.A. is rendering our troops .. "When we arrived at this sector, he writes, " .. You cannot imagine a Pig Islander enjoying soup, can you? Tea and coffee, yes! ... <i>(Sep 6, 1917,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  IT IS TOWN TALK .. That "Pig Islander" is a term of endearment reserved by Australians for New Zealand friends. <i>(Sep 18, 1909,Free Lance,22</i>)

>  He said that the "Noo Zealanders 'adn't got a bloomin' 'ope," he opined that the Walers would wallop Hades out of them, and that things generally were to be made to hum for the "Pig Islanders." <i>(Jul 11, 1903,Free Lance,14</i>)

>  There were three brave Pigislanders <i>(1951,NZ Parliamentary Debates,295/557</i>)

>  Every night will seem novel to New Zealanders, or, as the W.A. men style us, "Pig Islanders." <i>(Jan 24, 1895,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  "Course not, y'iggerant Pig-Islander". <i>(Oct 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

>  The common method of addressing a New Zealander is to call him "digger," which, from being an abusive term, has been readily and universally adopted and approved by the Pig Islanders themselves. We, the N.Z.R.B., prefer to be addressed as "diggers" rather than "dinks" which was the original mode of address. <i>(May 31, 1918,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Towards the end, the cry of "Come on, you Pig Islanders" was often heard. <i>(Dec 18, 1935,NZ Free Lance,48</i>)

>  At the darne [sic] he didn't hear gentle Ellen chuck off, although it might have happened. ... He heard Arthur say he had enough of her slinging off. The lady didn't call Arthur a blanky pig-islander, and she didn't tell him to go to a warm place. <i>(Sep 18, 1909,NZ Truth,221/6/5</i>)

>  A few wounded "Pig Islanders" have occasionally risked a drenching in order to have their accustomed "Wow on the Wivver. <i>(Sep 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/2 : 48</i>)

>  The Pig Islanders turned out in force. <i>(May 24, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,III/44 : 180</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> "You are leetle Peeg Islander!". Oh! Why did the Dalmatian boarders have to keep calling me that. I hated it when they did. But I saw even then that calling a born New Zealander a name like Pig Islander was some satisfaction, the pay-off for what ranked them. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,25</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> We Pig Islanders are not nearly so hot-blooded in our manner of speaking [as Aussies]. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,65</i>) [NOTES: [1917 April 25] 1939 Miller C.T. and T's 65]

>  <b>McCANDLISH</b> '... one of the main items was a song ...[sic] about the soldiers from New Zealand. ... I suppose the Australians there said, `Those damned Pig Islanders have scored again!' <i>(Jan 5, 1915,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Jan 5 McCandlish letter in 1984 Pugsley Gallipoli 81]

>  `Peter': It was in `Y' ward of the 2nd. A.S.H., so well known to Billjims and Pig Islanders, that a discussion was taking place concerning what all `naice' people call limbs. <i>(Oct 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 4/5,1981 ed.</i>)

>  The Pig Islanders turned out in force. <i>(May 24, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,180</i>) [NOTES: [also Nov 22 206/2]]

>  `'Course not, y'iggerant Pig-Islander!' ... `But, Tom, do yer think - ... that a whole million Pig-Islanders is a goin' to let the Chows snavel Noo Zealand?' <i>(Oct 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pig Islander.</i> A New Zealander: Australian coll.: late C. 19-20. Ex the (formerly) numerous wild pigs in rural N.Z. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,627</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `If you're in any way different, in this country, ... you're made to feel such a fool. ... They call us Homies.' `A lot of people called the New Zealanders Pig Islanders, and the Australians the Aussies. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,164</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,43</i>) [NOTES: [see PIG ISLAND]]

>  <b>BENN</b> A citizen of New Zealand by birth is, or was (the word is obsolescent) a <i>pig-islander. </i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,88</i>)

>  Since the pig was introduced by Captain Cook ... [fn] Hence modern Newzealand slang a <i>Captain Cook</i> or <i>Captain Cooker</i>, for a wild pig. (Current since the late 19th century.) Also, slang, <i>Pig-islands</i>, Newzealand, <i>Pig-islander</i>, a Newzealander. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/225</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... <i>pig islander</i>: New Zealander <i>(1945,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> An Australian, ... , decided that he would have a go also, after the others had let me down. His theory was that if a Pig Islander can do it, surely an Australian can. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,76</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Yes, and my guardian was a Pommie too. Always running down New Zealand. Pig Islanders. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,223</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Pig Islander: New Zealander. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Two grateful Australian N.C.O.s, as they were being borne away called out: `Hey, you Pig Islanders, there are swags of cigarettes in those packs we left behind.' <i>(1968,Seddons, The,272</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Meeting with little response and, in an effort to arouse from apathy `the Pig Islanders' - as he called his fellow New Zealanders, Terry took the dramatic step of murdering an elderly Chinese on ... 24 September 1905. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/386</i>)

>  <i>Pig Islands n</i>. New Zealand. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The old name of Pig Islanders for New Zealanders at times seems singularly apt. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,60</i>)

>  <i>Pig Islander n. N.Z. inf</i>. a New Zealander. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,857</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 568; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 365]]

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> But I saw even then that calling a born New Zealander a name like Pig Islander was some satisfaction, the pay-off for what rankled [the Dalmatians]. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,25</i>)

>  Further evidence for the defence was called to show that the girl James had called Adams a "pig-islander", and advised him to "get a character." <i>(Sep 11, 1909,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  It is thought that when Tier spoke of a pig islander, he was referring to a New Zealander, as among a certain class of Australians New Zealanders are so known. <i>(Jul 18, 1912,Evening Post,7</i>)

### Pig Islands
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> New Zealand [aka Pig Island]

>  I .. was regaled with his exploits and experiences during his 50 years sojourn in these "Pig Islands", which is the general way of designating New Zealand in the parlance of old whalers. <i>(Jun 16, 1883,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  The fact that this company is built upon Dominion capital and that all of its surplus funds will be invested in the dear Pig Islands is most gratifying. <i>(Jun 21, 1913,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  Space again pulls me up, and will not let me tell you one-tenth of his doings in sunny New South Wales, so we will pass them over, and pick him up again in "the pig islands" as he had learned to call these little isles of the ocean while away. Arriving here, he spent some time in Southland ... <i>(Sep 7, 1904,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  Since the pig was introduced by Captain Cook .. [fn] Hence modern Newzealand slang a <i>Captain Cook</i> or <i>Captain Cooker</i>, for a wild pig. (Current since the late 19th century.) Also, slang, <i>Pig-islands</i>, Newzealand, <i>Pig-islander</i>, a Newzealander. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/225 fn</i>)

### pig jobber
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I may observe, that both the Wai-pa and the Wai-kato River, are infested with men of the very lowest grade, under the name of Pig Jobbers <i>ci-devant</i> sawyers, and people of every disreputable denomination, whose sole employment consists in cheating and demoralising the natives, and endeavouring to throw difficulties in the way of the <i>few </i> industrious and honest Europeans who are fighting an up-hill game for the support of themselves and their families. <i>(Aug 5, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

>  His [the M.P.'s] venom not being quite exhausted he called me [Robert Parris] a `pig jobber' which I never was, but even if I had been there was nothing derogatory or disgraceful in it. <i>(Mar 27, 1899,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/622</i>) [NOTES: (Robert Parris)]

>  Imagine a farmer allowing a pig-jobber to take away a litter of pigs and return what he liked for them! <i>(Jan 21, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Mr. S. L. Brookes in a letter to the <i>Daily Times</i> asks "Civis" his definition of a pig-jobber, as probably grammatical opinions may differ on the point. <i>(Nov 12, 1896,Otago Witness,29</i>)

>  Absolutely nothing was done by the pig-jobber - and absolutely nothing was done by the Department. <i>(Mar 27, 1906,Evening Post,4</i>)

### pig lily
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One of the most widespread and best-known wannabes is the arum lily... Their large white cousins are referred to as funeral lilies and pig lilies (apparently because they make excellent animal fodder. <i>(Mar 31, 2000,Dominion,18</i>)

### pig paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a field where pigs are housed.

>  While the first course [spreading manure] is not practicable, we may mention that we have seen old pig-paddocks on the gumlands put into grass. <i>(1921,NZ Journal of Agriculture,266</i>)

### pig potato
 
 <i>n.</i> a small, unusable potato, fit only for pigs to eat [ca1910 Havelock]

### pig root
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hunch-backed bucking motion of horses. [aka pig-root, pigroot]

>  <b>STEWART</b> She could still give a gentle pig-rootwhich failed to dislodge me. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,118</i>)

>  I imagined that Silver's 'pig roots' were really first class bucks ... <i>(Jun 7, 1951,NZ Farmer,58</i>)

>  .. with a couple of preliminary pig roots we were off. <i>(1951,NZ Farmer,59</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> She could still give a gentle pig-root which failed to dislodge me. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,118</i>)

>  ... pigroots, bucks and other forms of equine misdemeanour ... <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,36</i>)

>  The famous gingham propped, bucked a bit, reared up and dropped, / Made a pig-root, throwing me up in the air. <i>(Dec 15, 1906,New Zealand Free Lance,16</i>)

### pig tin
 
 <i>n.</i> a tin for the slops destined for the pigs

### pig track
 
 <i>n.</i> a rough track beaten by a wild pig

>  <b>BURDON</b> They [put] ... in charge of the station a manager known as `Pig Track' Hudson, thus called from his unwavering faith in getting on to a pig track and following it up, to wherever it led, whenever he had the misfortune to get lost. <i>(1938,High Country,90</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a track formed on rough hill country or through bush by a wild pig. [aka pig-track]

>  We met with numerous pig tracks in every direction. <i>(Aug 23, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,3</i>)

>  Money enough has already been wasted in temporary expedients - the mere pig tracks which have been made to do duty for roads. <i>(Dec 12, 1868,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The pig track to Mahakipawa was brought up, and the Secretary was instructed to write to the Minister of Public Works requesting him to have money voted for the work expended thereon, and a proper dray road made, which the Board would be willing to take over directly it is completed. <i>(May 10, 1890,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  To get [to] it he and his family had to go over a pig track with his goods and chattels on the horse's back. <i>(Jul 2, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> .. if you diverge from the Beaten Track, either to the Right hand or the left You lose ground with those you are with As all Tracks seem to be Pig tracks in the first instance. <i>Ibid.</i> 62 The Pig Tracks or Paths the most miserable possible for my Feet. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> So up a pig track he started to the ferny face where the dogs were barking. <i>(1937,Thomas,102</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Pig-tracks, in a word, have been useless in the opening up of a run. <i>(1921,Tutira,179</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> .. in charge of the station a manager known as 'Pig Track' Hudson, thus called from his unwavering faith in getting on to a pig track and following it up ... <i>(1938,High Country: the evolution of a NZ Sheep Station,90</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> There were no roads in North Canterbury in those days, only pig and sheep tracks. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,261</i>)

>  <b>CHINN</b> I was leading the way, following a pig track running diagonally to the crown of the valley. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,67</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He [the local doctor] would go into the bush on his horse over the old pig tracks, ford rivers and visit camps in floods and gales. <i>(1997,Pigs and Me: Tales of the Bygone Bush.,63</i>)

>  Why, a pig-track through a bush of "supple-jacks" and "hold-me-tights" is nothing to it. <i>(Feb 26, 1859,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  The road (?) [sic] through the bush is merely a pig-track or Maori track (terms nearly synonymous), girth-deep in mud, but bearing the grand name of Great North Road. <i>(Mar 19, 1873,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

### pig tub
 
 <i>n.</i> a domestic slop-bucket [aka pig-tub] (See [pig bucket](per-pop.md#pig-bucket),[pig barrel](per-pop.md#pig-barrel))

>  The most offensive slops were thrown on the garden, and other kinds put in the pig tub. <i>(Sep 3, 1883,Star, The,3</i>)

>  He, however, nowadays regularly milks his cows from consideration of mercy, and afterwards takes the milk in to the dairy, where it is set up in clean scalded pans, and where other pans are skimmed and scalded, and their contents carried out to the pig-tub, &c, &, [sic] for considerations of mercy. <i>(May 27, 1892,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  The local rich frequently throw into the pig tub victuals that many a poor family would be glad of, and it is surmised that Joseph intervened and robbed the porkers. <i>(Dec 3, 1910,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  The evidence tended to show that .. the girl had been subjected to persistent ill-treatment by Mrs. Boddy, who had struck her .. put her in the pig-tub, thrown her among thorns .. and had not given her sufficient food. <i>(Jul 26, 1870,North Otago Times,7</i>)

>  She went to the back door and there saw the deceased child in the pig tub. <i>(Nov 9, 1872,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### pig's arse/pig's bum
 
 <i>exclam.</i> pig's + (usu. anatomical) noun as an exclamation or catchphrase of disbelief, disagreement, derision, contempt, etc.

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Head's always asking them to come along and claim watches and pens and what have you. And do they? Pig's arse. They're all too well off. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,102</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>all around the pig's arse there is pork</i> indicating acceptance, resignation, fatalism, in regard to a fait accompli, such as an opponent's winning snooker break. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,9</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Never mind Davie, she said. We won't tease you. Pig's arse we won't, said Emma, and laughed. <i>(1977,Of Ivory Accents,38</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pig's arse/bum/ear</i> derisive exclamations; eg `In a pig's arse to that notion, mate.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

### pig's Christmas parcel
 
 <i>exclam.</i> euphemism or rhyming slang for pig's arse(-hole), and kiss my arse(-hole), an exclamation of disbelief.

>  <b>BRUNO</b> And did he marry poor blind Nell ? He did - pig's christmas parcel. <i>Ibid.</i> 50 Minefield, pig's Christmas parcel. <i>(1944,Description Province Southland,23</i>)

### pig's ear
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A decoction made from the <i>rauriki (Sonchus)</i>, and the expressed juice of the `pig's ear' (<i>Mesembryanthemum</i> sp.) were used locally ... <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> beer [rhyming slang]

>  There are many examples of rhyming slang in use at Paparua:..<i>pig's ear</i> for a beer. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie agreed, and went out to search Wallacetown for five gallons of pig's ear, which rounded off the Christmas dinner. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,62</i>)

>  I hop off at the rubbidy, borrow a couple of Plymouth Sounds and get on the pig's ear. pig's ear....beer <i>(May 19, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the succulent <i>Cotyledon orbiculata</i>, common on the limestone face of Napier's Bluff Hill and on Banks Peninsula [Listed in <i>Common Names of Plants in New Zealand</i> / comp. E.R.Nicol (Lincoln, NZ: Manaaki Whenua Press, 1997) ISBN 0478093101; also in <i>Standard Common Names for Weeds in New Zealand</i>/ comp. A.J. Healy (Hastings, NZ : New Zealand Weed and Pest Control Society, 1984) (2nd rev. ed. - haven't checked for more recent edition yet).]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a plant, Crassula cotyledon, suitable for some stock feed.

>  Crassula cotyledon 'obitulata' is an excellent succulent ... <i>(Mar 2001,Growing Today,5</i>)

### pig's face
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i>, ice-plant, horokaka. (See [also pig face](per-pop.md#pig-face))

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pig-face, Pig-faces</i>, and <i>Pig's-face</i>, or <i>Pig's-faces</i>. Names given to an indigenous `ice-plant,' <i>Mesembryanthemum aequilaterale</i>, Haw., <i>N.O. Ficoideae</i>, deriving its generic name from the habit of expanding its flower about noon.[1834] <i>(1898,Austral English,352</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> pig's faces <i>(1899,Students' Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> pig's-face <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Mesembryanthemum australe (The Southern Mesembryanthemum)</i> ... Called by the colonists, <i>pigs' faces</i> or <i>ice-plant. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,160,1940,p. 167 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> pig's face <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> pigs-face <i>(1909,NZ Naturalist's Calendar</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The ice-plant (<i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i>, pig's-face, horokaka), which so frequently drapes the coastal cliffs ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,63</i>)

>  <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> pigsface <i>(1911,AJHR,C-13</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on the Dune-areas of NZ]

>  <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> pig's-face <i>(Jul 1920,Victorian Naturalist</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>A popular name for the fleshy-leaved `ice-plant' or mesembryanthemum. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),301</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Bare clay banks free from other vegetation commonly have sheets of the pink-flowered ICEPLANT or PIG'S-FACE (<i>Disphyma australe</i>) ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,244,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pigface n.</i> indigenous creeper, with bright purple flowers pigfaces, the five-sided fruit. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>pigface n</i>. indigenous creeper with bright purple flowers <i>pigfaces</i>, the five-sided fruit. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>Pig&#769;.face&#769;.</i> (p&ibreve.g&#769;.f&amacr.s&#769;.), <i>n.; pl. PIGFACES. } </i>(Bot.) <i>The Pig's&#769;. face&#769;.</i> (p&ibreve.gz&#769;.-); pl. PIGS' FACES. } fruit of the Australian ice plant (<i>Mesembryanthemum sequilaterale</i>) : - called also <i>canajong</i>. In form it resembles a pig's head and face. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  Further away from the sea .. the weather-beaten sea-cottages, with .. their mounds of the dark green mesembryanthemum - "pig's-face" - give place to more pretentious villas and two-storeyed houses. <i>(Jun 7, 1894,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  Our house was never in much danger, as it was erected on a small flat close to a creek, the ground being covered with weeds called "pigface" and hayweed, which were always green, proving an effectual protection to our homestead. <i>(Jun 24, 1903,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  On large stretches of country there is no grass at all, but innumerable weeds, such as .. pig-face .... <i>(Aug 15, 1874,Otago Witness,17</i>)

### pig's whisper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Suddenly one of the young ladies seemed to have something on her mind .. and, in which Biddy would call 'a pig's whisper', suggested that 'Hemmer should ask the hold party 'ow long he 'ad been in the Colonies'. <i>(Jun 6, 1874,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  My asthma won't let me speak much above a pig's whisper. <i>(Oct 28, 1876,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  I have heard a pig's whisper that your weedy Montrose II is a bobby-dazzler to let in to our Grand National Hurdle Race. <i>(Jul 2, 1891,Otago Witness,26</i>)

### pig-barrel
 
 <i>n.</i> a (domestic) slop-tin

### pig-bridge
 
 <i>n.</i>

### pig-fence
 
 <i>n.</i> a fence usually made of wire netting 1.07 m high

>  As the visitant advances, for a mile before he rises the hillock under which the pah is situate, he finds the track on each side fenced by the well-known Maori pig fence. <i>(Oct 25, 1881,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Strong barbed wire with the barbs closer than usual is generally used for pig fences. Strong wire netting fastened to posts may serve, but the barb fence is the most reliable. About five or six of these wires, placed not more than 5in apart, well stretched and stapled to stout posts, should be sufficient to check the exploring tendencies of any ordinary and reasonable pig. <i>(Oct 8, 1896,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  An Extra Strong Pig Fence is made with 6in cross ties. <i>(Nov 18, 1908,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  Special Pig Fence also made. <i>(Jul 22, 1913,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Usually made of wire netting 1.07 m high. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)

### pig-hunt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The gentlemen, seemingly, did not lack for amusement, for we heard from all sides glowing accounts of impromptu pig hunts, the excitement of which must have been great. <i>(Oct 12, 1870,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  A party of Nightcaps Sports visited the Waiau district on a wild pig hunt at Easter and they were very successful, as they report twenty pigs shot, a few of the best being brought home. <i>(May 6, 1919,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Let those who habitually or occasionally travelled remember that it was but at rare intervals they saw a solitary hawk or a small bird, and let them also remember that a number of well-to-do youth were kept away from our shores because we could offer them no better sport than a pig hunt [hear, hear]. <i>(May 15, 1861,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Harry proposed that next afternoon I should join him in a pig-hunt. <i>(1904,NZ Observer,Xmas Issue : 10</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> A PIG-HUNT [Chapter heading] <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/318</i>)

### pig-hunter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  TO RUNHOLDERS AND PIG-HUNTERS. WANTED to purchase 500 to 1000 Pig Skins. <i>(Mar 8, 1882,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  A party of pig hunters tried their luck in the Lilburn Valley at Easter, and though weather conditions were most unfavourable they succeeded in capturing about thirty head. <i>(Apr 24, 1922,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  An hour spent with three pig hunters and nine dogs is an education in itself. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,40</i>)

>  Aaron, accompanied by Bert - although only 12 years old he was an experienced pig hunter - chased after the pig into the hills. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,No. 47/52</i>)

>  <b>FROBISHER</b> Now's your chance for winning your spurs as a pig-hunter. <i>(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,48</i>)

>  "Pig-hunting for Ladies in New Zealand" forms the subject of a highly-written paper in <i>Bell's Weekly Messenger</i>. The pig-hunter, with her husband and a girlfriend, managed to kill 40 wild pigs in an expedition near Lake Hauroto. <i>(Dec 22, 1894,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  (Reprinted from the <i>Marlborough Express</i>) One pig-hunter last week earned over GBP15 at this very profitable calling. <i>(Jun 1, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The <i>Temuka Leader</i> states that a party of pig-hunters had some splendid sport on the ranges, about 10 or 12 miles to the back of Four Peaks Station, going out with several of the Orari Gorge station hands. <i>(Sep 4, 1901,Otago Witness,50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  the Natives and one Man started a Pig hunting the remainder employed grass cutting. [Nov 17] the Pig hunters returned but with no luck <i>(Nov 16, 1837,Piraki Log,60,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  There are plenty of wild pigs; ... they hunt them with dogs and large knives. ... The pig-hunters wear boar-skin shoes when they go a pig-hunting. <i>(Jul 20, 1848,Letters from Otago,15,1978 ed.</i>)

### pig-hunting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Pig-hunting for Ladies in New Zealand" forms the subject of a highly-written paper in <i>Bell's Weekly Messenger</i>. The pig-hunter, with her husband and a girlfriend, managed to kill 40 wild pigs in an expedition near Lake Hauroto. <i>(Dec 22, 1894,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> He got round with the crowd who liked fighting and drinking and pig-hunting and chasing girls. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,117</i>)

>  A fatality occurred at Tataramoa yesterday afternoon, when two brothers named Lovejoy, Dannevirke residents, were resting after pig hunting. <i>(Sep 9, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Good pig hunting is to be had up here. <i>(Dec 4, 1930,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  THAT PIG-HUNTING EXPEDITION. <i>(Sep 30, 1924,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  This is a man reared in the heartland, a man of the land who, when he's not playing rugby, passes time pig hunting with the "old man". <i>(Nov 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  And then there's the pig hunting. <i>(Nov 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  Pighunting and horse racing became major recreations, along with going into town for a weekend binge of drinking, whoring and carousing. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  Pig hunting lesson number one: when you're in the passenger seat with a 35-year-old who started hunting at four, has hundreds of big boar to his credit and is taken seriously by good hunters, it's better to listen than to shout your mouth off... Months earlier I had been in Nelson and wandering into the tourist office asked if they knew of any pig hunting clubs. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> I do remember discussing it, too, with myridiculous pig-hunting dog, Bill. <i>(1983,Speaking for Ourselves,220</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Late in spring ... we were all returning from a great pig-hunting expedition, when I saw one of the party coming ... with a small ... wild pig under each arm ... <i>(1867,Station Days in Maoriland,106,1870 ed.</i>)

>  The fate of a pig-hunting party at Matata has been ascertained. <i>(May 7, 1889,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  Pig hunting is a perfect way to spend a couple of days, says Tama David, who has been pig hunting since he was a boy... <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

### pig-jump
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Many expected to see a little bit of fun here, but with the exception of a very mild prop and pig-jump they were disappointed, and in half-an-hour's time he was almost entirely under words of command, such as "stop," "walk," "trot," c., and the tamer was soon able to mount the seat behind the horse and drive him pretty well at pleasure. <i>(Feb 28, 1890,Clutha Leader,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  He jumps one fence in his stride - then the next he goes right up to it, says good-day, and then pig-jumps it. <i>(Jul 14, 1927,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  He just "pig jumped" over the sticks until clear of the last jump, then strolled up in time to be weighed in before the saddling bell rang for the next race. <i>(Oct 21, 1911,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> [The horse] pig-jumped, kicked mulewise, struck out .... <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,145</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> He bucked before I was ready. Not pig-jumped - he bucked hard. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a long knife for killing or sticking pigs; pigsticker

>  CAMP OVENS of all sizes, Iron pots .. Cattle Bells, Dog Chains, Pig Knives .. may be had at the STORE of I. M. HILL, Ironmonger, &c., Haven-road. <i>(Nov 2, 1850,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,141</i>)

>  The other day an editor consulted the staff as to a good heading for a murder case. One reporter suggested .. "Pierced by a Pig-Knife." <i>(Oct 10, 1881,Star, The,4</i>)

>  Higgin's intentions might be gauged by the fact that in addition to the revolver and a plentiful supply of ammunition, he was also armed with a large pig knife and with several sticks of explosive, to which were attached caps and fuses, and when he saw that the school master WAS TRYING TO DELAY HIM he suddenly consulted his watch and, jumping to the study door, cried, as he kept Reid covered: "You are having them sneaked out while you keep me here." <i>(Nov 3, 1923,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  I allers slep with Mollie in my arms, an' a big pig knife under my pillar ready to kill the little dear afore ever they touched her. <i>(Dec 9, 1895,Observer (Auckland),12</i>)

>  He got a pig-knife and struck her about the head and body with it. <i>(Mar 29, 1877,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> [Colonists' dress includes] a belt containing a pig-knife. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,71</i>)

>  The Maoris use a pig-knife in scraping gum or cutting flax. <i>(May 19, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

### pig-netting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We all moved in to enjoy an evening bythe fire and later disenjoy a night onthe new pig-netting bunks. <i>(1941,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,X/39</i>)

### pig-potato
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The yield was as follows:.. pig-potatoes, 1.87 tons. <i>(Nov 2, 1917,Morrinsville Star,3</i>)

### pig-proof
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fences and paddocks] capable of keeping out wild carnivore pigs [1800s & early 1900s.]

>  A three railed fence will be easily put up, the wood lying close at hand, and palings can be split afterwards, and a substantial fowl and pig proof fence completed at some less busy time. <i>(Jan 22, 1861,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  A letter was read from Mr Robertson, Poundkeeper, stating that he had been threatened with an action for allowing pigs to escape from the Public Pound, and saying that the Pound was not pig proof. <i>(Apr 17, 1878,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  Others openly contend for the <i>right</i> of keeping anything they like on their neighbours' land, and say they are unjustly treated, if anyone disputes that right: they say, fence your land pig proof, and our pigs will not get on it. <i>(Aug 26, 1856,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>ARTHUR</b> whose produce is grown in the proximity of bush or scrubby ground have .... [to make] their ground pig-proof <i>(1894,Kangaroo and Kauri,98</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... both kinds [of fence], when properly made, were pig-proof, which a modern wire fence might not be. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,99</i>)

>  71 acres, pig-proof fences. <i>(Aug 17, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  acres .. 2 pig proof paddocks. <i>(Aug 28, 1900,Hawera Star,201</i>)

>  .. with good fences of totara posts and wire, 4 pig-proof paddocks. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Weekly News,2</i>)

>  The cost of raising stores, and difficulty of making pig-proof fences have all combined to prevent that large quantity of pork being fed, which would be required to glut the market. <i>(Mar 14, 1885,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  For sale. Farm of 139 acres, situated on Rama Road .. with pig-proof fences ... <i>(Jun 12, 1895,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

### pig-raiser
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pig-breeder.

>  Pig-raisers should therefore arrange for a series of paddocks, so that the pigs can be shifted occasionally. <i>(1920,NZ Journal of Agriculture,243</i>)

>  The writer's object is to point out the principles underlying health and disease, and to outline the methods which the pig-raiser may employ in combating the common troubles. <i>(1920,NZ Journal of Agriculture,244</i>)

### pig-root
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [horses] to buck or arch one's back.

>  Silver had a habit, now and then, of 'pig-rooting' but this and my growing ability to miss the bumps in the trot only served to make me think I was becoming an expert horsewoman. <i>(Jun 7, 1951,NZ Farmer,58</i>)

>  Silver, wiser than I no doubt, began pig-rooting with a sideways movement that carried us off the road. <i>(Jun 7, 1951,NZ Farmer,59</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> His horse instantly began pigrooting and then bucking. <i>(1974,Backblocks,63</i>)

>  <i>v.int.</i> used of a horse, to plant both forelegs on the ground and kick with the hind legs, whence <i>pigrooter. </i> <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>pig-root vb. (intr.) Austral. & N.Z. sl.</i> (of a horse) to buck slightly. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,857</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 568]]

### pig-rooting
 
 <i>n.</i> the turning over of turf by pigs.

>  Upon arriving on the course, I found that instead of a green sward to run on, it was a ploughed gallop, for the Jockey Club found, on clearing the course, that there were so many pig rootings on the land, that it was necessary to plough it, which was done very judiciously. <i>(Nov 2, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  As I must certainly give him due praise for being most particular in detail, he might as well have told us that, close to the yards before spoken of, he saw a good quantity of pig rootings, with certain other unequivocal signs of pigs having recently been there. <i>(Aug 16, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The part formed and not metalled is more like pig rooting than road forming. <i>(Jun 18, 1904,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  Frederick Harding, while out cattle shooting, placed the gun in a pig rooting with the stock downward. <i>(Nov 23, 1881,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  He sought refuge in a small excavation in the ground caused by pig-rooting, and lay amidst suffocating smoke whilst the flames passed over one half of his body. <i>(Sep 24, 1894,Evening Post,3</i>)

### pig-run
 
 <i>n.</i> uncultivated tracts of land on which pigs run wild

>  Are they worthy of the name streets? Back block roads, cattle tracks, pig runs would be more appropriate! <i>(Jul 29, 1903,Northern Advocate,4</i>)

>  The Natives then, as now, resided at the Otago Heads, but the country in the vicinity of this reserve, which is located at the upper end of the upper harbour, was, and has been "from time immemorial," occupied by them as a ranch or pig run. <i>(Dec 8, 1892,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  The Mayor moved, "That the Local Board of Health, having visited the slaughter yards in company with their medical officer, fail to discover any nuisance arising from the slaughter yards, and have the belief that any effluvia arising in their vicinity, is from the land at the rear of the yards which is swampy, and has been used as a pig run for many years." <i>(Apr 1, 1882,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> Thousands of acres on all sides of you .... here and there little patches are under cultivation - the rest are `pig-runs'. <i>(1848,Letters & Journals,II-381</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> [The Maori] desire to have large tracts of land for pig and cattle runs, over which the herds may range without danger of trespass on the white man's cultivation, ... <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,21</i>)

>  Their customers are the city's homeless, who will be waiting for them in K Road's Pigeon Park, Myers Park, and down near the docks. <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,113</i>)

>  Also for Auckland, if it could not accomodate families of "ten, nine, and eight children!!" This argument might satisfy a person who wanted a pig-run, but we cannot see the application in this case. <i>(May 20, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### pig-stalking
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. he takes his rifle and we go over the hills pig-stalking ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,109,1870 ed.</i>)

### pig-sticker
 
 <i>n.</i> a longish, sharp-pointed sheath-knife

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> By this time our gallant pig-sticker had returned to the charge ... <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,25</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> ... predatory bands of pig-stickers ravaged the remotest regions <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,65</i>)

>  He buckled on his pig sticker <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/254</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> A long tom .303/ Lay easy on his knee./ He'd a whacking great pig-sticker and an axe! <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,32</i>)

>  Usually there's just one gun and everyone else has knifes - pig stickers. <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

### pig-sticking
 
 <i>n.</i> hunting with dogs of wild pigs which when bailed up are killed by stabbing rather than by shooting.; hunting wild pigs with a sheath knife (and dogs) [in DNZE; cf. ODE; usually hyphenated but aka pig sticking, pigsticking]

>  There is nothing whatever doing at Manawapou, where the General and the most advanced force were stationed, and the soldiers appear to be employed in destroying native crops, with occasional intervals of amusement at cattle hunting and pig sticking. <i>(Mar 30, 1865,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  Alec Beattie also corroborated, and said that with regard to the bayonet, he took it to the shop to get a handle on it for pig-sticking, but found that to do so would destroy the catch that fixed the bayonet to the rifle. <i>(Jul 1, 1916,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  In a speech at Adelaide two weeks ago, the Earl of Onslow, Governor of New Zealand, expressed great delight that in his newly-adopted land the noble sport of "pig-sticking" was to be had, and intimated his intention of entering lustily into that elevating pastime. <i>(Apr 27, 1889,Observer (Auckland),11</i>)

>  They go pig sticking. <i>(Oct 9, 1878,Star, The,2</i>)

>  Sports: Pig-Sticking, Haggis Netting. <i>(Mar 22, 1928,NZ Truth,16</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY-BUCKNALL</b> We had any amount of `pig-sticking' all round our new quarters. <i>(1878,Search for Fortune, A,44</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> Still less did pig-sticking resemble the grand wild boar hunt of yore. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick</i>)

>  <b>ARTHUR</b> Wild-pig hunting, or, as it is more commonly called, pig-sticking. <i>(1894,Kangaroo and Kauri,97</i>)

>  The Minister caustically remarked that he was evidently unfit to earn his living at bushwacking or pigsticking, qualifications more highly prized here than literary powers. <i>(Oct 4, 1894,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The book proper has shape as a large aggregation of well-ordered paragraphs, from among which the interested, would-be sportsman will find .. such information as will help him towards success in fly-fishing, pig-sticking, deer-stalking, the pursuit of winged game, etc. <i>(Aug 17, 1904,Otago Witness,78</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [pig-barrel](per-pop.md#pig-barrel))

### pigeon
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> = sailer q.v.?

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> We had been out fishing and were expecting at any time to hear the signal, `There she spouts!' or <i>`Ehi pauta!' the pigeon-Maori version of the warning. </i> <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,75</i>)

### Pigeon Park
 
 <i>n.</i> a reserve in Wellington (?also Auckland)

>  The unofficial name by which [this reserve] is known to the citizens of Wellington is "Pigeon Park," and most fitting is this description, for over the years the pigeons have established forthemselves a veritable sanctuary in the heart of one of the city's busiest thoroughfares. <i>(Jan 23, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,7</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I lay down on the grass / In Pigeon Park <i>(1979,Collected Poems,532</i>)

>  On Wednesday.. the Public Trial and execution of Merv Wellington was staged at Pigeon Park. <i>(Jul 2, 1979,Salient,11</i>)

>  [Caption] Walking past Pigeon Park / Hopelessly late... <i>(Mar 22, 1982,Salient,2</i>)

>  At least one person in Wellington loves the sculpture in Pigeon Park. I do. <i>(Mar 3, 1992,Dominion,7</i>)

>  So we are to have a film about the making of Pigeon Park. <i>(Mar 13, 1992,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  I remember Pigeon Park. A little oasis of grass and bird droppings in the middle of Wellington's central shopping precinct - the sort of place you could crash on a sunny afternoon, read a book, meet a street kid and laugh at the motorists who didn't know not to park under the tree where all the pigeons hung out. <i>(Dec 12, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>)

>  Perhaps [he] would like to end all usage of nick-names. No more Pigeon Park, no Beehive, and no more references to the Dom'. <i>(May 5, 2004,Capital Times,?19</i>)

>  A Wellington police spokesman says police have problems with drunken teens in Pigeon Park. <i>(Jun 5, 2000,Evening Post,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  I hope the renaming of Chaffers Park will be as unsuccessful as the renaming of Pigeon Park. <i>(Jun 16, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### pigeon post
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  THE PIGEON "POST." [sic]- Carrier pigeons were, in the old times, long enough before the invention of electric telegraphs, or before post-offices were established, used instead of both. <i>(Feb 13, 1871,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  By private enterprise a pigeon post has just been established between Great Barrier Island and the city of Auckland. <i>(May 1, 1897,Mataura Ensign,4</i>)

>  We saw again the island's unique pigeon post mail service and then it was on to Waiheke. <i>(Jun 29, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  A message received by pigeon post from the search party reports the finding of the dead body of the captain of the Alfheld on Golddiggers' Beach. <i>(Oct 24, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>v.</i> (climbers')

### pigeongram
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "A MESSAGE FROM THE SEA." - Under this heading the Auckland <i>Star</i> received two pigeongrams - why should we not coin a new word like our neighbours? - received by carrier pigeons from on board the ship City of Auckland, which recently sailed from the port of that name. <i>(Jan 25, 1873,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

>  The Premier has accepted an offer by the Great Barrier Pigeongram Agency to supply 10 homing pigeons. <i>(Jan 4, 1900,Mataura Ensign,5</i>)

>  The following pigeongram was received to-day but was dispatched from the Terawhiti at 1 p.m. yesterday:- "Tide gone back. Indrabarah is heading west to seaward ..." <i>(Jun 7, 1913,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  A Star pigeongram from Te Aroha says that gold has been got at the reef at the prospectors' claim to-day. <i>(Oct 30, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  A Thames Star pigeongram from Paeroa, this afternoon, states that the judgment of the native council was delivered at 3 p.m. today. <i>(Sep 20, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  The Barrier Island offered not one but two pigeongram services, each struggling to assert its aerial supremacy over the other... On one well-publicised occasion a bird was despatched wih a pigeongram to Auckland at 8 am and a reply received by 9.15 the same morning. <i>(Jan 1991,NZ Geographic,115</i>)

>  A Thames Star pigeongram states that "Samuel Young got the first miner's right, and every horse in the place was engaged last night." <i>(Mar 4, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  A pigeongram from Ohinemuri to-day reports rumours of reef and alluvial finds, but nothing definite. <i>(Mar 13, 1875,Otago Witness,13</i>)

### pigeonwood
 
 <i>n.</i> pigeon(-)wood <i>Hedycarya arborea</i>, Poro kaiwhiri(a), Poporo kauwhiri(a); also called poro-kaiwhiri; a small tree bearing long red berries [[1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers, supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) <i>Hedycarya arborea</i> pigeonwood [a1927] Ver. = Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) (Stony Bay) <i>Hedycarya arborea</i> pigeon wood; a1926 Story Bay, Banks Peninsula (Vernac). (Long, orange-red berries, presumably attractive to pigeons: cf <i>Pigeonberry</i>)]

>  <i>Hedycarya arborea</i> (pigeonwood).. [is one of] a good list to select from; most have grown and are growing on the ranges about Wellington. <i>(Aug 12, 1912,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  &#65279;There is a large amount of towa [sic] inthis bush, nio, matipo, some rimu,pigeon wood, etc., struggle one withanother in rich profusion for anexistence <i>(Oct 28, 1899,NZ Times,3</i>)

### pigfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  AGRIOPUS LEUCOPOECILUS. Richardson. <i>Pig Fish. </i> <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/211</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Ichthyology)]

>  There is another fish, termed the <i>Agriopus leucopoecilus</i> [leucopoccilus sic] (Leather Jacket, or Pigfish), quite different from the fish of that name in the North, which is very palatable, ... but it is very seldom eaten ... <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/486</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  24. <i>Puysegur Point</i> - Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/204</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  4. Pig-fish - <i>Agriopus leucopoecilus</i>. This is called `leather-jacket' in Dunedin, where the fish occurs in immense numbers. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/407</i>)

>  <i>On the Anatomy of the Pig-fish</i> (Agriopus leucopoecilus). <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/111</i>)

>  Congiopodus leucopoecilus, [sic] <i>Richardson. </i> The fish is popularly known as `pig-fish', on account of the grunting noise it makes when taken out of the water and has to gasp for air. It is sometimes called `leather-jacket', a name which, however, is more correctly applied to [<i>Navodon] scaber</i>, another very common fish in Otago Harbour. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/545</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  CONGIOPODUS LEUCOPAECILUS (Richardson). <i>Pigfish; Puramorua</i>. Large numbers commonly sold throughout the year in Christchurch under the name of `silver trumpeter.' Rarely seen in Dunedin markets unless other fish are scarce. ... Distribution: South Island and southern coasts of North Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> (Richardson). PIGFISH; PURAMORUA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,53,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Soldier-fish (<i>Scorpaena cruenta</i>) and the variegated horror with the droll name of Hapuka's Grandfather (<i>Helicolenus percoides</i>) are members of the same order, as also are the Pig-fish (<i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> ) and the Toad-fish (<i>Neophrynichthys latus</i>) whose names appropriately suggest something out of the run of ordinary fish shapes. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,201</i>) [NOTES: Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury']

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> (Richardson). Pigfish. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/417</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Pigfish <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> (Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,112</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 112]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> PIGFISH (PURAMORUA) <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> (Richardson) ...This fish is sometimes called Leather-jacket by many fishermen, probably on account of its leathery skin, but this name is misleading as the proper and popular Leather Jacket is a smaller fish with a tough rough and leathery skin and with one single spine on its back. Many years ago these fish were sold to the public in large numbers but only for a short period when others were exceptionally scarce. scarce. The fish were skinned so that they looked quite different from the fish in its natural state. They were then railed to a country town and sold readily under the name of Snap Jacks or Skip Jacks. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,350</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Purumorua,</i> n. <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> , pig fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,315</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PIGFISH ... <i>Other names: Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i>; puramorua (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,290</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Pigfish .. .. puramorua .. .. <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Fig. 69: MORE PERCH-LIKE FISHES ... 7. Wrasses (a-c. spotties, pigfish, etc) <i>Ibid.</i> 231 Family CONGIOPIDAE. The pigfish (<i>Congiopodus</i> ... ) has a protractile mouth, blotchy protective coloration, and long spines; it grunts when taken from the water. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,223 [caption]</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PIGFISH [<i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> ] ... The Maori name puramorua is not now heard. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,24</i>)

>  <i>pig fish n.</i> See <i>leather-jacket. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  [species name] pigfish .. [scientific name] <i>Congiopodus leucopaecilus</i> .. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> pigfish 1231-41, 1740 PIGFISHES 1230-41 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  Experienced men have called it variously a pig-fish, a devil-fish, a torpedo-fish, and other names, the first of which is the most likely, as there are many varieties of pig-fish. <i>(Aug 16, 1905,Otago Witness,56</i>)

>  Observations have been made on the spawning, ova, and fry of a number of our native food-fishes - vis., blue-cod, sole.. as well as of pig-fish, kokopuru, crayfish.. etc. <i>(Dec 1, 1909,Progress,51</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Agriopus leucopoecilus</i> Rich. Pig-fish <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,300</i>)

### pigmy button daisy
 
 <i>n.</i> Leptinella nana

>  Among New Zealand most-threatened species, the pygmy button daisy, Leptinella nana, is alive and well on the Titahi Bay coast, one of just three colonies left in the country which are being carefully nurtured back to being self-sustaining in the wild. <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)

### pignut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Hunting pignuts on all fours/ In the great funfair none do well./ Tom likes boys and Bob likes whores/ And both will share a bunk in Hell. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,237,1961 ed.</i>)

### Pigroot
 
 <i>n.</i> (the Pigroot) the inland road (State Highway 85) from Palmerston, north of Dunedin, into Central Otago, originally a trail followed by 19th-century goldminers. [probably from the presence of wild pigs in the area] [1889 cit. l.c. a kind of hill, cf. hogback]

>  Some gravelling between the Pigroot and Red cutting was required, and from present end of metal at Daddy's Pinch towards Pigroot. <i>(Nov 30, 1888,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  Palmerston folk, I notice, intend to endeavour to prevent us from getting out rights; but it won't do, dear friends; Mrs. Partington could not keep back the Atlantic, neither can you keep Craig and Co. on the Pigroot line. <i>(Feb 25, 1892,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> Dansey's Pass often had heavy snow at that time of the year, so Theo took the route through Oamaru and then up the Pigroot from Palmerston. <i>(2007,Drybread,265</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Only think how much more graceful all the landscape will appear. / No more pigroots, whalebacks, hogbacks - these names that shall not last; / ... <i>Negro-heads</i> shall be re-christened. I for one will take no heed, / While to me there yet remaineth Negrohead, the fragrant weed. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,3</i>)

>  No map the collector has consulted has shown Pigroot Hill or hotel, and yet it is the most prominent and most used name between Palmerston and Kyeburn. Again and again the collector has heard the remark, `I am going into the Central by the Pigroot,' or `I came down by the Pigroot.' So frequently is it used that one might imagine that certain people used it thinking that the word `root' is in reality `route,' but be that as it may, common usage is the deciding factor in place-names, and Pigroot, with its associations back into the fifties, has come to stay. The name was originally [9] bestowed because the wild pigs rooted up the hillside looking for the roots of the taramea, or speargrass, which they enjoy eating, and which is probably one of the main reasons why speargrass is so rare nowadays. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,8</i>)

>  (Reprinted from the <i>Molyneux Mail</i>) Mr. Kilgour.. has called for tenders to remove and re-erect his premises from the Pigroot to this place. <i>(Oct 15, 1864,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  On the Waihemo road the Shag River has similarly prevented the coach from reaching the Pigroot. <i>(Sep 27, 1873,Otago Witness,11</i>)

### pigs nest
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift shelter in bush country.

>  <b>SPENCER</b> They stopped for the night at Peg 58. They made a 'Pigs Nest' of dried twigs and punga fronds. <i>(1992,Waioeka Pioneering Saga,97</i>)

### pigskin
 
 <i>n.</i> a riding saddle

>  Never since the memorable year 1804, when "Mrs. Thornton" appeared on the pigskin as the jockey of Col. Thornton's Vinagarella.. has York witnessed so extraordinary an assemblage of people. <i>(Nov 1, 1851,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,143</i>)

>  He would rather stick to the pigskin than take his ease in a carriage. <i>(Jul 9, 1897,NZ Tablet,5</i>)

>  Now the veriest tyro in equitation knows the difference between riding and bumping the pigskin in the awkward fashion these clumsy equestrians are set to do. <i>(Jul 27, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  His mount negotiated the first two fences well, but slewed over the wing of the last and tripped to somersault and land on the rider, who stuck to the pigskin. <i>(Mar 5, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  They seemed to understand the management of their horses, and stuck well to the pigskin. <i>(Jun 12, 1875,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  This horse had a big spill at the bank, but Northcott stuck to the pigskin. <i>(Nov 11, 1884,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All rolling country, ... and all mustering is done per `pigskin'. <i>Ibid.</i> 149 Next morning found us in the `pigskin', headed for the Deep Creek hut in Eskhead. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,21</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Riding saddle. To travel per pigskin is to ride on horseback.</i> <i>((9/0/1949),High Country Days,196,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> He also has an immense amount of riding to do, and is as much at home in the pigskin as some men are in their easy chairs. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,204</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We travelled .... just forty [miles] per pigskin. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,120</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> `stick to the pigskin' is the converse of `show daylight' (between one's seat and saddle). <i>(Apr 21, 1952,Letter</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> And once Togo got the bit between his teeth, it was a case of `Stick to the pigskin' and hope for the best. <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,117</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> travel per pigskin = - on horseback. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Today ... it is all done in the `pig-skin' (saddle) where the few feet of height gained from being mounted make all the difference to visibility. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,99</i>)

>  It was clear he had better have stuck to the pigskin - a thing, by the way, often easier said than done. <i>(Sep 14, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  If you're a dandy rider, and you fancy you can stick / To the pigskin, when it's strapped round any horse's hide / If you're only five-and-twenty, and feel in rattling nick - / The Cape's the place for you ... <i>(Feb 22, 1900,Otago Witness,66</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [fig.] having savings.

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> He made up his mind that when he got into the 'pigskin' again, he would ask her to marry him. <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,45</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) horseback riding with a saddle.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In all, we travelled seventy miles by goods train and just on forty per pigskin - and thought it worth it. <i>(1947,Straggle Muster,120</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were travelling per 'pigskin' with their 'swags' lashed to the pommels of their saddles ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,12</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> To travel 'pigskin' from the Pohokura hut at the southern end of Timahanga to the Mangaminga hut at the head of the Ngaruroro River involves a ride of sixty miles. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,149</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> These [mustering] gangs were made up of men who'd taken up the "burnt chops," ate "hockey sticks", wore "Bill Masseys" slept in a "scratcher" and rode pigskin .. A scratcher's a bunk and when you're riding pigskin, you're in a saddle. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  They do not walk, they hoof it or ride their hobnails, and on horseback they ride the pigskin. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### pigskin beat
 
 <i>n.</i> a musterer's beat which necessitates using a horse

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>(Apr 21, 1952,Letter</i>)

### pigskin country
 
 <i>n.</i> country which is negotiable (only) on horseback

>  Pigskin-country-that which can be ridden over <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The bulk of Castle Hill was pigskin country (ie. rideable) <i>(1947,Wayleggo,90</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [ high country mustering] land that is not too steep to be traversed by saddle horses.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The bulk of Castle Hill was pigskin country (i.e. rideable)... <i>(1947,Wayleggo,90</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Craigieburn, shearing only 5,000 sheep, was, except for two days on Bernard, all pigskin country, and presented few problems to a musterer. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,147</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Rocklands of course is all 'pigskin' country. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,162</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pigskin country: country that can be mustered on horseback. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,61</i>)

The old pig tail standard will last and last ... <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,18</i>)

>  Farm Clearing Sale: portable dagging plant, docking gates, cradle, scrim and standards. Electric fence reels and pigtails. [pigstail; pig tail] <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)
