### pigweed
 
 <i>n.</i> various herbs eaten by pigs

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> The slope below had a few fragile ponga dotted here and there, a light covering of the pink-stemmed pigweed, but otherwise no handholds at all. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,125</i>)

>  [The Russian thistle] belongs to the same family as the tumble weed, and also as goosefoot and the common pigweed. <i>(Mar 29, 1894,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  A much more serious measure has arisen in the spread of "ragwort" and "pigweed". <i>(Mar 21, 1902,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Any gardener who wishes to grow roses must be merciless in regard to pigweed. <i>(1911,Triad, The,18/9/36</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PORTULACA (<i>Portulaca oleracea</i>), called also pig weed, hog weed, purslane, and ice plant, is very common on garden paths etc., in Auckland, becoming less and less common as far south as Nelson. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,72,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pigweed [=] redroot: rhagodia: twin cress: wart cress: wild portulaca: willow weed: wireweed</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,58</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> wire weed

>  Polygonum aviculare Pigweed <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/126</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WIREWEED (<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>), also called road weed, mat weed, knot grass, `pig weed,' and hog weed, is common in cultivated land all over the country. It is an annual, with stems up to 2ft. in length trailing over the ground. The stems have swollen nodes (`knot grass') and are very tough (`wireweed'). <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,1967,66 ed.</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> red root

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> RED ROOT (<i>Amaranthus [hybridus</i> and <i>A. retroflexus</i>), also called red shank and pig weed, is found in gardens and waste places all over the North Island and as far south as Nelson [Canterbury]. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,70,1967 ed.</i>)

### pigwood
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>GENIOSTOMA LIGUSTRIFOLIUM, hangehange</i>. A common, lightly branched shrub of lowland forest margins and track-sides in the North Island ... The waxy-surfaced, apple-green leaves ... might commend this as a desirable garden shrub were it not that the small greenish flowers, ... have an odd smell that gives rise to the name `pigwood' in some districts. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,118</i>)

### piharau
 
 <i>n.</i> lamprey

>  From 8 a.m. to 4 p.m., preparations were made for the refreshment of the people, when 200 birds (titi), 200 fishes (piharau), 200 loaves of bread, besides several pigs and a supply of potatoes, were served up. <i>(Aug 27, 1864,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  They caught some of those fish as well as tuna and piharau. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 55</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Blind eels like tuere and piharau - veryunusual taste. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,39</i>)

>  It is a species of lamprey, and known to the Maoris as piharau. <i>(Nov 1, 1894,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  These people had certain fishing rights in the Huatoki river, and had set the usual traps for the piharau, or lamprey eels, in the river bed. <i>(Oct 2, 1905,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I found my former quarters not very fragrant, having about 400 piha piharau [?<i>piha</i> error], (lampreys) which were presented to us when we were here before ... <i>(Jun 2, 1846,Journal,4/33</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Geotria chilensis</i> Gray Lamprey, Piharau, or Puhikorokoro <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,306</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Piharau, n</i>. Maori name for<i> Geotria chilensis</i>, Gray, a New Zealand <i>Lamprey</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,353</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> `We procured an abundant supply of piarau, a `lamprey,' which is taken in large numbers in this river, and some others in the neighbourhood, when the waters are swollen.' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/15</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Lampreys (<i>piharau</i>) were caught in loose mats of fern laid in small streams ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,108</i>)

>  GEOTRIA AUSTRALIS. <i>Lamprey.</i> Called `piharu' [sic] and `korokoro' by the Maoris. <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/97</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

>  1. <i>Geotria australis</i> Gray. Lamprey. Maori: Korokoro, Piharau. <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/139</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]



 
 <i>n.</i> whitebait [a mistake? Given in most Maori dictionaries as "lamprey"]

>  <b>MILLER</b> Here the sea teemed with fish, the streams with eels, and in the season the <i>piharau</i> or whitebait was to be found in the Waitara River. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Pihapiharau </i>(the lamprey), is almost eighteen inches long, of a silvery color; there is a saying, <i>`No Rangiriri te pihapiharau,' `The lamprey comes from the fountain of Rangiriri.</i>' The name of the fish is derived from its many gills. <i>Ibid.</i> 627 Fam. <i>Petromyzidae, (Pihapiharau,)</i> the Lamprey, which is found in most of the rivers to the south. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,412</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a girdle

 <i>n.</i>

### pihoihoi
 
 <i>n.</i> the (native) pipit a native ground lark, Anthus novaeseelandiae [also pihoi hoi]

>  The "Pihoihoi" (or swallow sitting aloneon the housetop) was too weak an advocateto be admitted to tread with impunity onthe infirmities of the self-satisfiedstate of affairs than [sic] subsistingamongst the Maoris of the Waikato. <i>(Feb 12, 1864,Otago Witness</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>Anthus novae-zelandiae</i>, Gray, Voy. Ereb. and Terror, Birds, p. 4 (1844) ..Pihoihoi and Whioi; "Ground-Lark" of the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/63</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Pihoihoi</i> - This bird resembles the canary in shape and size .. It might not be improperly designated the ground-lark, which it very much resembles. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),58</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>piohiohi</i> is a species of `poor cock-robin,' equally fitted to `point a moral, and adorn a tale,' as his antipodal species. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/300</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Alanda Novae Seelandiae. Gmel. .. Kogoo aroure of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst.</i> Kataitai of the natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. A `Ground Lark' is given under the name of Pihoihoi, by Mr Yate; Piohiohi, by Mr Polack; Pi-o-oie, by M. Lesson, which may prove to be the above species. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/192</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192 35.]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pihoi hoi - name of a little bird <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/378</i>)

>  Anthus Novae Zelandiae. Pihoihoi. Lark. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  ANTHUS NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Gml. Pihoihoi. Lark. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/61</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  ANTHUS NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE. - Ground Lark <i>(Pihoihoi). </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/102</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 33. ANTHUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Ground Lark. Pihoihoi. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,13</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW -ZEALAND PIPIT.) .. <i>Native names</i>. Pihoihoi and Whioi; `Ground-Lark' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,132,1888 2nd edn 1/63 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Piihoihoi</i> n. <i>ground lark; anthus Novae Zelandiae. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,115</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Pihoi (<i>Anthus N.Z.</i>) is the so-called native `lark.' It is a ground pipit, and may often be seen fluttering and chirping about a bush road. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  32. <i>Anthus Novae-Zealandiae</i>, Gml. Lark, Pipit, Pihoihoi. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,288</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pihoihoi</i> .. Maori name for a New Zealand bird, the Ground-lark (q.v.). The word has five syllables. <i>(1898,Austral English,353</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I never saw the native quail, the cuckoo nor the morepork in the Central [Otago, c1860s], but I saw the sparrow-hawk, the rock wren, and plenty of hawks and pihoihoi (ground larks). <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121</i>)

>  The Government paper was christened Te Pihoihoi Mohemohe or "The Sparrow that sitteth alone upon the house-top." <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(9) : 20</i>)

>  <i>pihoihoi</i>: New Zealand pipit. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND PIPIT <i>Anthus novaeseelandiae Other names</i>: Pihoihoi, Sandlark (Chathams). .. The Skylark, with which it is commonly confused, lives in pasture land where the Pipit is generally absent, but the ranges of the two species overlap in rough open country, etc. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,216</i>)

>  <i>Pipit Anthus novaeseelandiae </i>(GMELIN, 1789) .. <i>New Zealand Pipit A.n. novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1789). <i>Auckland Islands Pipit A.n. aucklandicus</i> GRAY, 1862. <i>Antipodes Pipit A.n. steindachneri</i> REISCHEK, 1889. .. OTHER NAMES: <i>Pihoihoi, groundlark, native lark. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,270</i>)

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NAT `PIHOIHOI' (NZ Lark) <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

>  The sweet notes of the pihoihoi or New Zealand robin - first chorister of the dawn - echo from the bush. <i>(Apr 14, 1892,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  The pihoihoi, the native ground-lark, is a common bird here. <i>(Dec 24, 1908,Evening Post,15</i>)

### pikao
 
 <i>n.</i> [see pingao]

### pikari
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> PROSTHEMADRA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (TUI OR PARSON-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Tui and Koko; the young bird distinguished as Pi-tui or Pikari. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,87,1888 2nd edn 1/94 ed.</i>)

### pikau
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to carry on one's back. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The whole of the stolen articles.. were placed upon the prisoner's back, and he was compelled to pikau them to the Hill Farm, whence he had taken them. <i>(Jul 1, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  The winding nature of the river makes the distance up to where the reef was found very long and wearisome, all the stores, &c., necessary on such an expedition having to be carried "pikau," as there is no means of getting horses in. <i>(Apr 5, 1890,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> I crossed a Stream a dozen times, mostly on a Mans back called a Pekow (pikau). <i>Ibid.</i> 72 He picked out two of the strongest of the Boys about the place to carry "Pekow" us over the River. <i>(1834,New Zealand (Heaphy),42</i>) [NOTES: 1963 edition]

>  "You pikau te canoe, quick." <i>(Apr 15, 1931,Aussie (NZ Section),XI</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... nay, he may with due deference to public opinion, carry (<i>pekau</i>) pickback, the said foreigner, be his dimensions long or short, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/171-172 The flax in a basket generally weighs about from [172] eighty to a hundred pounds, and is either carried (<i>pikou</i>), on men's shoulders or in canoes, to the house of the purchaser. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/59</i>)

>  They do not need to pikau, or carry, their produce to the town. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  It will scarcely be argued that exhibitors will <i>pikau</i> the result of their labours 10 or 15 miles to a show when they have neglected one at their own doors. <i>(Apr 2, 1892,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

>  <b>J WILLIAMS</b> Henry plastered it [the gash] under my directions and we sent for a couple of his mates to pikau him home. <i>(Oct 10, 1840,Journal,133</i>) [NOTES: in Porter (1974) Turanga Journals]

>  <b>HAY</b> Both men and women are able to pikau ( hump, or carry on the back and shoulders ) great weights. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,II 94</i>)

>  News arrived that 200 men are expected to be down from the Waikato, to "pikau" these guns across the country. <i>(Oct 3, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Many of them continue for years to pikau their provisions over roads that can never be made passable for drays. <i>(Jun 14, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... Jake exerted himself and got five Boys to Pekow the Traps across at 2lb [tobacco] each to Kiddy Kiddy. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),61,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> `I tell you what we must do ... we will not carry (<i>pikau</i> ) the provisions, we will <i>hiki</i> them' (<i>Hiki</i> is the word in Maori which describes the act of carrying an infant in the arms.) <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(viii) 129</i>)

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> `Tommy' and Rairaina <i>pikaued </i>the swag. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,79</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Three grand days in the saddle, the last winding down the bush road that skirted the ... Ohura ... then `pikau' and foot-slog for the great roadless forest. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,6</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [ca1950 (North Island Trampers') NCC Small]



 
 <i>n.</i> a garment [2 citations, doubtful]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|ikau, <i>s</i>. A garment so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,192</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pikau - a load, burden, a garment <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/378</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [2 x attrib. pikau burden, pikau straps]

>  We carefully packed in our blankets, which, fitted with 'pikau' straps, formed our swags. <i>(Feb 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  We were 6 in party, ourselves, young Stark and 3 Maoris with `pikau' burdens. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/79,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Richmond)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [in relation to piggy back but not quite! ca1950 (North Island Trampers') Pickaback NCCS.]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> My father took charge of his offspring, carrying him on his back 'pikau' (Maori fashion.) <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,285</i>)

>  `Connected with pick-a-back' This is incorrect <i>pikau</i> seems to have Polynesian cognates. The word itself may have been associated with <i>pickaback</i> by English speakers. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: cf 1946 J.C. Andersen Maori words]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> I crossed a Stream a dozen times, mostly on a Mans back called a (Pekow [<i>pikau</i>] is to carry Upanga [<i>hapainga</i>], lift me up.) [1963 fn] Though the meaning of this sentence is fairly clear, punctuation and syntax are even more defective than usual: <i>pikau</i> means to carry on the back; <i>hapainga</i> is the passive form of <i>hapai,</i> lift up, raise. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42,1963 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a back pack. [also refers to a packed lunch often carried in a pikau]

>  On their arrival at Mokau, the people were all away; and Newton, supposing that due deference would be paid to his visit, returned - and Mr. Swainson, with his "pikau" men (native carriers) proceeded. <i>(Apr 11, 1846,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> At night we made a pikau - a bag slung on the back - for me to carry my gum in. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,106</i>)

>  Hers was a life of constant moving.. always labouring, in the gum fields or at the pa, carrying pikaus of gum or preparing the food. <i>(Oct 4, 1943,NZEF Times,III/118 : 10</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Once somebody asked him where he was going, pikau on his back, rifle slung over his shoulder and axe in hand. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,45</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Deceased had a pekau on his back. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,6</i>)

>  They had to endure all these discomforts, carry their own pikau (as I have seen Bishop Selwyn do alone) climb slippery mud hills, and encounter all the petty ills so graphically sketched by your correspondent, and what else? <i>(Oct 22, 1873,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  When Harry first came to these hills hecarried his supplies in a sugar sackpikau over his shoulder. <i>(Jan 9, 1994,Sunday Times (NZ),19</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I left some of my pikaus with Mr. Duncan. <i>(1849,Journals and Correspondence,6/9 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Several [natives] carried their <i>pikau</i> (or knapsacks strapped over their shoulders with leaves of flax. <i>(1850,Savage Life,2/3,1850 ed.</i>)

>  The Maoris .... made up all our packages ... into three pikaws or bundles of about 35lb. each which they tied up and slung over their shoulders with strips of native flax leaf. <i>(Feb 23, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/82,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.R.Richmond)]

>  <b>ABRAHAM</b> My <i>pikau</i> (or burden) weighed 30lbs. <i>(1856,Walk with the Bishop</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> One [Maori prisoner] ... had on an oilskin, a hat, and carried a small `pikau', his wardrobe I suppose. <i>(Nov 1863,Journal,123,1963 ed.</i>)

>  On his return fron Rangatiki, the blue blanket pikau was gone. <i>(1869,McLean Papers,30/147</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... our pikaus became apparently heavier ... as we trudged to our destination. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,195</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> These Ngati-Mutunga ... mde up small casks of powder in flax-basket <i>pikaus</i> or back-loads, and transported them through all the forests and ranges of Pukerua and along the northern shore of the bay. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/122</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> .. this huge midden [of shellfish] .. had been brought by pikau on the backs of old Maori women ... <i>(1952,Faring South,80</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> [He] sat down with his pikau, in which he had a freshly-slain leg of pork, strategically placed in his lap. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,70</i>)

>  <b>HOGAN</b> Days of Billy-can and Pikau,/ Horse and Buggy, sleet and snow,/ Railway camp and crowded `Boozer',/ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,1</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> pikau: sugar-bag used for carrying food or other things, shouldered by means of ropes attached to the corners. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> As we leave the hut for the day's work we each have on our backs a `pikau' - a sugar-bag rucksack containing tucker for our lunch and `smoke-o's'. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,88</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The pikau (load for the back) was a half-sack, with a strap attached to each of the bottom corners and joined at the middle of the top - in the manner of a knapsack. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,50</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I had on my shoulders my few items of clothing ... all packed into a sugar bag transformed by a loop of light rope into that handy pack for trampers which the Maoris called <i>pikau</i> . <i>(1977,More Than Enough,25</i>)

>  Stories are still told of the prodigious loads they would carry over the hills in their pikaus. <i>Ibid.</i> 33 Still another sack made the standard haversack type of container with shoulder straps for carrying loads on the back. This essential piece of equipment was invariably called a pikau, a Maori word meaning to carry on the back, but also used in either language as a noun. <i>(1970,Way Up North,8</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> The rest of his luggage would be a <i>pikau</i> slung on his shoulders; and my mother ... would ask him what he meant by daring to appear before respectable people looking like a swagger. <i>(1973,Once Is Enough,12</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> [The pighunter] ... dipping his hand into his pikau and lifting out the remarkably well-preserved skull ... <i>Ibid.</i> 43 Both of [the Canterbury pighunters] carried a pikau (a daypack which fits snugly over one's shoulders and is often fashioned out of a sugar-bag) as they ranged those big [sheep] stations. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,40</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> After sowing grass seed ... by hand from pikaus carried on their backs, they started on the farm's first permanent buildings. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,24</i>)

>  <i>pikau n. NZ</i> swag. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,568</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 366]]

>  We were 6 in party, ourselves, young Stark & 3 Maoris with `pikau' burdens. ... After tiring of [singing] they set off with burning brand & tomahawk to catch crawfish & tuna, the eels of the country, in a little brook near our encampment. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/79,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <i>pikau</i> , pack carried on the back: to carry on the back: to mount on the back of another. Connected with pick-a-back. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,5/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>pikau</i> : pack carried on the back. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> I then ordered the pikaus', or packages, to be shouldered, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/235</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pikau - a load, burden, a garment <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/378</i>)

>  ... but all ideas of carrying anything ourselves, were dispelled by the Maoris, who speedily made up all our packages together with some of their own, into three <i>pikaws</i> or bundles of about 35lb each which they tied up & slung over their shoulders with strips of native flax leaf which serves for such purposes without any preparation. <i>(Feb 23, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/82,1960 ed.</i>)

>  The outfit required [for gum-digging] is a spade, a spear, and a pikau. <i>(Aug 31, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 8</i>)

>  <b>J WILLIAMS</b> Nonekai who went to carry one of Henry's pikaus as far as Toanga, not having returned, I began to fear he might be in some difficulty ... <i>(Oct 30, 1840,Journal,135</i>) [NOTES: in Porter (1974) Turanga Journals]

>  <b>J WILLIAMS</b> The horses at the door for Wm & Mr Dudley on one side, and the Waiapu natives clamouring for their pikaus at the garden gate on the other side... <i>(Oct 5, 1842,Journal,216</i>) [NOTES: in Porter (1974) Turanga Journals]

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> We were 6 in party, ourselves, young Stark and 3 Maoris with 'pikau' burdens. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Letter,79</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>WATERSON</b> The tackle, bait and a few sandwiches were carried out of harm's way in a pikau on our backs ... 2. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,144</i>)

>  A meal you eat in a paddock or on the job: a pikau. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> So `there and then' [the wild pig's] inside was torn out ... and, his legs tied together so as to make him a handy `pikau' ... <i>Ibid.</i> 201 My quarry (pig) - a la pikau - on my back ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,49</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... a week's provisions and other fixings in your rolled-up `Pikau' of oilskin on your back; ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,10</i>)

>  ... then swag and footslog for the roadless forest. ... our provisions ... divided into equal sized `pikaus' among us ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... Maclean ... must have walked some thousands of miles ... carrying his swag as the Maoris carried their <i>pikau</i> . <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,4</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Clad in our `bush' costume, but without weapons, and each with a<i> toko toko</i> or long walking-staff in our hands, my fellow traveller and myself set off in excellent spirits, accompanied by five Maori lads, who carried our baggage ... in their <i>pikau</i> or knapsacks, strapped over their shoulders with the leaves of flax. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/3</i>)

>  One who seemed somewhat of a "swell", had on an oilskin, a hat, and carried a small "pikau", his wardrobe I suppose. <i>(Nov 26, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  We found it no joke to travel through the bush with mud in many places nearly to our knees with a pikau on our backs. <i>(Jul 3, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,9</i>)

>  The settler found he could not swim ashore with the <i>pikau </i>on his back, and asked the natives for help. <i>(Feb 7, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [figurative]

>  <b>COWAN</b> But the Maori generally is manfully shouldering the <i>pikau</i> of this twentieth century and is in some quarters showing that, given the same facilities, he can even outstrip the <i>pakeha</i> in the walks of civilized life. <i>(1930,Maori, The,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [back pack]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> esp. one made of a sugarbag

>  <b>BROOKS</b> Most of us were now loaded with heavy pekoes or swags on our backs. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,148</i>)

>  The rest of [the farmer's] luggage would be a <i>peko</i> slung on his shoulders. <i>(1950,Landfall,16/285</i>) [NOTES: (Auckland)]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He.. sat down again with his pikau, in which he had a freshly-slain leg of pork strategically in his lap. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,70</i>)

>  One of the natives left us at Woon's .... so we had to put the pico on the mule and trudge along. <i>(1834,McLean Papers,5/265</i>) [NOTES: (Camphell, Wanganui)]

>  The couple were loaned an old horse and went off home with a pikau loaded up with meat, vegetables and fresh bread. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No.30/12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [piggy back]

>  <b>POLACK</b> [The Maori] may with due deference to public opinion, carry (Pekau) Pickback the said foreigner. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/59</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Some natives pikaued me across them <i>(Oct 29, 1842,Journal,5</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Riria Raihi came to his assistance and although Mr Graham was no light weight, took him on her back and pikaued him to the top <i>(1879,Waikato War,86</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... woe betide the tender, forming backs, as they grew strong enough to waha, or pikau (carry) the baby. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,89</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> To get upon the back Pekou <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/337</i>)

>  <i>Pikau</i>, pack carried on the back: to carry on the back: to mount on the back of another. Connected with pick-a-back. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [back pack]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [gumfields / back pack]

>  ANY one found tresspassing on the property of the undersigned with spade, spear or pikau in his possession will be prosecuted. <i>(Sep 16, 1899,Northern Advocate,5</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> He commenced to fill his <i>pikau</i>. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,50</i>)

>  The last time he saw deceased alive was about 9 a.m. on April 5 going by the Wairakei bridge to follow his occupation of gum-digging, having his pikau, with spear, spade, and axe in his possession. <i>(Apr 29, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/2</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> ... lonehand gumdiggers ... spade in hand, pikau on back ... <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,5</i>)

>  The gumdiggers used a <i>pikau</i> , a bag with shoulder straps, a good deal smaller than the usual tramper's pack. They were sold under that name by Leroy's in Auckland in the thirties. If <i>pikau</i> had been current further south trampers might have adopted it. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,27</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>REED</b> Pouch strap-ped at his [the gum-digger's] side, <i>pikau</i> (bag with shoulder straps) thrown down in the shelter of a clump of tea tree. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,102</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> [`pikau'] <i>(1905,Fall of Bush,281 pass.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [Ma] split sack or saddlebag used on a horse [saddlebag; incl peacow]

>  The farthest back-settler could bring in the produce in one pikau on his horse. <i>(Feb 21, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Rua very kindly strapped the "pikau nui" on one of his steeds. <i>(1929,NZ Railways Magazine,4(7) : 43</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `I often wonder on looking back now,' remarked Jane, `how I ever managed, wet and almost numb with the cold as I sometimes was, to keep the pikau of mail and parcels in place, and get through some of those dreadful gates ... [1890's] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,101</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> ...pulling a hindquarter of vension from his sugar-sack pikau... <i>(1964,Stag Party,80</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. and what the Maoris call a pikau - a split sack which forms two capacious pouches across the withers of a horse. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,16</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> David hunted up a sacking pikau to drop over his saddle in case there should be venison and skins to bring home. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,102</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> .. In a farming context it is usually a sugarbag made into an extemporised rucksack with some rope or a strip of flaxleaf. It can also be a chaff-sack, closed at both ends, and one side slit across at the centre and used with this side uppermost. Thrown across, either under or over the saddle, the two large saddlebags thus formed could be used to bring home some wool plucked from a dead sheep, or as a sheltered place to carry a weakling lamb. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,61</i>)

>  The couple were loaned an old horse and went off home with a pikau loaded up with meat, vegetables and fresh bread. 3. <i>(2001,NZ Memories,June/July  12</i>)

>  <b>WATTS</b> They would arrive about lunchtime with peacows slung over their saddles. A peacow is a chaff sack, sewn up at both ends with a slit made across the centre of one side of the sack. When slung over the saddle it formed two large pockets in which the men carried their 'glad rags' or best clothes. <i>(1990,Wild Horses & Me,52</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> He vaguely remembered ... cutting the tail off the animal and putting it in the pekau on the saddle of his horse. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,39</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> David hunted up a sacking pikau to drop over his saddle in case there should be venison ... to bring back. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,102</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Dad led the pack horse, and a troop of us youngsters followed on our ponies (bareback), each with a pikau laden with goods. Occasionally an unevenly balanced pikau would slide off, taking the unlucky rider with it. ... Mum supervised the unpacking of the cases [of big stores orders]. Certain packages and parcels were placed in her pikau, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 66 After removing our pikaus, saddle bags and swag, we unharnessed the horses and turned them loose in the `house paddock'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,34</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> two bags slung each side of a horse's back to carry wool etc in; pocket [ca1930 (D. Singe.)]

### pikau bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> ... the heavy canvas pikau bags which draped over the saddle and hung down on either side to about stirrup level ... were waterproof when fording rivers. <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,65</i>)

### pikau load
 
 <i>n.</i> [single cit which refers to saddle bag]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The few possessions that we had at the homestead we were able to pack over on a pack saddle, plus a pikau load on the hack ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,100</i>)

### pikaw
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pikau](per-pop.md#pikau))

>  .. but all ideas of carrying anything ourselves, were dispelled by the Maoris, who speedily made up all our packages together with some of their own, into three pikaws or bundles of about 35lb each which they tied up & slung over their shoulders with strips of native flax leaf which serves for such purposes without any preparation. <i>(1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/82,1960 ed.</i>)

### pike
 
 <i>v.</i> to back out of or shirk a commitment [ca1941]

>  <b>SIMONS</b> Nagged at me every day to show her. And now she's piking - the `scaredy cat'. <i>(1979,Harper's Mother,116</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Sphyraena obtusata</i> Cuv. & Val. PIKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,30,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Pike <i>Sphyraena grandisquamis</i> Steindachner <i>(1956,Sea Fishes of NZ,405</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 405]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BARRACOUTA </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Thyrsites atun</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Pike; Couta; Snoek; Barra Fillets. (c) Other common names: B/couta; Bait. (d) Principal localities where taken: Gisborne, Motueka, Nelson, Timaru, Port Chalmers. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,11</i>)

>  [species name] barracuda .. [scientific name] <i>Sphyraena</i> sp. .. [other name(s)] true barracuda, sea pike <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] pike 1661-2 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### pike on
 
 <i>v.</i> to let down

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pike on</i> to let down; eg 'Hal had the caterers in and a ton of booze, huge waste, half his mates piked on him.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

>  <b>BRANDON</b> <i>PIKELETS </i>... Flour ... Soda ... [Taretaric] Acid dissolved in a Cupful of Good Milk ... Sugar ... Milk to make a good batter ... [no method given] <i>(1905,'Ukneadit' Tried Recipes,66</i>) [NOTES: 1905 Brandon `UNKNEADIT'. Home for Incurables Bazaar 66]

### pike out
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pike out</i> to depart, usually from lusty drinking session, to disapproval of those continuing, but also any agreed activity or deal; eg `Don't pike out now, Reg, the fun's just starting.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,84</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> But we're not piking out, there are other ways we can defend ourselves. <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,28</i>)

### pike whale
 
 <i>n.</i> [not clear that there is any specific NZ usage]

>  Mr Joseph Day, Sumner - Skeleton of Balaenoptera Huttoni, male (Hutton's pike whale), stranded on Sumner beach. <i>(Apr 29, 1880,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Mr F. Grosch, German conchologist, has found a remarkable beak and skull, three feet long, weighing about 40lb, on the beach at Bream Bay, near Waipu. It is believed to have been the head of a pike whale. <i>(Jun 16, 1892,Auckland  Star,3</i>)

>  On the pike whale the beak-like head, when raised out of the water, would suggest that of a pelican, as the throat sags heavily. <i>(Jul 7, 1930,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i>Stickleback </i> - A minke whale. <i> Balaenoptera acutorostrata </i> . The Peranos killed only one of these. They found them uneconomic to hunt. The stickleback was also sometimes called the pike whale by the Perano whalers. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,228</i>)

>  The pike whale (<i> Balaenoptera acutorostrata </i> ) is the smallest of the rorquals, reaching a length of 33 ft. only <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/134</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  The whale was a little fellow, a bull of the variety known as the piked whale, which attains a length of only 15 ft. in its life of 15 years. <i>(Jun 23, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),2</i>)

### pike-pole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  To his astonishment, however, on approaching the raft with men and gear, he found it manned by about a dozen excited Maoris armed with pike-poles, and eight or nine Europeans, together with the gentleman to whom he had given notice of his intention. <i>(Nov 7, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The men chopped and pried with their pike poles for several hours while little streams of water shot through between the massed timbers, and as the spray arose it covered their clothing with ice, which together with their great efforts benumbed arms, legs, and body, and added to their exhaustion greatly. <i>(Apr 25, 1903,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> To another bad place two miles further I sent five more men. The remaining seven and least active of the party I stationed two miles above the boom, where they had to lend a hand as occasion [sc a log-jam] may require. All were armed with either a `pike-pole,' timber-jack, or axe. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,177</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Succeeding logs were pushed in position by pike poles. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,225</i>)

>  This scoundrel was armed with a pike-pole, and stabbed one of Craig's men below the heart. <i>(Nov 1, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

### pikelet
 
 <i>n.</i> a small pancake.

>  The farmer's wife at length tried the experiment of boiling the flour, and then pounding it; and admirably it succeeded, more beautiful bread, cakes, and pikelets than this flour produced were never eaten. <i>(Apr 12, 1871,Bruce Herald,7</i>)

>  Cakes, Pastry, Scones, Buns, Pikelets and other dainties are sure to be successful when you use Edmonds "Sure to Rise" Baking Powder. <i>(Feb 3, 1923,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  <i>PIKELETS</i> Grease a slide with butter and drop a tablespoon of mixture on it ... -->girdle and frying pan co-exist as means for cooking PIKELETS ... <i>PIKELETS</i> Fry in a little butter <i>(1911,Our Boys Cookery Book,111</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>PIKELETS</i> . Mrs.C.A.Lahmert, Hanmer. Two eggs, 1/2 cup sugar, 1 cup milk, 2 cups flour, 1 teaspoon cr of tartar, 1/2 teaspoon soda, 2 tablespoons melted butter. Beat eggs and sugar well ... Cook on hot greased girdle till nicely browned - <i>PIKELETS</i> . Mrs.Crampton, Timalyn. Two eggs [etc] ..., and as much flour as will make a thin batter. Drop on buttered girdle or over sheet. <i>(1915,Amuri Cookery Book,71,1927 ed.</i>)

>  <i>PIKELETS</i> ... flour ... sugar ... egg ... Edmonds' Baking Powder ... milk ... butter ... Cook in small lots on a hot greased girdle. <i>(1916,Edmonds Cookery Book,29,4th ed.</i>)

>  <b>FUTTER</b> <i>PIKELETS</i> ... rub pan with butter, drop in spoonfuls batter, cook ... <i>(1926,Home Cookery for NZ,206</i>)

>  <b>McKAY</b> <i>PIKELETS</i> bake on a hot girdle ... <i>(1929,Practical Home Cookery,179</i>)

>  Pikelets. One breakfast cup wholemeal, dessertspoon brown sugar, 1 egg, 1/2 teaspoon soda, 1 cup or more milk, 1oz butter or little cream, last 1 teaspoon cream of tartar. Pikelets. Beat 2 tablespoons of sugar with 1 egg till light and frothy. Add scant cup of milk, 1-1/2 cups of flour, 1 teaspoon cream of tartar, 1/2 teaspoon soda. Mix well together and then add 1 tablespoonful hot water and 1 tablespoonful of butter (water and butter are secret of light pancakes). Drop in spoonfuls on greased girdle or oven tray. <i>(1936,Cookery Book of NZ Women's Institutes,107</i>) [NOTES: [3rd??? recipe also in 1955 edn]]

>  <i>PIKELETS</i> ... sugar ... 1 egg ... milk ... flour ... cream of <i>(1938,'Milkmade' Cook Book,41</i>)

>  <i>PIKELETS</i> ... cream of tartar ... soda ... milk ... butter ... 1 egg ... sugar ... Pour batter from tip of a tablespoon, ... As soon as bubbles begin to appear, turn over ... They will be about a half-inch thick ... and stay spongy. <i>(1930,Electric Cookery - Hints Instruction and Recipes,42</i>)

>  <i>PIKELETS</i> ... flour ... sugar ... baking powder ... 1 egg ... cream. Mix with milk until thickness of light batter. <i>(1936,Merry Meal Maker Cookery Book,33</i>)

>  PIKELETS (2)/ Beat 2 tablespoons of sugar with one egg till light and frothy. Add scant cup of milk, 1[1/2] cups of flour, 1 teaspoon cream of tartar, 1/2 teaspoon soda. Mix well together then add 1 tablespoonful hot water and one tablespoon of butter (water and butter are the secret of light pancakes). Drop in spoonfuls on hot greased girdle. [cp.] SCOTCH PIKELETS (1) ... 2 cups flour, 2 eggs, 1/4 cup sugar, 2 teaspoons cream of tartar, 1 dessertspoon syrup, 1 teaspoon baking soda, small piece of butter, 1/2 teaspoon salt, 1/2 cup milk. Beat well and cook on hot girdle. <i>(1955,New Home Cookery,102</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> She ... was greasing the girdle for pikelets that would be made on the coal stove, the batter dropped in spoonfuls on the smoking girdle, and rising and bubbling and browning... <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,72,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> `Crickey! I nearly forgot!' said Alf Saunders. `The wife gave me a few pikelets for supper. She thought you would've been too busy to do any cooking yet.' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,18</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Then she made pikelets for tea. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,119</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i> pikelet</i>: A small pancake (like a silver dollar pancake) usually served cold, topped with butter, jam or whipped cream to accompany morning or afternoon tea. The English call these griddle scones or scotch pancakes. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,77</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Pikelets are to afternoon tea what pavlova is to the wedding breakfast : indispensable! Early Scottish immigrants added baking powder to a recipe known in their home country as `drop scones' and the result - pikelets - has become a New Zealand specialty. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,152</i>)

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> <i>pikelet</i> Scotch pancake, drop scone. I.M. Peacocke's <i>The Good Intentions of Angela </i>(1937) page 25. <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> Here comes Mrs Harrison with her tatty kids and her gruesome pikelets. <i>(1986,Then Again,194</i>)

>  <i>pikelet n. NZ & Austral.</i> small pancake made by dropping spoonful of mixture on heated surface of pan. [shortened form of Eng. dial, <i>bara-picklet</i> (W <i>bara pyglyd</i> pitchy bread] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,569</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 366]]

>  The next item brought the first part of the programme to a close, Mrs Denniston giving a capital recipe for pikelets. <i>(Jul 14, 1892,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  DUTCH CAKES, Hot Muffins, and Pikelets, 4 p.m. <i>(May 19, 1884,Star, The,2</i>)

>  The fruit cake classes were not so outstanding, but pikelets were a wonderful exhibit, 5 prizes being awarded to give a fair decision. <i>(Jun 4, 1930,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Power of pikelets to lure US tourists...But north of Wellington, some agents told a woman at a farm gate they were after a cow cocky and within minutes came the rural hospitality - a cup of tea and a plate of pikelets. The pikelets were the clincher, some of the agents said. <i>(May 30, 2003,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The drops fall and stiffen like cooked pikelets, miles of tiny pikelets dripping down one on top of the other, and after a while you have sand towers growing up and up to where the last drop has stuck. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,153</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> After protracted legal negotiations with the owners over a cup of tea and fresh pikelets, it was agreed. <i>(1985,Moaville Magic,30</i>)

>  After all those valiant attempts, sheproduces a spongy, stream-lined, super-pikelet, then our selfish "Sec" hoardsher secret. <i>(Feb 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> She holds a tray, and on it are some of those oblate, flour-based entities known as pikelets. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,150</i>)

>  Billy, having finished his cold mutton and salad, attacked the pikelets. <i>(Aug 12, 1924,Otago Witness,72</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> At the booth you can munch pikelets and date scones. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,159</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> At the booth you can munch pikelets and date scones. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,134</i>)

>  The scones, pikelets, Afghans, Anzac biscuits and shortbread with which Sharon filled the biscuit tins on a Saturday had been taught to her by her mum, who in turn had learned them from her Scottish mother. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,13 : 19</i>)

>  A casual mention.. or a post-pikelets discussion.. are just as likely to begin a commercial deal. <i>(Mar 28, 1992,Dominion,10</i>)

>  <i>PIKELETS OR DROP SCONES</i> ... Grease a girdle with butter and drop a tablespoon of mixture on to it ... <i>(1906,Tetaka Kai: A Book of Well Tried Recipes,81</i>)

>  <i>YEAST PIKELETS</i> ... milk and yeast to ... egg ... flour ... Make into small round cakes, and bake on the girdle ... May also be baked in the oven. Serve hot and buttered. <i>(1910,Colonial Everyday Cookery,261</i>) [NOTES: a gem mixture orig. [more like scones or muffins]]

>  <b>BEATON</b> <i>PIKELETS</i> ... same mixture as for gems ... nice and hot; take sharp-pointed tablespoon of the mixture ... drop off the point of the spoon onto the hot greased girdle. Brown on both sides. <i>(1910,Universal Cookery Book,30</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> <i>PIKELETS OR DROP SCONES</i> Flour ... Soda ... Eggs ... Cream of Tartar ... Sugar ... Milk ... <i>(1917,Economic Technical Cookery Book,264</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> <i>PIKELETS OR DROP SCONES</i> Flour ... Soda ... Eggs ... Cream of Tartar ... Sugar ... Milk ... <i>(1917,Economic Technical Cookery Book,264</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> <i>PIKELETS</i> ... Flour ... Carbonate Soda ... Salt ... 1 Egg ... Cream of Tartar ... Milk ... Sugar. ... Mix to a smooth batter with egg and milk. Cook on a well-greased girdle. <i>(1920,Cathedral Brand Essences Cookery Book,25 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SAW</b> `I can still use the top of the stove, and my New Zealand pikelets are coming right.' <i>(1960,In to Id,159</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> [kept as a dry mixture and used, moistened with buttermilk, as required;baked on a girdle] <i>PIKELETS</i> ... Flour ... Salt ... Sugar ... 1 egg ... Soda ... prepared Flour ... Cream of Tartar ... Buttermilk ... Mix dry ingredients well, and put in tin; ... <i>(1917,Economic Technical Cookery Book,265</i>)

>  What are Luncheonettes? A refreshing Cup of Tea, Coffee, or Cocoa, and a Steak and Kidney Pie or Mutton Pie, or a Plate of Scones, Cakes, Buns, Pikelets, Biscuits, or an assortment of all. <i>(Nov 8, 1906,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  The following is the prize list [for the Central Otago Show].. One dozen pikelets (six entries) - Mrs. J. Leask 1, Miss A. Mee 2, Miss A. Gunr 3. <i>(Nov 17, 1909,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <i>PIKELETS</i> smooth batter ... girdle, and put the batter on in dessertspoonfuls. When bubbles form ... brown the other side. Serve hot. <i>(1911,Kirkcaldie & Stains Cookery Book,11</i>)

### piker
 
 <i>n.</i> a wild bull

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> The roaring tangle of pikers, steers and fierce old scrub bulls. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,24</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> [The wild cattle] were a mixed haul: two-year-olds, poddies and pikers. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a shirker, dodger

>  Don't be a Piker! A Piker is a man without appreciation, without gratitude, and without the ability to change old habits for better ones, No! let us live with the times, and be fitted out by the leading Tailor and Mercer, Main street, Otautau. <i>(Oct 8, 1912,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,5</i>)

>  Then they start operations and when it comes to betting they are by no means pikers. <i>(Mar 1, 1928,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>piker </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `I gotta get away.'`Ya bloody piker! A man oughta wooden ya out. I spose you're tearin off to see some sheila if the truth's known.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43</i>)

>  <b>MACKAY</b> Bill had never heard of Baron Munchausen but, as the most cheerful liar of this or any age, he made that titled gentleman look a mere piker. <i>(1961,Puborama,72</i>)

>  <i>piker ... n</i>. ... one who loses courage at the last moment. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `I'm browned off, boy,' he replied ... `Go on, you piker,' Joe said with a laugh. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,94</i>)

>  <i>piker</i>: person who lets you down <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>piker</i>: one who does not pay his way or slyly tries to get out of paying; e.g., `Come on mate, your shout or are you a piker.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> The words flew, and enriched Celia's [an English visitor's] vocabulary. `Duck shoving' was the local name for buck-passing between government departments. A `Queen Street farmer' was a city slicker who owned land or stock for tax-fiddle reasons. `Skite' and `drongo' were terms of abuse. A `cow-cockie' was a dairy farmer and inferior for some reason to a `runholder' concerned only with sheep. A `piker' was a faint-hearted person who gave up the fight too soon, ... <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,26</i>)

>  <i>piker n. NZ & Austral. sl</i>. one who avoids doing his fair share, a shirker. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,569</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 858]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> So to cope with the rituals of tramping ... a slang language has developed to suit. ... `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936 ...[Not so: HWO]), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather) ... <i>(Mar 1988,Hills, The</i>) [NOTES: [page proofs: Reed Publishing]]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `Aw, come on. Have a beer.' 'No thanks.' `Piker! Ya old man will.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,114</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> So to cope with the rituals of tramping, its pains and camaraderie, a slang language has developed to suit. Lots of quick short words to fit in the breathless pauses as you pant uphill. `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936; he travelled 40 km by rowing boat, horse and foot in 21 hours), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather), and many, many more. <i>(1988,Hills, The,8</i>)

### piki-piki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pikopiko](per-pop.md#pikopiko))

>  The kiwi is passable when one is hungry, though personally I do not like him, but being more nutritious than savoury, it is not to be despised, and is almost nice when boiled with piki-piki fern and rice. <i>(1992,Quotable New Zealanders,not numbered</i>)

### pikiarero
 
 <i>n.</i> the native clematis.

>  Thick leafy willows trail their long streamers in the stream, and further up give place to native vegetation, festooned in the too brief season of bush blossoms with the white starry flowers of the pikiarero, the New Zealand clematis. <i>(Jul 23, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Every one of them, even Paul Tuhaere, who was related by marriage to the deceased chief, had around their heads or hats massive wreaths of the creeping plant known as pohue, and in some places as pikiarero. <i>(Sep 23, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  6d Pikiarero (Clematis). <i>(Dec 3, 1959,NZ Philatelic Bulletin,XIX : 1</i>)

>  In most lowland forest areas of New Zealand spring is heralded by the flowering of the climber now called clematis paniculata but formerly known as clematis indivisa .. In his original drawing Mr Fuller showed the Maori name of the flower as "puawananga''. The Botany Division of the Department of Scientific and Industrial Research advised that it should be shown as "pikiarero". <i>(1964,Postage Stamps of NZ,IV/186</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Piki-arero - the ligament of the tongue, a climbing plant <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/379</i>)

>  Pikiarero,<i> Col[enso]. Clematis hexasepala. </i> <i>(1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,767</i>) [NOTES: [?beef tongue]]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  .. the bark and stems of the Pikiarero (<i>Clematis hexasepala</i>), and the root of the Tatarahake (<i>Coprosma acerosa.</i>) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/39</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  [Maoris used] as slightly alterative, a decoction of the bark and stems of the pikiarero (<i>Clematis hexasepala</i>), and the root of the tataraheke (<i>Coprosma acerosa</i>) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/1-58</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Colenso TrNZI 1/1-58 (On the Botany of the North Island) in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants 16]

>  Mr. Probert likewise exhibits a creeper, named pikiarero, which he has discovered yields a fine silky fibre when prepared. <i>(Dec 10, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Far different is this cool white trail of clematis. She is a shy Dryad, this Piki-arero, as the Maoris called her. <i>(Oct 26, 1899,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Tarata ... Pikiarero <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,502</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Pikiarero [Maori name] .. Clematis [Settlers' name] .. Clematis hexasepala .. Climber <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Clematis indivisa (The Entire-leaved Clematis) ... Maori names, <i>Pua-wananga, Pikiarero. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,164,1940 p. 173 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pikiarero,</i> a climbing plant (Clematis hexasepala); pua-wa[a]nanga is C. indivisa. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### Pikikiruna schist
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  The latter will draw comparisons with Pikikiruna Schist and Arthur Marble structures. <i>(Jul 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,57/20</i>)

 <i>n.</i> shirking

>  <i>(1959,Numbers,9/13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [pikopo](per-pop.md#pikopo))

 <i>n.</i> red mistletoe (See [red mistletoe](ra-red.md#red-mistletoe))
