### pikopiko
 
 <i>n.</i> young curved edible fern shoots, with a taste similar to asparagus. [also piko piko; piki piki]

>  People from Rotorua would cook pikopiko in a hot box or steam box. Or they might boil it in salty water and add fish or mussels to make a sort of soup. <i>(Jun 9, 2004,Capital Times,12</i>)

>  Mr Royal said piko piko, a pale-green shoot, 15-20 centimetres long, was traditionally boiled and only certain people went out to harvest it. There are more than 300 varieties of the herb, some of which are carcinogenic. <i>(Aug 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

>  Now and again his mother sends Morgan consignments of piko piko, or tender fernheads. <i>(Jun 1, 1999,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  .. Marlborough salmon is pan-roasted and served on olive and herb mash with "creamy crustacean" sauce and tempura pikopiko. <i>(Jul 26, 2006,Viva,12</i>)

>  Horopito, kawakawa and piko piko, pickled daily in Rotorua and the central North Island, are emerging as delicious additions to mainstream menus. <i>(Jul 27, 2005,NZ Herald,VIVA p3</i>)

>  Pitopito [<i>sic</i>] are fern fronds, which taste like lightly flavoured asparagus when cooked. .. "It's a good time to pick pikopiko. Matariki is a time for putting the past to rest and looking forward to the future." <i>(Jun 25, 2003,Capital Times,18</i>)

>  Among the kawakawa, koromiko, puha and horopito inhabiting wild menus is the peppery premature ponga frond pikopiko .. It turned up in a dish created by Ohio-born, Colorado-trained chef who was asked how he'd come across such an obscure indigenous ingredient when menus he'd been used to creating were more likely to feature T-bones, sassafras and sarsparilla. "Oh, we have 'em over there," he said, "cept they're called 'fern shoots.'" <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Pikopiko (<i>Polystichum richardii</i>) is a native fern easily found throughout the local bush. I call it New Zealand asparagus but it doesn't taste anything like vegetable asparagus (<i>Asparagus officinalis</i>). <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,41</i>)

>  Pikopiko - shield fern - tastes like asparagus, and is a special spring treat, because there are only about nine edible shoots per plant. <i>(May 2000,NZ Gardener,81</i>)

>  In between serving up heart-warming dishes, he has been plumbing his octogenarian father for information on the "real" hangi, and working on introducing foods such as pikopiko to the menu. <i>(Oct 12, 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,46</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> There were dishes of pikopiko on the tables with the mutton, the curling green shoots covered in fine brown hairs. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,157</i>) [NOTES: (Alice Tawhai)]

>  The doughnut shaped fillet of beef is criss-crossed with kumara chips, the mashed potato is garnished with blanched pikopiko fern fronds, delightfully curled at their tops. <i>(Nov 2003,AA Directions,No. 58/69</i>)

>  The Monteith's dish was wild duck (shot near Foxton, we were told) with Urenika potatoes, steamed pikopiko and a sweet and spicy butternut sauce. <i>(Aug 24, 2006,Wellingtonian,5</i>)

>  KAI TIME. Maori chef Charles Royal gathers herbs and plants from the bush including pikopiko heads, kawakawa shoots, horopito and piripiri to make dry rubs, relishes and pickles. <i>(2005,AA Directions,Spring 2005, p54</i>)

>  ON OUR PLATE. Wild foods on the world stage: *Pikopiko heads (fern frond) *Piripiri (New Zealand chili) *Kawakawa (bush basil) <i>(May 21, 2005,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

>  .. dried karengo is now commercially available (and is being used brilliantly by Canterbury baker Rachel Scott, whose karengo bread is to be a permanent fixture on Icon's menu), while fresh piko piko fern fronds, which taste a little like asparagus, are creating a sensation now that they are being gathered from the wild and promoted to the hospitality industry... <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  .. we can easily visualise the Ringatu captive Mere Maihi kneading her kete of pikopiko there in the pool, watched by Mair on the opposite bank. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  The "pikipiki" and "mamuku", two descriptions of edible fern, which were then much sought after, do not look very inviting. <i>(Jan 4, 1873,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  They are without potatoes or flour, and are said to be eating karaka and tawa berries, pikopiko (young shoots of ferns), etc. <i>(Aug 23, 1898,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> .. I have often been glad of even a small feed of Piki-piki fern (<i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i>) [when bushed]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> `It was usual, too,' added the old <i>pakeha-Maori,</i> `to cook some <i>pikopiko</i>, the young curly fronds of the <i>mauku</i> or ground-fern, with the [human] meat; it added to the flavour. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/437 The Maori .. was kneading a kit of <i>pikopiko</i>, the young curling fronds of the fern <i>asplenium</i> (or <i>Aspidium Richardi</i>), to get rid of the hairy scales. [The <i>pikopiko</i> when prepared is a vegetable much like asparagus.] [sic] <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/219</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Pikopiko, the young uncurled shoots of the <i>Asplenium bulbiferium</i> fern, were also sought after, as they are today .. <i>Ibid.</i> 36 FERN SHOOTS (PIKOPIKO) The <i>Polystichum richardii</i> shield-fern is found growing in the damper shady part of the bush. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,7</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Pikopiko (fern buds) Pikopiko, the young, curling buds of the hen and chicken fern (<i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i>), were collected in the spring. They taste similar to asparagus when steamed. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)

>  The usual diet was fern root .. with the pikopiko fern (the asparagus of the Maori) and the succulent shoot of the mamaku fern for green vegetables. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,20</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> So Kerewin's next meal featured a salad with fourteen different greens in it, all plucked within a one-mile radius of the Tower [West Coast]. Sorrel, wild turnip, lambsquarters, dandelions, pikopiko curls, puha, cress, young yarrow ... Pikopiko = fern, young fronds of which are edible [.] <i>(1983,Bone People, The,110</i>)

>  On Wednesday afternoon .. Mr Stan Phillips disappeared while picking piko piko with friends. <i>(Aug 13, 1988,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Young pikopiko fronds (<i>Polystichum richardii</i>) are esteemed as 'bush asparagus" by those in the know. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

### pikopo
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [He] said he was there alone amongst the heathen and pickapo tribe ... <i>(Jul 1, 1846,Journal,4/68</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for Roman Catholic (bishop).

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Conversation with some who had attended `Pikopo' (The Roman Catholic Bishop). [Bishop Pompallier arrived on 10 Jan 1838] <i>(Oct 6, 1839,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,446,1961 ed.</i>)

>  `Pikopo' is the name given to the Roman Bishop. <i>(1839,Journeys</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Colenso. M.S. Memo. Journeys amongst Natives 50]

>  <b>BRIGHT</b> M.Pompaliere; the natives call him <i>Pikypo</i>, for what reason I know not. <i>(1841,Handbook for Emigrants,126</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> `If Pikopo and his priest go in, we [Colenso et al.], for the sake of our position among the Natives, should go in also.' [fn] The common Maori name by which the Roman Catholic bishop and the priests were known. <i>(1890,Treaty of Waitangi,13</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a (Roman) Catholic

>  A few of the people here are missionary natives, some are pikopo, or catholics, but the majority are heathen. <i>(Jul 11, 1846,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  [Tapu] has been laid on by an old Pikopo Chief, who lives up in the interior, about 100 miles from this place. <i>(Apr 11, 1846,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  (Reprinted from the <i> Auckland Herald </i> ) "I am a Pikopo," (the Maori word for Catholic) Scott replied. <i>(Mar 24, 1886,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>HEBERLEY</b> [The Missionaries] told [the natives] we were all Pickopoes. <i>(1839,Autobiography,37,1874 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... encamping close by the sea by a beautiful amphitheatre of powa takawa trees ... Mr Williams addressed a party of Pickapos the best part of the afternoon <i>(Oct 17, 1842,Journal,4</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Protestant natives regard their Roman Catholic brethren as belonging to the devils: they are called pikipo, which M. Pompalier explained as being derived from <i>episcopus</i>, which seems to me the most probable derivation. The Protestant missionaries however, say that it means piki-po - always bowing [370] - (piki, bend - po, night). The Protestants, therefore, call their bishop pihopa, while the Roman Catholics have retained the title pikipo for theirs. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/369</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> We were Pikapo (the Maori corruption of episcopoi, or Roman Catholics) <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/168</i>)

>  The natives at Taupo are nearly all <i>pikipoi (episcopoi)</i> as they call themselves. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,127/621</i>) [NOTES: (R.Harrison)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Mungahaku [of Tongariro], the chief of this pah, is a <i>pikopo</i>, or Roman Catholic; several of his people have also embraced Popery, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/152-153 Those natives who are still heathen are generally styled `devils' by the Christianized people; and they themselves adhere to the [153] appellation. Hongi Hongi, ... in answer to my inquiry as to whether he was a `mihorari' or a `pikopo'(Catholic), confessed with evident delight that he was a `devil'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/121</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Mungahaku [of Tongariro], the chief of this pah, is a <i>pikopo</i>, or Roman Catholic; several of his people have also embraced Popery, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/152-153 Those natives who are still heathen are generally styled `devils' by the Christianized people; and they themselves adhere to the [153] appellation. Hongi Hongi, ... in answer to my inquiry as to whether he was a `mihorari' or a `pikopo'(Catholic), confessed with evident delight that he was a `devil'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/121</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> They took the name of Pikopo. This no doubt was maorified from the Latin word <i>episcopos</i>; but, unfortunately for them, it had the ominous meaning in the Maori tongue, - <i>creeping in the dark.</i> <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,295</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> Even their name [sc Roman Catholics] may have hindered their progress. They were called Pikopo; the Maori attempt to reproduce the word <i>episcopos</i>. Unhappily for them, the word also means `creeping in the dark' : an idea which would not be helpful. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,99</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> In his discussions with the Maoris, Bishop Pompallier had stresed the point that he held the advantage over the Protestant missionaries in that he was a member of the Episcopacy. The best attempt on the part of the natives to render into their own language the word Episcopos, in its varied forms, was <i>pikopo</i>, hence the Bishop and his converts became known as <i>Pikopo</i>. <i>(1936,Treaty of Waitangi,118fn</i>)

### pilchard
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Sardinops neopilchardus </i>mohimohi, Picton herring

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] pilchard 515-40 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  There are plenty of mackerel here, but no nets to catch them, and there are pilchards; please to bring one good pilchard net. <i>(Feb 10, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,1968 ed.</i>)

>  There are large shoals of the pilchard at the head of the Sound. They are from 6 to 10 inches long. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/486</i>) [NOTES: (Sutherland)]

>  ...<i> large numbers of aua or kataha</i> (Agonostoma forsteri); <i>about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (Clupea sagax),</i> two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>), and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i>Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>) [NOTES: Mair Fish in Piako River]

>  There appear to be three species of fish belonging to the family Clupeidae in New Zealand waters, viz., <i>Engraulis antipodum</i>, or anchovy; <i>Sardinia neopilchardus</i>, or pilchard, and <i>Harengula antipoda</i>, or sprat. <i>Ibid.</i> 316 The sprat is the smaller of the two, but in the numerous notices of these fish, the name `sprat' probably includes in many cases immature pilchards. <i>Ibid.</i> 319 In the case of fish-stomachs examined at the station, sprats have been found in red cod, blue cod, southern king-fish, barracouta, and spined dog-fish; and pilchards in barracouta. <i>(1926,TrNZI,56/314</i>) [NOTES: (Young and Thomson)]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>ANCHOVY </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Engraulis australis</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Anchovy. (c) Other common names: the following names of related Clupeids and Engraulid fish are sometimes applied to anchovy - Herring, Sprat, Sardine, Pilchard, although the true Herring of European waters (<i>Clupea harengus</i>), does not occur in New Zealand. (d) Principal localities where taken: Hauraki Gulf, Gisborne, Tasman Bay, Marlborough Sounds. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 <i>PILCHARD </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Sardinops neopilchardus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Pilchard; Sardine. (c) Other common names: Sardine; Sprat; Anchovy; Herring. (d) Principal localities where taken: Auckland, Gisborne, Tasman Bay, Marlborough Sounds. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,10</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> ...,with the following results: 581 eels (from 1ft. to 4ft. in length); 8 dozen flounders, of various sizes, large numbers of aua, or kataka (mullet); about 70lb. or 70lb. weight of pilchard, or mohimohi; a few snapper and kahawai; hundreds of red cod and rarii; and a number of kokopu or rawaru. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,64</i>)

>  <i>Clupea Neopilchardus</i> ... Pilchard. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/158</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  SARDINA NEOPILCHARDUS (Steindachner). <i>Pilchard; Mohimohi</i>. Sold in a smoked condition in Wellington as `Picton herring'. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (unknown, Bay of Islands). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  During the month of July, 1924, Wellington harbour was visited by an enormous shoal of pilchards, or sardines (<i>Sardinia neopilchardus</i>). ... This fish is identical with the well-known `Picton herring', which is sold in a smoked condition every winter in Wellington. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/191</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Sardinia neopicharda</i> Steindachner. PILCHARD; MOHIMOHI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,12,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Sardinia neopicharda</i> Steindacher. Pilchard. Mohimohi. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/402</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> PILCHARD (MOHIMOHI) Sardinops neopilchardus (Steindacher) The Pilchard is known from the northern end of the South Island as the Picton Herring, and off Picton large shoals of these fish have from time to time been recorded. It has no relation to the so-called New Zealand Herring or Yellow-eyed Mullet (Agonostomus forsteri). <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,104</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> With regard to its popular names, [the New Zealand sprat] has frequently been referred to as the Pilchard, New Zealand Herring, and by other local names. <i>Ibid.</i> 29 THE PILCHARD <i>Sardinia neopichardus</i> (<i>Steindachner</i>) ... Maori name: MOHIMOHI. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Pilchard Sardinops neopilchardus Sardine; Picton Herring (Syn. <i>Clupea lata</i> Richardson) <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,28</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pilchard Sardinops neopilchardus</i> ... Sometimes called the sardine. Also occurs around southern Australia. ... In the 1880s a fishery in the Marlborough Sounds produced salted and smoked `Picton Herring', and small fisheries have developed briefly elsewhere. ... Pilchard are caught for groper bait (`bloater'), and have been used also for tuna. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,40</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PILCHARD Sardinops neopilchardus </i>... When schooling these fish swim very close together ... These schools are often referred to as baitfish by fishermen. ... Around New Zealand pilchard schools are most abundant Zealand Sprat (<i>Clupea antipodum</i>) are known from Canterbury, ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,104,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Sardinia neopicharda</i> : Sardine, pilchard, or `Picton herring.' <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> PILCHARD OR MOHIMOHI <i>Sardinia neopicharda</i> (Steindachner). ... Fifty years ago the pilchard was much more common around our southern coasts than it now is. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Pilchard <i>Sardinia neopichardus</i> (Steindachner) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,398</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 398]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Mohi, ... 2. Sardinia neopilcharda, pilchard. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,205</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Schools of pilchard (<i>Sardinops</i> ... - related to the Norwegian sardine) enter harbours in the winter ... . <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,207</i>)

>  [species name] pilchard .. [scientific name] <i>Sardinops neopilchardus</i> .. [other name(s)] sardine, bloater <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sardine ?=NZ sprat

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 100. CLUPEA SAGAX. Jenyns. Pilchard or Sardine. <i>(1872,Fishes,63</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> PILCHARD OR SARDINE (<i>Clupea sagax</i>). ... [73] As the New Zealand pilchard is regarded as only `a climatal variety' of the English pilchard, some details about the pilchard industry may not be out of place. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,71</i>)

>  29. <i>Milford Sound</i> - Horse-mackerel, rock-cod, mullet, red-cod <i>Macrurus australis</i>, pilchard. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/204</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  35. Pilchard, or Sardine (<i>Clupea sagax</i>). 36. Sprat (<i>Clupea sprattus</i>). The recorders were mostly unable to distinguish between these species. They are only noted from Moeraki, Catlin's River, Waipapapa Point, and Milford Sound. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/213</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Pilchard or sardine .. .. mohimohi .. .. 2. varieties and species: <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Southern Pilchard (<i>Sardinia neopilchardu</i>,...) and the New <i>Pinus radiata</i> or any other commercial timber. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,195</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 195]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Unusually large catches of New Zealand pilchards have been made in Pelorus Sound in the last six weeks. <i>(Dec 30, 1944,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> ...the New Zealand Pilchard, a member of the most primitive order (isospondyli) of bony fishes. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,194</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' In Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 194]

>  [caption] N.Z. PILCHARD <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/208</i>)

### pilcher
 
 <i>n.</i> a covering (now including plastic) for baby's nappy

>  Pilchers in waterproof and wool, Sale price 2s 11d to 4s 11d. <i>(Jan 18, 1921,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  INFANT'S OVERALL PILCHERS; all Wool. <i>(Jul 12, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>pilchers</i>: BABIES [sic] PLASTIC PANTS <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,77</i>)

>  .. Children's and Infant's Goods, consisting of Cashmere Cloaks and Hoods .. Pinafores, Aprons, Pilchers ... <i>(Jan 3, 1860,Wellington Independent,4</i>)

>  Gowns, Robes, Slips, Flanneels [sic], Shawls, Swaiths, Pilchers, Bootees for the baby at Herbert's. <i>(Jan 31, 1908,Taranaki Herald,7</i>)

>  Infants' Pilchers - In stockingette . . . <i>(May 1, 1915,Evening Post,3</i>)

### pile
 
 <i>n.</i> house or building pile (Australia =stump) (See [cf. house block](hom-hyp.md#house-block))

>  .. I was there when a piece of wood, about two foot long, such as they use for foundation piles or blocks [sic]; I said to the girl, where did you get the piece of wood from; she made no reply; I said to the girl, where did you get the piece of wood from; she made no reply; I said that piece of wood belongs to Mr. R. Davies, and it cost the man 6d. or 1s., and it is a shame that it should be burnt when he wants it for his building ... <i>(Aug 10, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was preceded by two dray-loads of small rough-hewn stone piles, which are first let into the ground six or eight feet apart: the foundation joists rest on these, so as just to keep the flooring from and rails, &c. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,49,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Put 2 piles under the cottage, ... transplanted 20 bluegums. <i>(Oct 14, 1887,Chudleigh Diary,358,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Bill Holland carting piles and Pollard's goods on return trips. <i>(Dec 21, 1850,Torlesse Papers,183,1958 ed.</i>)

>  Put 2 piles under the cottage, ... transplanted 20 bluegums. <i>(Oct 14, 1887,Chudleigh Diary,358,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARRICK</b> Timber, fencing, building-piles, culverts, bridges, ... could all be had within the place. <i>(1892,New Zealand's Lone Lands,24</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[eptospermum] ericioides, A.Rich.</> ... Abundant from North Cape to the Bluff, ... <i>Kanuka; Maru</i>. ... Easily distinguished from [manuka] by its greater size, ... wood durable; much used for piles, house-blocks, posts <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,161</i>)

>  The heart of it was still decaying, the borer spreading, the piles left to rot, the tiles on the roof slipping groggily. <i>(1987,MsCellany: Women Writers' Prose & Poetry,6</i>) [NOTES: (Brooke-White)]

>  The pile abutment at the North end of the Ourawera bridge on the Riverton-Orepuki Main Highway has sunk some six inches on account of decay of the piles and I have arranged for new stud piles to be put in at once and hope that the work can be carried out without disturbing road traffic. <i>(Sep 23, 1930,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Pile driving on the reclamation works would have been stopped for want of timber, but for the timely arrival of the Hannah Bloomfield with a fresh supply. <i>(Aug 29, 1879,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The first pile of the Waitara wharf will be driven at four o'clock on Monday next. <i>(Jun 21, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> fortune [dateline missing ... the one thought and aim is to <i>make money</i>, and it is a hard, hard struggle now-a-days to get along in New Zealand, or make there much desired `little piles'.]

>  No heavy `piles' are likely to be made on [Waipori] <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,19</i>)

>  When the water sinks ... they are the spots to make their `piles' at. <i>(1858,Handbook to Nelson,15</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... men make for weeks merely good wages, then their luck turns, and they make their `pile'. <i>(Feb 5, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,111,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... [we] were crowing over our luck at being among the favoured few destined to make their `piles' in Maori Gully. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,99</i>)

>  I'll work on till I've made my pile; ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,2/x/73,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> ... off to the first new rush, ... with hopes of making a pile ... <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,21</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Some time after Arthur had made his pile and was well out of the rags he arrived in, an old Victorian miner washed up 200 ozs. in one day, left behind by Arthur and others. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,69</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  They will repay the steady, persevering miner with wages and from that to `a pile' occasionally. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,27</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> I afterwards heard that a party of men drove a tunnel into the terrace ..., and got a pile out of it. <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,19</i>)

### pile claim
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. pile](per-pop.md#pile))

>  I repeat again Waipori has no "pile claims," and the miner, who is searching after that New Zealand "phantom," a defined lead, would be surely disappointed here. <i>(Jun 28, 1862,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  It was with the greatest reluctance that the Waste Lands Board reserved the section from sale, that has now been rushed, and on which there is at least one "pile" claim, and possibly others. <i>(Apr 7, 1877,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  This locality has a "rare history for pile claims," and confidence in this dredge is firm. <i>(Apr 27, 1901,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The ground is rich beyond expectations and promises to be "a pile claim." <i>(Jun 5, 1893,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  For the long period of forty years, with odd intervals he dug for gold in that gully, and though he worked like a Trojan fickle fortune near [sic] gave him a pile claim, but only hope, beautiful boundless hope - the digger's perpetual friend. <i>(Jul 4, 1910,Grey River Argus,7</i>)

>  In the one or two holes that have been sunk gold has been obtained. ... Waipori has no `pile claims' ... <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,21</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> We found The miners doing fairly, but no `pile' claims <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,155</i>)

### pile up
 
 <i>v.</i> to be wrecked

>  <b>EADDY</b> She had gone ashore on what was known as the `graveyard', on the northern side of the entrance to the Kaipara river, and here she had piled up. <i>(1933,Hull Down,157</i>)

### piler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> It was a matter of nice judgement. The digger recognized four classes of payable ground: `tucker ground' kept him in food with nothing to spare; a `wages claim' gave him a pound or two above living expenses; a `riser' was a rich claim; and a `piler' or `homeward bounder' the richest of all. Probably most of the `pilers' were beach-claims, where the leads were shallow and rich, the outlay small and provisions cheap because of easy access. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,244,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> <i>Pilers</i>: See CLAIMS below.(?) ... <i>CLAIMS </i>Worthless claims or ground were called schicers or duffers. Those yielding up to [pounds]3 per week per man were known as `tucker' claims; up to [pounds]8 per week were known as `wages' claims; above [pounds]8 were known as `pilers'; the richest of all were known as `Home-ward-bounders.' <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

### Pilgrim
 
 <i>n.</i> ?agriculturalist v. pastoralist

>  (`Pilgrims and Prophets') Gaily the pilgrim harnessed his plough, / When he had built up a roof o'er his head; / Singing, `From Albion hither I come;' / `Land of mine! land of mine! grow me some bread.' Proudly the prophet flourished his crook, / When he had landed his sheep from the west; / Singing, `From Philipland hither I come;' / `Silly men! silly men! wool pays the best.' <i>(Aug 9, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  Gaily the pilgrim harnessed the plough,/ ... / Singing, `From Albion hither I come;/ Land of mine! land of mine! Grow me some bread.'/ .../ Proudly the prophet flourished his crook,/ When he had landed his sheep from the West;/ Singing, `From Philipland hither I came;/ Silly men! Silly men! Wool pays the best'./ [13] Shortly the pilgrim and prophet agreed/ The plough and the crook couldn't live far apart;/ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,12</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Aug in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 12-13]



 
 <i>n.</i> usually pl. the first Canterbury Association immigrants into Canterbury as a Church of England settlement

>  This gale has completely starled the `big-hearted Pilgrims', and a public meeting is to be held today to put down these periodical disasters. <i>(Jul 4, 1851,Australian & NZ Gazette,30/483</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from Chch)]

>  Two pilgrims came here on horse. <i>(Aug 11, 1851,Deans' Letters,293,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... An old Australian squatter, who visited the Canterbury settlement in 1851, divided the inhabitants into pilgrims, shagruns, and prophets; the first were the original colonists, the second were people from other parts of New Zealand, and the last were settlers from Australia. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/186</i>)

>  ... it is hard to believe that Canterbury ever had existed before the pilgrims came there in eighteen hundred and fifty. <i>(Jun 1960,Forest Creek MS,42</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> The town of Lyttelton seemed much more imposing ...; fifteen years ago a few sheds received the `Pilgrims,' as the first comers are always called. ... I am told that these four ships, sent out with the pilgrims shingle of river beds]. <i>Ibid.</i> 124 I find the greatest delight in listening to the stories of the Pilgrims ... [i.e.] people who came here in the very early days of the colony - only sixteen years ago after all. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,20,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PRATT</b> In the `Dream of a Shagroon' which bore the date Ko Motinau, April 1851, and which first appeared in the `Wellington Spectator' of May 7th, the term Pilgrim was first applied to the settlers; it was also predicted in it that the `Pilgrims', would be `smashed' and the `Shagroons' left in undisputed possession of the country for their flocks and herds. <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,234</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> The pilgrim was not only quite another man from those who have followed him: he was also a colonist of a stamp distinct in many respects from any other class of pioneer in Australasia. <i>(1883,Canterbury Rhymes,iv</i>)

>  In the `Dream of the Shagroon', which bore the date Ko Motinau, April 1851, and which first appeared in the Wellington Spectator of May 7th, the term `Pilgrim' was first applied to settlers; it was predicted in it that the `Pilgrims' would be `smashed' and the `Shagroons' left in undisputed possession of the country for their flocks and herds. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,83</i>) [NOTES: (Pratt)]

>  Pilgrim n. an old name for an early settler in parts of New Zealand. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> The pilgrims therefore made shift to carry on without a bishop; but they could not exist long without farming. <i>(1938,High Country,33</i>)

>  Pilgrim, n. One of the Canterbury Association's settlers. P., prophet and pre-Adamite (q.v.) are the only three words which can be proved pure Canterbury productions. ([1898 Morris; C.RH., p. 5) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>REED</b> To the Hays and Sinclairs and other `pre-Adamites' - as those few who had arrived before the `Pilgrims' came jocularly to be called - these ships represented shops, schools, churches, ... <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,55</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The shake [in Christchurch, 1888] ... was described as the most severe since the arrival of the pilgrims. <i>(1972,Letter from James,143</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Although the Pilgrims could cross the hills to the Plain over the Bridle Path - and could carry their lighter belongings; all heavy luggage had to go to Christchurch by sea and then up the Avon River. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early Canterbury runholder from Britain [farming before the arrival of the ''prophet'' from Australia.]

>  Shortly the pilgrim and prophet agreed The plough and the crook couldn't live far apart. <i>(1851,BLANK ENTRY,5</i>) [NOTES: in REEVES (1883) Canterbury Rhymes]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> An old Australian squatter, who visited the Canterbury settlement in 1851, divided the inhabitants into pilgrims, shagruns and prophets; the first were the original colonists, the second were people from other parts of New Zealand and the last were settlers from Australia. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,II 186</i>)

>  ibid. 125 The Canterbury pasturage system was quickly adapted to the requirements of sheep-farming on a large scale and 'pilgrims' and prophets were soon merged into one class as Canterbury runholders. <i>(1883</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Most of the Pilgrims were small farmers. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,142</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Weld shows out the true blood of an old settler. Your pilgrim muffs were too slow even to have stirred themselves in the matter <i>(1853,Canterbury Papers,2/91</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Canterbury Papers Letters to Godley 2/91 (Hamilton)]

>  <b>HART</b> ... it was hardly a matter for surprise that amongst the ranks of the Pilgrim Settlers who - as the Puritan founders of new England did in the Mayflower - set sail for Canterbury in 1850, we should have many scions of the aristocracy ... <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,6</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> ... narrated this Pilgrim mother. <i>(1912,Brown Bread,130</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> But how, he thought, as the pilgrim town loomed on the edges of the plain, do I explain it to a bullock driver? <i>(1970,Mackenzie,16</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> Even at this early period [c 1850s] ... there were some four-wheeled open chaises, ... [193] ... The pilgrim mothers and daughters ... who occupied the humbler contraptions were none the less happy ... <i>(1932,Early Days of Canterbury,192</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> These colonists [of the Canterbury Association], denominated Canterbury pilgrims, were likened to the pilgrim fathers; but the name was their only point of resemblance, for the pilgrim fathers migrated to enjoy religious freedom, the Canterbury pilgrims to found a settlement where they alone should have religious freedom; ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/184</i>)

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> We found ourselves .... among the people .... who are still called the Canterbury Pilgrims <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),78</i>)

>  <b>JOLLIE</b> The Canterbury Settlers, or pilgrims, as they were called. <i>(1878,Reminiscences,26,1850 ed.</i>)

>  [1851 Apr 19 Lyttelton Times `Government might have founded a colony composed of stragglers from other settlements, of whale-fishers and stock-drivers, but would they have persuaded a body of English gentlemen to transfer their homes to these shores?' The following was written in reply:] `Dream of Shagroon' [title] I saw men of another aspect arrive at the pilgrim-land. ... The Pilgrims looked with contemptuous eye on this people of strange aspect and impure blood, and did despise them. ... I awoke - and lo! the land which was to have been sacred to the orthodox foot only of a `body of English gentlemen', was in possession of the `stragglers, the whalers, and the stock-drivers from other settlements'. Alas! for the goodly land, - the land of the Canterbury Pilgrims. <i>(May 7, 1851,NZ Spectator</i>) [NOTES: 1851 May 7 NZ Spectator and Cook's Strait Guardian (Wellington) in 1947 Reed Story of Canterbury 111]

>  `A slice of England cut from top to bottom was despatched to the Antipodes last September. ... a deliberate, long-considered, solemn and devoted pilgrimage to a temple erected by nature for the good of all comers. `At the head of the pilgrims stood an actual bishop, behind him were working clergy, working schoolmasters, working landlords, working labourers, workers every one. <i>(Jul 5, 1851,Times (London)</i>) [NOTES: 1851 July 5 The Times (London) in 1950 Carrington Godley 117]

>  I have been gathering all the information I can on the subject from old settlers, old Australians, and Canterbury Pilgrims, who are <i>novis [sic] homines</i> in the pastoral world. <i>(May 1855,Journal of Henry Sewell,2/147</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... it is worthy of notice, that the colonists, soon after the formation of all the settlements, acquired distinguishing epithets; thus there was an Auckland cove, a Wellington swell, a Nelson snob, a Taranaki exquisite, an Otago cockney, and a Canterbury pilgrim. These epithets, already forgotten, are too characteristic to be buried in oblivion, as each originated in some peculiarity of the leading colonists. The Otago settlers derived theirs from their ignorance of the country in which they lived; the Auckland people from their extensive Sydney connexions; and the origin of the other names are sufficiently obvious without explanation. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/188</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> In the month of September [1850] the band of Canterbury Pilgrims ... set sail for Canterbury from Gravesend in four vessels, now known as the historical four ships, viz., <i>Sir George Seymour, Charlotte Jane, Randolph</i>, and the <i>Cressy. </i> <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,7</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> In the early days of New Zealand emigration, it was a not unusual custom to speed a departing emigrant vessel by means of some function - ... Probably the last of the `send-offs' was in 1850, when the `Canterbury Pilgrims' sailed. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,94</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Walton Kite's father had arived from England on the heels of the Canterbury Pilgrims, who had laid out that lovely cityof Christchurch, on the edge of the Canterbury Plains. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,120</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> It appears to have been the Australian squatters [let in to Canterbury by Godley to provide capital and flocks for unsold land] who jocularly termed the pioneer English settlers the `Canterbury Pilgrims', ... The Australian squatters were dubbed `Shagroons', a term the origin of which appears to be somewhat elusive. [Quotes Webster and Funk and Wagnall: both definitions erroneous] <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,109</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> A pioneer herself, the daughter of one Canterbury pilgrim, ... and the sister of another, Sir Arthur Dobson, she had seen the development of the Province, ... <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,xi</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> At the same time frequent letters kept him appraised of what was happening to the Canterbury Pilgrims of the <i>Southern</i> seas. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,19</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> We learnt that those who can claim to belong to a family who came out to New Zealand in one of the first four ships to arrive in Lyttelton ... in 1850 belong to an <i>&eacut.lite</i> society known as the Canterbury Pilgrims. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,56</i>)

>  <i>canterbury pilgrim n.</i> first English settlers of Canterbury province, South Island, N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 104; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 70]]

>  The Canterbury "Pilgrims" had arrived on the scene and taken possession of their "promised land." <i>(Sep 25, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  One of the six remaining Canterbury pilgrims who arrived in the First Four Ships in 1850 to found the province of Canterbury.. celebrated his ninetieth birthday on Thursday. <i>(Apr 27, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  Another Canterbury pilgrim, Mrs. Thomas Abrahams, has passed away. She arrived in Lyttleton in 1850, in the first of the pioneer ships, the Charlotte Jane. <i>(Aug 22, 1899,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Canterbury Pilgrims, The first settlers in Canterbury, New Zealand, were so called in allusion to the pilgrims to the shrine of St. Thomas Becket. Chaucer's `Canterbury Tales' were told by such pilgrims. The name was given probably by Mr William Lyon, who in 1851 wrote the `Dream.' See quotation, 1877. <i>(1898,Austral English,353</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> `The `Pilgrims,' as the first comers are always called. I like the name; it is so pretty and suggestive.' <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>PRATT</b> In the `Dream of a Shagroon', which bore the date Ko Matinau, April 1851, and which first appearted in the `Wellington Spectator' of May 7, the term `Pilgrim' was first applied to the settlers; it was also predicted in it that the `Pilgrims' would be `smashed', and the Shagroons left in undisputed possession of the country for their flocks and herds.' <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,234</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,24</i>) [NOTES: [Baker quots 1851 NZ Spectator May 7 check]]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [1957 July p.c. R.Mason heard Christchurch: ref to Canterbury Pilgrims These plants are the pilgrims, the pioneers [of plants invading the touching the earth.]

### Pilgrim Father
 
 <i>n.</i> [Canterbury history]

>  By the end of the previous year ('51) the Pilgrim Fathers.. had begun stringing up the steep bridle track in Indian file. <i>(Sep 25, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  I admire Mr. Roberts as a pioneer and pilgrim father, who has.. seen Oamaru rise from the tussock into a town the admiration of all who behold it. <i>(Mar 22, 1897,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  <b>REECE</b> [Hdg] The "Pilgrim Fathers" of Canterbury. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,15</i>)

### pill
 
 <i>n.</i> a testicle (cf. pill = ball)

>  All day long, on your knees, or up to your pills in freezing water, sluicing away for gold that was cut right out of it, a hundred years back. <i>(1970,End of the Golden Weather, The,18</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]



 
 <i>n.</i> a weak or feeble person

>  pill: weak or feeble person <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rugby ball

>  Matey, mindless chit-chat about the oval leather "pill".. is one hell of a way to start an action-packed weekend of rugby. <i>(Sep 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  You didn't see that much of me Ponies mate Brookie, but that was because every time a ruck opened up he was the joker on the bottom, closer to the pill than I was to a DB tube after the game. <i>(Jun 29, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> pill, the rugby ball. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,84</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  It was suggested that Hall's mind became deranged because it has been suggested that Hall was `pilled' in two clubs in Wellington. Whether he was `cold-shouldered' in one toney club `Truth' has not endeavored [sic] to ascertain. (but he was asked to leave one Club) <i>(Jun 17, 1911,NZ Truth,312/7/1</i>)

### pill-box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `Murder on the old sergeant-major.' `Ten frog half-way, cobber. Right, she's set.' `Come on, me lucky lads, you come 'ere in a pill-box and go away in a Rolls-Royce.' `The last spin, digs, so plank it down thick and heavy now; you pick 'em and I'll pay 'em.' <i>(Aug 10, 1921,Quick March,27</i>)

### pill-shopper
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who buys ephedrine substances from pharmacies to use in illicit drug manufacture

>  Thirty people known as pill-shoppers were arrested during the first phase... <i>(Oct 9, 2003,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  Operation Choker had run in two phases, the first aimed at "pill shoppers" - buyers of pseudephedrine-based medicines, that could be used to produce methamphetamine. <i>(Oct 10, 2003,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### pillar
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  <b>HAWES</b> To get the coal out, you divvied the seam up into 'pillars' a square chain wide. Then you drove alleyways called 'bords' to the very back of the pillar and you worked back towards the main shaft, taking 'lifts' off the pillars as you went... As you worked at the pillar - picking, shotfiring, shovelling an' that - it got thinner an' thinner until in the end it looked like a big black tornado, or a tree holding up the sky. That's why the last strand of the pillar is called a 'mahuta'. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,85</i>)

>  When the seam is worked out in this way the question of taking out the remaining blocks of coal, or pillars arises. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,4/612</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> I made a pretty good cheque off the pillars this pay <i>(1927,Old Savage,283</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> ... drives were broken off at right angles to [the dip heading] at distances of two chains apart ; thus forming pillars on either side of the dip, two chains square. These drives were called main levels. The bords were driven about two chains off the dip, and the `stenton' would be driven from the lower level to the one above. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,169</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Pillar</i>: That portion of a lode left standing as a support to keep the ore in place. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Pillars</i>: The vertical blocks of quartz left in position between two stopes or round the shafts to support the country. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),329</i>)

### pillar-coal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The system of work [in the Grey coalfield] is mainly by bord and pillar, a small portion only of this coal having been worked by the longwall method. In the bord-and-pillar system the bords are 18ft. wide, and pillars 14 yards thick. The pillars are left intact until the boundary of a section is reached, when they are worked back. The roof has in [418] main proved good, enabling a large percentage of the pillar-coal to be won. <i>(1906,Mining Handbook,417</i>)

### pillaring
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  .. for the miner ... what is termed pillaring is really the most dangerous work. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,4/612</i>)

### pillhead
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a girl (or sometimes youth) who rides pillion with a milkbar cowboy q.v. [also heard as pillion pickup (1953)]

Came off once too. ...' <i>(1956,Street Society in Christchurch,15</i>) [NOTES: [Christchurch 1956 to tall gum-chewing youth]]

### pillwort
 
 <i>n.</i> Pilularia novae-zealandiae

>  Pillwort (Pilularia novae-zealandiae) and quillworts (species of Isoetes) are found in lowland lakes and alpine tarns, but their undistinguished grass-like leaves mean that they are easily overlooked. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>PILULARIA NOVAE-ZELANDIAE</i> ... The New Zealand pillwort grows on margins of lakes... in the Bay of Plenty, and in Tasman Bay and the Marlborough Sounds. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,220</i>)

### pilly
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a pilchard [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Toss over a pillie, a jig or a soft bait and drift. More often than not you'll catch a fish in such circumstances. <i>(2008,Mana,84/67</i>)

### pilot
 
 <i>n.</i> a whale

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales.. hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

### pilot horse
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. flood horse](fin-fly.md#flood-horse))

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> When the rivers are at all high the `pilot' horse is indispensable - a clever, steady animal who spends his live in crossing and recrossing the river. It is interesting to watch the `fordman' trying for a possible crossing on his horse .... and the horse's readiness to `turn' a boulder too big to step over. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,188</i>)

### pilotfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> He was accompanied by two beautifully striped pilot fish - the never-failing attendants of the shark. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,1/10</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 26. NAUCRATES DUCTOR. L. Pilot Fish <i>(1872,Fishes,18</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CARANGIDAE. This family contains ... the yellowtail or kingfish ... and the curious, striped pilot fish ... , which swims in small groups just ahead of sharks. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PILOTFISH A remarkable smart looking fish the Pilotfish inhabits our warmer, northern waters where it is found usually on, or near, the surface. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,180</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] oceans, reaching northern New Zealand in summer. <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  The mango or <i>shark</i> (Squalus);<i> pilot-fish</i> (Scomber ductor); <i>flying-fish</i> (Exocoetus volitans);<i> hammer-headed shark</i> (Squalus zygoena); frequent the coast, expecially the River Thames, in vast shoals, and are preserved by the natives as winter food. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/323</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Naucrates ductor</i> L. Pilot-fish <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,301</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Naucrates indicus</i> (Linnaeus). PILOT-FISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,34,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Naucrates angeli</i> Whitley <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406 Pilot Fish]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PILOTFISH ... <i>Other names: Naucrates ductor</i>. Only the name pilotfish seems to be in common usage. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,235</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PILOTFISH [Naucrates ductor] ... Only the name pilotfish is in general use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,44</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Pilotfish Naucrates ductor A world-wide species of warm procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,91</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a plant

[40] Its note in the early morning is like the Maori syllables <i>ngi-i-ru, ngiru-ngiru</i>, from which it derives its name, the first syllable being rather prolonged. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/42 MYIOMOIRA MACROCEPHALA. (SOUTH-ISLAND TOMTIT.) <i>Native names.</i> Miromiro, Komiromiro, Pimiromiro, Ngirungiru, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori. ... Its note in the early morning is like the Maori syllables <i>ngi-i-ru, ngiru-ngiru</i>, from which it derives its name, the first syllable being rather prolonged. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/39</i>)

### pimise
 
 <i>n.</i> ?pinus

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> Some of the timber trees are of large growth, particularly three sorts; the first is of the pine tribe and is known by the name of Pimise (Pinus??) in three varieties on the north. The second is something like the Lucombe Oack, and the third is called the Red Gum or Eucalipticus I have seen it in England. ... The three varieties of pine are very tall and handsome, and one in appearance like the Lucombe oak grows tall but not so straight, the Eucalipticy grows to a great thickeness in the stem and frequently not less than fifteen feet in girt but not more than 15 or 20 feet in height before it spreads its large branches in every direction, ... <i>(1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 356]

### pimp
 
 <i>n.</i> (of children etc) someone who tells tales on or about; (of informers) someone who informs (police or authorities)

>  T'other day in a Lambton Quay pub, he, with some friends, was drinking, and the pimp thought he saw the barman `poleing', and reported him to the boss, who subsequently sacked the bar-tender, who informs `Truth' that he did not thieve, and was asked to stop on at his job, but very rightly refused. <i>(Sep 12, 1908,NZ Truth,169/1/3</i>) [NOTES: ?vbl n]]

>  T'other day in a Lambton Quay pub, he, with some friends, was drinking, and the pimp thought he saw the barman `poleing', and reported him to the boss, who subsequently sacked the bar-tender, who informs `Truth' that he did not thieve, and was asked to stop on at his job, but very rightly refused. <i>(Sep 12, 1908,NZ Truth,169/1/3</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pimp.</i> 3. One who tells tales on others: New Zealand coll.: C.20. Ex sense 1. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,630</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1961,Maori Girl,229</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pimp</i> to sneak or tell-tale; eg `There was no doubt how they had got caught; everybody knew Cyril was the class pimp.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,84</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> An informer, to inform, to the police: Australian: since ca. 1910. Baker 1942. In Australia, a white-slave pimp is heartily despised and contemned. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,513,1961 ed.</i>)

### pimp on
 
 <i>v.</i> (of children etc.) to tell tales on; (of informers) to inform (police or authorities) on [ca1930 (both tr. and intr.) e.g. He's always pimping to the teacher. He pimped on Joe to the Income Tax people. I was going to keep quiet but someone pimped.] (See [cf. pip](per-pop.md#pip))

It is perennnial, has creeping stems lying flat on the ground and rooting every inch or two, and running to a length of 2 ft. ...  <i>n.</i>

>  He gave instructions to Alexander Davey, head labourer, to take 4 [and-a-half] inches out of the bottom of the wall, and to leave three or four bricks, or `pins' to support the wall, which was the usual method of throwing down a structure of this kind. <i>(Jul 22, 1922,NZ Truth,317/6/3</i>)

### pin bullock
 
 <i>n.</i>

### pin dip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Pin dips were also another popular amusement. `A pin or a bull or a button, To see a rary rary show show show!' (1875; Tahataki). A piece of glass backed by cardboard was covered with a piece of paper that could be flicked up to show the `rary rary show' or had a slit in it through which one could peep. One saw through the glass a variety of colors, pieces of colored paper, flower petals, pictures, and later pieces off cigarette boxes. Sometimes these had been arranged in some pattern, at other times they were kaleidoscopic only, and the perceiver had himself to add the configurations of intelligibility. The price for the view was always a pin `which we did not want anyhow but always accepted,' a relic from an age when such things were rare. Pin dips were known also as `poppy shows' (perhaps it was the similar flash of color that gave the same name - `the poppy show' - to the exciting experience of seeing a girl's dress blown upward by the wind; pins were not charged for flowers of this sort). In fact, the pin dip or the prick book may have developed from the poppy show. The poppy show was common before 1900 and the prick book after that date, a fact connected with the ever-increasing stock of picture cards and magazine pictures available to children. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,223</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> remember this [nf P8]

 <i>n.</i> injecting drugs?

### pinch
 
 <i>n.</i> a difficult period

>  After the spring flush of feed on medium-light land pastures there is nearly always a `pinch' period before rape and other fattening feed is ready. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/323A</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of a tree being sawn down) to close tightly on (the saw) (See [snapperback](sho-spa.md#snapperback))



 
 <i>n.</i> a steep slope

>  My mates can relieve their horses and secure their own safety by dismounting at all `pinches' <i>(1886,Maori, The,20</i>) [NOTES: 1886-90 The Maori at Home 20]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Descending, they crossed the narrow valley ..., climbed the steep pinch on whose summit is Mornington, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> The road was pretty crooked with a lot of elbow corners and ... the grade in many pinches wasn't less than one in ten. <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,49</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> The wagons used to travel in twos and threes to help each other over bad pinches [in the road]. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,118</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Up that old route across the range,/ I've met with swaggers quaint and strange,/ And with them up the pinches toiled,/ And sat by creeks while billies boiled,/ And yarned until the sun had gone,/ Along the road to Erewhon./ <i>iBID.</i> 135 Another thing I noticed was that the old road in the hilly parts, seemed to have led up needlessly steep pinches, and along the crests of wind swept ridges. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,55</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to restrict the feed of farm stock.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> The dry stock may have to be 'pinched' for a period while rabbit-poisoning is in progress and must be closely boundary-kept for about six weeks. <i>(1969,Molesworth,193</i>)

>  Make sure you arrive at October 1st without 'pinching' the cows and having quality feed available. <i>(May 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  Then the autumn cows can be pinched a bit as they dry off. <i>(Sep 2001,Growing Today,63</i>)

### pinch bar
 
 <i>n.</i>

### pinch out
 
 <i>v.</i> of a lead, to thin out and disappear

>  <b>LYON</b> This mine ... had a habit of pinching out, which meant the payable block of stone or running reef vanished without warning ... . <i>(1952,Faring South,172</i>)

>  <i>Pinch</i> (p&ibreve.nch), <i>v. i. [Add.] To pinch out (Mining),</i> to taper rapidly to a point and thus fail utterly; - said of a vein of auriferous quartz. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  <i>pinch out</i> v.t.-pinch out' v.i. of a seam, to thin out and disappear. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The stone was in about 400-ton blocks and then pinched out with no lead to show where to pick it up again. <i>(1952,Faring South,169</i>)

### pinch time
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a season or part of a season when pasture feed is short. (See [feed pinch](f69-fin.md#feed-pinch))

>  It's the pinch time for most farmers now so it means I can run eight extra sheep on every acre that's not idle. <i>(Oct 10, 1974,NZ Farmer,59</i>)

>  The feed pinch time under early lambs comes at any stage from August to mid-September. <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,05</i>)

### pincher
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a disoriented unlambed ewe that attempts to steal the lamb of another ewe.

>  <b>GORDON</b> A few ewes in this build up period, can get a bit confused and decide they've had their lamb; they'll choose a youngster that's nearby and try and walk off with it. Many's the happy group that's been split by an old 'pincher.' <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,60</i>)

>  Ewe that tries to adopt the lamb of another: pincher. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### pine
 
 <i>n.</i> indigenous pine unspecified [kauri pine, screwpine, etc. In general Maori-derived names have been replacing the -pine names, at a slower rate for the South Island, and probably slower for the names of the timbers as distinct from the trees.] (See [kauri pine](k-kea.md#kauri-pine),[screwpine](sa-sho.md#screwpine),[etc](../E#etc))

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> [?a podocarp] Some of the timber trees are of large growth, particularly three sorts; the first is of the pine tribe and is known by the name of Pimise (Pinus??) in three varieties on the north. The second is something like the Lucombe Oack, and the third is called the Red Gum or Eucalipticus I have seen it in England. ... The three varieties of pine are very tall and handsome, and one in appearance like the Lucombe oak grows tall but not so straight, the Eucalipticy grows to a great thickeness in the stem and frequently not less than fifteen feet in girt but not more than 15 or 20 feet in height before it spreads its large branches in every direction, ... <i>(1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 356]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> [?white pine] Another species of the pine, but much inferior to the one I have described, and the same that Captain Cook mentions as bearing a berry with a leaf like that of the yew, presented itself in great abundance upon the banks which bordered the sides of the cove, but its size was inconsiderable, and its wood knotty, and only fit for carpenter's work, such as window-frames, mouldings, &c. ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/233</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> ... the roof [of the station buildings, Canterbury c1859] were of round pine framing covered with rickers (young pine plants) and thatched with snow grass. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,24</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> We had a little better walking ..., passing over about a mile of very good pine forest, but again came to our black birch country <i>(Feb 26, 1847,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Feb 26 Brunner Expedition Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 268]

>  <b>BARKER</b> Blazing logs of pine and black birch made every room The oysters and pine apple he stowed away in the box ...] <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,78,1870 ed.</i>)

>  The flat land and low spurs are covered with the common species of Pines and Birch, such as Rimu, ...Weinmannia (Karmahi), and Fagus (Tawai) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/111/4</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Red Pine Rimu .. White Pine Kahikatea .. Silver Pine Monoao Dacrydium Intermedium Black Pine Maitai[sic] .. <i>(1889,Catalogue, Canterbury Court Exhibition,35</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>The Pines are widely distributed in Australasia ... The name, with various epithets, is given to a few other trees besides those of</i> the Natural order <i>Coniferae. ... </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,354</i>)

>  <i>~ Black p</i>. Podocarpus spicatus. Called <i>matai</i> in the North Island and, I am told, <i>red pine</i> in Nelson, Our <i>r.p. (Dacrydium cupressinum)</i> is <i>rimu</i> in the north and our white (<i>Podocarpus dacrydioides)</i> is <i>kahikatea</i>. (C).) eg 1841 Stephens Letters 22 Red and white pine. <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>The word</i> pine, <i>with various colour adjectives, is used for so many native trees in the South Island of New Zealand that a builder who wants to refer to timber which is really pine (Pinus radiata)<i> has to call it</i> pinus. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,60</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> Loads of excellent pine are to be found. <i>(Jun 17, 1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,63,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 June 17 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 63]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> Our way lay through a wood composed of various kinds of timber together with the noble pine. <i>(1815,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,99,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The timber in this grove [up the Kawa Kawa] was not large, nor could I observe any trees of the pine species, though there were several that appeared of an excellent quality, ... we entered a noble forest of pines, growing to the height of eighty and a hundred feet, before they branched out, and all of them as straight as if they had been [225] shaped by nature, for no other purpose then to shew [sic] her regularity. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/224</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> He was delighted with the houses in the pines and the suggestion of old and rambling gardens about them ... It was small enough to please him, just a little place, a kind of depot for the settlers and the camps of the diggers out back. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,41,1971 ed.</i>)

>  [The mountains in Caswell Harbour] are for the most part covered with trees, such as iron-wood, pine &c, ... <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,161,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 161]

>  The trees are easily cleared away, being either pine or other soft wood; except the iron wood, which grows to a large size, the wood of this tree is a dark red colour, ... <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,103,1986 ed.</i>)

>  There are several varieties of the pine [at Akaroa]; all the trees, however, appear to be evergreens. <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 106</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 28 Thomas Bunbury's Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841]

>  Fine forest, totaras, pines &c. <i>(Mar 26, 1849,Torlesse Papers,70,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The greater portion finely timbered with totara, pine, hinau, &c., ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,240</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Two or three hundred yards beyond begins an irregular fringe of native bush, broadleaf, veronica, fuchsia, pittosporum, and tangled supplejack; beyond these rise the lofty pines and other forest giants. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  <i>Pine</i> (p&imacr.n), <i>n. (Bot.) [Add.]</i> ... In New Zealand the word<i> pine</i> is applied to many trees, some of which also bear other names. Thus, -<i> black pine</i> (see MATAI and MIRO), <i>celery-topped pine (Phyllocladus trichomanoides), kauri pine,</i> called also <i>cowdie pine (Dacrydium pine, or Westland pine (Dacrydium Westlandicum), white pine</i> see KAHIKATEA <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a pineapple [[check Constable edition text here; and check Aloe text]]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> At the Chinaman's shop next door he bought a pineapple in the pink of condition, and noticing a basket of fresh black cherries he told John to put him in a pound of those as well. The oysters and pine he stowed away in the box under the front seat, but the cherries he kept in his hand. <i>(1920,Stories,240,1984 Alpers ed.</i>) [NOTES: [cf 1916 Mansfield The Aloe (1982 O'Sullivan) 95]

### pine beer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The panting bushmen rush at the stump, break off hollow pieces of fern and suck up long draughts of the sour sap - "pine beer," as they call it. <i>(Oct 23, 1883,Star, The,4</i>)

### pine flat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  From the Mungaroa hill the line descends one in fifty-five, crossing a valley at a white pine flat. <i>(Dec 10, 1870,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  The eight families living over in the pine flats hustled into their waggons and rattled away into the woods. <i>(Sep 6, 1898,Bruce Herald,3</i>)

>  Penetrating the pine flat for a few hundred yards, a convenient spot was reached on which to build their fire, and the fish were soon broiling on wooden spits. <i>(Jun 3, 1905,Star, The,1</i>)

>  Good pine flats on each side of the river; the hills sandstone (similar to that used for grindstones) overlying slate, with the exception of one part, where a patch of gneiss occurs. <i>(Dec 24, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Mr Dale to move, "That a letter be written to the Waste Lands Board, asking the Board to recommend the Government to have a Timber Reserve made at the Kokatahi in the silver pine flat of about 1000 acres, more or less, and to have the said reserve vested in the County Council; and to empower the Council to draw up rules and by-laws for the guidance of parties getting timber." <i>(Dec 14, 1881,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### pine tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A young man named Fredk. Butcher was gathering cones from a pine tree on Saturday when he slipped, and falling on top of a fence sustained two broken wrists, a broken leg, and a broken jaw and nose. <i>(Mar 18, 1907,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  [Inland from Kaikora is] dotted with groves of pine-trees ... <i>(Aug 19, 1841,NZ Journal,No. 95/211</i>) [NOTES: [Inland from Kaikora is] dotted with groves of pine-trees ...]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Towards evening we camped ... near a grove of pine trees and arborescent ferns. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,102</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... Waiheki had many a stately kouri [fn. <i>Pine-tree</i>. growing on it. <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/73</i>)

>  <b>STACK</b> The river presented a striking contrast to the dark pine trees that lined its opposite bank ... <i>(1935,More Maoriland Adventures</i>) [NOTES: 1935 J.W.Stack More Maoriland Adventures in [kahikatea] Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938) 139]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> There is here and there the root of a pine which has been burnt into the surface of the ground, and pieces of rosin, which have come from the pine tree, are lying on the ground in all directions. <i>(Oct 22, 1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,210,1932 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> usu. pinus radiata (often earlier called fir)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Most of all the wind, when it meets with the thick belts of pine-trees, which with their myriad separate needles make ready fine instruments, gives a music so subtle and soft the ear is charmed beyond beyond the Thames (Horake[a-e-acute]). <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,26</i>)

 <i>n.</i> matai-beer (cf bush-beer 2.)

The tree containing beer may usually be known by a black, smutty-looking stain that extends some way up the trunk; and if a hole be bored at the base the tree may be plugged and tapped like a barrel. <i>(1926,TrNZI,662</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Andersen Popular Names)]

### pineapple
 
 <i>n.</i> a mortar bomb

>  PINEAPPLE. - A German trench mortar bomb of small size. <i>Ibid.</i> 16 ... even if it's rainin' pineapples an' dud three-ought-threes. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xi</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> At 5.45 a.m. ... a perfect tornado of shells `rumjars' and `pineapples' burst afresh on the same section of the line. <i>(1921,NZ Division,152</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> `There's a pineapple gun that's dropping pineapples around here,' someone said as I went out to take my turn on watch. ... [69] A pineapple exploded nearby and the candle went out and my mates cursed. <i>(1976,Soldier,68</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Then the Hun let fly with whizzbangs, pineapples and the most dreaded of all - with minenwerfers. <i>(1978,Twilight Hour,58</i>)

>  <b>SMALL</b> Also <i>pineapple</i> used in WW1 by NZ soldiers as slang for grenade. My grandfather's diaries have: `Fritz sent us a pineapple today' = `The Germans bombed/grenaded us today.' <i>(Dec 5, 1986,Personal Communication</i>)

>  PINEAPPLE. - A German trench mortar bomb of small size. <i>Ibid.</i> 16 ... even if it's rainin' pineapples an' dud three-ought-threes. <i>((6/0/1917),New Zealand At the Front,xi</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Kiekie, a creeping thing, called by some the `New Zealand Pine-apple', fruits every third year. <i>(1849,Settlement of New Plymouth,18</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Kie-kie (New Zealand's pine-apple) ... is a vegetable impostor, like a sweet artichoke flavoured with turpentine. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/136</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The <i>tawheras</i> that had been missed had dried and shrivelled, and the white of the <i>tiore</i> had decayed, but the fingers of the `gentlemen' had swollen considerably and now provided us with New Zealand `pineapples.' The really good ones were eight inches long and two-and-a-half inches in diameter. They were wrenched out in the same way as giggies. These `pineapples' were never very much sought after by the general public. They needed what might be called an acquired taste. They were tricky eating too. You bit off all the outside hard, nobbly but not prickly covering, and then gnawed the soft, sweet flesh to the bone. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,117</i>) [NOTES: 1880 Taranaki in Sutton-Smith History of Children's Play (1981) 117]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Kiekie, Uriuri, Ori, Tiore, Patangatanga... It is called New Zealand's pine-apple. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),158</i>)

>  He also left a small sample of fibre, prepared from the leaf of the tawera plant - New Zealand pine-apple, - but the tawera fibre does not appear to be of any commercial value. <i>(Jun 7, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

Here the vegetation, though dense, assumes a dwarf and stunted appearance, and consists largely of shrubby veronicas, white and yellow-flowered daisy trees [fn <i>Olearia</i> and <i>Senecio.</i>], and various curious representatives of the heath order, amongst which ... the quaint <i>Dracophyllum traversii</i>, a much-branched tree, whose branches end each in a tuft of stiff foliage, reminding one of the crown of a pine-apple.] <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,188</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> ... his collection of specimens [from Dusky Sound] included two fine New Zealand trees, the silver birch (<i>Nothofagus menziesii</i>) and the pineapple scrub (<i>Dracophyllum menziesii</i>). <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,44</i>)

### Pinex
 
 <i>n.</i> (trademark) a pulp-based wallboard

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... a square rigger of ink almost empty ..., a blackboard pitted with unrewarding labour and scuffed in rolling billows of rubbed chalk, ... sprays [99] of ink blobs on the green pinex walls, ... The pre-fab, stifling in February ... a criss-sross of rafters and beams; pinus and pinex and unpainted corrugated iron, all bolted and jointed and thrown up behind the trees close to the boundary of the school ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,98</i>)

>  It was a two-chair place run by the Moutere brothers, middle-aged men who kept the old-style striped pole outside, the racing over the radio, the grey Pinex walls covered with sporting pictures. and magazines like the <i>Post</i> and <i>Turf Digest</i> in the small, tobacco-stained waiting area. <i>(Jan 14, 1991,BLANK ENTRY,69</i>)

>  <b>Gee</b> I had not bothered to close the garage door and the wind blew straight in all night, bringing Pinex dust down from the ceiling... <i>(2005,Blindsight,53</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> The sheet of pinex that for years had leaned up against his [sc. the Assistant Borough Clerk's] Borough Office wall.. accompanied Hedley to his new premises. <i>(1985,Moaville Magic,59</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Crikey dick if the load of four be twosand pinex didn't arrive. <i>(1980,Prisoner of Mother England,21</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> The baches all smelt the same, awonderful summer smell of Pinex wallboard or the raw wood of converted Armyhuts. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,54</i>)

>  I looked up at the white pinex tileceiling, frowned. <i>(Feb 1993,Metro,112</i>)

 <i>v.</i> inject (a drug) [also ping (someone) up]

>  "I think she said something about he pinged her up [helped inject drugs]. He gave her some bad medicine [drugs] or someone gave him some bad medicine... He just told me he'd pinged her up at the time ... that she fell off the bed." <i>(Oct 11, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Alan Bell told the court McLean had told him that Ms Wickliffe died after he "pinged her up" [helped inject] with drugs, and that he did not mean to hurt her. <i>(Feb 19, 2002,Dominion,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> dose of narcotic in a syringe [noun.]

 <i>n.</i> an Asian

### pingao
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an endangered native sandsedge, <i>Desmoschoenus spiralis </i>. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Desmoschoenus <i>spiralis</i>, Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Pingao', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 272</i>)

>  Desmochoenus spiralis Pingao <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/127</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  the pingao (<i>Desmoschoenus spiralis</i>) and the<i> Spinifex hirsutus</i>, which may be obtained in unlimited quantities on all coast sand-hills in the colony, <i>(1873,TrNZI,6/46</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Sometimes a sea-side-spreading plant named <i>pingao</i> (Demoschoenus spiralis) was used for making belts, and the soft leaves of <i>kahakaha</i> (Astelia bamksii) were also taken into the service of mat-makers. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,238</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> In New Zealand there is a most excellent example [of a sand-binding plant] in the pingao (<i>Scirpus frondosus</i>) ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,69</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>pingao</i> , sea-shore plant (Scirpus frondosus), tumble-weed, after the stiff-spined globular seed-heads: the leaves, drying a bright-orange much used for plaiting ornamental girdles and for tukutuku (lattice-work) becoming rare, is the most robust ... of these [sanbd binders]. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I cross over and go along the sand track, pocked with prints, between the native <i>pingao</i> and the introduced marram and lupin and up on to the sandhill. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,52</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>DESMOSCHOENUS SPIRALIS.</i> Pingao ... provided Maori women with bright strands for their weaving. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,204</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pingae or pingao, <i>Desmoschoenus spiralis</i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Desmoschoenus spiralis</i> PIINGAO ... Orange tufts about 30 to 70 cm tall, growing in rows along stout, woody, sand-binding rhizomes which appear like frayed ropes where the sand has blown out. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,368</i>)

>  <i>pingao n</i>. NZ sand-plant with bronze leaves. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,570</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>HULME</b> There, suitable low sandhills are covered with marramgrass [sic] and pingao and sand convolvulus. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,43</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The sedge pingao (<i>Desmoschoenus spiralis</i>) ..., now beyond the Thames (Horake[a-e-acute]). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,157</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Now the tawny spread of pingao has vanished. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,111</i>)

>  The bright yellow leaves of the pingao were woven into useful purse-like girdles. <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  ..pingao, the golden sand sedge. Desmoschoenus spiralis, pigao (if you're from the north) or pikao (if you're from the south)... <i>(Mar 26, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  It is true that pingao is an endangered species, and also the only plant of its kind in the world. <i>(Apr 19, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Pingao, a native sandsedge unique to New Zealand, stabilises sand dunes in the coast areas where it grows, by trapping wind-blown sand around its base and in its leaves ands roots. The biggest threats to pingao are beach vehicles and introduced plants. <i>(Jan 11, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  In places there were tufts of the pingao grass, whose tough leathery leaves were formerly used by the Maoris for making girdles, and are still used for the manufacture of kits. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,38</i>)
