### pinhooker
 
 <i>n.</i> [US usage in OED : 1944 Richmond (Virginia) Times-Despatch 5 Oct. 18 The fixation of prices this year is bad business for the time-honoured 'pinhooker', the man with relatively small operating capital who, during the more spacious days of tobacco selling when the auctioneer got the green light for a sale from wall to wall, bought tobacco when the market was low and held it until it was high, pocketing the difference. The prices this year are so narrow that the pinhooker's business has been practically squeezed out.] WEBSTER : pinhooker - a small speculator in tobacco at a local market]

>  Aynsley admits that it might seem strange for someone who never bets at the races to risk tens of thousands of dollars on the pinhooking game, one of racing's biggest gambles... It also failed to clear the most tricky hurdle facing pinhookers - trying to pick the sire which will be fashionable in six months' time. <i>(May 9, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B8</i>)

### pink
 
 <i>n.</i> new kid [1984 16 M E85 Pakuranga Coll. 25 PINKS [new kids]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a marble

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> usu. pink 'em, to make a very good job of shearing a sheep (shearers sometimes call this a `special cut'), by shearing sheep close to the skin (sc ?make the pink skin show through (contrast rough'em)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> Dick could shear if he was willing, pink' em if he wanted to./ But he wouldn't shear at Main[?m]ai, started in to rough them through./ <i>(1897,Station Ballads,34</i>) [NOTES: (`The Bloke That Ran across a Snag')]

>  <b>MEEK</b> PINK: To shear a sheep close to the skin. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> PINK 'EM. To make a very good or better than average job of a sheep. Shearers sometimes call this a `special cut.' <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>)

>  <i>Pink 'em:</i> Make a very good job of shearing a sheep. Shearers sometimes call this a `special cut'. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,57</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Pink</i>. To shear the sheep too close to the skin. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

>  <i>pink</i>: To make the pink skin of the sheep show by shearing closely. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear a sheep so closely that its pink skin shows evenly [ regarded as a job well done.]

>  Dick could shear if he was willing, pink 'em if he wanted to. <i>(Sep 3, 1896,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Dick could shear if he was willing, pink 'em if he wanted to. <i>(1898,Station Ballads,36</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> Old Bill was there to 'pink' a few and shear 'em dry or wet. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,59</i>)

>  Fellow Southlander Nathan Stratford pinked his pen to perfection and had the lowest score of 1.000 on the board ... <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,32</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Dianthus deltoides</i>

### Pink and White Terraces
 
 <i>n.</i> [incl the Terraces (at Rotomahana)]

>  They came to enhance their health in mineral spas, enjoy the natural features like the Pink and White Terraces - destroyed in the 1886 eruption of Mt Tarawera - and soak up the rich history and culture of the local Maori. <i>(Jul 1, 2002,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  Mr. Crompton Smith's letter may have satisfied your readers that there is little likelihood of the Terraces at Rotomahana ever being seen again; but I should like to supplement his letter with a description of the scene, Lake Rotomahana, immediately after the outburst. <i>(Sep 25, 1931,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Cascading down opposite sides of Lake Rotomahana, the Pink and White Terraces were colonial New Zealand's premier tourist attraction. Visitors were spellbound by the beauty of the terraces' silica buttresses and mineral pools, which, according to one writer, shimmered "such a blue as is seen nowhere else in nature." <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/90</i>)

>  At Rotomahana we saw the terraces of enamelled baths of pink and white. <i>(May 10, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  The photographs Valentine took of Otukapuarangi and Te Tarata or Mt Tarawera's Pink and White Terraces, as well as other landscapes, are striking for their clarity and composition. <i>(Jun 2004,North & South,115</i>)

>  The celebrated White Terrace.. is a thing of the past, having changed from a pure white to a dirty brown... There is no change in the colour of the pink terrace. <i>(Dec 20, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  THE NEW POSTAGE STAMPS .. The design at present in use for 1d (Lake Taupo) will be used for the 4d, and the present 4d design (White Terrace) for 1d. <i>(Jun 13, 1898,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Sir Robert Stout wishes it to be understood, says the Argus that "he is the centre of political activity in New Zealand; that his policy takes the place of the famous Pink and White Terraces of the colony". <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,25</i>)

>  An elderly vulcanologist... knocked on the door of the Limbs Dance Studio in Auckland and suggested they do a dance about the destruction of the Pink and White Terraces. <i>(Jun 14, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  WHERE THE FAMOUS PINK AND WHITE TERRACES WERE SITUATED, DESTROYED BY THE TARAWERA ERUPTION IN 1886. <i>(Aug 9, 1905,Otago Witness,43</i>)

>  The project that has been mooted more than once before is being seriously discussed again, namely, the possibility of recovering the famous terraces at Rotomahana. <i>(Feb 21, 1910,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  The fairyland beauties of the white and pink terraces of the Rotomahana.. have often been celebrated. <i>(Apr 12, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  It must have taken countless ages to gradually build up the Pink and White Terraces into the beautiful proportions they assumed previous to their destruction. <i>(Mar 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,28</i>)

>  The Pink and White Terraces, whose peerless beauty was a petrified dream of fairyland, are now, alas! only a memory. <i>(Dec 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. : 2</i>)

### Pink Batt
 
 <i>n.</i> (trademark) (usually plural) an insulation batt made of pink fibreglass

>  The most well-known brand of insulation is, probably, fibreglass Pink Batts, which have ben advertised in New Zealand for years. <i>(Apr 10, 2002,Capital Times,15</i>)

>  PINK BATTS Keep you warm in winter and cool in summer. <i>(Mar 10, 1992,Mitre,[3]</i>)

>  <b>Geary</b> I had a dream about a dero. He'd got pissed, and turned up too late to sleep at the night shelter. Had to break into a building site to bed down. Covered himself in Pink Batts. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,12</i>)

>  The Gaiety Hall in Rue Jolie is 125 years old and for the past 10 of those years its ceiling has been a squat to a scrum of possums, rats and mice making whoopee among the Pink Batts. <i>(May 5, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  [Advt] What's more.... Pink Batts are completely safe. They won't burn. Neither will they spread flame or develop smoke. <i>(Jun 14, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  [Advt] Just as new Pink Batt control heat, new Sound Barrier reduces sound. <i>(Feb 24, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

### pink maomao
 
 <i>n.</i> = longfin, mata (maataa)

>  Two fishes, one, from the Bay of Islands, known popularly as `pink maomao', and another, sold in Auckland as `barraconda', are omitted from the list. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/114</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Maomao, pink .. mata</i> .. Caprodon longimanus <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PINK MAOMAO [<i>Caprodon longimanus</i>] ... Longfin and mata (Maori) are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,44</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PINK MAOMAO or LONGFIN ... Although commonly called pink maomao, particularly in the far north, this species is a member of the groper and butterfly perch family (Serranidae), as is not strictly as maomao. <i>Other names: Caprodon longimanus</i>; mata (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,227</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MAOMAO </i>(two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Scorpis violaceus</i> (ii) <i>Caprodon longimanus.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Blue maomao; Blue fish (ii) Pink maomao; Pink fish. (c) Other common names: None listed for either species (d) Principal localities where taken: [both] North Auckland, Hauraki Gulf, Bay of Plenty. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,35</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The Pink Maomao grows a few inches longer than the Blue (up to about 18 inches or 46cm). <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,163</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PINK MAOMAO</i> (Mata) <i>Caprodon longimanus </i>... The pink maomao is a relatively large sea perch ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,207,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pink maomao Caprodon longimanus</i> New Zealand and south-east Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,85</i>)

>  [species name] pink maomao .. [scientific name] <i>Caprodon longimanus</i> .. [other name(s)] longfin, pinkfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [index to common names 283-293] maomao, pink 1265-6, 1314 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### pink rock
 
 <i>n.</i> a low grade of heroin

>  A Hamilton seizure of `pink rock' - poor quality heroin - is the first heroin seizure in New Zealand in about nine months. ... The `pink rock', which takes its name from the discolouration [sic] caused by shoddy manufacture, came from Thailand ... The `pink rock' has less than 40 per cent purity. It can also be coloured grey or brown by impurities, and is a number three grade heroin. <i>(Mar 26, 1982,Dominion,2</i>)

### pink tree broom
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a native broom, Carmichaelia carmichaeliae , syn. <i> Notospartium carmichaeliae </i>, endemic to Marlborough.

>  Pink tree broom, Marlborough rock daisy and weeping broom are native to the park and endemic to south Marlborough. <i>(Aug 2008,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,30</i>)

>  The pink broom is one of Marlborough's most dramatic flowering plants .. Originally it was thought to be endangered but subsequent research has found it to be fairly widespread on bluffs and steep places throughout the eastern Marlborough river systems. <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>)

>  "The new Park's natural treasures include varied and unique plants, with species such as pink tree broom, coral daisy and New Zealand lilac." <i>(Jun 7, 2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.doc.govt.nz/templates/news.aspx?id=49145]

### pink-eye
 
 <i>n.</i> ophthalmia in sheep

>  <i>*Pink-eye</i>.-Ophthalmia: an affliction of sheep's eyes resulting in partial or temporary blindness. Generally caused by dust with travelling mobs. In general use throughout New Zealand. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (1941 Baker NZ Slang 40 adds to this `ophthalmia suffered by sheep and shepherd ...')]

>  <i> pink-eye n.</i> eye-disease of sheep and cattle, caused by dust. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>pink-eye n</i>. eye-disease of sheep and cattle, caused by dust. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a common contagious disease affecting the eyes of sheep, that can lead to blindness.

>  The name pink-eye is given to an acute inflammation of the eyes of sheep and is referred to as contagious opthalmia or contagious irido-cyclitis. <i>(Apr 20, 1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,224</i>)

>  For the treatment of pink-eye old shepherds use a barbarous method, that of putting powdered glass or sugar in the eye ... <i>(Apr 20, 1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,225</i>)

>  Antepartum paralysis or 'sleepy sickness' was the major complaint, but 'pink-eye' also caused several fatalities. <i>(Oct 25, 1951,NZ Farmer,31</i>)

>  Another and more practical use of chloromycetin is in the treatment of pink eye, which is often seen in sheep that have travelled in mobs or are being held in paddocks during periods of danger in facial eczema. <i>(Jan 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,83</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Pink Eye (Contagious Ophthalmia) Pink eye is a very common contagious disease affecting the eyes of sheep. It is caused by micro-organisms which spread from sheep to sheep when they are in close contact, as during handling in yards under hot dusty conditions. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,131</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The small blood-vessels of the eye become congested and red, giving rise to the name pink eye. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,132</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> We struck trouble with hoggets on a crop of chou years before when they had a bad run of pink eye. [pink eye] <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,74</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a bruise, a mark

### pinus
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pinus radiata</i> usu. in NZ as a generic name (<i>P. insignis</i>)

>  Amongst others we may mention the foliola of several of the Cycadeae as having taken root, as also did.. tufts of Pinus, &c.; and all without the aid of a previously formed bud. <i>(May 13, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>GRANT & FOSTER</b> P. radiata <i>(1880,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/15</i>)

>  P. radiata <i>(Jun 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,535/3</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> I have ... an invoice from the firm of Residential Construction Ltd ... It is for pinus, plus cartage. <i>(Oct 5, 1952,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Oct 5 A.R.D. Fairburn in Letters (1981) 209]

>  <b>DUFF</b> In addition the boys have cut down rimu, totara, matai, and pinus growing on the property and built a 4-stand woolshed without help from outside. <i>(Dec 22, 1954,Shepherd's Calendar,202,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,XX</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word <i>pine</i>, with various colour adjectives, is used for so many native trees in the South island of New Zealand that a builder who wants to refer to timber which is really pine (Pinus radiata) <i>has to call it</i> pinus. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,60</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The pre-fab, stifling in February ... a criss-sross of rafters and beams; pinus and pinex and unpainted corrugated iron, all bolted and jointed and thrown up behind the trees close to the boundary of the school ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,99</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> And those bookshelves made from Pinus boards and concrete blocks. Bit out of place here, said Dick. <i>(1977,Of Ivory Accents,10</i>)

>  <i>pinus n.</i> short for <i>pinus insignis</i> (see INSIGNIS] or<i> pinus radiata</i> (see RADIATA). [L] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,570</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 860; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 367]]

### Pinus insignis
 
 <i>n.</i> early term for radiata pine [aka insignis, Pines insignis, Monterey pine]

>  [Caption]] A sawmill at Otakaike.. Cutting insignis pine timber for building purposes. <i>(Jul 18, 1928,NZ Free Lance,27</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> ... and round the outward boundary of the enclosure a shelter belt of the invariable macrocarpa and <i>pinus insignis</i> had been planted. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,36</i>)

>  <b>RHODES</b> ... the endless gorse hedges and ugly clumps of macrocarpa and pinus insignis that are the hall-marks of the Canterbury Plain flashing past in monotonous succession. <i>(1952,Fly Away Peter,12</i>)

>  INSIGNIS PINE : Pinus radiata.. Monterey, California. The name radiata pines is more commonly used. <i>(Aug 1957,NZ Timber Journal,57</i>)

>  A.G., has reserved two cases of coniferous trees, as per list, expressly for this market, which will be sold as mentioned above, in the interval of the steamer's arrival and departure .. 50 Pines insignis .. 12 [Pines] Radiata ... <i>(Jun 1, 1865,Wellington Independent,4</i>)

>  Pinus Insignis (Monterey Pine) 1 year .. 6 to 12 [inches] 80 to 100 [ultimate height in feet] 0/6 [per dozen] 3/0 [per hundred]. <i>(Apr 22, 1893,Northern Advocate,3</i>)

>  [Advt.] Those about to plant shelter trees should call and see the large stock of Pinus insignis, Pinus muricata, Cupressus Macrocarpa gums, etc, at right prices. <i>(Jun 14, 1913,NZ Bulletin,10</i>)

>  In an article dealing with milling Insignis Pine, Mr L. E. Baigent of Nelson, who has probably milled more Insignis Pine than anyone else in New Zealand states... <i>(1933,NZ Railways Magazine,8(6) : 89</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He [sc. a New Zealander] burns the bush and plants Pinus insignis. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,156</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> ... the ubiquitous Pinus insignis. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,65</i>)

>  A notable development has occurred in the production of the exotic insignis pine (Pinus radiata), which increased from about 7-1/2 to 13-1/2 million feet between 1928 and 1932. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,576</i>)

>  insignis n. (also pinus insignis) Monterey pine, native to southern California, widely grown in NZ. [L, = remarkable] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,389</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]

>  The giant pinus insignis have now been removed and oriental plane trees have been substituted. <i>(Nov 17, 1900,Egmont Star,14</i>)

>  A large number of currents, pinus insignis, macrocarpas, and roses will also be submitted. <i>(Jun 5, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He burns the bush and plants Pinus insignis; and he hasn't got a decent picture-gallery in the two islands. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,156</i>)

>  In two beds of insignis there were supposed to be some 6000 plants. <i>(Jul 19, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> introduced conifer, grown extensively for timber [radiata pine, pinus aka insignis, Monterey.]

### pioke
 
 <i>n.</i> dogfish

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pi |oke; A fish so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,193</i>)

>  SQUALUS FERNANDINUS (Molina). <i>Spiny Dogfish; Pioke</i>. Large numbers are smoked and readily sold in the Auckland markets under the name of `silver-spots.' Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  SQUALUS FERNANDINUS. <i>Pioke.</i> The pioke has been found in the Auckland market continuously throughout the year. It is popularly sold as `silver-strips,' or `silver-spots.' Probably many of the fish are <i>Mustelus antarcticus. </i> <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/97</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

>  SPINY DOGFISH. <i>Squalus fernandinus,</i> Molina. The spiny dogfish (<i>Squalus fernandinus</i>) is one of those species of shark which has attained importance as an economic species. ... It is generally called oke-oke, or kapeta, by [Maori]; the term pioke is applied by some tribes to this species, while to other tribes pioke is any small species of shark. <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/223</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <i>Mustelus antarcticus:</i> Common dogfish, pioke (Auckland), rigg (Canterbury). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> NORTHERN DOGFISH ... <i>Other names: Squalus blainvillii</i>; Griffin's dogfish, common spined dogfish, brown dogfish; pioke, okeoke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,186</i>)

>  <b>BRAMBLEY</b> I lose patience with people who prattle about `gummy' sharks when they mean pioke, or dogfish - `lemon-fish' when it reaches the shops. Instead of teeth, the doggy has cross-hatched boney ridges ... <i>(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,43</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SMOOTHOUND </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Mustelus antarcticus.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Small Flake Fillets (restricted to this species); Smoothound. (c) Other common names: Kini; Pioke; Lemons; Lemonfish; Dogfish; Rig; Silver Strip (fillets); Sweet William; Flake. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,44</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SPOTTED SMOOTH-HOUND Mustelus lenticulatus</i> (Gummy shark, spotted dogfish, rig, pioke) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,56,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] rig .. [scientific name] <i>Mustelus lenticulatus</i> .. [other name(s)] spotted dogfish, gummy shark, kini, pioke, smooth hound, deepsea bream, lemonfish, spotted gummy shark, sweet william, dogi <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Rig Mustelus lenticulatus</i> ... Other names include spotted dogfish, gummy shark, smoothhound and pioke. Often listed as <i>M. antarcticus</i>, but this name now applies only to a similar Australian species. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,30</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] pioke 214, 225-6 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### pioneer
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> She veiled her disappointment, however, and with true pioneer philosophy rode down to the infant township. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,18,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Some sheep were placed on the earliest runs in 1858, but it was really 1859 before many of the runs received their first accessions of stock, so when young ... Kennard went up-country early in 1860 he found things still in the pioneer state as a rule. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,118</i>)

>  Long after the time of the `land-grabbers' of the 'thirties and 'forties of last century, we have pioneer documents giving Maori names. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,73</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Death of a Taranaki Pioneer. William Jones, an old whaler, who has resided in Taranaki since early in the thirties, died this morning, aged 89. <i>(Jan 11, 1898,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Instead of acknowledging the more acute penetration, and the greater enterprise of the settlers of Port Nicholson, and recognising and legalising their measures, Ministers have preferred begriming themselves with the odium of an ex post facto law involving the ruin of the very men who were their pioneers, and therefore entitled to special protection and encouragement. <i>(Oct 17, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  James Clarke, one of the pioneers of the West Coast, died to-day after a lingering illness. <i>(Oct 29, 1887,Star, The,3</i>)

>  He has remained at the Bluff for the last sixty-five years, following the callings of whaling and fishing, so that he was one of New Zealand's earliest pioneers. <i>(Nov 7, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> In the United States the pioneer has always had the right of pre-emption, for there is no one better entitled to it than he who gives the land a marketable value. <i>Ibid.</i> 192 [There are] numerous other places, of which very little is known, and where pioneers are gradually finding their way, on sufferance from the Maories, or on payment of a small consideration for the use of the land. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,123</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> As we wound about the [Rimutaka] hills the devastation and desolation became more apparent, for pioneers burn all before them, ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,65</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Feb 10 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 65]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Of stalwart frame, great endurance, and an excellent pedestrian, he was the <i>beau ideal</i> of a pioneer. <i>Ibid.</i> 62 The foremost of these [first-comers to Otago] deserve special mention, inasmuch as they must be credited with being the <i>bona</i>[a-circumflex] <i>fide</i> pioneers of the new settlement. <i>Ibid </i> 82. By the middle of 1847 the staff of surveyors had completed their labours [at Port Chalmers], ... Everything was ready for the advent of the pioneers; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 200 Amongst the older pioneers there was much regret and some demur at the great change [of the 1860s]. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,41</i>)

>  Anyone who considers what Canterbury is to-day and what she was fifty years ago must be filled with admiration for those early settlers we well name `pioneers'. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,8</i>)

>  At the fringes of settlement, where the pioneers are hewing their homes out of the wilderness, the history of road-making is as follows: First comes the bridle-track, that gradually grows into the dray-road; and then comes the metal, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,33</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [<i>Pounamu</i>] was as great an attraction to the New Zealanders of that [pre-European] era as the nuggets ... of Westland were to the white pioneers two and a half centuries later. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 85</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Trees were the pioneer's natural enemy; ... <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,59</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Here on hundreds of acres of brown grass, ringed in by the stately old English trees planted by the pioneers, tents went up like toadstools. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,52</i>)

>  A pioneer is `one who goes before, as into the wilderness, preparing for others to follow,' and today women are still preparing the way for those who will follow after them. <i>Ibid.</i> 30 There were ... wild pigs in the surrounding bush; fuchsia berries could be gathered for tarts ... For tea, a concoction of bidi-bidi was used, and I have heard it described by one of these early pioneer women as horrible stuff to! <i>Ibid.</i> 299 These are a few examples of the pioneers of the '40s and '50s. Several of them ...have recorded details of their daily lives; the slush lamps, ... the plague of mice and rats that necessitated the erection of futtahs (whatas) ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,viii,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> I suppose you have to give it in that the Pioneers weren't so bad. They came out to the land, but they weren't men of the land. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,3</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> She was a member of the third generation of a famous pioneer family, and lived ... in a large and prosperous white homestead near the town. The homestead ... was often visited by those who wished to find an authentic colonial atmosphere. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,18,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> The energising force in New Zealand life up to now has been this myth of the pioneer, the frontier ... <i>(1976,Pacific Star,19</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> Jim and Nance Gregory are buried in the Hunt cemetery on Pitt Island where the old Hunt pioneers are buried. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,128</i>)

>  Mr. Bell's name is well known to the readers of Mr. Wakefield's "Adventures in New Zealand" as the pioneer of the Wanganui settlement. <i>(Jun 26, 1847,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,63</i>)

>  George Wakelin, an early Wairarapa pioneer, died at Greytown last night. <i>(May 30, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  We remember further back than that, when the small community that then pioneered the destinies of this town found sufficient talent and leisure to give capital musical entertainments. <i>(Jan 28, 1890,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> A study ... shows that there was a marked interest in these lands by Australian settlers who ... settled and pioneered them ... <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,87</i>)

### Pioneer
 
 <i>n.</i> member of the (Maori) Pioneer Battalion in WW1

>  The pioneers who got everything into working order were then turned out of their tents bag and baggage to find a new place the best way they could. <i>(Mar 27, 1915,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  Today, he says, we wrongly see the Maori Pioneers as somehow being second-class soldiers... While there were many Maori serving in the ranks of the Wellington and Auckland infantry battalions, the principal Maori effort in World War I was with the Pioneers. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 86</i>)

>  Another welcome additon was made by a detachment of 43 Maori Pioneers lent to assist in the big job. The pioneers were great favourites with everybody, splendid toilers, always willing and cheerful; they supplied an inexhaustible fund of drollery and laughter that was sadly missed when they were recalled. <i>(Apr 19, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Unfortunately, though we could fight likethe Infantry, the Infantry could not diglike us. So the Powers that be made usPioneers. [Fn. The Pioneer Battalion wasformed from the Otago Mounted Rifles andthe Maori Contingent.] <i>(Apr 18, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/16 : 78</i>)

>  [Hdg] With the Maori (Pioneer)Battalion. <i>(Aug 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,v/51 : 56</i>)

### Pioneer Club
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> ...a maid, a cook and a gardener to call her 'Madam'; ...an open sesame to the Pioneer Club, the best homes in town and the most exclusive circles of the racing world. <i>(1981,Predicament,40,1974 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [1930s-40]]

### pioneer settler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He anticipated not only <i>material</i> but <i>intellectual</i> eminence for New Zealand - <i>material,</i> first indeed, for the pioneer settlers in a new country are impelled by necessity to direct primary attention to what is immediately and practically useful; but intellectual also. <i>(Dec 3, 1850,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  It may be said that we who were early pioneer settlers had as great or greater hardships to undergo, and this may be true. <i>(Mar 10, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The death is announced of William Telford, one of the pioneer settlers of Clutha. <i>(Aug 25, 1888,Star, The,3</i>)

>  With the passing of Mr John Lindsay, of "Strathmore," there is removed from the Western district of Southland one of its typical outstanding pioneer settlers, and one of the most transparently loved and upright men that have transplanted themselves from the Old World to this far outpost of Empire. <i>(Nov 6, 1923,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  She was born at Gropers Bush in 1863, being a daughter of the late Guiseppi Valli, one of the pioneer settlers of the district. <i>(Jun 28, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Mr John Hugh Murray, a pioneer settler in the Waituna district, is dead. <i>(Jan 20, 1910,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  The natural pastures provided the only grazing available for the stock of the pioneer settlers, and in consequence early New Zealand settlement began mainly in the South Island. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,358</i>)

>  ... by this means the bush was thoroughly explored by Natives and pioneer settlers ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/499</i>)

>  Thomas Munro, a pioneer settler of Mangaweka, is dead, aged 65. <i>(Nov 1, 1902,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  A pioneer settler of this district, Mr. Samuel Oates, died at Alfredton yesterday, aged 78. It is said of him that he brought the first vehicle over the Rimutaka - viz, a wheelbarrow, with 6cwt of goods, which he wheeled from Wellington, there being no road for the greater part of the journey. <i>(Aug 30, 1892,Evening Post,2</i>)

### pioneering
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We readily admit the palm of pioneering in the colony of New Zealand to be due to the settlers in Wellington, ... <i>(May 24, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  We have tried ... to depict every phase of women's part in pioneering. There are stories of the wives of missionaries, ... storekeepers, ... farmers, runholders, accommodation-house keepers, and numerous others. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,vii</i>)

>  The expenditure was incurred in providing Church and School for a small and slowly increasing number, who could do nothing for themselves whilst engaged in the rough and thriftless task of pioneering for the whole settlement ... <i>(Jul 17, 1852,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  This was the pleasantest sight of all - this young settler, pioneering just as faithfully as father and grandfather pioneered. <i>(Nov 3, 1910,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  As one of this community - alive to this pioneering - I tender my mite of knowledge, in support of the appropriate selection of the site of Auckland for the capital of the colony of New Zealand. <i>(May 16, 1846,New Zealander, The,3</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  In support of the motion the mover dwelt at some length on the great pioneering services rendered New Zealand by Wakefield. <i>(Oct 30, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  The sooner any person arrives in a new country after the first pioneering difficulties are over, the better chance he has of succeeding. <i>(May 5, 1860,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  "Good progress has been made with the preparation of a standard history of the wars in New Zealand, embracing the pioneering period between 1840 and 1872," states the annual report of the Department of Internal Affairs. <i>(Nov 3, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> Everyone in latter years seems to want to equate living conditions of today with the pioneering conditions of the nineteenth century and even of the first half of the twentieth. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,65</i>)

### piopio
 
 <i>n.</i> the rare native thrush. [Maori]Turnagra tanagra; thrush, New Zealand

>  It is interesting to note that the Piopio, or Native <i>Thrush<./i> (Turnagra crassirostris), <i>and the Long-tailed Cuckoo ... are somewhat [similar] in appearance. </i> <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/124</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The Zosterops was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <i>Turnagra crassirostris</i>. - Piopio, Southern Thrush. South Island. Now rare, and in many parts extinct. ... <i>T. hectori</i>. - Piopio, Northern Thrush. Southern part of North Island. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  [Bird] called Biobio <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/86</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 20. <i>Turnagra crassirostris. G.R.Gray,</i> ... Golobieo of the natives of Dusky Bay, or Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Pio Pio of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Dieffenbach.</i> Keropia et Koko Eou [?Kokoea (shining cuckoo) HWO] of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy</i> et Gaim. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/190</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/190]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [word list] Piopio Turnagra Craporostris <i>(Oct 7, 1842,Journal,2</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The forest was enlivened by many of the common birds, and I brought home one of a new species, called pio-pio [fn Turnagra crassirostris, G.R.Gray. ...]. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/119</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The <i>Turdidae</i> family has only one species in the olony; the Piopio, a bird about the size of the thrush, which is supposed to be a visitor in the NorthIsland from the south. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/25</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Another ... belonging to the family Turdidae, is the piopio (Turnagra crassirostris), the thrush of the settlers. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 36. KEROPIA TANAGRA. Schleg. Thrush. Pio-pio. <i>Tumagra Hectori. BULLER </i> <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,14</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> TURNAGRA HECTORI. (NORTH ISLAND THRUSH.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Piopio, {Koropio,} Korohea, and Tiutiukata. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,135,1888 2nd edn 1/26 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Piopio, n.</i> Maori name for a thrush of New Zealand, <i>Turnagra crassirostris</i>, Gmel. See <i>Thrush. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,356</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> In one [of Buller's aviary cages], there were parrakeets, yellow heads, New Zealand canary, fantails, robins and tom tits, and a pio pio (New Zealand thrush) one of our best songsters - ... <i>(1935,Annals of a NZ Family,129</i>)

>  <i> piopio</i>, a bush-bird (Turnagra tanagara), the North Island thrush and (T. capensis) the South Island form: both extinct soon after 1900. A riverbed bird, the dotterel, and a naroow-leaved plant, dianella, also bear this name, but the thrush is usually associated with the name: in any case the dianella has a name tuurutu. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <i>piopio</i>: New Zealand thrush. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND THRUSH <i>Turnagra capensis Maori name</i>: Piopio <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,238</i>)

>  <i>piopio ... n</i>. N.Z. thrush. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>piopio n</i>. New Zealand thrush. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>281.a. Turnagra capensis tanagra</i> (Schlegel, 1865) <i>North Island Thrush (Piopio)</i> ... now rare and local, perhaps extinct ... <i>281.b. Turnagra capensis capensis</i> (Sparrman, 1787) <i>South Island Thrush (Piopio) </i>... now possibly extinct ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,73,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Extinct birds ... New Zealand Thrush Turnagra capensis</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) OTHER NAMES: <i>Piopio, korohea. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,308</i>)

>  <i>piopio n.</i> NZ variety of thrush. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,571</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NOC `PIOPIO' (NZ Thrush) <i>(1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> In the strictest sense of the term, New Zealand is without `song-birds'; but such species as the Tui, the Korimako, and the Piopio possess vocal powers of a very respectable kind, the compass and variety of their notes adding greatly to the charm of the New-Zealand woods. <i>(1888,History of NZ Birds, A,1/x/iii</i>)

>  Potts (1882: 199) observed that the South Island piopio (<i>Turnagra c. capensis</i>) was "indifferent" as to food, feeding on insects when procurable, or on grasses, seeds, or fruit. <i>(2004,Notornis,No. 51(4) : 206</i>) [NOTES: Medway]

>  The.. following native birds should be saved, if possible.. Piopio: A thrush. Has a short bill, red tail, yellow breast, and brown back. <i>(Apr 8, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  The natural history specimens shown included specimens of the fast disappearing native thrush or piopio (<i>Tanagra hectori</i>). <i>(Oct 17, 1900,Evening Post,5</i>)

### pip
 
 <i>n.</i> a pied stilt

>  <b>BULLER</b> PIED STILT <i>Himantopus leucocephalus Other names</i>: Stilt, Barker, Poaka, White-headed Stilt, Daddy Long-legs, Torea (Rotorua), Pip (Kaipara). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,147</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to tell (on); to pimp on [ca1940 Don't you pip now]

### pip-silly
 
 <i>adj.</i> applied to an officer in a territorial or ASC unit who was proud or conscious of rank or authority [c1940 (Les Cleveland) (nf from P: <i>pip</i> 4. the star on the jacket of a uniform)] (See [cf. US rank-happy](../U#us-rank-happy))

### pipe off
 
 <i>v.</i> to imitate, `take off'

>  <b>SARGESON</b> We'd stand in shop doorways and Terry'd pipe off everyone that went past. <i>(1946,That Summer,78</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to inspect

>  <b>SARGESON</b> pipe off: watch (someone) critically and perhaps make comments. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> The hop had started when we got inside, so we took a seat and piped off all the good-lookers. I could see Gus's eye following a tall, willowy fairy as she waltzed round ... . <i>(1924,Me and Gus,12,(1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm) ed.</i>)

### pipe up
 
 <i>v.</i> ? to rise or increase, as the wind (O.E.D. 1901) or? burn up as in a pipe or tunnel (formed by roots or parasitic growth) [? omit]

>  This [purakau at the base of a kauri] ... once ignited will burn for weeks together, smouldering underneath ... and `piping up' with every drying breeze. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/494</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

### pipe wood
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Schefflera digitata,</i> patete, ohau (??is this from PIPE a tubular cavity in wood [[a1927] Ver. = Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) (Stony Bay) <i>Schefflera digitata</i> pipe wood]

### pipe-clay
 
 <i>n.</i> (a local Central Otago and Northland, a spec. use of general English sense)

>  The lowest bed is exposed on the bank of the river at the Chinese camp. It is (1) a greyish-white plastic clay, locally known as pipe-clay. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 5. (N.S.)/31</i>) [NOTES: (Park-Cromwell) (Otago)]

>  Clay stone areas are locally known as pipe clay country [North Auckland], and are characterized by the stunted vegetation which they carry ... <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/190</i>) [NOTES: (Ferrar)]

>  <b>WALL & CRANWELL</b> Usually the soil profile shows several bands of colour beginning with a few inches of dark grey, peaty material overlying the famous white `pipeclay', so rich in silica. <i>(1943,Botany of Auckland, The,22</i>)

### pipe-cover
 
 <i>n.</i> a (? tobacconists') trade euphemism for a French letter [ca1954 (Wgton)]

### pipe-opener
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the exertion experienced in a steep climbing situation.

>  We had our pipe-opener for the year's mustering with the bringing-in of the ewes for pre-lamb shearing. <i>(Sep 1961,NZ Farmer,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Politics) [high country mustering] an extremely steep track or route, through which sheep can pass and musterers pant.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> This was a musterer's 'pipe-owner' which went straight up the steepest part of the hill. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,85</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> In those days the climb to the junction was a good pipe-opener, especially as everything to sustain us for three weeks was on our backs. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,63</i>)

### pipefish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pipe-fish, n</i>. common fish-name. The species present in Australia and New Zealand is <i>Ichthyocampus filum</i>, Gunth.,family <i>Syngnathidae</i>, or <i>Pipe-fishes.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,356</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> The wharf was a great place to fish for piper and sprats, and you could gaze down into the clear water and watch huge kingfish come in and nuzzle the piles. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,114</i>)

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> As we nosed out of the bay, the needle-nosed garfish - "piper" were darting along the surface of the water, like a shoal of silver snakes. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,15</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON & HECTOR</b> Angling for garfish in Auckland Harbour, where it is known as the piper, is graphically described in `The Field', London, Nov 25, 1871. `The Pipers are `jest awfu' cannibals', and you will be often informed on Auckland wharf that `pipers in deeth on piper'. <i>(1872,Fishes,118</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Piper, n</i>. an Auckland name for the <i>Garfish</i> (q.v.). The name is applied to other fishes in the Northern Hemisphere. <i>(1898,Austral English,356</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Piper, n</i>. an Auckland name for the <i>Garfish</i> (q.v.). The name is applied to other fishes in the Northern Hemisphere. <i>(1898,Austral English,356</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Angling for Gar Fish in Auckland Harbour, where it is known as the Piper, is graphically described ... [in `The Field', London, November 25, 1871] `... you will be often informed on Auckland wharf that `Pipers is deeth on Piper.'' <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,118</i>)

>  HEMIRAMPHUS INTERMEDIUS Cantor.<i> Garfish</i>, or <i>Piper; Takeke</i>. Occurs in abundance around all the northern coasts. Common, Otago, in spring and early summer. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. ... SCOMBRESOX FORSTERI Cuvier and Valenciennes.<i> Skipper</i>. A highly esteemed edible fish, known as `deep-sea piper' at Bay of Islands. Secured midwinter. ... Distribution: Examples have been secured as far south as Hokitika. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/120</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The well-known garfish or piper (<i>Hyporhamphus intermedius</i>) has the lower jaw protruding far beyond the upper, and uses it for grubbing among the seagrass in shallow bays. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,198</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 198]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> Cantor. GARFISH or PIPER; TAKEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,21,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> : Garfish or piper. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> Cantor. Garfish (piper). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/404</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>POWELL</b> PIPER (<i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> ). This is common in the harbours and estuaries of both the North and South Islands. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,66</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GARFISH or PIPER (TAKEKE) <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> Phillipps. This fish is known in Auckland as Piper, although in Otago it is called Garfish or Guard Fish. ... It is on account of this peculiarity [a long bottom jaw] that they are sometimes called Halfbeaks. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,157</i>)

>  A name used in New Zealand for the edible fish which is also called `garfish'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),303</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GARFISH ... <i>Other names: Reporhamphus ihi</i>; piper, half-beak; ihe, takeke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,206</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GARFISH [<i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> ] ... Also called piper, halfbeak, ihi, and takake. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,16</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Garfish Reporhamphus ihi Piper; Guard-fish; Half-beak <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Piper or garfish .. .. ihe, takeke .. .. <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>HALFBEAK</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> . (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Garfish; Piper. (c) Other common names: None known. (d) Principal localities where taken: North Auckland, Auckland and Cook Strait. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,29</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PIPER: <i>see Garfish </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,176</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PIPER </i>(Garfish, ihi) <i>Hyporhamphus ihi</i> ... The piper is an elongate fish with a long beak-like lower jaw. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,171,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Garfish Hyporhamphus ihi</i> ... Often called piper. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,66</i>)

>  <i>piper n. ... NZ</i> garfish. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,571</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>piper n. ... NZ</i> garfish. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,571</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] piper 956-70 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  The tip of Point Chevalier was a park known as `the Reserve', ringed with pine trees and macrocarpa which led to the Piper Rocks, where boys and men ... caught sprats and piper, which were excellent eating. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair `At the Point')]

>  The piper we caught at Cornwallis [Manukau] were superb, but still not a patch on the mau-mau we caught off a single rock ... <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,21</i>) [NOTES: (K. Ireland)]

>  Piper schools feed around us, like miniature marlins, the sun glinting off their blue-green flanks. <i>(May 24, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  We used to go fishing at Huia orCornwallis with a dinghy and net andbring home barrowloads of piper. <i>(Oct 1992,North & South,10</i>)

### pipey liver
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> (Liver fluke of sheep and cattle occurs in parts of New Zealand, but this fluke, <i>Fasciola</i> is an introduced parasite whose intermediate hosts, the freshwater snails <i>Myxas Limnaea</i>, are also introduced. In New Zealand the adult fluke has been reported only in cattle, sheep and goats, in which unpleasant symptoms such as `pipey liver' and `bottle jaw' may occur. Black disease can kill a sheep within a few hours. The fluke concerned occurs in many parts of the world <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,50</i>)

### pipfruit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] I knew it wouldn't be easy says pipfruit permit chief. <i>(Feb 25, 2000,Dominion,17</i>)

### Pipfruit New Zealand
 
 <i>n.</i> a pipfruit grower organisation and exporter

>  He has been at the helm of grower organisation Pipfruit New Zealand for five of the stormiest years of the industry's 100-year life. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

### pipi
 
 <i>n.</i> any of several edible bivalve molluscs, especially the smooth-shelled cockle Paphies australis (now the main sense), also the ribbed cockle Austrovenus stutchburyi.

>  Went on to Kapoai ... had dinner - well supplied with pipis but alas no tupakihi ... saw another noteworthy sight ...[sic] a young karoro just out of the nest, running along in a curious human way all by himself. <i>(Jan 21, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/680,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <i>pipi</i>, cockle in general among the Maori: but by Pakeha more applied to Mesodesma australe. Pipi-komore and pipi-taiari was applied to Dentalium, the tusk-shell. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BULLER</b> On those shoals the women gather cockles (pipi), and at night the men, with torches in their hands, spear the patiti [sic] (a flat fish like the plaice). <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>pipi</i>: cockle, shellfish. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A cockle Pippee <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/337</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|ipi, <i>s.</i> Cockle. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,193</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> A few natives gathering <i>pipis</i> on the beds. <i>Ibid.</i> 257 A few natives gathering <i>pipis</i> on the beds. <i>(Aug 26, 1832,Journal,255,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The rivers are well stocked with fine fish ... - the very creeks swarm with them. There are abundance of lobsters, crawfish, oysters, prawns, and shrimps; besides clams, peppies, muscles, limpets, and cockles. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,15,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> I have many times made a grand Meal in a Kanga Mouri or Native Village on Pippies [pipi], sitting round the Coppre Mouries, each helping themselves till all was done, then I would give the Natives, a dram of Rum to wash it down. .... Now Why heckie was the name of a bed of Pippies or Cockles, in the River. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),45</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... pippies, or native cockles. <i>(1840,Present State of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ... pippy ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,48,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ...as the tide was out, the [Maori] women were busily employed in gathering pipis , a species of cockle, from the uncovered flats. The <i>pinna mussel (pinna Zealandica)</i> was found in considerable abundance, sticking in the mud at the mouth of a small river that discharged itself into the [Wellington] harbour. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/296 ... the whitened heaps of <i>pepi</i> shells that lie scattered in immense quantities about these slopes, that once formed the sites of their fortified pahs. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/246</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The `pipi', a shell fish, which is to be found on the beach, appears to be its favourite food. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> The white shells of the `pipi' or cockle <i>(1852,Our Antipodes,116</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The common name for all fish is <i>ika</i>, or <i>ngohengohe</i>; of all univalve shells, <i>pupu</i>; and of bivalves, <i>pipi</i> and <i>anga</i>, which includes both kinds. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,415</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> (no date) [pepi] ... one of them scooped out his eyes with a pepi (or mussel) shell and eat [sic] them ... <i>(Apr 1865,Journal</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> The women boiled our kettle, and when food was ready they set before us some boiled flour, potatoes and `pipis' (cockles). <i>(Dec 1, 1866,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Dec 1 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 157]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> With most deliciously cooked kumeras, potatoes and peppies. <i>Ibid.</i> 321 [We dug] <i>peppies</i> out of the beach at low water. <i>(1881,Poenamo,107</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Pipi (pip-pee):</i> A kind of cockle. <i>(1908,Poenamo,50</i>)

>  <i>CLAMS (OR PIPIS)</i> ... Fry bacon and ... onion, add chopped potato and ... tomato ... Simmer ... add clams, and cook ... Thicken the gravy ... <i>(1910,Colonial Everyday Cookery,114</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pipi</i>. incorrectly of a cockle: New Zealand: C. 19-20. Morris. Occ. <i>peppy. pippy. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,634</i>)

>  <i>pipi</i>: shellfish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... two Maori women bending over and half a dozen kids putting the pippis in flax kits. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,44,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>pipi</i>(p. 634). The forms <i>peppy, pippy</i>, are solecistic; but <i>pipi</i> is a `Maori name for a bivalve-like cockle' (Sidney J. Baker, letter, 1946) and is therefore ineligible. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1136</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> pipis: edible sand-burrowing shellfish. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> pipi: edible salt-water shellfish. <i>(1973,Brown Bread,141</i>)

>  The Maori name of a common edible shellfish which supplied the old-time Maoris with much of their food. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),303</i>)

>  <i>pippy n</i>. small shellfish; marine worm; in New Zealand pipi [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PIPI Once the staple diet of coastal-dwelling Maori tribes the name pipi covers a variety of small shellfish which include tuatuas and cockles. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,179</i>)

>  The pipi were delicious and salty, raw or cooked. The kuia had their backs to us, selling the rewena bread and pottles of pipi...and they didn't see how much we ate. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,Ihimaera ed.,155 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Alice Tawhai)]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> A large species of cockle called by the natives "pippi" are very abundant on the coast. <i>(1973,Traveller under Concern,83</i>) [NOTES: Quote from 1 March 1853]

>  One youthful urchin afterwards declared that for two days they had been "living on pipis." <i>(Dec 13, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  It appeared that the Natives in crossing the plain to Papakura, had taken a short cut across a creek, where one of them had stopped to search in the mud for pipi, a species of mussel. <i>(Oct 13, 1847,New Zealander, The,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 219. <i>Venus intermedia</i>. `Called `<i>Pepa</i>' [e,a macron] by the natives; they are extremely abundant, and are eaten as food by the natives. The name appears generic for this edible bivalve.' <i>Dr.Sinclair.</i> `East Coast; much eaten by the natives; called <i>Pipi</i>.'[i2 macron] <i>Dr.Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/1262</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/l262]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 219. <i>Venus intermedia</i>. `Called `<i>Pepa</i>' [e,a macron] by the natives; they are extremely abundant, and are eaten as food by the natives. The name appears generic for this edible bivalve.' <i>Dr.Sinclair.</i> `East Coast; much eaten by the natives; called <i>Pipi</i>.'[i2 macron] <i>Dr.Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/1262</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/l262]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Then follows a series of shell beds, consisting of the remains of the following species, now still inhabiting the estuary;- <i>Chione stutchburyi </i>(Cockle); <i>Huai</i> or Pipi, <i>Mesodesma chemnitzii</i>, Pipi; <i>Amphibola avellana</i> (Periwinkle) Hetikutiku, and <i>Mytilus smaragdinus</i> (Mussel) Kuku; <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,416</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori name of a shellfish, sometimes (erroneously)</i> called the cockle, <i>Mezodesma novae-zelandiae.</i> [1820] <i>(1898,Austral English,356</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> Europeans frequenting our sandy beaches have given the name pipi to Mesodesma australe, a smooth-shelled bivalve .... generally keeping the name cockle, for the smaller, roundish, ridged bivalve more common on mud-flats. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,446</i>)

>  <i>pipi</i>, cockle in general among the Maori: but by Pakeha more applied to Mesodesma australe. Pipi-komore and pipi-taiari was applied to Dentalium, the tusk-shell. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen)]

>  <i>pipi n</i>, pl <i>pipi</i> or <i>pipis</i> [Maori] 1 : a bivalve mollusk (<i>Mesodesma novae-zelandiae</i>) used as food in New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1722</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Pipi .. .. kokota, pipi, taiawa .. .. <i>Amphidesma australe </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Local clams include toheroa (<i>Amphidesma ventricosum</i>), tuatua (the heavier, more triangular <i>A. subtriangulatum</i>), and the common white pipi (<i>A.--or Paphies--australis</i>). Clams live under sand, <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,79</i>)

>  <i>pipi n. </i>small N.Z. shellfish. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>pipi n. 1</i>. an edible shellfish of New Zealand. 2. an Australian mollusc of sandy beaches, widely used as bait. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,861</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 571; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 367]]

>  <b>BURTON</b> The pipi, together with the toheroa and tuatua, belongs to a family of shellfish unique to New Zealand. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,56</i>)

>  [species name] pipi .. [scientific name] <i>Paphies australis</i> .. [other name(s)] slovata, kokota <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pipi Paphies australis </i>The name pipi is sometimes applied to a number of New Zealand's common bivalves which are collected for food, but it properly refers to this species, a close relative of the tuatua and toheroa ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,159</i>)

>  Bad news for those of us of simple pleasures. Two Gold Coast residents have written to me complaining about this new scheme of dredging for pipis in-shore along the Paraparamu beaches. <i>(Jul 11, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses, esp. pipi shell]

>  We looked in vain to see what is said to be seen on the hard sand between Opotiki and Wakatane [sic] - a green and white penguin, the Korora, (<i>Spheniscus minor</i>) taking a pipi shell to a great height, from whence it lets it fall on the beach, and then descends itself to pick out the flesh from the broken fragments of the shell. <i>(Apr 24, 1852,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  [Photo caption] The above is a photo of the representation of a fish in what appears to be mother of pearl within a pipi shell. <i>(Nov 6, 1907,Otago Witness,49</i>)

>  Their true history, so far as it can be ascertained; their mythical history, which is abundant if collected, as to some extent it has been in the records of the Native Lands Court .. the records of their existence and works exhibited in the enormous pipi beds, and in their hangis, all over the country .. all these would tend to throw light on the history of the race. <i>(Jun 9, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Indeed, pipis abounded in many places along the main beach, while other shellfish made their homes on the rocks, and even when the winning of land from the sea had removed some of the sources of supply there were - right up to less than two decades ago - well stocked pipi beds at Pipitea and Evans Bay, and in Oriental Bay, where Maoris used to improve the shining hour by obtaining supplies, not only for their own use but for European tables - it was a common sight to see Maori women carrying flax kits of pipis from house to house for sale. <i>(Feb 8, 1915,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  The designs are produced by cutting the face with a sharp "pipi" shell in the required patterns, and the soot of ferns is rubbed into the cuts. <i>(Dec 27, 1924,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Pipis, on his death-bed, he desired, pipis (a small shellfish similar to the English cockle) he must have: ... <i>(1934,Journalese,169</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PIPI Shellfish, cockle group. <i>Chione Stutchburyi, Mesodesma australe</i>, etc. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> <i>pepi</i> shells. <i>(1850,Savage Life,2/297</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> `There, she is a wife for you,' he said, nodding his head in the direction of a good-looking native young lady, at that moment ... scraping potatoes with a pipi-shell. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,116</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> The deposit consisted of a layer of cockle and pipi shells about 2 feet in depth .... <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,121</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Yeah, we were glad of a good feed of pipi fritters. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,72</i>)

>  The river bottom is compact sand and clay, and pipi beds. <i>(Nov 7, 1901,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  On the following morning, after five or six hours' fruitless search all over the headland, I completed the search in this same cavity, and unearthed another axe of very hard stone .. some pipi shells, and bones of the barracouta. <i>(Aug 16, 1893,Tuapeka Times,4</i>)

>  The flax fibre, after being stripped from the leaf of the plant by means of the pipi shell or of the nail, had to be soaked in water so that the fibres might be separated. <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Pipi - the shellfish (mytilus lutus), altogether different from the North island `pipi'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Even pipi does not mean the same thing all round New Zealand (a Stewart Island Maori of the older generation would use this name for the mussel) and it would mean nothing at all outside New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 79 M [ytilus] planulatus [the smooth, navy-blue southern mussel]/ called pipi by the Stewart Island Maoris, goes down well if frothed up three times in a kerosene tin over a beach fire and then tipped out on fern leaves. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... his pincers of pipi-shell. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,48</i>)

>  A number of Pipi (<i>Mesodesma Novae-Zealandiae</i>) and pawa shells ... were dug out ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/195</i>)

>  Don't pull a pipi face; come join the guitar hooley. <i>(Nov 12, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Beneath the infinite whorls of Whiria Pa Hill / Pipi-gatherers stray about the wet shore. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,26</i>) [NOTES: Quote from Charles Brasch (1957) Rain over Mitimiti Mountains]

>  Jive can't figure out why his mum keeps calling his toes pipi diggers. <i>(Aug 16, 2003,Dominion Post,F10</i>)

>  There will be Twelve Grand Prizes... [Prize]13. Will be subdivided into suitable portions to embrace the necessary advantages of pepi gathering, flax cutting, kapi picking, swamps for eel catching, scoria for dyke building, clay for brick-making, and numerous other pursuits too numerous to particularize, the whole of this lot forming 988 shares. <i>(Mar 31, 1849,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  How could British law define the title to an eel-pa, or protect the rights of the owners of a pipi bed? <i>(Jun 16, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  [He] estimates that there are only 40 properties a pipi's throw from the sea along the Thames coast. <i>(Dec 7, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (recipe)

### pipiharau
 
 <i>n.</i> a lamprey

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The lamprey, Pipiharau, is, properly speaking, a salt-water fish which enters the river to spawn. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,30,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The principal ones are the<i> tuna</i>, eel, the <i>pipiharau</i>, lamprey, the <i>kokopu</i>, and <i>inanga</i>;... <i>(1870,Te Ika a Maui,498</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <i>Brown Creeper Finschia novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Pipipi. ... </i>DISTRIBUTION <i>South Island, Stewart Island and their nearer outlying islands.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,275</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a turkey

### pipit
 
 <i>n.</i> a small, brown, ground-nesting bird, Anthus novaeseelandiae (also called ground (or native) lark, kataitai, pihoihoi). (See [also New Zealand pipit](new-no.md#new-zealand-pipit))

>  32. <i>Anthus Novae-Zealandiae</i>, Gml. Lark, Pipit, Pihoihoi. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,288</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pipit, n</i>. another name for<i>Ground-Lark</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,356</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Pihoihoi, anthus Novae Zelandiae, ground-lark. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,134</i>)

>  Austranthus novaeseelandiae Gm. Ground-lark; Pipit. This engaging little bird is so well adapted to open country and native tussock associations that with the spread of pastoral areas it is more than maintaining its numbers. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/96</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>MANNERING</b> [In Canterbury in the 1870's] Stilts were common on the river - blackbill gulls, black-backed gulls, shags, dotterel, native larks (pipits). <i>(1943,Eighty Years,22</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> Every morning when I come down the hill with Elsie I am met on the track by two pipits who fly or run ahead of me for three or four hundred yards and then leave me. <i>(Oct 4, 1952,Shepherd's Calendar,96,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hioi, Anthus novaeseelandiae, pipit or ground-lark. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,52</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>pipit</i>-The native lark; very much like the English skylark in size and colour. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,182</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Pipit .. .. pihoihoi .. .. <i>Anthus novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family MOTACILLIDAE (pipits). The New Zealand pipit belongs to the same family as the European wagtail, but the Australian wagtail is a <i>Rhipidura</i>, like our fantail. Pipit is a dull brownish-grey streaky bird, inconspicuous until with a white flick of the outer tail feathers it rises from the ground. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,282</i>)

>  In the fields adjoining are numerous skylarks and an occasional native pipit. <i>(Feb 2, 1899,Otago Witness,53</i>)

### pipiwharauroa
 
 <i>n.</i> (also called shining cuckoo, bronze-winged cuckoo) the smaller of the two migrant cuckoos that summer in New Zealand. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Birds met with in Pelorus ... A small kind of cuckoo, not larger than a sparrow - called pipiwawaroa by the natives - a migratory bird. <i>(Jan 6, 1855,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  When the pipiwharauroa arrive in September, everything changes in riroriro's cosy little nest. <i>(Jun 2006,Tumai,76/30</i>)

>  During the quiet summer nights the deep rich notes of the Koheperoa, or Long-tailed Cuckoo, may be heard at intervals till break of day. ... The Pipiwarauroa, or Shining Cuckoo, is of milder disposition ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/13</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  CHRYSOCOCCYX LUCIDUS, Gml. Pipiwharaupa. [sic for -wharauroa] Bronze-winged Cuckoo, Whistler. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/65</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CHRYSOCOCCYX LUCIDUS. (SHINING CUCKOO.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Warauroa, Pipiauroa, and Pipiwarauroa. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,77,1888 2nd edn 1/132 ed.</i>)

>  45. <i>Chrysococcyx lucidus</i>, Gml. Whistler, Bronze-winged Cuckoo, Pipiwarauroa. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,477</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> The shining cuckoo, or <i>pipi-wharauroa</i>, and the long-tailed cuckoo, called koekoea and also kohoperoa, are regular summer migrants from the tropics, ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 170</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The shining cuckoo, or <i>pipi-wharauroa</i>, and the long-tailed cuckoo, called koekoea and also kohoperoa, are regular summer migrants from the tropics, ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 170</i>)

>  <i>pipiwharauroa</i>, often shortened to wharauroa, a migratory bird (Chalcococcyx lucidus), shining- or bronze-wing cuckoo: winters in New Caledonia and adjacent islands: the long-tailed cuckoo is koekoea[a]. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>pipiwharauroa</i>: shining cuckoo. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SHINING CUCKOO <i>Chalcites lucidus Other names:</i> Pipiwharauroa, Whistler. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,180</i>)

>  <i>227. [Chrysococcyx lucidus lucidus]</i> (Gmelin, 1788) <i>Shining Cuckoo (Pipiwharauroa) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,60,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pipiwharauroa n</i>. migratory bird, shining cuckoo <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,367</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  <i>Shining Cuckoo Chrysococcyx lucidus lucidus</i> (GMELIN, 1788) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pipiwharauroa, bronze cuckoo, whistler. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,254</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... Paroquets - two kinds, ... called Kakariki by natives. A small kind of Cuckoo, - ... called Pipiwawaroa by the natives - a migratory bird. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,65</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Pipiwawaroa</i> - This is a bird of passage ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),64</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Two migra-[123]tory cuckoos (the Cuculus fasciatus, Forst.), called kohaperoa, or koekoia, and a very small and beautiful kind (the Cuculus nitems of the same author), called by the natives pipiwawaroa - which appear on the coast at Christmas - mark the period of the first potato-harvest. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/122</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 41. <i>Chrysococcyx lucidus</i>. - ... [194] Poopoo arouro of the natives. Forst. Pipiwawaroa of the natives. <i>Yate, Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/193</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/193-194]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> [this needs to be rechecked: koekoea usu. long-taled cuckk By the natives of [the East Coast the shining cuckoo] is called Koekoea; but by the northern tribes Pipiwarauroa. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion North Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 15]

>  I heard a pipiwharauroa today-first time this springs. <i>(Sep 1855,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/179</i>) [NOTES: (A.S.Atkinson)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Two migratory <i>Cuckoos</i> visit New Zealand in summer from Australia and the southern islands, and depart in autumn. During the warm summer nights they sing sweetly. The Kohoperoa, one of these cuckoos, is about the size and colour of the sparrow-hawk; the Pipiwarauroa cuckoo is a much smaller and a more variegated-feathered bird. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/26</i>)

>  ... Read the papers & did Maori, went for about half an hour in the Domain & found a fantail nest (young ones) & a riroriro's with two young ones & an egg. I am inclined to think the egg is a pipiwharauroa's (white, with purple spots at the larger end). Splitting firewood in the afn. I found a fine large centipede - a weri - body brown, with the articulations very plainly marked, legs a pale blue ... <i>(Oct 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/645</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Oct 20 Richmond-Atkinson Papers (1960) 1/645 (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 55. CHRYSOCOCCYX LUCIDUS. Gml. Shining Cuckoo. Whistler. Pipiuwaroa <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,21</i>)

>  We refer to the Pipiwharauroa or Zebra bird, a native of New Zealand, which during the present season had developed a remarkable appetite for the black leech. <i>(Mar 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,36</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> pipiwharauroa, <i>Chalcitis lucidas </> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  Early on, [Binney] leaned towards the abstract, as in "Pipiwhararua mating" (1963)... <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Judging from the description of the bird.. I presume it is the "Shining Cuckoo" - native name "pipiwarauroa" - which appears in New Zealand only as a summer visitor. <i>(Mar 3, 1885,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  I have read your nature notes with much interest, especially when they deal with birds of passage, like the shining cuckoo or pipiwharauroa, the silver eye, and the godwit. <i>(Jul 14, 1909,Otago Witness,13</i>)
