### pirate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> ... the Kauri Timber Company employed a man, known as the Company's `pirate', whose job it was to travel the banks of all the waterways in search of logs stranded above high water mark, and, following periodical reports, men were sent to jack the logs into the water and prepare them for towing to the mills. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,242</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The last two cabs left in Auckland city [in 1983] who don't have installed radio telephones. Post- war legislation deemed that all new licensees have RT's. Some of the `old guard' didn't bother. They prefer to work off ranks. One of Auckland's better-known taxi drivers is a pirate -- `Waverley' Bill Claney -- so named because he used to hunt the old Waverley hotel rank. These days he's known by a new generation as `Kojak' --he declined an interview saying: `I'll be in touch when I want to write my memoirs'. Here's hoping Bill. <i>(Jul 1983,Metro,25/81</i>)

### pirate ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an unlambed ewe that takes or attempts to take the lamb of another, causing mismothering. (See [Aunty; pincher; robber ewe](../A#aunty;-pincher;-robber-ewe))

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Rarely will a season conclude without the appearance of 'pirate' or 'robber' ewes at some time during the lambing. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,53</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> I noticed one of the most annoying things to be met with on a round. That is a 'pirate ewe.' She has not lambed herself but is determined to make off with another ewe's lamb, preferably a twin. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,143</i>)

>  a ewe that tries to steal the lamb of another: pirate. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### pirikahu
 
 <i>n.</i> piripiri

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Having no tea or sugar left I used the Pirikahu a creeping burr instead. It was a pretty fair substitute. [Wanganui River] <i>(Nov 9, 1848,Journal,5/282</i>)

>  Acaena <i>Sanguisorbae</i>, Vahl. ... Nat. names, `Hutiwai', Middle Island, <i>Lyall</i>; `Pirikahu' and Piri piri', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 54</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pirikahu, <i>Col[enso]. Acaena Sanguisorbae. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence the plant [<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i>] is only too familiar, as the `Native Burr' to the sheep farmers alike of New Zealand, Australia, and Tasmania. ... To the Otago settler the plant is also known as the `Bidi-bidi,' or `Beta-beta;' to the North Island Maori as `Hutiwai,' `Piri-piri,' (Colenso), or `Piri-kahu,' (Colenso). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Piripiri-kahu [Maori name] .. Burr [Settlers' name] .. Acoena sanguisorbae .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

### Piripauan
 
 <i>adj.</i> geol. classification

>  G. Warren: The Haumurian and Piripauan type sections, Haumuri gulf. <i>(Jul 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,23/3</i>)

### piripiri
 
 <i>n.</i> biddy-bid [bidibidi See also biddy-bid, hutuwai, utuwai] (See [bidibidi](../B#bidibidi))

>  A problem which faces our sheep farmersis that of the native "piripiri" or"utuwai" in late shorn wool. <i>(Nov 27, 1917,Levin Daily Chronicle,2</i>)

>  A problem which faces our sheep farmers is that of the native "piripiri" or "utuwai" in late shorn sheep. <i>(Nov 29, 1917,Press, The,3</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The common <i>Acaena</i> was called by the Maoris Piri-piri, but colonists frequently [usually] corrupt the Maori name, and call the plant Bidi-bidi. As a general rule, the Maori names are more incorrectly pronounced as we go southward from Auckland to Otago. This is largely due to the fact that, in the north, Europeans have been brought into closer contact with the Maoris than in the south. In some few cases, the difference in pronunciation may be due to differences in the Maori dialects. The changes that take place often follow phonetic laws - thus the Maori `p' is softened into the European `b' as above, while `r' is replaced by `l' or `d'. Poro-poro (<i>Solanum aviculare</i>) becomes Bulli-bulli, and Puriri becomes Boradi. This also explains such a form as `Kowdie' pine for Kauri pine. Korari - a flax-stalk - similarly becomes Koradi or even Kaladi [1907 edn ?mistakenly, <i>Kalladi</i>]. `K' is also sometimes altered into `g,' this Kie-kie (<i>Freycinetia Banksii</i>) becomes ghi-ghi. The last vowel in a reduplicated syllable was faintly pronounced in Maori, and often disappears altogether in the European form of the word, <i>e.g.</i>, Poro-poro gives Bulli-bull, and Piri-piri gives Bidi-bid. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,202,1940 p 211 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PIRI PIRI (<i>Acaena ... sanguisorbae</i>). This name is commonly corrupted into [called] bidi bid. In the North Island the plant is called hutiwai or utiwai and by mistake poroporo. It is a native species well known to all New Zealanders, and is a weed chiefly on land in process of being brought into occupation. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Attuned to the geographical region were some particularly troublesome growths, and of these, the hard fern (or ring fern) became a special pest difficult to control and to guard against. ... This hard fern and piripiri were greatly encouraged by set grazing with sheep, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 239 He [the pioneer] did not realise the insidious spread of secondary growth and of bracken fern, hard fern, water fern, manuka, and piripiri under the close and continuous nibbling of the turf by sheep. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,15,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>ACAENA ... sanguisorbae ... var. A.</i> ... Burr, Biddi-biddi. PIRIPIRI ... <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,31</i>)

>  <i>piripiri n</i> -s [Maori] <i>1 a</i> : a troublesome New Zealand weed (<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i>) bearing burs covered with hooked bristles<i> b</i> : WHITE MAPAU 2 also <i>pirijiri</i> : a fragrant Asiatic and Australasian herb (<i>Haloragis micrantha</i>) of the family Haloragidaceae <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1723</i>)

>  <i>piripiri</i>, [s] <i>Acaena anserinifolia</i> (Forst.) Druce, <i>Acaena novae-zelandiae</i> T. Kirk, <i>Acaena viridior</i> (Ckn.) Allan ... <i>piri piri [=] piripiri</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)

>  <i>piripiri n. NZ</i> = BIDDY-BID. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,571</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 368]]

>  <i>Pi&#769;.ri-pi&#769;.ri</i> (p&emacr.&#769;.re.-p&emacr.&#769;.r|e;<i> colloq</i>. p&ibreve.r&#769;.&ibreve.-p&ibreve.r&#769;.~i), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.)</i> A saxifragaceous tree of New Zealand <i>(Carodetus serratus)</i>; - called also <i>white mapau, white maple</i>, and <i>birch.</i> It bears a cluster of black berries, resembling those of the ivy, and called by settlers <i>biddy-biddy</i> or <i>biddy-bid</i>. See BIDDY-BID. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Acaena <i>Sanguisorbae</i>, Vahl. ... Nat. names, `Hutiwai', Middle Island, <i>Lyall</i>; `Pirikahu' and Piri piri', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 54</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Piri-piri, <i>Col[enso]. Acaena Sanguisorbae. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence the plant [<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i>] is only too familiar, as the `Native Burr' to the sheep farmers alike of New Zealand, Australia, and Tasmania. ... To the Otago settler the plant is also known as the `Bidi-bidi,' or `Beta-beta;' to the North Island Maori as `Hutiwai,' `Piri-piri,' (Colenso), or `Piri-kahu,' (Colenso). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

>  Piripiri Acaena Sanguisorbae, Vahl. ... Piripiri. Haloragis depressa, Hook. f. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/49 Piri piri. Bolbophyllum pygmaeum, Lindl. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  The soil [on Campbell Island] is very wet ... the surface between the tussocks (Patiti) carpeted with beautiful mosses ... ### piss

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>piss</i> any alcohol; eg `Nice drop of piss, Fred.' NZA. 2. in various collocations or catchphrases, often in transf. or fig. use <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,84</i>)

>  On instruction, we took out the expression `plenty of piss' ... <i>(1971,Public Eye, The</i>) [NOTES: 1971 Edwards The Public Eye referring to TV programme Jul 1970 (Griffiths Collection)]

 <i>phr.</i> to flatter

 <i>phr.</i> something easily done or achieved

### piss-
 
 <i>adv.</i> as an intensifying derogative prefix, placed usu. before (usu. a polysyllabic) aj, and possibly formed onpiss-poorP8 1. pennil;ess, hence 2. RAF abominable, hence 3. general services, to describe anything bad, feeble, ineffective, disgusting, etc still current 1975. P8 does not give any other examples of the usage, nor does it give <i>poor as piss, </i> possibly a re-formation. NZEng <i>piss-awful</i> a common usage; but cf the general structural use: Too piss-clever for his boots; Too piss-beautiful for words; etc. <i>piss-good, piss bad</i> doesn't seem to work. <i>piss-poor</i> I associate with University in the 1940s; <i>piss

### piss-wee
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [peewee](p-per.md#peewee))

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### pit
 
 <i>n.</i> a sleeping bag or bed

>  <b>McGEE</b> By the time they get into the pit they're too shikkered and tired and knocked up to even think about giving someone else a bit of pleasure. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,49</i>)

>  WHILE YOU are lurking slothfully in your pit - bed for those not familiar with the language of the hills- ... <i>(Sep 22, 1980,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35/1</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> So to cope with the rituals of tramping, its pains and camaraderie, a slang language has developed to suit. Lots of quick short words to fit in the breathless pauses as you pant uphill. `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936; he travelled 40 km by rowing boat, horse and foot in 21 hours), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather), and many, many more. <i>(1988,Hills, The,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kumera pit

### pit-sawing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> The only means of getting sawn timber was to cut it yourself, by what was called pit sawing. Dad used to make a pit about 20 ft long, and 6 ft deep, place logs around it, roll the logs he wanted sawn into position in the middle... Dad's mate used to go into the pit and dad, who was the taller, stood on top of the log. Dad had to drive the saw downwards, and his mate pulled it, and up and down they sawed, squared the log, cut boards and four by two and so on until they had sufficient timber for rafters, uprights and floor joists. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,107</i>)

>  The first such memory is from an 1860 pit sawing site in Stewart Island. <i>(Aug 1980,NZ Antique Bottle Collector,4(4)/10</i>)

The native names for this genus are Mamaku, Pitau, Korau, and Ponga. <i>(Dec 18, 1869,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

It generally grows from 20 to 40 feet without branches, and the wood is much like Norway Pitch Pine, but whiter. <i>(Jan 1796,Journal,1796</i>) [NOTES: 1796 Jan Murry's Journal of the `Britannia' in 1908 McNab Hist. ### pittosporum
 
 <i>n.</i> a generic name often used as a common name

>  The following trees will be supplied to the schools named .. Island Bay School: 27 pittosporum, 12 coprosmas, 200 matipo, 2 laurels, 1 <i>Buddlea golbosa</i>, 3 cabbage trees, 6 veronicas (varied), 1 laurestinus, 100 privets. <i>(Jul 10, 1913,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> I think we should plant a hedge inside [the fence] but the best one will be Pittosporum or as they call it here [?in NZ rather than Aust] the Matipo or the Ngaio which makes I believe a beautiful hedge ... when it does not grow straggly. <i>(May 15, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May 15 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 191]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Two or three hundred yards beyond begins an irregular fringe of native bush, broadleaf, veronica, fuchsia, pittosporum, and tangled supplejack; beyond these rise the lofty pines and other forest giants. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Pittosporum .. .. karo, kohuhu, tarata, tawhiri .. .. <i>Pittosporum </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>pittosporum n</i>. genus of handsome evergreen shrubs with fragrant white flowers. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>HALEY</b> Walter found a convenient pittosporum leaf. <i>(1986,Settlement, The,123</i>)

>  <i>pittosporum n</i>. member of large genus of NZ and Australian evergreen shrubs with white or yellowish flowers and having seeds embedded in a viscous substance. [Gk <i>pitta</i> pitch, <i>sporos</i> seed] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,572</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>Pit&#769;.tos&#769;.po-rum</i> (p&ibreve.t-t&obreve.s&#769;.po.-r&ubreve.m),<i> n</i>. [NL., fr. Gr. {pitta} pitch + {sporos} seed.] (Bot.) A genus of trees and shrubs, the type of the order <i>Pittosporeae. </i>The seeds, imbedded in a black gluten (whence the name) are contained in berries, which grow in clusters and are indehiscent. Of 55 species 9 are found in Australia and 6 in New Zealand. The scientific term is used as the vernacular name; but various species are also known by other names. Thus <i>Pittosporum undulatum </i>is called <i>native laurel</i> or <i>mock orange; P. tenuifolium, black mapou; P. eugenoides, tarata</i> or <i>white mapou; P. phillyraeoides, poison berry tree, butter bush, willow</i>, and <i>native willow</i>. See under LAUREL, MAPOU, POISON, and TARATA. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  I would direct the thoughtful sender's attention to the desirability of rendering also the species of Pittosporum of New Zealand, nearly a dozen kinds, largely accessible to us ... Unlike the seed of the Karaka, that of the Pittospora will retain its power of germination. <i>(Jul 29, 1865,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  The trees and shrubs avaiable include coprosma, cabbage trees, pittosporum, Indian hawthorn, and <i>Olearia fosterii</i> (ake-ake). <i>(Jul 7, 1915,Evening Post,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The pittosporums, or matipos, takeprominent place among the springflowering trees. <i>(Oct 12, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,14</i>)

>  Pittosporum Green Globe is a favourite. You wouldn't recognise it as a pittosporum because it stays really compact and looks as if it has been trimmed when it hasn't. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D5</i>)

>  Along the edge of the stream were cabbage trees, toetoe and native sedges; above the flood line were flax pittosporum and hoheria; and further back, under an already existing canopy of kanuka and pest species such as tree privet and wattle, had been planted kawakawa and hangehange. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,60/56</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Variegated coprosmas and pittosporums, with red, yellow and green flaxes beneath, covered the bank, and raupo grew in the shallow water at the lake edge. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,146</i>)

>  Pittosporums make very beautiful specimens, or as a group can be an excellent background as well as an excellent windbreak. <i>(Mar 1991,NZ Gardener,42</i>)

>  Another reason might be that the plants have coloured foliage such as cultivars of flax, Pittosporum, Coprosma and Hebe. <i>(Mar 25, 1993,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  The one-year-old pittosporum came oneevening with the Manawatu EveningStandard. <i>(Jun 1992,North & South,65</i>)

### Pittosporum dallii
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  In 1973 the Hikutai Sanctuary was set aside to protect <i>Pittosporum dallii</i>, a small tree which grows only in the headwaters of the Cobb, Snow and Slate Valleys. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,62</i>)

### Pittosporum patulum
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a rare shrub

>  Enticing green pools can be seen through the trees and Patulums Pool, named after the rare shrub <i>Pittosporum patulum</i> found round these parts... <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,95</i>)

### pitty
 
 <i>n.</i> pit-bull terrier

>  "Maybe because I've got a really good American pitty I don't think it's fair." <i>(Apr 16, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### piu-piu
 
 <i>n.</i> presumably a (mis)spelling of piopio, the native thrush

>  And there are other birds of more silent voice, but beautiful to behold - the russet-feathered saddle-back, the piu-piu, with plumage rivaling in delicate colouring the subdued tints of the dove. <i>(Nov 19, 1900,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### piupiu
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern, crown fern

>  Heralding spring, a cluster of young fronds unfurls in the centre of a piupiu or crown fern, <i>Blechnum discolor</i>. Piupiu which often forms extensive colonies on the forest floor, is one of the more ubiquitous species in this land of ferns. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/82</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Piupiu, species of fern grows very ornamental. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,110,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Piu-piu, <i>Col[enso]. Polypodium pennigerum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>BLECHNUM </i>... or <i>LOMARIA ... discol[or]</i> ... Bird's Nest Fern. PIU PIU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,11</i>)

>  <i>piupiu [=] kiokio</i> [s] [= <i>Blechnum capense</i>] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cyclosorus penniger (Forst.f.) Copel.</i> Common name: <i>Gully fern</i> Maori name: <i>Piupiu </i>... This common fern is found in woods throughout New Zealand. It has been placed in <i>Polypodium, Goniopteris, Aspidium, Nephrodium, Dryopteris</i>, and <i>Thelypteris.</i> [Williams identifies as <i>Blechnum</i> spp.] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,28</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Blechnum discolor</i> CROWN FERN ...Piupiu ... Crowns of numerous, stiffly upright fronds up to a metre long, ... Crown fern dominates wide stretches of forest floor ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,460</i>)

>  Piupiu. Polypodium pennigerum, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/53</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  [O]f the many different ways of making such couches perhaps the most comfortable and driest is the plan of laying small saplings side by side, with the ends raised on cross pieces a few inches from the ground, and strewing such a platform with quantities of the fronds of piupiu - a peculiarly dry fern. <i>(Jun 17, 1878,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  I visited Day's Bay for the first time last week; and, within half a mile of the sea, and below 150 feet above sea-level, I saw, without leaving the footpath, the following trees, shrubs, etc .. Koromiko, (several species), kohekohe .. korokio (water fern), piupiu (basket fern) ... <i>(Aug 29, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in traditional Maori dress, a skirt made of a fringe of dried flax leaves attached to a waistband. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Her dress consists of a beautiful feather robe, made of pidgeon and kaka feathers in alternate squares, and a piupiu, or petticoat, of curled flax leaves in black and yellow. <i>(Aug 31, 1893,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> Piupius swinging, making their own music, pois bobbing. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,205</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> swinging plastic pois / in a piupiu from Woolworths <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,260</i>)

>  Traditionally, flax fibres were soaked in tannin made from hinau bark and ironrich mud or paru, producing the black used in designs in piu piu skirts and other woven work ... <i>(Nov 30, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  When he went to live and work in Australia he intended to hang up his piupiu and take a break. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 18</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. something hidden away.-v.t. to hide. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Who so cunning and skilful in the weaving of piu piu, korowai, fancy mats, fancy plaits, and household play as Te Aatarangi? <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,90</i>)

>  PIUPIU. - A Maori garment extending from waist to knee, from which the kilt was copied. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> In appearance [Hapanaia] was tall and lean; he as stripped except for a short <i>piupiu</i> of flax around his waist, and was armed with a gun. We went into battle ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/24,1983 ed.</i>)

>  There moved the young women in <i>piupiu</i> whose pipe-strings, dyed yellow and black, and hanging from the waist, showed well under their cloaks as they walked, and rattled softly [31] to the movement of their limbs. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,30</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> For every Maori who could provide himself with a ... feathered headband, a <i>piupiu</i> (a reed kirtle) ... betook himself ... to ... Waitangi. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,18,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,30, 67</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> She would have been a beauty, dressed in the old fashion, in the <i>piupiu</i> skirt, with her hair flowing free under a narrow <i>taniko</i> head band. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,116</i>)

>  <i>piupiu n</i>, pl <i>piupiu</i> or <i>piupius</i> [Maori]: a short kilt made usu. of strips of flax and worn by Maoris for native dances and on ceremonial occasions <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1726</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> SOME MAORI WORDS ... <i>piupiu</i> flax skirt <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PIOPIO [sic, spg error] Flax kilt. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> They have taken the piupiu from the body of the land <i>(1979,Collected Poems,449,1969 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> <i>(1969,Awatea,19,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> The building itself was unusual in that all the figures in the meetinghouse had carved skirts, or <i>piu piu</i> . <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,108</i>)

>  [caption] Maori child wearing a piupiu, typical costume worn nowadays by Maori concert parties. <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/74</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> piupiu: traditional skirt made of rolled strips of flax, now worn only by performers on ceremonial occasions or at concerts. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... Wi left his bed and ... went to the wardrobe and took down his piupiu from the hook. <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,106</i>)

>  <i>piupiu n</i>. a skirt made from leaves of the New Zealand flax, worn by Maoris on ceremonial occasions. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,864</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 572; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 368]]

>  Prime examples [of surviving traditional technology] are the techniques of building and decorating meeting houses and of making piupiu (dance skirts). <i>(1984,Te Maori,30</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `Just as well I didn't make you fellas get into maro and piupiu,' I joke <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,92</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Maori action songs at Takitimu Hall. ... The swishing and crackling of the piupius. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,107</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> In one of the books there were pictures of Maori on the beaches getting shellfish. They were old, with piupiu and cloaks, waka in the water, nets in the sea. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,20</i>) [NOTES: (Lindsay Charman-Love)]

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> So we draped feather cloaks over our shoulders, and the women tied piupiu around their waists. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,139</i>) [NOTES: (Paula Morris)]

>  In the dark, the rustle of dried flax piupiu as the student performers enter seems like a nervous whisper, a distinctive sound you will hear only in Aotearoa. <i>(Jun 29, 2004,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  The high class women ... stayed at home and made mats - piupiu from the fibre of flax; pokeka, rain mats from dried flax; ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,252,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> When clad in a shoulder mat, Native fashion, or when stripped to the waist with the haft of a tomahawk in his flax-cord belt, which supported his kilt-like <i>piupiu</i> ... just out of the nest, running along in a curious human way all by himself. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,37</i>)

>  <i>piupiu</i>, a garment consisting of a heavy fringe, about nine inches wide or more, made of flax and craped at intervals and dyed black. The flax when drying curling up into pipes. This fringe hung down the bare thighs, and as the wearer moved the gentle click-click of the hard tubes made a sound that was liked as to the frou-frou of a silk dress. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  Although you expect kilts and bagpipes in Scotland, the way some tourists expect piupiu here, only a decade ago they were limited to paunchy old men in baggy Arran jumpers. <i>(Aug 20, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  The Scotsman may find serious cause for offence in the association of the kilt with the piupiu. <i>(Nov 2, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)

>  Piu-piu waist-mats rustled. <i>(Oct 12, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,94</i>)

>  Tame Iti should take his taiaha and piupiu into the Kaimanawas and stay there. <i>(May 16, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C11</i>)

>  <b>2</b> Other items [in Mr. F. P. Corkhill's Maori collection are] Teipu, carved top or funnel for calabash, two whalebone meres .. and clothing, comprising flax, piupiu, huna, and koka ... <i>(Aug 31, 1887,Taranaki Herald</i>)

>  Captain Mair exhibits in his collection a piupiu or waist mat, a kuiri or stringed mat .. and a maronui or married woman's girdle. <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

### piwaiwaka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> RHIPIDURA FLABILLIFERA. (PIED -FANTAIL.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Piwaiwaka, Tiwai-waka, Piwakawaka, {Tirairaka, Pirairaka} and Tiwakawaka {and Pitakataka}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,143,1888 2nd edn 1/69 ed.</i>)

### piwakawaka
 
 <i>n.</i> fantail; <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, and <i>R.fuliginosa</i>, =(esp.?pied) fantail

>  The charming little piwakawaka (fantail) follows us everywhere, flitting around our heads, as is the custom of this beautiful fly-catcher. <i>(Aug 15, 1900,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  Flying halfway round the globe to look after a little bird called piwakawaka or fantail is not something everyone would consider ... <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,40</i>)

>  A rustle, then I see a piwakawaka (fantail) fluttering close to the ground, while above it a tieke works the trunk of a treefern. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/23</i>)

>  Maori have a number of names for the fantail, including hiwaiwaka, tirairaka, tiwakawaka, and titakataka, most of which mean something like "flitting about". <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/76</i>)

>  The small piwakawaka (fantail) sings most cheerfully at the head of the spring. <i>(Oct 29, 1947,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> There is another small bird called a Pea Walker [piwakawaka]. <i>(1834,New Zealand (Heaphy),41</i>) [NOTES: 1963 edition]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the birds that distinguished themselves in this woodland concert, were the tui, or mocking-bird, the korimaku [a-acute], kohapiroa, and tiaki [a-acute]. The little restless piwakawaka [a2,a4-acute], a kind of thrush, who is incessantly hopping from twig to twig, put forth its single note. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/298 The <i>Piwaka-waka</i> [a2's-acute] is the Musicapa flabellifera, or fan-tailed fly-catcher. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/108</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... when we stopped to notice the symbolical carvings on a large tomb, at the [124] moment a small bird called <i>Piwakawaka</i> [as-acute] struck up its vocal strain, which sounded as peculiarly plaintive in the deserted place. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/123</i>)

>  <i>Pi-wa&#769;.ka-wa&#769;.ka</i> (pe.-wa.&#769;.k&adot.-wa.&#769;.k&adot.; <i>colloq.</i> p&ibreve.-w&abreve.k&#769;.&adot.-w&abreve.k&#769;.&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Zoo.l.) </i>The New Zealand fantail. See FANTAIL, in the Dictionary. <i>[Local, N. Z.]</i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>piwakawaka</i>: fantail. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  ACANTHISITTA CHLORIS, Sparrm. Pi wau wau. Wren. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/57</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> XENICUS LONGPIPES. (BUSH-WREN.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Matukituki, Piwauwau, {Puano,} and Huru-pounamu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,115,1888 2nd edn 1/108 ed.</i>)

>  17. <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>, Sparrm. Wren, Titipounamu, Pi-wau-wau. - Often builds in a small hole in a tree. I know one old konini which is resorted to yearly ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,282</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>pi[i]wakawaka</i>, a bird (Rhipidura flabellifera), pied fantail: the so-called black fantail, which is chocolate-colour (R. fuliginosa) is ti[i]wakawaka. The former is common throughout Newzealand; the latter is common in the South Island but rather rare in the North and is thought to be a mutant of the former. Both names were applied to both birds by the Maori, but for distinction pi[i]wakawaka for the pied and ti[i]wakawaka for the black have been adopted by the Pakeha. The bird is noted for the disaster it caused to Maui in his struggle with the mother of night, Hine-nui-te-po, Death personified. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A Stillness, a fine sky over head! no Noise! now and then a Fish Will leap or King fisher dart down and a beautiful little bird called a `colly Mocko' who flutters about a Flower more like a Butter fly with all his feathers spread so that he looks large ... There is another small bird called a Pea Walker [<i>piwakawaka</i>]. [ed fn. suggesting Markham is describing at first the piwakawaka, fantail, and not korimako or bellbird.] <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Piwakawaka</i>, or <i>Tirakaraka</i> - This restless little bird is continually on the wing, or hopping from twig to twig. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),57</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Puakuwaka [Piwakawaka] is the most striking contrast to the Common Hawk, being one of one of the smallest birds; it is a species of the waterwagtail. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,114,1956 ed.</i>)

>  Rhipidura flabellifera. Piwaka-waka. Fantail. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

OTHER NAMES: <i>Piwakawaka, pied fantail, black fantail. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,280</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 21. <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>. - ... Diggowagh. Wagh of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst</i>. Piwaka-waka [a2 and 4 acutes] of the natives. <i>Polack, Dieffenbach.</i> Pi-oua-ka-oua-ka of the natives. <i>Less. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/190</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/190]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> In the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. I shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the neat fantail flycatcher [fn Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. Piwakawaka.], and a social bird of a yellow colour, which has been depicted by Forster [fn Orthornyx heteroclytus, Lef. Popokatea.], and which had much the habits of the finch. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the wren

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 11. <i>Acanthisitta tenuirostris. Lafr.</i> ... Piwauwau of the natives, a bird confined to the upper regions of the hills. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/188</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/188]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... a little bird, which is peculiar to these heights [of Mt. Egmont], busied itself in our neighbourhood; ... of a dark green plumage. It is the Acanthisitta tenuirostris of our Index, and called piwauwau by the natives. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/154</i>)

### pizzle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The condition in these contracts as regards New Zealand mutton is that carcases must not weigh less than 50lb, and that the pizzles of wethers and udders of ewes must not be taken away. <i>(Jul 8, 1903,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  The pizzle disease exists, but to no great extent ... <i>(1899,Department of Agriculture Report,15</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>GORDON</b> <i>Tally</i>: You <i>cut tally</i> if you get 100 per man, and you drop so many off the <i>tally</i> for <i>stags</i> (one-ball sheep) and rams (the penises and balls of both these are cut out, and this is how one counts them; <i>pizzles</i> (undeveloped penises) are cut off wethers. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The two worst cuts of all are ewes' teats cut or cut off, and wethers' pizzles cut or cut off. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>)

>  [I]n case [the shearer] cut the teat of any ewe or pizzle of any wether or ram, he shall immediately report the same to person in charge of the shed. <i>(Jun 4, 1891,Otago Witness,22</i>)

### pizzle jumper
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rough shearer who does not clean round a sheep's pizzle.

>  <b>SCURR</b> Pizzle jumper:- shearer who doesn't clean round pizzle <i>(2000,Rabbiters' Glossary,3</i>)

### pizzle rot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a disease of rams and wethers in the summer and autumn, affecting the sheath.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Pizzle rot, sheath rot or balanitis. A disease of rams and wethers, of unknown origin, which has also been known to affect male lambs a few weeks old. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,124</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Some years ago an observant farmer chanced on a cure for a distressing complaint in wethers known as 'pizzle-rot.' <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,110</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> The most common condition affecting the penis and prepuce of rams is balano-posthitis or pizzle rot .. it may be necessary to carefully restrict the diet of male animals to produce an acid urine which will automatically clear minor external lesions of pizzle rot. <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,33</i>)

>  What attracts flies? Foot rot, pizzle rot, fleece rot, mycotic dermatitis (lumpy wool.) <i>(Feb 28, 2002,Fencepost.com,Expert farmer Livestock.</i>)

### pizzle stain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an unscourable urine stain in fleece wool.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... I was allowed to miss school to help in the shed by picking up the bellywool and removing the pizzle stains from the wether bellywool. I was also `tar-boy', which meant putting dabs of Stockholm tar on any cuts inflicted by the shearers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

>  The sheep may not have been crutched previously, then dung and pizzle stain will be removed. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> Inferior lambs bodywool : remove pizzle stains and daggy wool. <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,108</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Pizzle stain: Unscourable urine staining in wether and ram bellies, and ewe crutchings. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,57</i>)

### pizzle-ring
 
 <i>v.</i> [NB No definition is given but it seems to mean shielding (probably by hand) the pizzle during crutching so it is not cut "Extra care needs to be taken to be sure of not cutting the pizzle."]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> No wether should be crutched without being pizzle-ringed. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,62</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear wool from around the pizzle of a male sheep.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> No wether should be crutched without being pizzle-ringed. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,62</i>)

### pizzleguard
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i> pizzleguard</i>: An imaginary piece of equipment called for when shearing rams or wethers. Good for a joke with a green shedhand. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### PJs
 
 <i>abbr.</i> pyjamas [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  To cut a long story short, Jeannie had left her PJs to soak with a pair of stocking and the dye had run. <i>(May 17, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  I remember watching Diana get married - everyone crowded into the TV room, in their nighties and pjs, glued to the TV. <i>(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 43</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> Also besides `Jarmies' childish for `pajamas' [Macquarie] could have two common NZ expressions `PJ's' and `pyjams'. <i>(Jul 1984,Letter</i>) [NOTES: >1984 July Letter Bill Griffiths (Pakuranga College)]

>  ... he rushed outside in pjs and lucky no page 3 blonde was there to see him haka???. <i>(1986,New Outlook,21/50</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Macrae (The Kingsland Kid) in New Outlook 21/50]

### place
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [conversational] a farm or station.

>  J.A.McColl got his early training on the run, and became one of the best horsemen on a place noted for high-class cross-country horses and good hacks. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,15</i>)

>  The wind was so strong it blew everything off the place except the mortgage; best cow on the place, the place was in need of a good manager, he knew every inch of the place. <i>(1999,Personal Communication</i>)

### placer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  First, quartz reefs and mineral lodes. Second, placer-mining, as in California, and surface workings, as in many parts of Victoria and New South Wales. <i>(Nov 29, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  As far as Nelson is concerned, ... it appears that the metal is found ... in placer or detrital formations. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/451</i>)

>  It must be remembered that gold is now chiefly obtained by quartz-mining. The placer-claim is no longer the chief source of the gold-supply, and consequently the supply is no longer subject to the vicissitudes invariably connected with alluvial-gold mining. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/129</i>)

>  [T]he alluvial miner .. is one who works the deeper deposits, or deep placer mines, as they have been denominated. <i>(Jun 12, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  A convenient distinction is drawn between the alluvial digger and the alluvial miner, the former term being applied to the man who extracts gold from the surface and from the more recent deposits only; the latter to the worker of deep placers. <i>(Jul 10, 1869,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  The prospectors state that the placer field embraces a tract of gravel about 225,000 acres in extent. <i>(Sep 22, 1900,Evening Post,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that does not graze with other animals, but is attached to a particular place, as in a hermit sheep [mainly North Is.] (See [hermit](hea-hom.md#hermit))

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Rock, log, nettle-clump, bush, or tree-stump may, as chance determines, become the foster-parent of the 'placer'... <i>(1921,Tutira,383</i>)

>  Placer - when a ewe dies, her lamb usually stays by her body .. he sometimes transfers his filial affection to some object close to where his mother died, perhaps a bush or large stone. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Lambs which lose their mothers when they are young sometimes form an attachment for an inanimate object like a bush or rock and become what are known as 'hermits', sometimes known as 'placer sheep.' <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,110</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> placer: a lamb whose mother has died and who has adopted a stone, bush or other object as a mother substitute. Australians also use the word for a lamb or sheep that stays in one place. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,81</i>)

### placing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### plaice
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> sand-flounder

>  (Reprinted from the <i>Southern News</i>) [The fish of Stewart's Island] comprise black cod, grouper, rock cod, trumpeter, barracouta, perch, hug, congie [sic] eel, whiting, white fish, bastard trumpeter, skate, and plaice. <i>(May 31, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>ROSS</b> ... the soles, though small, are very good, and the plaice of large size are equal in flavour to the Dutch fish : the Barracouta is caught in proper season, ... <i>(1847,Voyage of Discovery to North Pacific Ocean,2/117</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Fish of all sorts and sizes abound on every part of the sea-coast. The only sorts similar to ours are the <i>Conger-eel, Sole, Plaice</i>, and <i>Flounder</i> <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,160</i>)

>  [Flatfish submitted for opinion included] (1.) The flounder, or patiki, wrongly named `plaice,' about one-tenth adult size. (2) The turbot sole, wrongly [559] named `brill,' about one-fortieth its adult size: ... (3.) The lemon sole, wrongly named `flounder,' ... (4.) The New Zealand sole ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/558</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND SAND-FLOUNDER (PATIKI) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (Richardson)... In and around Nelson the Sand-flounder is known as Plaice. It is not unlike this fish in shape, but the English Plaice is of a deep brown colour with orange-red spots on the upper surface. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,191</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>FLOUNDER</i> (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i)<i> Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (ii) <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> (iii) <i>Rhombosolea tapirina</i> (iv) <i>Rhombosolea leporina.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] plaice 2284, 2301, 2306 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  The patiki, an excellent fish, which is something like a plaice, comes in about the middle of October. These are taken with spears in great numbers on the sand banks. <i>(May 8, 1844,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  The part of New Zealand to which I belong is the Province of Canterbury; the harbour is six or seven miles long. In the neighbouring rivers and rocks are plaice and cod, very similar to ours. <i>(Jan 1, 1859,Otago Witness,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] plaice, diamond 2289 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Diamond Plaice <i>Rhombosolea (Adamasoma) retiaria adamas</i> Whitley and Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,405</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 405]

### plain
 
 <i>adj.</i> plain-eyed



 
 <i>adj.</i> (of ice cream) vanilla-flavoured

>  In my teens when I began reading cookbooks, I was hit with the realisation that what we had thought was plain ice cream was actually vanilla. Vanilla wasn't plain, it was a flavour. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,F5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [AND has articles at PLAIN, as a spec. use 1a. an extensive tract of land which is open and generally suitable for pasture, frequently undulating and lightly treed] -->of various localities, but perhaps chiefly of the Canterbury Plains (esp. when used in quotation marks) [see quot. Bush-clearing]

>  A Christchurch paper remarks: "As a plain matter of demonstrable fact, there is more potential wealth buried away in the great stretch of country between Auckland and Doubtless Bay than there is in any similar stretch of country in any other part of the Dominion, not excepting the fruitful plains of Canterbury and Otago." <i>(Jan 20, 1915,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  One of the most favourable localities .... was not far from the gorge of one our Southern rivers, where the monotonous flatness of `the plains' gives way to a more broken and undulating surface. <i>(1869,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: T.H. Potts]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Although the Pilgrims could cross the hills to the Plain over the Bridle Path - and could carry their lighter belongings; all heavy luggage had to go to Christchurch by sea and then up the Avon River. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,26</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> (The Shagroon's Lament) All huddled in vain are the sheep of the Plain,/ <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,18</i>)

>  Ashburton. There was a sharp thunderstorm on the plains yesterday, and a horse was struck dead by lightning at Valetta. <i>(Sep 1, 1892,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  [I]t appears that beyond the mountains in which the Hutt has its source, there is an extensive champaign [sic] country which stretches away to the east coast .. Nearly the whole of this plain belongs to the New Zealand Land Company. <i>(May 23, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  In ascending Mount Egmont I walked during ten days through an almost level country, before I came to the foot of the mountain, which is a cone rising from the plain. <i>(Aug 1, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  The Ngatiruanui portion of the released prisoners returned to the plains yesterday. On their way they are reported to have destroyed part of a ditch and bank fence which intersected one of their old dray tracks on the Plains. <i>(Jul 6, 1881,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

### plain as a pikelet
 
 <i>phr.</i> very plain (of a girl) (? confused with plain as a pikestaff, in sense, `clear'; pikelet a variant of pikestaff, or a solecism?) [ca1981 Feb 25 F. McKay.]

>  [heading of political commentary] <i>Plain as a pikelet </i>It's candidate selection time again, and all over the country aspiring MPs are jockeying for position in a sort of political beauty contest. <i>(Dec 18, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### plain country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The lower hills, downs, and better kinds of plain country have been widely cultivated, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,25</i>)

### plain-bodied
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  His ram was 18 months, bred by Hardie, of Napier, was a magnificently-woolled sheep, plain-headed and plain-bodied, and the judges could not overlook its wool and condition, although Bell and Son's entry was better figured. <i>(Nov 19, 1896,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> However, with the modern trend of breeding, tending to eliminate excessive skin folds and wrinkles, there are many nice-cutting reasonably plain-bodied Merinos that shear well. <i>Ibid.</i> 132 The average Corriedale is ... plain-bodied apart from an odd neck wrinkle or dew-lap, ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,130</i>)

>  The largest class in the show was composed of merinos of Vermont blood - that is, for unwrinkled sheep, or plain-bodied, the condition being that there should be no wrinkles behind the elbow. <i>(Sep 14, 1904,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  His choice, however, was considerably limited by the fact that the Tasmanian merinos are now mostly bred with heavy folds or wrinkles on thin skins, whereas Mr. Hugonin required plain-bodied sheep. <i>(Apr 4, 1906,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  The halfbreds which command the highest prices year after year .. are bred from merino ewes of what is popularly known as the "Murray" type - plain-bodied sheep ... <i>(Jul 31, 1907,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### plain-eyed
 
 <i>adj.</i> of a dog

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Plain-eyed</i>:Most of our heading dogs show what is known as `eye', i.e. when working a few sheep they `set' them much as does a setter or pointing dog setting game. Those that do not have this trait are called plain-eyed. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,188</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> Glen was a plain-eyed heading dog. That is, he did not try to mesmerise the sheep by staring them in the eyes till their wills broke and they would turn and run. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,118</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He was plain-eyed and barked when pulling, but was an outstanding hill dog. ... He used to `clap' and would lie there barking. <i>Ibid.</i> 168 (Glossary) A very strong-eyed dog ... works sheep in a manner similar to a cat stalking a bird. A plain-eyed dog is one which does not have this characteristic. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> My dog, Boss, was also a plain-eyed dog and he too was one of those born hill dogs. <i>(1996,Boss's Story, The,141</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheepdogs] that do not 'set' sheep or fix them with a hypnotic stare.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Jack McIver had his Bruce, a big heavy plain-eyed dog that he's picked up down on the mudflats. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,84</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Bob had a very fine, smooth-haired, black, tan and white plain-eyed heading dog called Spike. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,22</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Lady was a safe header but, like most plain-eyed dogs, a poor puller. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,68</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Many of these so-called colonial dogs were what's known as plain-eyed and could be best described as gathering or stopping dogs ... <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,62</i>)

### plain-working
 
 <i>adj.</i> plain-eyed

>  <b>STRONACH</b> At Molesworth in those days most of our heading dogs were the `plain-working' kind. They did not `eye' the sheep or exercise any mesmerism, as is done by the present-day trial dog. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,43</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Plain Working Dog</i>: Heading dog with no `eye'. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,124</i>)

### plains
 
 <i>n.</i> also the plains in quotes

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was some time before the terrified women could be induced to get into the coach again; and it was only by Jim's asseverations ... that if they were not `all aboard' in half a minute, he would drive on and leave them in the middle of the plains, that they were persuaded to clamber in ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,144,1870 ed.</i>)

>  [One] was not far from the gorge of one of our great southern rivers, where the monotonous flatness of `the plains' gives way to a more broken ... surface ... <i>Ibid.</i> 49 Thousands of sheep now depasture on that well-remembered corner of `the plains,' on those gently-swelling downs; ... we have the bleating of flocks, the bark of the colley[sic] as it <i>rounds up</i> its charge, the loud crack of the stockwhip, the hearty curse of the bullock driver delivered `ore rotundo'; ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  For fences were not, ...and kennelled boundary-dogs strung off the invisible line that severed the runs throughout the plains. <i>(1987,Happy Endings,105</i>) [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in 1987 Happy Endings 105]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (w. def. art.) the Canterbury plains (or Plains) (DNZE s.v. plain, sense a.)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... its large size, more pointed wings, and dark colour distinguished it at once from the New Zealand Harrier (<i>Circus Gouldii</i>), so common on our plains, but which I never observed in the alpine regions. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,135</i>)

>  61. <i>Charadrius obscurus</i>, Gml. Dusky, Red-breasted, or Mountain Plover, Big Dottrel [si], Tuturiwatu, Paturiwhata, Tituriwhatu-pukunui. - This excellent gamebird formerly bred on the Canterbury Plains ...; since farms and settlements have occupied the waste lands of the plains, for the most part it has retired to the mountains of the back country. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,506</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  67. <i>Haematopus unicolor</i>, Forst. Black Oyster-catcher, Black Red-bill, Torea. - Breeds out on `the plains' in riverbeds ... <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,511</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  The merino occupies and thrives on the wild lands of the colony, from the snow-line to the border of the plains, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,92</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Some of them remained in the emigrant barracks at Port Cooper for a few days; but almost immediately the greater number started to walk over a recently made Bridle Path to the plains beyond. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,120</i>)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> `When daylight broke I found we were on the descent from the mountain country to the plains. We were passing through a fine pastoral country, with grassy valleys and hills decreasing in height.' <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,109</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Wallie was rather lethargic socially, but did enjoy the jaunts around the agricultural areas, situated near the environs of the city of `The Plains'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,152</i>)

>  People were cooheeing all over the plains last night, they lost their ways. <i>(Aug 7, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,52,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Anyone acquainted with our `plains' must have observed ... how certain parts (termed by the geologists `fans') are thickly covered with stones, as ... some miles below the gorges of the Rakaia and Rangitata. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,218</i>)

>  <i>(1870,TrNZI,3/93 or 1869 2/68</i>) [NOTES: T H Potts]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The sharp prickles of the small shrub `Tumatakuru' ... which is very common on the plains ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,210,1851 ed.</i>)

>  I walked with Cass over the plains fixing on trigonometrical stations, at Courtenay, ... &c. <i>(Sep 3, 1849,Torlesse Papers,102,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The plains are certainly, at first sight, monotonous, but they improve as you know them. <i>(Aug 1851,New Zealand (various authors/titles),272</i>)

>  [Advt] GENTLEMEN and FAMILIES visiting the Plains will find every accommodation at the above Establishment. <i>(1854,Canterbury Almanac, The,104 ff</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The Plains - extending from the river Waipara to Timaru .. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),195</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The greatest drawback of the [Canterbury] Plains is the want of shelter in the strong gales, `Southerly Bursters', which occasionally occur. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/372</i>)

>  ... the folks on the plains/ Turned ... uncommonly brown,/ In all the Nor'-westers and easterly rains,/ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,79</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Apr in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 79]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (non-Canterbury reference, DNZE s.v. plain, sense b.)

>  Cook sums up his account by saying that the hills and mountains are covered with wood, and every valley has a rivulet of water; the soil in these valleys, and in the plains, of which there are many that are not overgrown with wood, is in general light but fertile; and, in the opinion of Mr Banks and Dr Solander, as well as of every other gentleman on board, every kind of European grain, plant, and fruit, would flourish here in the utmost luxuriance. <i>(Sep 6, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,7</i>)

>  Attention is being paid to the vast area of Dr. Peak's lands which are to be known as the Hauraki Plains. <i>(Apr 28, 1906,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  A fire at Hauraki Plains did considerable damage to some unoccupied sections. <i>(Oct 27, 1913,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... we bid farewell to the Mania or plains and entered the wood about 6 ... [97] these plains [about Taupo] or Mania agree with the prairie and pampas of America Mar 18 We passed through several small Manias today ... <i>(Mar 17, 1845,Journal,3/96</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Travellers talk of the solitude of the forests, but there is society in trees which men miss on immense plains; it is on the prairie alone that the solitary traveller has a sensation of loneliness ... On the coast plains in the North Island ferns and flax plants supply the place of grasses. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/17</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Never was he happier than ... when, as at mustering [57] seasons, madly galloping over the plains, he chased the wild cattle, waking the echoes with the crack of the long stockman's whip, which none could more dexterously [sic] flourish than he. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,2/iii/56-57,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>WISNIOWSKI</b> We frequently emerged into open spaces, almost completely bare of trees, known locally as `plains', altogether unlike our European meadows. <i>(1877,Tikera,50,(trans. Podstolski 1972) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The river [Thames] winds through what are called plains here, but the term is only relatively applied. The `plains' are broken with spurs and undulations from the higher ranges that bound them, and the country is anything but one uniform level. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/283</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There were innumerable lagoons on the plains in those days [c1860s] - mostly storm water, and not deep. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

### plains station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pastoral holding on down or plains country.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> On some of the plains stations 'crow's nests' were built - platforms on poles or up cabbage trees. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,19</i>)

### plait
 
 <i>v.</i> to speed (away)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> `As soon as I put the yellow dog after him he took off. Gawd, didn't he plait! The yellow dog never blew wind in his arse!'. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,199</i>)

### plank it
 
 <i>v.</i> to sleep on planks. (? nonce after rough it, etc)

>  <b>DONALDSON</b> Through the day we will rough it, at night we will plank it. We know feather beds are scarce things in the bush. <i>(1860,Bush Lays and Rhymes,41</i>)

### plank road
 
 <i>n.</i> corduroy road

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> The formation of the road surface, at this place, consisted of tree trunks fastened together and laid across the road going over the swamp. It was known as a `plank' or `corduroy' road. ... The other passengers were shaken off while going over the `corduroy' section of the road <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,19</i>)

### plank tent
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GODLEY</b> You would laugh if you saw the place from which I am writing. It is a little `plank tent' or `V. hut', as a thing made of long boards leaning against each other is indiscriminately called; its locality is the side of a small wood, by which runs a clear stream, on the plain, about ten miles from Lyttelton. <i>(Feb 27, 1857,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Feb 27 Godley letter in 1950 Carrington Godley 125]

### planned economy
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The Second World War, as in many other countries, encouraged what was already the chief tendency of the Labour Government, and speeded the progress towards a `planned economy' via `price control' and `stabilization'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,267</i>)

### Planning Council
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Something called the Planning Council - yes, Virginia, they had planning then - called for an end to import licensing, then hastily had to retract this radical view, lest it offend the government. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### planning judge
 
 <i>n.</i> title of judge sitting in the (now) Environment Court

### Planning Tribunal
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a court whose principal function is to hear appeals from planning (since 1991 resource consent) decisions. [Jim Cameron writes : Historical 1953-1996. Renamed Environment Court in 1996.]

>  Resource and environment law has contributed a substantial number of terms to NZE. .Some of these have not found their way into dictionaries, for instance Planning Tribunal, now the Environment Court. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### planny
 
 <i>n.</i> (pine) plantation [Frame passim]

>  <b>FRAME</b> .. and the pine plantations, to be known as the `plannies";... <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,56</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,33</i>)

### plant
 
 <i>v.</i> (often surreptitiously); often used as an adverbial ptc in same way ashidden

>  A young man .. has confessed to stealing from the sluice-boxes about 50oz of gold, which had been planted. <i>(Sep 9, 1903,Otago Witness,29</i>)

>  GBP200 worth of jewellery, stolen from the Wellington, has been found planted in Freeman's Bay. <i>(Nov 24, 1882,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The new-born lamb will follow its mother, and does not plant. ... Even when well grown and following in the flock the kid is liable to `plant' or bolt away for hiding when the flock is mustered. <i>(1900,TrNZI,32/283</i>)

>  If compelled to give up the guns I shall `plant' the locks. <i>(1846,McLean Papers,3/86</i>) [NOTES: (W.Mantell)]

>  I had always carried the gold about with me; but on the day I left it planted in my stretcher. <i>(Apr 2, 1864,All the Year Round</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 2 All the Year Round in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 18]

>  <b>MONEY</b> Here we began to look out for signs of stores ... that we should find `planted' in the bush out of reach of rats. [113] It appeared that a party had been sent up with the stores to plant them in the bush, and to blaze a track some distance up ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,112</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Some may wonder we did not dig a hole and bury all our gear in preference to `planting' them forty feet from the earth in the fork of a big tree. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,266</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> It was ... [167] on a regatta day that I became acquainted with a singular colonial institution known by the name of `planting.' ... There were plants (of the class alcoholic) in all direction that day, from the humble beer to the haughty three star branding plant. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,166</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> common in Australia for <i>to hide</i>, and for the thing hidden away. As remarked in the quotations, the word is thieves' English. [1827] <i>(1898,Austral English,359</i>)

>  The main crime [of the University students] was entering the girls' quarters with no other ulterior motive than to `plant' their academic gowns. <i>(Sep 2, 1905,NZ Truth,11/1/8</i>)

>  They either mix the stolen [sheep] with their own flock, or travel at night, or `plant' them. <i>(Oct 21, 1905,NZ Truth,18/1/8</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I carried it home and tied it in the cow-yard, in the hope that if I planted [Hawera Star edition: `planked': possibly the other meaning of `plant oneself down' (to settle firmly) is meant] myself out of sight the heifer would eventually pluck up enough resolution to pay it a visit. <i>(1924,Me and Gus,8,1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm ed.</i>)

>  <i>Plant</i>.-To hide (trans. and intrans.) <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: [Acland]]

>  Waters and Elliot, notorious in connection with the still planting case, have been convicted and sentenced. <i>(Sep 6, 1890,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a trove, a hidden hoard; something concealed surreptitiously

>  Made a plant of some 60 yds. of rope near 22 trig pole. <i>(Aug 19, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,54,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> <i>(1863,Incidents of the Maori War,399</i>) [NOTES: [see FIND n]]

>  Well, now, I guess the plant ain't sprung; though for the matter of that I'd ante up every ounce of gold to have saved poor Harry, I would, Sir. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/viii/99,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> The inscription had no relation to the `plant' near the beach. <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,53</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> It was on our first regatta day I became acquainted with a singular colonial institution known by the name of `planting' .... A gentleman ... whispered ... that he had a plant near ... He conmenced groping about in the undergrowth and produced a bottle containing ... brandy and water. <i>(1889,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,167</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> He guessed that ... every man about the place knew of the plant of money at the boss's cottage awaiting the arrival of the bush contractor. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,53,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HATHAWAY</b> The cook was constantly discovering some `plants' of food, hidden by those who had fled. <i>(1889,Reminiscences of Northern War,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> ... he therefore began by accusing Mr Rees of keeping the stores back, so that they would have to pay the boat fare at the Shotover ferry - several times for every pannikin of flour they got; and he said the whole thing was a got up plant in order to rob them and fill his own purse <i>(1888,Wakatipians,96</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shearing equipment

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Make sure, however, that the handpiece is in good order, as it is the most important part of all shearing plant and must be right. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,79</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of an animal) to hide or be hidden

>  We all looked for him [sc. a horse] ... but could not finde him. I supposed him to be planted in the bush as he is given to that. <i>(Apr 29, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,179,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I returned to the cattle again. Alan had found the eight [missing ones] planted two miles in the bush. When we got these, three more planted, and it was four p.m. when we reached Hokitika. <i>(May 8, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,182,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The new-born lamb will follow its mother, and does not `plant'. ... <i>Ibid.</i>] The kid is liable to `plant' or bolt away for hiding when the flock is mustered. <i>(1900,TrNZI,32/283</i>) [NOTES: (White)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to hide while grazing.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> plant, to: a cow or hind hides while she is grazing. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,62</i>)

### plant about
 
 <i>n.</i> a boys' game in which a ball is kicked about from one to another [?variant of punt about - see SOED (entered in Orsman under punt about)]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> Here [on the marae] the dogs fight and the Maori youth plays `plant about' with an old football. <i>(1909,Tragedy of Flirtation,41</i>)

### plant the boot
 
 <i>phr.</i> to accelerate

>  <i>to plant the boot:</i> to accelerate rapidly in a car <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>(Sep 10, 1949,Economist,566</i>) [NOTES: [OED2: attrib]]

### plant up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to plant an area.

>  If the area to be planted up is scrubby or has gorse or blackberry, this must be dealt with as soon as possible. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 56</i>)

### plant-caterpillar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MORRIS</b> name given in Australasia to species of caterpillars which are attacked by spores of certain fungi; when chrysalating in the earth the fungus frows inside the body of the caterpillar, kills the latter, and then forces its way out between the head joints, and sends an upgrowth which projects beyond the surface of the ground and gives rise to fresh spores. Many examples are known, of which the more common are <i>Cordyceps robertsii</i>, Hook., in New Zealand; <i>Cordyceps gunni<i>i, berk, in Tasmania; <i>Cordyceps taylori</i> Berk, in Australia.<i> See</i> Aweto. <i>(1898,Austral English,360</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> I remained in this forest till the 20th, finding many kauri grubs; while under the rata roots I also found the Aweta, or plant-caterpillar, which to change into a chrysalis creeps underground, and in many cases instead of becoming a moth becomes - a mushroom! <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,70,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COOKE</b> `The New Zealanders' name for this plant-caterpillar is `Hotete,' `Weri,' and `Anuhe'... The interior of the insect becomes completely filled by the inner plant, or thallus (mycelium): after which the growing head of the outer plant or fungus, passing to a state of maturity, usually forces its way out through the tissue of the joint between the head and the first segment of the thorax ... it is stated that this caterpillar settles head upward to undergo its change, when the vegetable developes [sic] itself.' <i>(1892,Vegetable Wasps and Plant Worms,139</i>)

### plantain
 
 <i>n.</i> various, mainly introduced, plants of <i>Plantago</i> species [rib grass, lamb's tongue, horse plantain, soldiers]

>  The operations of the gardener this month are very light, being chiefly in eradicating sow thistle, dock, plantain, and other weeds - collecting the flower seeds that have now ripened - cutting away withered stems, and staking the dahliahs and chrysanthemums. <i>(Feb 1, 1854,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Plantains, grass, thistles, and docks, are perhaps the most difficult to eradicate. <i>(Apr 13, 1878,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Plantain. <i>Plantago</i> various sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Plantain. Plantago lanigera, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/47</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Plantago major Plantain <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/126</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PLANTAIN (<i>Plantago lanceolata</i>) is commonly called rib grass, lamb's tongue, horse plantain, and soldiers. It is common in pastures and waste places throughout. It forms a rosette of lanceolate leaves about 6 inches long, with prominent or converging veins, giving the name `rib grass'. From the rosette rises the slender flower stalk a foot or more in length, with a crowded mass of tiny brown or black flowers at its tip. These heads are used by children for playing `soldiers,' and so in Scotland the weed is called `Carl Doddie"-that is `Charles and George"- a souvenir of 1745. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,161,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>plantain [=] broad-leaved plantain: narrow-leaved plantain: swamp plantain</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> PLANTAINS (<i>Plantago </i>spp.) ... This plant, native to Eurasia, is one of the most widespread weeds in New Zealand, common in lawns, playing fields, untended ground, pasture and in arable crops sown out of pasture, ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,88</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ratstail, birdseed, lamb's tongue, greater plantain

>  <i>broad-leaved plantain</i> [s] <i> Plantago major</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 59 <i> plantain, broad-leaved</i> [s] <i> Plantago major</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,8</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BROAD-LEAVED PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago major</i> ), also called ratstail, birdseed, lamb's tongue, and greater plantain, is common on roadsides and trodden places throughout the country and in pastures in the King Country. ... From the middle of the rosette ascends the flower stalk ..., which afterwards give[s] place to seeds, and produce[s] the ratstail appearance that gives the plant one of its trivial names. The long spikes of seed pods are often used for feeding caged birds. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,161,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> Plantago Raoullii </i> common New Zealand plantain <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> Plantago Raoulii</i> Raoul's plantain <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> plantain, Rugel's</i> [s] <i> Plantago rugelii </i> Decne. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> hoary plantain

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> HOARY PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago media</i> ), is also called lamb's tongue and swamp plantain; it is found in fields and waste places, but appears nowhere very common. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,163,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> plantain, swamp</i> [s] <i> Plantago hirtella</i> HBK. <i>Ibid.</i> 76 <i> swamp plantain</i> [s] <i> Plantago hirtella</i> HBK. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WATER PLANTAIN (<i> Alisma plantago</i> ), a pink or pale purple-flowered weed growing round the edges of ponds. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,22,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> plantain, water</i> [s] <i> Alisma plantago-aquatica </i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 84 <i> water plaintain</i> [s]<i> Alisma plantago-aquatica</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> buck's horn plantain

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BUCK'S HORN PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago coronopus</i> ), also called elk horn plantain, is found in sandy or gravelly places in scattered localities over both islands [usually in seaside marshes]. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,163,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>plantain, bracted</i> [s] <i> Plantago aristata </i> Michx. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> elk horn plantain

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BUCK'S HORN PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago coronopus</i> ), also called elk horn plantain, is found in sandy or gravelly places in scattered localities over both islands [usually in seaside marshes]. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,163,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> buck's-horn plantain</i> [s] <i> Plantago coronopus</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 59 <i> plantain, buck's-horn</i> [s] <i> Plantago coronopus</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,8</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> BUCKS HORN PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago coronopus</i> ) Small annual recognised by its divided leaves [like buck's horns]. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Plantago Hamiltonii

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i> Plantago Hamiltonii</i> glossy plantain <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> broad-leaved plantain

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BROAD-LEAVED PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago major</i> ), also called ratstail, birdseed, lamb's tongue, and greater plantain, is common on roadsides and trodden places throughout the country and in pastures in the King Country. ... From the middle of the rosette ascends the flower stalk ... , which afterwards give[s] place to seeds, and produce[s] the ratstail appearance that gives the plant one of its trivial names. The long spikes of seed pods are often used for feeding caged birds. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,161,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> plantain, greater [=] broad-leaved plantain</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,59</i>)

>  [I]nstead of unsightly riddled leaves and flowering stems bitten through, we have a vigorous clean growth, and even the broad-leaved plantain lily is left untouched. <i>(Nov 5, 1886,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  <i> hoary plaintain [=] swamp plantain</i> [s] <i>Ibid.</>59 <i> plantain, hoary [=] swamp plantain</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,38</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PLANTAIN (<i> Plantago lanceolata</i> ) is commonly called rib grass, lamb's tongue, horse plantain, and soldiers. It is common in pastures and waste places throughout. It forms a rosette of lanceolate leaves about 6 inches long, with prominent or converging veins, giving the name `rib grass'. From the rosette rises the slender flower stalk a foot or more in length, with a crowded mass of tiny brown or black flowers at its tip. These heads are used by children for playing `soldiers,' and so in Scotland the weed is called `Carl Doddie"-that is `Charles and George"- a souvenir of 1745. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,161,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>
