### plantation
 
 <i>n.</i> gen. English sense of area cultivated for planting of domestic food-crops, esp. by natives: a maori garden, esp. kumera

>  ... we ... went there ashore, & showed them [sc Natives] our Gardens & plantations [of vegetables, etc], & traded with them for some trifles; ... <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/292</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> In this place there were some very fat hogs and fine plantations of potatoes. <i>Ibid.</i> 126 On our way we saw some beautiful plantations of potatoes and other vegetables. <i>(1815,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,105,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Boys at the Karaka at their plantation. [sic] <i>(Sep 8, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,258,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> We left this potatoe plantation, and, after about two hours' walk, emerged from the wood, and again came on a lot of damp moorish ground, which lasted all the way to Pirata, the next settlement we reached. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,58,1952 ed.</i>)

>  ... we brought up for the night in an old deserted plantation, where we gained by digging, a few potatoes for supper. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/378</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Dec in 1895 TrNZI 27/378]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... not a house nor even a Maori plantation nor fishing-village ... <i>(1888,Fifty Years In Maoriland,32</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The bridle-track led past little bush-clearings with fenced-in Maori plantations; ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,169,1933 ed.</i>)

>  They have, within five miles of Kawia, large plantation grounds, and quantities of swine, of the value of which they are now perfectly cognizant. <i>(Aug 12, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a patch of planted, usu. exotic, trees, and esp. of pinus a pine plantation, a woodlot

>  A splendid monument to the prescience of those who, years ago, beheld in the unwooded state of the Ashburton County a menace to its future development, and in the prevailing winds a remorseless foe to its primary industries, is contained in the 4100 acres of pine, birch, larch, and bluegum plantations which are scattered at intervals over the Country to-day. <i>(Jan 21, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,5</i>)

>  About five miles west of Timaru, on the Downs, is Glengummell, the forest plantation of Messrs Sealy Bros., and Davidson. Its area contains 250 acres, and the timber is principally blue gum, mixed with red gum, stringy bark and silver wattle, with cuttings of willows and poplars along the gullies. <i>(Feb 16, 1875,Bruce Herald,6</i>)

>  ... gullies and plantations. <i>(1895,Diary of Jonathan Roberts,7</i>)

>  A plantation of pines and macrocarpa had already attained forward growth, and showed that this farm must have been one of the first in the locality to be cleared and improved. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,19</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> On Sunday mornings Pat, in the full glory of a clean blue shirt and corduroy trousers, took us for a walk in the great pine plantation. <i>(1899,Stories,7,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Here and there clumps of tall trees rise above the shrubs, and ... there is a thick plantation of red and blue gums, to shelter the garden ... <i>Ibid.</i> 63 ... it will be some years before our plantations can attain a respectable size, even allowing for the rapid growth in this climate. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 Thick clumps of plantations ... [which] look as if they have always been there [??native bush] <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,26,1870 ed.</i>)

>  I can get trees and gardener from the station and received directions how to plant for a solid plantation. <i>(Jun 17, 1880,Chudleigh Diary,289,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> Instead of smiling homesteads, with clusters of houses and plantations, nothing was then to be seen but the great wide bare plain ... <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,11</i>) [NOTES: [?=Southern Plantation]]

>  The grey shingles ... could be ... seen ..., looking out from some straggling plantations of blue-gum, willow, and Californian pine. <i>(1889,Zealandia,1/31</i>)

>  The whole is admirably set off by Hagley Park (400 acres in extent), the Domain and Botanical Gardens (79 acres), Lancaster Park, the Town Belts, and other public and private gardens and plantations. <i>Ibid.</i> 116 Twenty-five years ago it was computed that a timber plantation ought to begin after five years to turn in a revenue ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,55</i>)

>  <b>GLEN</b> We raced through the tussocks again, scrambled up the bank, through the plantation, and out on to the lawn. <i>(1917,Six Little New Zealanders,27,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> A young plantation and ornamental shrubs had begun to weave a pattern ... about the lawn and around the saddling paddock [of Awapuni racecourse]. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,249</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> It was El Dorado at last among the lupins offering cones of gold to the blue sky and the black battalions of the pine plantations. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,57</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> She fished, waded in the creeks, chased the wild Plymouth rocks, grays, leghorns, or orpingtons long domiciled in the plantations or with the larrikin elder brother Jim, following him round the rabbit traps in the evenings ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,30</i>)

>  They drove past plantations and into the open airy paddocks. <i>(1962,Landfall Country,164</i>) [NOTES: (Courage `An Evening for a Fish')]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... the land where the pine trees thrown over by the nor-wester exposed roots gasping for soil. The land of the magpie and the myna, the pukeko scuttling into raupo and niggerhead, the plantations regimented across the landscape. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The girls skirted the plantation as far as the gate and soon entered the homestead horse paddock. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,11</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> The two front rooms were taken by Edward Kirk, who had come out with his young wife from England and worked as a boundary rider and ploughman at Roy's Hill, then drained land and planted trees along roadsides and in plantations for Tanner. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,43</i>)

>  [Photo caption] The tents were pitched under the friendly shelter of a plantation on Mr. Blair's "Abbotsford Farm". <i>(Oct 29, 1902,Otago Witness,40</i>)

>  Yesterday a plantation adjoining adjoining the racecourse was destroyed by fire. <i>(Jan 5, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### plaster
 
 <i>v.</i> to let (money) on mortgage

 <i>v.</i> to punish (with imprisonment, blows etc) [(<i>Plaster</i> in NZ also means<i> to beat severely in a fight, to plaster with blows</i> esp. in pass.:<i> he was plastered by </i>... from ca1930, as well as the general Eng. <i>plastered</i> `drunk')]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] a snowfall.

### plastic
 
 <i>adj.</i> made of fibreglass

>  <b>7</b> The Shadbolt, a 21-metre `plastic' (fibreglass) stern trawler, was launched in Florida seven years ago and steamed to new Zealand with her sister ship, the Independence. <i>(Dec 10, 1986,Sunday News</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a marble

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### plastic fantastic
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a nickname for a New Zealand fibreglass entrant (especially KZ-7) in the 1986-1987 America's Cup challenges

>  <i>(Aug 26, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: News]

>  [caption] THE PLASTIC fantastic KZ7. <i>(Apr 21, 1989,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Mr Chambers-Ross said he had enjoyed his share of what he described as racing "plastic fantastics" including two Sydney to Hobart races, but the call of the classic boat was an alluring one. <i>(Oct 31, 2002,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>plastic fantastic</i> revolutionary all-plastic hull designed by Bruce Farr for the yacht KZ7, in the Kiwi challenge for the 1987 America's Cup. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,81</i>)

>  [Hdg] Plastic fantastics... Coloured counters would have worked just as well.. but once the lid comes off the box it's the chance to set sail in their very own plastic fantastic that draws in the players. <i>(Sep 9, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),79</i>) [NOTES: Re America's Cup card game]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a credit card or credit cards collectively (transferred use)

>  Simply ask for credit cards in New Zealand and you will almost certainly receive. The national debt on plastic fantastic is soaring past $600 million ... <i>(Feb 22, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,1</i>)

>  [Advt] It's been called the plastic fantastic - the ultimate credit card. <i>(Mar 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),3</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a false tooth (transferred use)

### play the piano
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Play the piano</i>. 3. `To run one's fingers over the backs of sheep to find which are the easiest to shear,' B., 1941: New Zealand, hence Australian, orig. and mostly sheep shearers': C. 20. L.G.D. Acland, `Sheep Station Glossary' in <i>The</i> (Christchurch) <i>Press,</i> 1933-4. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1138</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>play the piano</i> run fingers across sheep backs to find best place to shear, or to detect defects in wool. [<i>Acland</i>] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,85</i>)

>  <i>play the piano</i>: A description of the action of quicky running the fingers over the sheep to find the softest and easiest to shear. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to run fingers across a sheep's back to assess the quality of fleece.

>  <b>MCGILL</b> play the piano: run fingers across sheep backs to find best place to shear, or to detect defects in wool. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,85</i>)

### playtime
 
 <i>n.</i> a break or `interval' for play between classes, usually mid-morning; play-period (?US recess) [ca1920 at least (Havelock and elsewhere)ca1931 GT Diary (Dannevirke)]

>  When our reporter first reached the school-house it was the children's playtime, and they were scampering about the muddy roads, and exposed to the passing showers. <i>(May 3, 1876,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The attendance was now becoming so large that the question of how the children should be supervised during playtime should be considered. <i>(Nov 1, 1879,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  The school gardens are also receiving attention, and already shows up conspicuously by its neatness. During playtime the teachers and the children get to work and the fruits of their labor can be seen from a distance. <i>(Sep 20, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Nothing remained of the shelter shed where we ate our lunches or waitedfor playtime showers to pass. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,297</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The loud ticking of the wall clock that ruled the distance between "playtime" and"hometime". <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,298</i>)

>  As it is playtime the teachers are not on duty, and have gone home to dinner. <i>(Feb 24, 1882,Star, The,3</i>)

>  They are weeping in the playtime of the others, / in the country of the free. <i>(Sep 13, 1862,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  She had been a splendid teacher, and not only in school hours, but at playtime and holidays she had always taken an interest in the school. <i>(Dec 16, 1895,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  One time when an umpire held his arm aloft, waiting for the sight-screen to be shifted, [Yabba] called: "It's no use ump, you'll have to wait till playtime like the rest of us." <i>(Mar 19, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>) [NOTES: This may be an Australian use: Yabba was an Australian barracker.]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Playtime came and Isabel was surrounded. The girls of her class nearly fought to put their arms round her, ... to be her special friend. <i>"Playtime"</i> was giving way to<i> interval</i>, the word used by the teacher and the bigger boys. <i>(1922,Stories,1984 Alpers</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Dignity would become the keynote. Play-time would become interval. Second year Fifth would become the Remove. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,13</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Playtime came and Isabel was surrounded. The girls of her class nearly fought to put their arms round her, ... to be her special friend. <i>"Playtime</i> was giving way to <i>interval</i>, the word used by the teacher and the bigger boys. <i>(1922,Stories,501,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

>  [Hdg] School scraps playtime for three weeks... Secondly, they were not going out at playtime to run around for 15 minutes and getting into difficulties which teachers had to sort out when they came back in. <i>(Sep 20, 2000,Dominion,11</i>)

>  He did not believe in working in playtime, but he might mention that the Caledonian Society had offered four valuable prizes, which would be awarded during next term, for proficiency in Scottish history. <i>(Dec 20, 1870,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  .. She came home in the playtime with her hand swollen and blue, which I consider was cruel. <i>(May 3, 1884,Te Aroha News,5</i>)

>  The ground fronts the railway line, and there is great danger of the children during playtime and dinner-hour being run over by passing special trains, which frequently run over this line. <i>(May 12, 1893,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  And do you remember, Cowrie, the day we all wrote out autographs in school, and how I lost Eve's at play-time? <i>(Dec 26, 1900,Otago Witness,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

### playway
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There was great harm in too liberal application of an educational slogan: "Learning the playway." <i>(Feb 12, 1947,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  <b>EWING</b> Along with them [sc. parents] there appeared conservatives from all sections of the community who deplored what they dubbed 'the playway.' <i>(1970,NZ Primary School Curriculum,264</i>)

>  `Bah!' you say, `the play way'. Wait a bit, my friends. Reflect on what your children of five here learnt by the play way before they ever went to school. [17] What is the `play-way'? Is it universally practised in schools today? The term `play-way' is well known to students of education as the name given by Caldwell Cooke to a highly successful experiment, mainly in the teaching of English, carried out by him at the Perse School, Cambridge - a fee-paying school, with a high staffing rate and a tradition of experimentation. <i>(Jan 1951,Here & Now,16</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The tots who learned at school the play way,.... <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,61</i>)

>  Present thinking on matters of education is difficult to understand. First we had the `play-way', then the dropping of mathematics ... <i>(Feb 7, 1969,Dominion,7</i>)

>  [Hdg] "The Play Way". An appallingindictment of New Zealand's educationsystem came with a recent report that of157 18-year-olds at Linton military camp,only six per cent could answer a simplegeography question. <i>(Mar 21, 1951,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  The "play way" of teaching was introducedand everything else that would make lifego along like a glad song for the childwho, above all, must not be allowed toexert himself to learn. <i>(Sep 22, 1954,NZ Truth,29</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> The collapse of morals, the untidiness ofstate house gardens, and the growingincidence of public nose-picking were allauthoritatively attributed to 'play-way'. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,51</i>)

### pleuro
 
 <i>n.</i> a beast that has pleuropneumonia

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In connection with stock the following terms were used: - `Let the sheep draw down to the Taieri"; `stags,' `crawlers,' `stragglers,' `a horse with couples in his mouth,' `the bull baked on the road,' `cut and tailed,' `lagoon washing,' `spaying cattle,' `killed a pleuro,' `herding lambs,' `buisting weaners,' `a camp for the cattle,' ... <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,85</i>) [NOTES: [1858-1869] Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 85]

 <i>n.</i> pleuropneumonia, a disease of farm animals [ORIGIN: abbreviation, aka pleura; ca1950 - also hospital slang for humans (Hugh Greer)]

>  T. and S. Morrin had the carcase of an animal supposed to be infected with pleuro sent to their yards. <i>(Feb 28, 1880,Waikato Times,3</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And in the cattle came,/ Before pleuro broke out, and spoilt/ That rosy little game./ <i>Ibid.</i>21 LECTURE 11. - On Pleuro-Pneumonia, with experiences of the Arcade Meat Shops, by Inspector Bust. [? health inspector] <i>(1865,Otago Songster,15</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Precautions were taken to prevent the spread of pleuro among cattle ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,193</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> The danger of tutu poisoning and the pleuro scare made constant observation of the cattle necessary, but all went well in this corner of the south. <i>(1937,Thomas,115</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> shortened forms of pieuro-pneumonia, disease of sheep. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>pleuro, ploorer n.</i> shortened forms of pleuro-pneumonia, disease of sheep. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  [We] docked a horse that is showing symptoms of pleura. It is a very severe inflammation of the lungs and if not attended to often kills in 24 hours. <i>(Aug 13, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,98,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> We are by this Pleuro haunted <i>(1864,Songs of the War,13</i>)

>  And they'd come to live [in Otago] on trouble,/ Pleuro and barracouta./ <i>(1865,Otago Songster,9</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Even <i>pleuro</i> short for <i>pleuropneumonia</i>, a disease which worried cattlemen last century, has an extended use ... in C.R. Thatcher's lines on the Wakamarina gold rush: And the butchers are talking of raising/Pleuro to a shilling a pound. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,148</i>)

>  Further Cases of Pleura. Inspector Runciman and Veterinary Surgeon Naden proceeded to Mr. Burke's farm at Te Awamutu, [and] caused three beasts to be slaughtered, two of which were in a very advanced stage of pleuro, the other affected to a lesser extent. <i>(Apr 13, 1880,Waikato Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> beef from a beast infected by pleuropneumonia; rejected beef

>  <b>THATCHER</b> ... Butchers are talking of raising Pleuro to a shilling a pound. <i>(1864,Songs of the War,5</i>)

>  A proclamation (under the recent Pleuro pneumonia Act) has appeared in the <i>Government Gazette</i>, prohibiting, for three months, the introduction of any cattle from Victoria, and directing the forfeiture, as contraband, of any which may be attempted to be introduced. <i>(May 28, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Pleurophyllum speciosum </i>

### Plimmer's Ark
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Plimmer's Ark back from the dead. A new chapter in the eventful saga of the <i>Inconstant</i> - the ship that became a building - has started following the recent opening of the new Plimmer's Ark Gallery on Queens Wharf... For many years life in Wellington basically revolved around the hulk of the <i>Inconstant</i>... Built in 1847 and launched on 3 Jan 1848 at Cape Breton in Nova Scotia, the <i>Inconstant</i> sailed to England and there had her hull clad with copper...On entering Wellington Harbour [in 1849] the ship ran on to the rocky beach between Pencarrow and the point now named Inconstant Point. The <i>Inconstant</i> was towed across the harbour to Te Aro. John Plimmer bought the by then derelict ship and had it moved in 1850 to Lambton Beach where he built a pitched roof over the mid section. It became known as Noah's [<i>sic</i>] Ark because of its appearance. <i>(Jun 2001,Absolutely Positively Wellington,10</i>)

>  Plimmer's Ark is one of the most significant pieces of maritime history entrusted to the Maritime Archaeological Association. In 1977, construction workers digging in the old Bank of New Zealand building on Wellington's Lambton Quay discovered a slab of wood. Archaeologists identified it as part of the <i>Inconstant</i>, a ship that was towed into Wellington and was eventually bought by John Plimmer who dragged it ashore and modified it for use as business premises. The ship became known as Plimmer's Ark. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Heritage,No. 90/6</i>)

### plix
 
 <i>n.</i> plastic plix for packing kiwifruit [? trade name]

>  Some prefer plastic plix ... for kiwifruit <i>(Jun 26, 1986,Farm and Rural Reporter</i>)

### plonk
 
 <i>n.</i> any alcoholic liquor, booze

>  <b>DAVIN</b> We'd had the odd plonk under our belts. <i>(1947,Gorse Blooms Pale, The,200</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> Do you think we spend too much time watching the gee-gees and footy and going on the plonk. <i>iIbid.</i> 168 Anyway the plonk's cut. Mmm[????????], I'm tired. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,91</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> Boy, am I going to get silly with the plonk when this year's over? <i>(1960,Word for Word,235</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to place (a bet)

>  <b>COOZE</b> [I] plonked my all on my own little pick. <i>(1946,Ten Bob Each Way,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cheap wine, locally in NZ often, e.g. Henderson plonk, Dally plonk (See [Dally plonk](d-dif.md#dally-plonk))

>  He.. brings out a bottle of this Eytie plonk. <i>(Mar 22, 1943,NZEF Times,II/91 : 10</i>)

>  On the plonk again? <i>(Mar 29, 1943,NZEF Times,II/92 : 4</i>)

>  I hiked along to Madame Plonk's bar for some inspiration. <i>(Sep 6, 1943,NZEF Times,III/115 : 6</i>)

>  I remembered how he had gone on the plonk at Tripolsh. <i>(Oct 25, 1943,NZEF Times,III/122 : 11</i>)

>  Nessuno.. had been on the plonk most of the day. <i>(Oct 2, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/171 : 10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Mortar Bombs broke up Jerry "Pork and Plonk" Party. <i>(Jan 22, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/187 : 00</i>) [NOTES: Check page and issue (could be IV/188 : 00)]

>  As the source of jolly plonk moves offshore, so winemakers are forced either to pull up their chenin blanc or try to make decent wine with it. <i>(Apr 10, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  His watch spanned the industry's transformation from rough-plonk manufacturing to fine-wine craft, and as such he had to be adept at both forms of production. <i>(Jun 19, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  [New Zealand] was the land of plonk. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Rough red wine .... for days the Kiwis drank it like beer, and called it `plonk' <i>(1944,Troop Target,213</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> This is genuine gut-rot `plonk', a crude red wine costing fifteen <i>lire</i> ... a bottle. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,149</i>)

>  <i>Plonk:</i> Anything masquerading under the name of wine. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> Glossy. Plonk (Army slang) Alcoholic beverage - usually light wine. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,69</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> He always take a half-gallon jar of wine, bur he also take along the loaf of bread so the plonk will not put them on their ear. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,27</i>)

>  <i>n. (slang)</i>. cheap wine <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>plonk n. (Sl.)</i> cheap wine. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  (Farmer) ... plonk can be defined as `badly made wine', and fortunately in New Zealand, the ridiculed `Henderson Plonk' is almost a thing of the past. <i>(Feb 1981,Insight,81</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Whew! What plonk! Where'd you get this rotgut?' `Don't be a ratbag, Len.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,62</i>)

>  Women and plonk don't mix. <i>(1968,Landfall,22(1) : 41</i>)

>  Gisborne was at the centre of the 1970s wine boom, and was consequently lumbered with huge areas of vineyard that were intended to supply moderate quality wines at best, and cheap plonk at worst. <i>(Feb 5, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  Football and Shafto's and Ensa shows, / Plonk sessions under the stars. <i>(May 8, 1944,NZEF Times,III/150 : 7</i>)

>  The first time I saw Shorty was at the plonk bar. <i>(Dec 4, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/180 : 5</i>)

>  "In search of a plonk shop", interjected little Cissy. <i>(Oct 2, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/171 : 10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> Terms for strong drink [shared with Australia] as <i>lunatic soup, Africa speaks, plonk</i>, [etc] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  `I shouldn't wonder,' said the bloke. `Wish I had some of the plonk I've flogged to those wizzicks [US soldiers] again now.' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7</i>)

>  Gilbert I ... took a bottle of plonk. <i>(1948,Landfall,4/175</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Cheap Australian wine, laced with methylated spirits: Austra lian: since ca. 1919. Baker 1942. Ex Fr. `vin blanc', prob. influenced by 1914-18 war s. plonk, `mud'. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,521,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Plonk</i>. n. 1, 2: current in New Zealand also. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1138</i>)

>  Australian importations include ... <i>plonk</i> for wine,. . . <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> I really liked the flavour of it, after the Australian and American plonk we'd drunk up in the Islands. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,134</i>)

### plonked
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>Grace</b> 'My big mouth gets me in trouble sometimes when I've had a few. It just won't shut up. I remember yesterday...still half plonked asking about your leg.' <i>(2001,Dogside Story,76</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> Herbert was slightly <i>plonked</i>. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,42</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> She looked very plonked. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,51</i>)

### plonker
 
 <i>n.</i> an ill-conceived plan, folly

>  At Gallipoli it was pretty obvious that the tactical plan was a plonker and the troops, who had to follow orders, had no option but to press on even when it became obvious that they were bound to lose. <i>(Apr 23, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,2</i>)

>  Then a kind of social amnesia takes over and you misremember your past - you head off to all those plonker school reunions and look up old girlfriends who are seven dress sizes older and look like their grandmothers. <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a trawler using lights to chase fish into nets

>  They see "plonkers" operating - trawlers using lights to chase fish into nets. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/44</i>)

### plonkerdom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Men behave quirkily then move into plonkerdom... "The two actors are just about out of character but retain enough of their laddish plonkerdom to carry along the uncommitted, who wouldn't give a Four-X for Australian social science". <i>(Sep 7, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>) [NOTES: check page]

### plough
 
 <i>n.</i> [[entries at swing, lever, stilt]]

>  When the first settlers came to New Zealand, they didn't bring many ploughs with them from their home countries. ... [Blacksmiths] set up their smithies and began making implements more suitable for the terrain. One of the first of [the New Zealand made ploughs] was a long sleek job known as a swing plough. Just why is hard to say ... The furrows were the result of the accuracy of the ploughman's eye, as it had no wheels whatever. The depth was graduated by the bridle adjustment but was corrected in uneven ground by leverage from the long handles, or stilts as they were called. If the the share struck anything these [swing ploughs] had a kick like a run cow. It is said that a good man could land on his feet six yards in front of the horses ... The next development was the lever plough which ran on three wheels and had no handles except a stubby one at the back. <i>(Oct 30, 1963,Weekly News,[farming page]</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> While on this subject of cultivation, I am told that the Maoris [on the East Coast] had turn-over ploughs, so that when the end of a furrow was reached, the plough was turned round, the mechanism turned over, and this gave a mould-board on the other side of the plough. I haven't seen any of these implements, so cannot describe one. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,104,1935 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OVERTON</b> Each team had to average three acres per day with a double digger plough... <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,44</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[farming]

>  When the first settlers came to New Zealand, they didn't bring many ploughs with them from their home countries. ... One of the first of [the New Zealand made ploughs] was a long sleekjob known as a swing plough. ... The next development was the lever plough which ran on three wheels and had no handles except a stubby one at the back. <i>(Oct 30, 1963,Auckland Weekly News,farming page</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The greatest objection to the mole plough is the fact that the drain is made at the same depth all over the field, irrespective of the contour of the land. <i>(Nov 20, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> Mole ploughs were used for underground ploughing which.. would require teams of up to sixteen or eighteen horses. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,11</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> Rabbit ploughs seemed to come in several different shapes, although probably the most common was the light walking plough used for making a shallow burrow in which to lay the bait... Another one had a tank which would be used for carrying oats or cut carrots along the furrow. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,27</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> Swamp plough [sic] were very heavy and would require a large team to operate, as they were made for use in swampy ground. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,15</i>)

>  Giant discs are being used by farmers today as an alternative to heavy swamp ploughs to control rush growth of cultivable [sic] land. <i>(Sep 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,109/215</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> For the swamp plough they used six horses. <i>(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),30</i>)

### plough a line
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The next job is to `plough a line'. Rabbits are attracted by newly-turned earth, so with a light plough or any home-made inplement that will make a good scratch a furrow is made across the piece of country to be poisoned. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,60</i>)

### plough bird
 
 <i>n.</i> black-fronted tern (See [also ploughboy](per-pop.md#ploughboy))

>  <b>BULLER</b> Mr. Kirk writes that the local name for this bird [<i>Sterna antarctica</i>, Black-fronted T], in the neighbourhood of Cape Kidnappers, is the `Plough Bird' or `Plough Boy', given on account of the persistent manner in which it follows the farmer's plough for the purpose of picking up the grubs and worms that are exposed in this operation.. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/72</i>)

>  The local name of this bird in the neighbourhood of Cape Kidnappers [ ] is `The Plough Bird,' or `Plough Boy' given on account of its habit of [closely] following the [farmer's]plough[.] for the purpose of obtaining the grubs thus etc ... exposed. <i>(1888,TrNZI,20/30</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  No.1 is the common tern, or sea swallow, often called the plough bird, from its habit of following the plough in spring time, for grubs, worms, &c. <i>(Nov 3, 1892,Otago Witness,24</i>)

### plough camp
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a travelling horse-drawn camp in which itinerant contract ploughmen and their ancillary staff lived. [Originally consisting of tents, then later cookhouse hut and sleeping quarters were drawn on sled or wheels.]

>  Next morning the boss of the station wanted a cook for a plough camp and came down to the whare to see if he could get a man. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/6</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mr Murray told me he was getting another for ploughcamp. <i>(Jun 13, 1907,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It [evening primrose] appeared about a plough camp, a plant here and a plant there, on the sites of the tents of contractors whose last work had been done on a coastal run. <i>(1921,Tutira,251</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> .. it was the time of big station plough camps, the breaking in of huge areas to the plough, and the clearing of much bush. <i>(1931,Farming: Facts, Faults & Fancies,69</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> There was a movable plough-camp, when the Clydesdale horses were kept on the job as new areas were laid down to English grass. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,18</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They also talk of the 'plough camps,' dotted all over the run ... <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,241</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Plough-camps on large estates comprised a number of small moveable huts on wheels; these included a cookshop complete with cook, sleeping huts and dining rooms. [plough-camp; ploughcamp] <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,163</i>)

### ploughable country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hill land that can be cultivated [usually known as rolling, easy or easing to hill country.] (See [tractor country](tot-tyr.md#tractor-country))

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> On ploughable country the control of bracken fern presents no problem. It is on unploughable country that the control is difficult. <i>(1961,Agriculture,490</i>)

### ploughboy
 
 <i>n.</i> black-fronted tern (See [also plough bird](per-pop.md#plough-bird))

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK-FRONTED TERN <i>Chlidonias albostriatus Other names:</i> Tara, Sea Martin, Ploughboy, Inland Tern, Riverbed Tern. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,157</i>)

### plover
 
 <i>n.</i> . [variously phrased and unspecified plovers; <i>Charadrius</i> spp]

>  The great objects of attraction were the rooks and plover. <i>(Mar 30, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  What could be more tasty than a haunch of venison, roast peacock with trimmings, some kangaroo soup, a brace of pheasants, quail on "toast," birds of paradise, turtle soup or plovers' eggs - the latter boiled or scrambled? <i>(Feb 2, 1928,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Ducks, shags, black oyster-catchers, and some sorts of plovers were very numerous here [at Dusky Bay]. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/133</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> On the summit of the mountains I met with a very shy bird, resembling closely the plover (Charadrius), which till then I had never seen. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,138</i>)

>  CHARADRIUS OBSCURUS, <i>Gml.</i> Plover <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/150</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  <b>GRAY</b> A lot of native plover came flying and running about. ... I believe they are only seen on the mountains. <i>(1984,Quiet with the Hills,59</i>) [NOTES: 1984 A.E. Peache in Constance Gray Quiet with the Hills in Knox Thousand Mountains Shining 59]

>  Gulls' eggs are particularly fine eating, and are sold, alas! as "plovers' eggs" often enough, though some dealers sell them for what they are. <i>(Aug 11, 1898,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  The word "game", under the Act of 1867, includes "pheasants, partridges, grouse, black game, quail, snipe, plover ... <i>(May 2, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### pluck
 
 <i>v.</i> (of an undercover police officer) arrest (a prostitute)

>  She and other prostitutes no longer have to worry about being "plucked" - arrested by an undercover police officer. <i>(Apr 28, 2005,Wellingtonian,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to pull wool from a dead sheep before burying the carcass. (See [peel a deader](p-per.md#peel-a-deader))

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Got in 32 sheep from reserve. One dead one. Plucked him, footrotted and bluestoned 8. <i>(Jul 1, 1893,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pikau: Thrown across, either under or over the saddle, the two large saddlebags thus formed could be used to bring home some wool plucked from a dead sheep. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,61</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> My first job was to pluck a dead sheep. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,148</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the name given in a meatworks to the lungs, heart, diaphragm, wind pipe and other pieces removed as one item from the chest cavity of slaughtered animals

>  The name given in a meatworks to the lungs, hear, diaphragm, wind pipe and other pieces removed as one item from the chest cavity of slaughtered animals. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to arrest

 <i>v.</i> to single out (an inmate) for punishment

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> If an inmate from our block ever got plucked and thrown into D block ... he'd probably not be seen again for months or until ... they decide to release him from segregation. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,63</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (coarse, dark) plug tobacco

### plucker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GORDON</b> The next job is <i>plucking</i>, cutting out the lungs and heart [?which, or at least the lungs, are hanging or `dropped' down), and going on the neck, and is done by the <i>plucker</i> or <i>plucky</i> [?pluck = heart or offal]. At Feilding<i> plucking</i> is quite a job, with pride and style attached to it, brandishing the knife with professional skill, etc. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

### plug
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Plug</i>. 3. An inferior horse: s.>, ca. 1920, coll.: Colonial and-prob. ex-U.S.: 1872, in U.S. Also<i> old plug</i>, ob. Ex <i>plug</i>, a stop-hole, perhaps influenced by<i> plug-tobacco</i>, often inferior and rank. But in Australia, from ca. 1880, a good steady, though slow horse, and in New Zealand, late C. 19-20, a horse that is `a good sort' O.E.D. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,641</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> weary travelling; hard, tedious going

>  ... Soft snow plug was a trial with the swags. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,5</i>)

>  Of the long, three-hour plug through soft snow up to the col the less said the better. <i>Ibid.</i> 76 We made hard work of the snow plug up to Muzzle Saddle, ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a caving feature

>  We walk into the cave via a "plug" and descend down an underground stream... <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  We walk into the cave via a "plug" and descend down an underground stream... <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> make one's way heavily or slowly; ?to stamp footholds in the snow; to tramp down (steps or footholes) in soft snow. (cf sb. above and sense of <i>plug</i> `to fill in' etc)

>  ... we plugged steps. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,9</i>)

>  We followed deer tracks zigzagging up through snow totara and <i>Dracophyllum</i>, then plugged up through soft snow to Brodrick Pass (5,308ft.), taking nearly two hours from camp. 1958 <i>Ibid </i> 56 Soft snow on the steep face into the Murchison made it necessary to plug steps down very carefully. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,49</i>)

### plug hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

### plum

 <i>n.</i> (See [also poorman's plum](../A#also-poorman's-plum))

### plum pudding
 
 <i>n.</i> medium trench mortar or its bombs [WW1]

Precisely at 12.30 a.m. our artillery and medium (Plum Puddings) and light (Stokes) Trench Mortars opened a devastating fire on the trench ... <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,83</i>)

>  <b>TREADWELL</b> One day ... we came upon a small working party concerned in bringing up to the front line a supply of `plum-puddings'; not the edible reminder of Christmas, but what was known to the technician[s] as M.T.M. (medium trench mortar) ammunition. <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,187</i>)

 <i>n.</i> toetoe

 <i>n.</i>

### plunge
 
 <i>v.</i> . [?a jargon like stunt??]

>  [Our Brigadier] seems to resent my asking for information and for not too readily allowing my men to be plunged ahead without reconnaissance and information. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 I had heard that a `plunge' attack by 100 men of the Mounted Rifles had been ordered by Headquarters for a couple of days ago. A mad fatal thing. It was to be a dash across open ground commanded by machine guns ... to the Turkish trenches and then back. <i>(May 10, 1915,Great Adventure,45,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Malone]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

It was to be a dash across open ground commanded by machine guns ... to the Turkish trenches and then back. <i>(May 20, 1915,Great Adventure,52,1988 ed.</i>)

### plunge dip
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to release sheep into a pot or plunge dip from a platform.

>  John and Craig Beattie of Kelso were pictured plunge dipping sheep on their 130 ha West Otago property. <i>(Feb 28, 1990,NZ Farmer,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pot or swim dip, where sheep are released into dipping fluid from a platform.

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> Dip - Plunge, spray, and pour-on are dipping systems. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,16</i>)

### plunge dipping
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the bathing of stock for treatment of lice, keds etc in a plunge or swim dip.

>  Plunge dipping is generally acknowledged as being the most efficient method of dipping sheep. <i>(Aug 27, 1970,NZ Farmer,57</i>)

>  The term 'dip' is a hangover from when plunge dipping was the only option - it has latterly been joined first by spray-dipping and now by the pour-ons. <i>(Aug 1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,36</i>)

>  Plunge dipping would be faster but the water has to be kept clean to avoid staining the wool. <i>(Jan 18, 1989,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

>  .. comparisons between shower and plunge dipping and handjetters favour the jetter. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  In an effort to bring the lice outbreaks undr control, a number of fine and mid-micron wool growers went back to plunge dipping this season. <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Autumn 5</i>)

### plunger
 
 <i>n.</i> a glass jug for making coffee by separating the grounds from the liquid by plunging a disc [Jim writes : In NZPOD = cafetiere. This sense of <i>plunger</i> is in AMO and implied in NZPOD but not in any other dictionary I have seen.]

### plunk(et) a baby
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have a (Plunket) baby [also get plunked]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... <i>plunk a baby</i>: to given [sic] an infant scientific care; derived from name of Plunket Society. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Plunk a baby; get plunked; get trubied; get karitanied.</i> To go into a maternity home and have one's baby there hence, to become pregnant: New Zealand: C. 20. B., 1941. The first (<i>plunk a baby</i>) refers to the Plunket Society, as obviously does the derivative second; the third refers to Sir Truby King, noted for sage advice upon, and sustenance for, the feeding of infants; the fourth comes ex Karitane Home. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1139</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Plunket</i> for the Plunket Society has developed a verbal use `to <i>plunket</i> (or <i>plunk</i>) a baby'. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> For the past twenty years [the Plunket Society] has been known as the Plunket, and from it we have derived to plunket a baby, or, even more tersely, to plunk a baby, with further extensions to get plunked, to get trubied (recalling the noted Sir Truby King), and to get Kari tanied (derived from the Karitane Home). <i>(1941,NZ Slang,58</i>)

### Plunket
 
 <i>n.</i> the popular name of the Royal New Zealand Plunket Society Inc., formerly the Royal New Zealand Society for the Health of Women and Children. Compare KARITANE. Plunket baby, a baby raised under a Plunket Society regime; Plunket nurse, a registered, general or maternity, nurse from the Plunket Society who checks and advises on the health of mothers and babies. [Lord Plunket, Governor of New Zealand 1904-1910]

>  <b>HYDE</b> 168 <i>(1934,Journalese</i>) [NOTES: They are Plunket babies, and theirabsurdly plump little brown cheeks nestleon snowy starched white pillows.]

>  "In the early 20th century Plunket served a real function for women, for whom paid employment outside of the home was not an acceptable option. It got middle class married women out of the home and into the public sphere where they could contribute meaningfully and it no longer serves that function"... Now women no longer have to channel their energies into voluntary organisations, such as Plunket to find a niche in the public world. <i>Ibid.</i> C7 She began with the view which arose out of 1970s feminism, that Plunket had been a foce that dictated to women and kept them in their place. <i>(Jul 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  Mother does everything including helping in the shed and "Plunket" feeding babies as well as calves. <i>Ibid.</i> 45 Last Saturday the Town Hall was the scene of the Plunket Party. <i>(Dec 18, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,38</i>)

>  As a proud mother, not of a Plunket baby, but of "Plunket twins" I would like to tell you how much your editorial on this subject was appreciated. <i>(Jul 7, 1938,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  Last Saturday the Town Hall was the scene of the Plunket Party. <i>(Dec 18, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,45</i>)

>  "Plunket Saturday" for the kiddies naturally reminds one of the Plunket kiddies and their pa and ma. <i>(Sep 13, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  But you can trace the rise of many of those problems to the dismantling of things like Plunket visits and basic services like public health nurses. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  [The baby] age 15 months, is Plunket-made. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,121</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> A Plunket Nurse sent to the settlement would be reduced to the gibbers in a week. ... the nearest I got the Maori mothers to `Plunket feeding' was in administering the babies' food out of a bottle instead of with a spoon. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,55,1961 ed.</i>)

>  Plunket is so underfunded it's designed to encourage massive non-use and dropouts. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,18</i>)

>  Dr Valintine himself volunteered [that the training] could be given at no other place in the Dominion than the Karitane Hospital (a 'Plunket' institution). <i>(Mar 7, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  [I]t was the pleasantest weather for the Plunket garden party at Petone, held in Mr. and Mrs. Scales's garden, lent for the occasion. <i>(Mar 18, 1915,Evening Post,9</i>)

### Plunket nurse
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. Karitane nurse](k-kea.md#karitane-nurse))

>  [Photo caption] Lady Plunket presenting the Plunket medal to Mrs. Murray (Nurse M'Kinnon), the first Plunket nurse in the Dominion. <i>(Aug 18, 1909,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  [Mothers] could make up their own minds and if they didn't like the Plunket nurse they just didn't have any more contact... "The Plunket nurse's importance was that they could build up a personal relationship with the mother." <i>(Jul 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

>  The younger one, also in the care of the local plunket nurse, failed to thrive. <i>(Jul 14, 1938,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  He suggested that the nurses proposed to be engaged for the noble work be called Plunket nurses, in order that Her Excellency's name might be for all time associated with the splendid work in which she was so profoundly interested. <i>(Mar 17, 1908,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Milking cows and drinking tea with the Mongrel Mob were just some of the everyday challenges faced by our early Plunket Nurses. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,No. 47/69</i>)

>  The distinction between a. Plunket nurse and a Karitane is an important one. Plunket nurses must be registered nurses before they embark on the 17-week Plunket training course and they often have additional midwifery or nursing qualifications. Karitanes have basic child-care training with no nursing qualification required. <i>(Nov 1992,North & South,108</i>)

>  <i>... plunket nurse. </i> <i>(1909,Ann. Rep. Society for .. Women and Children,9</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> A Plunket Nurse sent to the settlement would be reduced to the gibbers in a week. ... the nearest I got the Maori mothers to `Plunket feeding' was in administering the babies' food out of a bottle instead of with a spoon. `See my baby at the Plunket!' a proud mother would say ... [fn] A Plunket Nurse in New Zealand is one trained in the principles of baby feeding as set out by Sir Truby King. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,55,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> In general, little advice is asked from the Plunket nurse. ... Some mothers think Plunket care is a refinement that only middle-class pakeha mothers have the leisure to follow. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,121</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> There were no Plunket nurses or maternity hospitals at The Wade ... <i>(1948,Taina,107</i>)

>  Lavish expenditure on buildings for ZB atrocities, compared with ... draughty church-halls where mothers have to consult Plunket nurses. <i>(1949,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... my mother ... was always ready to smooth over their cares, act as `Plunket' nurse to their babies, and as physician in their many maladies. <i>(1951,Confessions,23</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... The Plunket nurse ran in/ To scissor off my valued foreskin,. . . <i>(1979,Collected Poems,291,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The next time Elsie saw the Plunket nurse at the house, she came over and watched her weighing the baby. <i>(1967,Empty Hills,41</i>)

>  We lived out in the wop-wops then - on a rehab place, miles from town up a metal road. Apart from a mate on the next farm I didn't have much to do with anyone outside the family. >  <b>SMITH</b> Some Plunket nurses are specially active [in circumcision]. They appear to collect foreskins with the same enthusiasm as the Red Indians did scalps. <i>(1942,Medical Advice from a Backblock Hospital,25,1943 ed.</i>)

>  The system is one by which nurses aretrained as Plunket nurses, with fullknowledge and understanding.. of the careof children. <i>(Jun 6, 1925,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  I have yet to see a Plunket nurse driving a Rolls-Royce with $300,000 yuppie plates. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,18</i>)

### Plunket Shield
 
 <i>n.</i> an interprovincial cricket trophy (revived in 2009)

>  There is a remote possibility of Canterbury challenging Auckland for the Plunket Shield. <i>(Feb 24, 1912,NZ Free Lance,26</i>)

>  Contrary to expectations, the Plunket Shield game at Lancaster Park, between Canterbury and Otago, proved to be a very disappointing game. <i>(Jan 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,61</i>)

>  He has a century to his credit in Shield cricket. <i>(Jan 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,61</i>)

>  THE PLUNKET SHIELD. At a meeting of the New Zealand Cricket Council the conditions for the Plunket Challenge Shield were adopted, subject to the approval of His Excellency. The shield is to be open for competition amongst all Associations affiliated to the New Zealand Cricket Council, who would allot the shield to the Association whose rep-team is considered to have the best record for the season 1906-07 ... <i>(Oct 30, 1906,Taranaki Herald,7</i>)

>  The Plunket Shield has been dusted off and will reappear on the domestic cricket calendar for the first time in 35 years. New Zealand Cricket (NZC) has decided to award the shield to the winners of the national four-day competition this summer. <i>(Nov 5, 2009,NZ Herald,internet ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> From his batting average in Plunket Shield games this season it is obvious that the next national team will have him as an opening batsman. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,218</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> `Hear the Plunket Shield score at all?' ... `Canterbury were two hundred for seven at lunch,' said Peter. <i>(1963,Point of Origin,176</i>)

>  <i>(1982,NZ Almanac,384</i>) [NOTES: [replaced as symbol of provincial superiority by Shell Trophy 1975-6]]

>  <i>Plunket Shield</i> a NZ cricket trophy. [Sir W. <i>Plunket</i>, former Governor General of New Zealand] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,578</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 372]]

>  The selector (Mr. W. J. R. Christophersen) has omitted J. A. Ongley (University) and J. R. Lamason (Wellington College Old Boys) from his team to meet Otago at Dunedin on February 9, 10, 12, and 13 in the final Plunket Shield match of the season. <i>(Jan 23, 1940,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Canterbury saved their revenge on Wellington for their defeat in the Shield match by properly stoushing the touring team by eight wickets. <i>(Feb 28, 1934,NZ Truth,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Plunket system
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dr Spencer has never at any time attacked the Plunket system. <i>(Jul 14, 1938,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> In the mind of anyone who has seen this same child in later days ... some fleeting doubts may have arisen about ... those numerous rites prescribed by the Plunket system. <i>(1938,High Country,88</i>)

>  Instead of studying the trade andcommerce of New Zealand, I spent themorning reading the wonderful account ofthe Plunket system. <i>(Jun 6, 1925,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Replacements [sc. calves] here are well reared - quite on a Plunket system. <i>(May 18, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,82</i>)

>  It is claimed by the promoters that the Plunket system has its necessary place in the social scheme, and is fully entitled to the State assistance asked. <i>(Mar 7, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

### Plunketline
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [caption] <b>Sarah Boyd</b> investigates the Plunketline saga and asks what the loss of the contract means for Plunket as it enters its 100th year. <i>(May 13, 2006,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

>  Thirteen mothers today used the first overnight shift of the reinstated 24-hour parent helpline, Plunketline. <i>(Apr 1, 2000,Evening Post,Flair : 3</i>)

>  <b>The lessons of Plunketline</b> Congratulations to the people at Plunketline who, when faced with closure, went out and secured funding to enable them to keep providing this valuable service. <i>(Oct 2, 2006,NZ Herald,A12</i>)

### pluot
 
 <i>n.</i> a hybrid fruit derived from apricots and plums

>  [Hdhg] Plum plus apricot equals pluot. Plums and aporicots are coming together nicely at Ray Anna's Marlborough orchard - as pluots... The sweet, juicy fruit, are predominantly plum, with a slight "fuzz" on the skins and an appreciable apricot flavour and texture. They were developed by Californian company Zaiger Genetics 11 years ago using an intricate method of hybridising. <i>(Dec 29, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Smooth skinned pluots are made up of two thirds of plum genes and one third of apricot genes and were invented by US man Floyd Zaiger. <i>(Sep 3, 2003,Capital Times,21</i>)

### plurry
 
 <i>adv.</i> `stage-Maori' representation of <i>bloody</i>, occasionally used as a general euphemision (from Maori transposition of English /B/ as /P/ and /D/ as /R/) [also used as an adj. a plurry mess]

>  A Greytown Maori describing the Lydite shell and its effects to a brother Maori, said: "By cripes! he the plurry boy to kill the Poa. When he burst, as soon you hear the smell, you dead!" <i>(Aug 4, 1900,New Zealand Free Lance,10</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> [Tom, the Aust black speaks] `You one plurry fool', he said angrily, `you one big plurry fool' <i>(1913,Marie Levant,23</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  PLURRY. - The great Australian adjective as expressed in the more mellifluous language of the Maori. <i>Ibid.</i> 161 He t'e plurry Porangi, I t'ink. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `<i>Plurry pakeha</i>, eh? You got the skin like the Maori, but you're not <i>tangata</i> Maori. There isn't any Maori who doesn't eat the <i>hou-hou.'</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 183 [Maori speaks] `Up at Wairoa, that Sergeant Wace, he's so plurry frightened he gets under the table and hides when he hears Starkie's coming.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,148</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,29</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> If, as appears to be suggested by raconteurs and cartoonists, the Maori finds <i>plurry</i> (bloody) and <i>py korry</i> necessary ingredients of normal conversation ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,71</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He could speak beautiful English but often he put on the plurry Maori eh just to have a crack at us ... <i>Ibid.</i> 240 Rata was right. I'll go round the plurry bend. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,206</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Anyway, it's no bloody good for a title. No one would know how to pronounce it [a Maori name]. Let's get right away from this plurry native stuff.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,109</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> The boss who owns the big flash Jaguar looks plurry crook because the production manager has just bought the Cadillac. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,53</i>)

>  <i>plurry n. NZ sl.</i> bloody. [M corruption of BLOODY] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,579</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>LATHAM</b> ... and we made our own hats. Believe me, there were some beauts! One Maori girl's remark summed it up in a lot of cases - `Call that plurry thing a potae (hat)? Looks more like a mimi pot!' <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,3</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>plurry</i> bloody; pidgin in Australia and New Zealand; Partridge says it is from Aboriginal's natural use of the word, OEDS says NZA slang, Turner thinks it a convention of journalists rather than Maori, in support of which was the slanguage of the character Hori in the bestselling book <i>The Half-Gallon Jar. </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,85</i>)

>  [A] Maori was asked on the Athletic Park, "You think New Zealand wins to-day?" The astonishing answer was: "No plurry fear! Englishmen he te win, heesy!" <i>(Aug 15, 1904,Evening Post,2</i>)

### PLUS
 
 <i>abbr.</i> people like us

### plush
 
 <i>n.</i> the cloth in the gold box to catch the gold specks

>  WANTED KNOWN that the BEST PLUSH for Mining Purposes can be had at W. KIRKPATRICK'S, STAFFORD. <i>(Apr 24, 1875,West Coast Times,1</i>)

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> [Walter D. Moffatt, d. 3.1.1882: poem] Yet still they dig and delve, be the weather foul or fair, / And talk of plush and tables, and a thousand pounds a share. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,70,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [a. 1882]]

>  Cheyne then got a board and one of the plushes from the manager's office, laid the plush on the board, and fixed the board from the gold box to the streaming down pipe. Cheyne then took a quantity of sand out of the box, put it on the board, and turned the water on, and washed the sand back into the gold box. The gold remained on the plush. <i>(Feb 25, 1905,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: 1905 Feb 25 Otago Daily Times in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 42]

>  He shovels the black sand into the gold box, washes it through with water pumped from the lagoon on the other side of the sand hills. The gold falls on to the plush (`I just use an ordinary towel') which is then shaken into a baby's bath. <i>(Nov 27, 1977,Sunday Times (NZ),17</i>)

>  But my plan would be to put a wide sluice-box in the tail end of the ground-sluice, line it well with plush or green baize or a blanket, and place a man with a fork a few yards in front of the box to throw out all the stones he could. By doing this you would find out whether you were losing gold or not. <i>(Dec 16, 1876,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  In reply to questions, the Minister stated that.. the Cabinet would consider the advisability of repealing the duty on plush and other textile fabrics used for gold saving. <i>(Aug 15, 1901,Wanganui Herald,1</i>)

### plute
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is Campbell-Bannerman, a plute of plutes - one of the richest of Scottish landlords, of enormous wealth, made by himself and his immediate progenitors, both by money-grubbing and land-grabbing. <i>(Jul 13, 1907,NZ Truth,108/1/1</i>)

### plutie/plut(e)y
 
 <i>adj.</i> wealthy [ca1945 (university and elsewhere) ca1952 Wellington Ruth Mason, espec. <i>Plutie type</i>, `one who has money'.]

>  <i>plutey a. NZ sl. derog</i>. rich, snobbish. [foll.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,579</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i> pluty</i> wealthy, but worse, perceived as assuming airs and graces above the level of the egalitarian Kiwi herd; refers also to suburbs that fancy themselves, such as Fendalton in Christchurch, Khandallah or Karori (`We are the Park Lane of New Zealand' overheard comment in London of a woman from Karori) in Wellington, Remuera in Auckland; comes from `plutocracy', the land barons of the South Island last century. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,85</i>)

### pluton
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Pluton</i>: a large body, often dome-shaped, of intrusive rock (such as granite, granodiorite, syenite, etc.) that has been exposed at or near the surface. The vicinites of plutons have been the source of many valuable mineral deposits. `Bubbles' are smaller plutons. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,113</i>)

### pluty
 
 <i>adj.</i> up-market

>  The Novotel Palm Cove Resort takes up most of one end of Palm Cove, which is the biggest posh seaside strip before you get to the even plutier Port Douglas an hour or so up the Captain Cook Highway. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  He's booked us into a pluty restaurant but now I find that some stylish acquaintances are going there and will be able to observe me with my "date". <i>(Nov 8, 1999,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Tiggie is an overweight 14-year-old whose parents think sending her to a pluty Auckland girls' school will help her. <i>(May 12, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Whether they are of the plooty, I'm speaking-like-this-because-I-have-a-huhu-grub-up-my-nose or weirdly regional, all connoisseurs treat the talent and their loved bits and pieces with equal servility, as if they were peering into a pram admiring a newborn. <i>(Jul 11, 2000,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  You pay as much for a whole year at Steiner or Montessori as you would for a single term at plutier places. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  One week Jenny's hoisting her hemline and banging on about her halcyon days in hoochy koochy bars, then the next minute the delegates are electing yet another woman to a position of power becaause it's fashionable and she hasn't even got a pluty accent. <i>(Jul 28, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Suppose a friend says to me, `You know that drongo who was on the chain with me at the works last summer? Just a little joker he was, remember? A real serious ankle-biter. Hailed from somewhere down the Wire Wrapper. A right useless bastard. Couldn't see the road to the dunny if it had red flags on it. Well, it seems that he's shacked up with some pluty Remmers sheila and she's about to drop her bundle.' ... A non-New Zealander will probably require a translation, ... My friend is communicating to me the information that a slow-witted, incompetent person of diminutive stature, who was reared in the Wairarapa district and worked for a time in the killing gang of an abattoir, is now cohabiting with a wealthy woman from the affluent Auckland suburb of Remuera. This woman is soon to give birth. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]

>  Speaking on the phone from his Chelsea flat, Fellowes' voice is not as plooty as that of Lord Kilwillie... <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,E 10</i>)

### Plymouth Sound
 
 <i>n.</i> a pound [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

>  I hop off at the rubbidy, borrow a couple of Plymouth Sounds and get on the pig's ear. Plymouth Sounds....pounds <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### Pmac
 
 <i>n.</i> the introduced biocontrol wasp, <i>Pseudaphycus maculipennis</i> [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  The new bio-control agent for the obscure mealybug was released in New Zealand after six years' work and approval from Erma... The wasp, with the scientific name <i>Pseudaphycus maculipennis</i>, is called Pmac for short. About 60,000 Pmacs were released into six apple orchards around Hastings in Februiary 2001, and have been left to their own devices. <i>(Jan 10, 2003,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> post-weaning multisystemic wasting syndrome - a disease of young pigs

### poaka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland Capping Book,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the pied stilt

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 78. HIMANTOPUS LEUCOCEPHALUS. Gould. Pied Stilt. Poaka. 79. HIMANTOPUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gould. Pied Stilt. Poaka. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,29</i>)

>  <i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i>, Gould. Pied Stilt, Poaka. - Makes a slight nest of grass, often by the edge of lagoons or meres, in swamps ... <i>(Nov 1985,NZ Journal of Science,558</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> WHITE-HEADED OR PIED STILT (<i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i>), Poaka of the Maoris, is widely distributed in both the North and South Islands of New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PIED STILT <i>Himantopus leucocephalus Other names</i>: Stilt, Barker, Poaka, White-headed Stilt, Daddy Long-legs, Torea (Rotorua), Pip (Kaipara). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,147</i>)

>  <i>poaka n.</i> New Zealand the white-headed stilt, a water-bird [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>183. Himantopus himantopus leucocephalus</i> Gould, 1837 <i>Pied Stilt (Poaka)</i> Australia and New Zealand ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,51,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>poaka n</i>. long-legged black and white NZ shore bird; pied or black stilt. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,579</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>Pied Stilt Himantopus himantopus leucocephalus </i>GOULD, 1837 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Poaka, white-headed stilt. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,212</i>)

>  HIMANTOPUS NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Gould. Poaka. Pied Stilt. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/70</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a pig; occas. pork (See [cf. porker](pop-pyo.md#porker))

>  There is no doubt of the fact that the natives will be sorely pinched for food this winter, and they are even now resorting to kinds of food that would be given to the poaka in time of plenty. <i>(May 2, 1906,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  It happened.. all on account of thefamily "poaka." <i>(Jun 4, 1910,NZ Free Lance,12</i>)

>  The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  "Kaituna," a locality of great repute for this staple article of the aboriginal's food, prior to the introduction of the "poaka" (pig), and the nearest approach to flesh (literally, eat eels) but probably intended to convey the idea of its being a noted fishing ground for this, to them, delicious and life-sustaining commodity - let us hope that it may remain undisturbed. <i>(Jul 3, 1894,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Mr. MacCormick cross-examined, with Mr. Hamlin interpreting, during which witness stated that they were taken out of the Waikato like pigs, having strings fastened to their heels, and Te Raiha calling out, "Poaka! poaka!" <i>(Jan 14, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Betty replied, "she has come for her pig;" Brown said "I have not seen your pig;" I then went outside the house, and stayed out there a little while, when the pig grunted; I called "poaka poaka," and the pig grunted a good deal; the noise came from a little house behind; I went to the house, and saw the pig inside grunting to me; I opened the door, and I found that the pig had been marked; the ear was cut, and the tail was cut off; the blood was quite fresh, it having been recently done; I examined the pig, and found it was the same that I had missed. <i>(Dec 6, 1861,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  <b>WALTON</b> The puerko, (so the natives name the pig or hog,) has proved himself to be a highly beneficial importation to the [27] country, ... <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pigs have only of late been introduced into many parts of the country; ... The native name is poaka; and although English [50] men think this word to be their own `pork,' with a native termination (porka), I am doubtful whether the New Zealanders had not some knowledge of this animal previous to its introduction by us. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/379 Poaka - pig, swine <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/49</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>A Hog</i> ... [New-Zeeland] <i>(have none) </i> <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|orka, <i>s</i>. Pork. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,194</i>)

>  Ancient grey headed cannibals who tell ... the gusto of the days when they `kaikai' (eat) conquered enemies or slaves, who discriminate the saltness of the `pakeha' from the sweetness of the maori and the superiority of both to `poaka' pork. [check date] <i>(Aug 13, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/103,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Richmond)]

>  Poaka. A Pig <i>(1913,Auckland Capping Book,27</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> She was immensely delighted about my coming, and immediately prepared a meal of `porka' and kumera, ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,156,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>VAILE</b> <i>Poaka</i> Porker: pig: hog <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>)

>  <i>poaka</i>: pig. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/110</i>) [NOTES: [get quot]]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> ... the wild pig - the poaka from the hills <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,282</i>)

>  <i>poaka ... n.</i> N.Z. waterbird, pied stilt; a pig (Maori transliteration of pork) [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> ANA ... Oh, boy. Here we go. Gee, they fine poakas. <i>(1969,Awatea,18,1978 ed.</i>)

>  One cannot look at some of the ancient grey headed cannibals who tell ...[sic] with gusto of the days when they `kaikai' (eat) conquered enemies ..., who discriminate the saltness of the `pakeha' from the sweetness of the maori, and the superiority of both to `poaka' pork ... <i>(Aug 13, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/103,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> ... how so large a `poaka' should weigh so little! <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,29</i>)

>  The [Maori] food has changed: no longer can poaka, kereru, and hurepo be caught in the bush ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,250,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> The pig, though present in Polynesia, is not mentioned in Maori traditions. After its introduction by Europeans, it was named <i>poaka</i> after the English name pork or porker which resembles the old Polynesian name of <i>puaka</i> but must not be confounded with it. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,64</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> As the New Zealanders call a dog pero, so they call a pig porka, evidently another European term. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,185</i>) [NOTES: ?does the spg suggest a non-rhotacised dalect of English]

>  [Hdg] Poaka pursuit. Pig hunting is a perfect way to spend a couple of days, says Tama David, who has been pig hunting since he was a boy... <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The native name is poaka; and although English men think this word to be their own 'pork', with a native termination (porka), I am doubtful whether the New Zealanders had not some knowledge of this animal previous to its introduction by us. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,II 49</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> In no time at all fifteen poaka were through and spreading out to forage for tucker. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,101</i>)

>  wild pig: poaka. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  Poaka pursuit: Pig hunting is a perfect way to spend a couple of days ... <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Canterbury Times,20</i>)

 <i>n.</i>
