>  <b>MARTIN</b> <i>Melicope simplex</i> Poataniwha. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,238</i>)

### poatekawa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pohutukawa](per-pop.md#pohutukawa))

### pob-pob
 
 <i>n.</i> a nursery hypocoristic re-formation of <i>porridge </i> [ca 1938] (See [cf. pog](per-pop.md#pog))

### pocket
 
 <i>n.</i> wool pack, often slung in pairs over a pack-horse [sheep farming]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> [Caption] <i>Packing wool pockets</i>. <i>(1926,Tutira,116</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The wool was sent to Napier. ... Over the hills it was carried by pack horses ... each taking about 150 pounds in what were called `pockets' - long-shaped packs slung one on each side of the saddle. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,170</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The wool is pressed by a miniature press into `pockets' of from 80 to 100 pounds and packed out by horse. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It is now the only station in Canterbury with no road access to its shearing shed. The wool is baled out at the shed in a miniature press and the `pockets', which weigh from 70 to 90 lb. each, are packed out to the homestead by horses. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a crevice or cavity in rock, holding gold [gold mining]

>  On proceeding to examine the lods in the No. 2 level, I find it is what is generally called the Dauntless leader, and a rich pocket was in sight; ... <i>(Mar 8, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We washed the sands of the river and examined the <i>`pockets</i>' of the rocks for gold. <i>(1857,Southern Districts of NZ,312</i>)

>  A golden leader, or rather `pockets' of gold have been opened up, which have yielded enormous returns. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,87</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> The heaviest gold always lies in `pockets' or rock-holes ... Our cradle was set, ... and we began to take up the ground in the bed of the creek to get down to the bottom, where the heaviest gold always lies in `pockets' or rock holes, and crevices. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> The Dunstan gold workings were of two kinds - technically known as `beach claims' and `bank claims'. The latter were on the gravelly river banks which the miners sluiced away bodily for the golden grains ... The former were on the sandy margins of the river, and were often extended by means of `wing-dams' and other ingenious devices far into the rushing waters. In these claims the gold was found lodged in `pockets' or fissures formed by the slate bars ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,Bk I iv 17,1889 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Gold lay in a series of rich `pockets' (as the crevices were termed) <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,51</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Some of this gold is obtained by washing the sands in sluice-boxes, just as in operations among the mountains; and deposits, or `pockets,' are occasionally discovered in the sand under the loose stones. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,216</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I bought and sold flour ..., got a 3-1/2 oz. nugget from the bottom of a hole that had been left, made a rise of [pounds]300 out of a pocket of gold like horse beans. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,115</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> I found a few pockets [of gold] in the mountains <i>(1890,Web of Spider,10</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Eventually it was proved that the find was but a `blind reef', a `pocket', a mere isolated dribble from the main continuous vein we had at first supposed we had struck. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/299</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Pocket</i>: A small mass of rich ore in a vein. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  A little gold was got on the sideling reef yesterday, and some are still hopeful of getting a pocket in the ground not yet touched under the elevators. <i>(Mar 15, 1889,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Driving in a northward direction the new shaft (down 55 feet), and, at a distance of 12 feet, a very nice pocket of specimens was brought to grass. <i>(Mar 16, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pack for carrying wool slung over the back of a pack-horse (cf pikau) [sheep farming]

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> A train of pack horse, heavily laden with the weighty `pockets' of wool, toil weekly past. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,239</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The shearing was a long, slow affair and dragged on into February [1861]. The wool clip of twenty-one bales was packed out in `pockets'. These were small bales which held about thirty-one kilograms of wool. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,50</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The wool was sent to Napier. For the first part of the journey over the hills it was carried by pack horses - sixty-two of them - each taking about 150 lb. in what were called `pockets' - long shaped packs slung one on each side of the saddle. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,183,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> [Caption] <i>Packing wool pockets</i>. <i>(1926,Tutira,116</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The wool is pressed by a miniature press into `pockets' of from 80 to 100 pounds and packed out by horse. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It is now the only station in Canterbury with no road access to its shearing shed. The wool is baled out at the shed in a miniature press and the `pockets', which weigh from 70 to 90 lb. each, are packed out to the homestead by horses. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp.use] a small area of land, often in a gully, and usually containing bush. [landscape]

>  Care was taken to leave pockets of native bush intact for shelter and aesthetic reasons. <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  Five pockets of native bush. <i>(Feb 19, 2002,Realenz.co.nz</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small bale, the size of a potato sack, used for wool packing by pack-horse. [sheep farming]

>  For scoured merino wool there was brisk competition, prices being well maintained, while scoured cross-bred was one penny lower than last sale; 7 bales and 7 bags greasy wool, 2 bales and 79 pockets scoured wool, 267 sheepskins, 5 packages tallow, and three hides were catalogued and all sold as follows ... <i>(Jun 14, 1880,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The wool was sent to Napier. For the first part of the journey over the hills it was carried by pack horses - sixty-two of them - each taking about 150 lb. in what were called 'pockets' - long shaped packs slung one on each side of the saddle. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,183</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the pockets, which weigh from 70 to 90 lb each, are packed out to the homestead by horses. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,78</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The 26-horse pack-train took out the year's wool clip pressed into 'pockets,' much smaller packs than the normal wool bale, holding a little more than a large potato sack. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,57</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> As we had sufficient pockets to handle the dry shearing we did not pack out the wool until we had finished the shearing. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,149</i>)

>  The Morayshire's outward cargo consisted of .. 92 pockets, and 2 sacks wool, 272 casks tallow, and 9 cases leather. <i>(Apr 12, 1902,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [gum digging]

>  <b>THOMAS</b> The posssibility of finding a `pocket' or better still a good `patch' added interest to the [gumdigger's] day's work. <i>(1970,Way Up North,31</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> If a man hits on a good `pocket' of gum ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,54</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> If a man hits on a good `pocket' of gum, he may make five or six pounds a week ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,54</i>)

>  In the shelf a "pocket" is scooped capable of containing a quart or two, and the gum is made to collect in it by scarifying the bark triangularly, with an angle pointing to the pocket ... When the "pocket" has become full, the gum is barreled and carried away to be distilled. <i>(Mar 29, 1873,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  Shallow fern gum, which is the most prized for the manufacture of varnish, is still in places fairly plentiful, and the fortunate digger who strikes a pocket of such is renumerated to a degree which his forerunners never in their wildest dreams imagined. <i>(Sep 8, 1909,Otago Witness,22</i>)

### pocket edition
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) a child [There used to be a NZ tobacco called "Pocket Edition']

>  <b>SARGESON</b> And all safely married now with half a dozen pocket editions to show for themselves. <i>(1976,Sunset Village,49</i>)

### pocket press
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wool press used to fill wool pockets.

>  An interesting discovery in the woolshed was an old 'pocket' press. <i>(Aug 5, 1967,Napier Daily Telegraph,6</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I also saw the old house and woolshed; the little pocket press is still there, the first one I had seen for a good many years. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,195</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They had one of the little 'pocket' presses out at the Bluff shed. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,87</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pocket press: one especially made to turn out pocket bales. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,62</i>)

### pocketing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... at Mount Criffel ... prospectors washed out during one summer about 1,200 oz of gold for a season's work (about seven months) several years in succession. This class of mining resembles what is known in California as `pocketing', and is in New Zealand only in its infancy. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,363</i>)

### pod
 
 <i>n.</i> a bodysurfer's handboard

>  The pod is a hydrodynamically designed handboard which fastens with a velcro strap to the palm of the hand and when used while bodysurfing provides propulsion, lift and control... The pod.. is not new, just refined. It has been around for a while now, with the Taylor's Mistake handboard being popular among surf lifesavers in New Zealand as far back as the 1970s. <i>(Dec 10, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

### poddied
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

### poddly
 
 <i>n.</i> a sea perch

>  I want now to ask the kind assistance of some amongst your readers in collecting from books, periodicals, or newspaper quotations to illustrate the use of the various words in the following list .. List of Fish .. Poddly, 1872 ... <i>(Feb 6, 1896,Otago Witness,40</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> SEA PERCH ... The Pohuiakaroa (<i>Sebastes percoides</i>), though not figured deserves mention, as it is a small fish representing in these seas the genus to which the Norway Haddock belongs. ... It is the proper <i>Sea Perch</i> of these waters, that name having been by mistake applied in the Catalogue to a small Wrasse (No.67), which is generally called the Spotty or Poddly. [No.67 is <i>Labrichthys celidota</i>) <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,108</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ a New Zealand and Australian fish, <i>Sebastes percoides, </i>Richards., called in Victoria <i>Red-Gurnet Perch</i>. The name is applied in England to a different fish. <i>(1898,Austral English,363</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON & HECTOR</b> `The pohuia-karou is the proper sea-perch of these waters, that name having been applied by mistake to a small wrasse, which is generally called the spotty or poddly.' <i>(1872,Fishes NZ,108</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] poddly [TS] 1745 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### poddy
 
 <i>n.</i> a ? calf -->1. a hand-fed calf or lamb (?or other farm animal): also esp. poddy-calf

>  They wished to regard the Government as a kind foster-mother, but they wanted the cream of the milk, and other people could be fed in the way farmers' poddy calves were nourished, with the skimmed milk. <i>(Aug 24, 1912,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  One day he got a telegram from his dear, forgiving old dad to come home and eat of the fatted poddie calf; <i>(Nov 13, 1906,NZ Truth,6/7/7</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> ... any undue disturbance of the [Merino] ewes at this time will result in much mis-mothering of lambs, many of which will subsequently die or survive as `poddies' and `weeds'; ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,94</i>)

>  <i>poddy, poddy-calf n</i>. hand-fed calf; similarly <i>poddy-foal. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>poddy, poddy-calf n.</i> a hand-fed calf, similarly poddy-foal. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>poddy n. NZ & Austral</i>. hand-fed calf (<i>poddy calf</i>), lamb, etc. [Eng. dial.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,579</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Mr. Andrew Chirnside's famous stallion Newminster was destroyed this month after suffering .. from a diseased bone in the head .. Bred by the late Mr. Thomas Chirnside in 1873, Newminster, by The Marquis (imp.) from Spa, was a "poddy", being bottle fed in consequence of his dam dying at his birth. <i>(Dec 27, 1894,Otago Witness,29</i>)

>  [H]e used Clarke's calf-feeders, by which the calves got the milk in a more natural way, and did not become "poddy". <i>(Dec 24, 1902,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  An orphan lamb is a "poddy". <i>(Dec 7, 1899,Otago Witness,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  From the total number forward 40 to 50 small poddys must be deducted as not classes, and these, with a respectable total of inferior old cows, left competition for good beef brisk. <i>(Oct 31, 1885,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  The action of the machine is as near as possible that of a calf at work on the cow, and if (says Mr Gullet) you place your finger in one of the cups and shut your eyes, you could easily believe that the suction was being done by a friendly little "poddy." <i>(Jul 15, 1910,Ashburton Guardian,2</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> [The wild cattle] were a mixed haul: two-year-olds, poddies and pikers. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,52</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a malnourished lamb or a motherless lamb or calf.

>  A profit can be made from a robust calf, but there can be none in a 'poddy', no matter how the market can be. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,705</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Later that day Alf and I were looking at his poddies - lambs not doing well. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,174</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a lamb which has survived the death of its mother and lived on grass from its young days and has a distended stomach on account of this

### podge-hole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> We went down a spur for two miles that was very soft from the late rain, and full of waterholes, called crab-holes. These holes were formed by the large snowgrass tussocks being burnt out roots and all, leaving a hole, or by the uprooting of trees, where the hole was very large ... In some places these holes made by(?) cattle mustering a most hazardous undertaking. In Mr Alex Garvie's report in 1858 he refers to `podge holes,' presumably a variety of `crab-holes.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,32</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Alex Garvie report in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 32]

### poe
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) tui [ORIGIN: Polynesian, also poe-bird]

>  Another ... was by the Gentlemen in the Endeavour's Voyage, called the poy-Bird ... Under its Throat hang two little tufts of curled, snow-white feathers called its <i>poy</i>, which is an Otahitee word for Ear-rings. [fn] <i>Poe</i>, a pearl. <i>(1773,Cook's Journals,2/786,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in Cook Journals (1961) 2/786]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The great noise caused by the Cascade is now & then conquered by the shriller note of a small bird, & by the graver pipe of wattle birds [[ed. fn] Either the South Island Kokako ... or the South Island Saddleback <i>Philesturnus carunculatus carunculatus</i>, or both.], Pohebirds [[ed. fn] Now the New Zealand Tui ...] & Mocking-birds [[ed. fn] Now the bellbird ...]. <i>(Apr 11, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/253,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> There are great plenty of a kind of birds much resembling our black birds, except they have a few white feathers in the wings and tail and some fine loose downy feathers round the neck which grow out among the black ones & has the appearance of fine white hairs intermixed with the feathers & under the throat are 4 fine curling white feathers growing in a tuff. These are called Poey-birds. They are thought to be the finest eating for delicacy & richness ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/213 During our ramble [in Queen Charlotte's Sound] I saw Wood Pigeons, Parroquets, Grey Parrots, Poey Birds & Quails & vast variety of singing Birds but no animal great or small or any fruit Trees of any kind whatever. <i>(Apr 12, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/206]

>  ... one [bird] of a black colour with a greenish cast is remarkable for having a tuft of white curled feathers hanging under the throat, and was call'd the Poy Bird by our people. <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/806 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>COOK</b> Amongst the small birds I must not omit to particularise the wattle- bird, poy-bird .... The poy-bird is less than the wattle-bird .... Under its throat hang two little tufts of curled snow-white feathers, called its poies, which being the Otaheitean word for ear-rings occasioned our giving that name to the bird ... [Morris note: In the illustration given it is spelt <i>poe-bird</i>, and in the list of plates it is spelt <i>poi</i>] <i>(1777,Voyage Towards the South Pole,97</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Cook's `Voyage towards South Pole' 1/97-98 (Morris)]

>  <b>MENZIES</b> We shot a dozen and a half Poe birds <i>(1791,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1791 Menzies's Journal (McNab. Hist. Recs. 2/492)]

>  Poe birds, as called by Cook <i>(1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham'</i>) [NOTES: 1791 Journal of `Chatham' McNab Hist. Recs. 2/500]

>  <b>BELL</b> - the principal of these [smaller birds] is the Poe Bird as call'd by Cook - <i>(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham'</i>) [NOTES: 1791 Nov E.Bell Journal of Voyage in H.M.S. `Chatham' (Vancouver expedition) (MS ATB) 51]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The poe, with its pendents tufts of white feathers, of which an accurate drawing is given in Cook's Voyages ... this native warbler, that no successful rival can be found for it among the woodland songsters of Europe. This bird was brought to us for sale by the natives, in small cages made of wicker-work. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/254</i>)

>  <b>WILKES</b> [The tui's] note [is] rather louder than that of the bird called by the Samoans `poe', ... <i>(Mar 1840,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/317</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 6. <i>Prosthemadera Novae Seelandiae. Strickl</i>. ... Poe, or Toi of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Toni of the natives. <i>Less</i>. Tui of the natives. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/187]

>  `Tui,' Parson-bird or Poe-bird of Capt. Cook Melliphagidae; Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/305</i>)

>  (T.H. Potts) <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,276</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Poe/[dieresis]</i>, n. <i>same as</i> Tui <i>(q.v.) and</i> Parson-bird<i> (q.v.). The name, which was not the Maori name, did not endure. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,363</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. the parson bird; noted for its plumage, fineness of song and powers of mimicry. [origin uncertain] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> `This bird they called the Wattle-bird, and also the Poy-bird, from its having little tufts of curled hair under its throat, which they called poies, from the Otaheitan word for ear-rings. The sweetness of this bird's note they described as extraordinary, and that its flesh was delicious, but that it was a shame to kill it.' <i>(1865,Discovery in Australia,1/III</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> The most remarkable bird is one to which Cook's people gave the name of mocking-bird, from the extraordinary variety of its notes. There is also another which is called by the English the poe, or poi bird, from a little tuft of white curled feathers which it has under its throat, and which seemed to them to resemble certain white flowers worn as ornaments by the people of otaheite, and known there [188] by a similar name. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,187</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [pounamu](pop-pyo.md#pounamu))

### poey bird
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [poe](per-pop.md#poe))

### pog
 
 <i>n.</i> porridge [cf. pob-pob ca1945-1946 Auckland Tramping Club D.Close, letter May 1951] (See [cf. pob-pob](per-pop.md#pob-pob))

>  "Pog" is.. Kiwi parlance for porridge, and "scroggin" is the mix of nuts, dried fruit and bits of choccy that trampers munch on the trail. <i>(Apr 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Pog for breakfast, stew for dinner, Scrog for lunch sure is a winner <i>(Jul 1968,Footprints,25/2</i>)

>  We hope you'll always burn the pog And ev'ry bite contain a wog. <i>(Jul 1967,Footprints,24/2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> untidy mess

>  Clean up your space, clean up any tools. Be a tidy kiwi. This may seem counteractive to letting creativity flow, but once you've been creative, if you don't clean up after yourself, you could be destroying someone else's flow by leaving pog (untidy mess) behind you. <i>(Aug 2004,tlc creativity Newsletter,No. 25/1</i>) [NOTES: http://www.tlc.ac.nz/newsletters/25_aug_04.htm]

### pogie
 
 <i>n.</i> a hand-insulator fixed to a paddle

>  As temperatures fell, drips from the paddles slicked the kayak decks with ice, and fingers and faces were numbed to the bone. At such times, the paddlers were thankful for their foam "pogies" - hand insulators fixed to the paddles. <i>(May 2001,NZ Geographic,51/25</i>)

### pogue
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is a lot of gentle snobbery and stirring that goes on between the teeth arms and the service corps or 'pogues' as they are known. (Americans call them 'REMFs' - Rear Echelon Mother Fuckers). <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,40</i>)

### pogue central
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>HUGHES</b> I had to ask what a BAW was. It stood for 'Base Area Wellington', an administrative grouping of the camps and units in that area... BAW comprised the inhabitants of Trentham Camp, Fort Dorset and other miscellaneous ordnance depots and TF units. It was quartered at Trentham, which was a crumbling old man's camp that housed a base workshop and a base supply battalion. It was pogue central. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,131</i>)

### poguery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUGHES</b> I was also favoured by the fact that Martin Taylor, the epitome of licentious poguery, was posted to the camp. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,139</i>)

### poha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They are one of only a few whanau who continue the tradition of making poha, or containers used for preserving muttonbirds. <i>(Feb 3, 2004,Dominion Post TV Week,8</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... the cargo with which they had been freighted, consisting chiefly of `poha-titi' or casks of preserved mutton birds. Many of these were from five to six feet high, and ornamented with feathers: they were all designed as presents to relatives at Waiateruati, or Banks's Peninsula; ... The `poha,' which I have called a cask - as it performs the office of one - is constructed by the natives in an ingenious manner, worthy of description. A kelp bag - the air bladder of a fucus - is easily found of the size required, made by nature. In this the young `titi' are packed, after being cooked, and the oil which has escaped in the cooking is poured on them. Over the exterior of the bag is then laid the bark of the `totara' tree, ... <i>(Jan 18, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,224</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> There is great taste shown by the natives in the poha, or bag of preserved wekas. <i>(1847,Great Journey,54,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> POHA, a sort of cask shaped like a sugar loaf, constructed from the air bladder of a species of sea-weed, strengthened outside by layers of the bark of the `totara,' and kept firmly together by means of stakes tied with flax. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,312</i>)

>  Out on line by door bull-kelp bags blown up and drying. Under the table finished pohas stacked. Birds in kelp bags with totara bark wrapping, looking fine in their new flax baskets. <i>(Apr 20, 1952,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 diary in 1982 Sansom In the Grip of an Island 153]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> There is great taste shown by the natives in the poha, or bag of preserved wekas; and I believe it is always made for a present, for which they expect a return. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,285</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from Brunner, 1847]

>  Then there is the "whare whata", or storehouse, in which the finished kits of mutton birds ("poha") are stored ready for shipment home. <i>(Jul 2, 1902,Otago Witness,72</i>)

>  For the tangi there were five bullocks, 10 sheep, 2000 mutton birds in poha (Maori bags), 1 ton potatoes, 60 or 70 tins of biscuits and lollies ... <i>(Jul 24, 1907,Otago Witness,39</i>)

### pohiri
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of powhiri q.v. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Pohiri at Maromahue Marae, Waiotahe 10 30 am: Waiotahe Urupa 12noon: RSA Cemetery, Whakatane 1.00: Hakari, 53 Goulstone Rd Whakatane <i>(Oct 28, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,44</i>)

>  Uncle's coming home from overseas. It might be a big pohiri because he was away for a long time. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,250</i>)

### pohou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The name `tutu' is applied to three distinct species of the ... natural order <i>Coriariae: Coriaria ruscifolia,</i> L. (<i>C. sarmentosa</i>, Forst., <i>C. arborea</i> and <i>C. tutu</i>, Lindsay, tutu, pohou, and tupakihi of the Maori), is commonly known as the tree-toot ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/347</i>)

### pohowera
 
 <i>n.</i> the dotterel

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 48. <i>Charadrius obscurus. Gmel</i>. ... Ha-poko-wera of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst</i> Tuturiwatu of the natives. <i>Yate. </i>To this order may also be referred two other birds spoken of by Mr. Yate under the names of Pukunui, Pututo. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/195</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/195]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 64. CHARADRIUS BICINCTUS. Jard. Dotterel. Pokowera <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,24</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CHARADRIUS BICINCTUS. (BANDED DOTTREL.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Tuturiwhata {, Tuturiwhatu,} and Pohowera. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,210,[1888 2nd edn 2/3] ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Ochthodromus ... bicinctus </i>... below white with a band of black on the chest and another of chestnut on the [80] upper abdomen; ... Banded Dotterel. POHOWERA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,79</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BANDED DOTTEREL <i>Charadrius bicinctus Other names</i>: Double-banded Dotterel, Mountain Plover, Tuturiwhatu, Pohowera. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,122</i>)

>  <i>Banded Dotterel Charadrius bicinctus </i>JARDINE & SELBY, 1827 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pohowera, tuturiwhatu, double-banded plover.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,180</i>)

### pohue
 
 <i>n.</i> a name used for several native climbing plants: a) convolvulus; b) clematis; c) a Muehlenbeckia. [Maori pohutukawa[o-macron]] <i>Calystegia sepium</i>, bell climber, bindweed, greater convolvulus

>  Every one of them, even Paul Tuhaere, who was related by marriage to the deceased chief, had around their heads or hats massive wreaths of the creeping plant known as pohue, and in some places as pikiarero. <i>(Sep 23, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  "When a warrior of old wished to warn of the deviousness of an enemy," says Murdoch Riley in his book <i>Maori Healing and Herbal</i>, "he would say simply 'the roots of the pohue are many,' or 'his mouth is like the creeping convolvulus' - meaning that his thoughts spread underground like the runners of the convolvulus." <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  <i>po[o]hue</i>, a vine (Calystegia Soldanella), sand-bine: C. sepium is paraha. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  The roots of the<i> pohue</i>, the common convolvulus or bindweed (<i>Convolvulus sepium)</i>, were also ... dug up and cooked for food. <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/27</i>)

>  When the Maories [sic] reached New Zealand, they lived on such things as were indigenous to the island, vis., manuka giant, low fern, pohue (a creeping plant), some few fruits, the native dog and rat, the guana, the kiwi, and the moa. <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,76</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Pohua the convolvulus <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,1842-43/50</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Calystegia <i>sepium</i>, Br. ... Nat. name, `Panake and Pohue', <i>Col</i>. ... This beautiful plant, the English `Bind-weed', is as common in the Southern Hemisphere as it is in the Northern, being found in Chili, Australia, Tasmania, and Java ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 183</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pohue, <i>Col[enso]. Convolvulus sepium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The roots of the common convolvulus (<i>pohue</i>: Convolvulus sepium) were carefully dug up and cooked ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,99</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Calystegia sepium, R.Br.] Pohue; Panahe; </i>Bindweed. ... Widely dispersed in most temperate countries and everywhere highly variable. <i>(1906,NZ Flora,476</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>C. sepium</i> was known to the Maoris as `<i> pohue,</i>' and the roots were used by them for food. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,343,1940 p. 363 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> GREATER CONVOLVULUS [BINDWEED] (<i> Calystegia sepium </i>) has the same popular names as the last species, [i.e. convolvulus] and in additions, beautiful white devil, and its native name of pohue. It is much like convolvulus [bindweed], but is larger in all its parts. The leaves are 3 to 5 inches in length and the flower 2 inches across, more usually white than pink. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,137,1967 ed.</i>)

>  a vine (Calystegia Soldanella), sand-bine: C. sepium is paraha. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,1/62. 4/492</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> pohuehue [=] large-leaved muehlenbeckia: wire vine </i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,60</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i> Muehlenbeckia ... australis </i> ... [51] Maori Vine. PO-HUE-HUE (creeper). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,50</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pohuehue, <i> Col[enso]. Polygonum complexum </i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pohuihui, <i> Geolog. Surv. Passiflora tetrandra. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Muehlenbeckia australis



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Muehlenbeckia Astoni

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> Muehlenbeckia axilaris

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> Muehlenbeckia complexa

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> SCRUB POHUEHUE <i> M. complexa </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  The following interest list of the native and botanical names of a number of indigenous trees and shrubs has been prepared by Mr. H. Graham, of the Brunner railway service, and placed at our disposal for publication .. Pohuehue, Muhlenbeckia complexa [sic] ... <i>(Oct 1, 1896,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  <i> small-leaved pohuehue [=] wire vine </i>[s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,70</i>)

### pohuehue
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> I climbed to old slip faces where the big bush had gone,replaced by grassy colonising plants - the densely laced cushions, the myriad small, round leaves of pohuehue, the toetoe, the mangemange, the sharp grasses. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,128</i>)

>  Typical of the sites he surveyed was Macraes Flat, where, in 1986, he noted that porcupine shrubs, snowberry plants and pohuehue vines were stripped of all their ripe fruit where they grew on outcrops inhabited by grand skinks, while plants on nearby outcrops without skinks were untouched. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/70</i>)

>  Taupata are a versatile plant - I've seen them grown as a beautiful windblown hedge, planted with creeping small-leafed pohuehue (Muehlenbackia complexa) along the base to fill in any gaps, making a fantastic foliage contrast. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

### pohuiakaroa
 
 <i>n.</i> sea-perch

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> SEA PERCH (<i> Sebastes percoides </i>). ... `The pohuiakaroa,' Dr. Hector says, `deserves mention, as it represents in these seas the genus to which the Norway haddock belongs. It is one of the most frequent and troublesome fish, caught in a moderate depth of water round the coast, and especially in rocky harbours, being almost worthless as food, and armed with so many sharp spines that it is a difficult task to detach it from the hook. It is the proper sea-perch of these waters.' <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i> Red Gurnet-Perch, n.</i> name given in Victoria to the fish <i> Sebastes percoides,</i> Richards., family <i> Scorpeaenidae. It is also called <i> Poddy; Red Gurnard </i>, or <i> Gurnet;</i> and in New Zealand, <i> Pohuikaroa </i>. [sic] <i>(1898,Austral English,384</i>)

>  HELICOLENUS PERCOIDES (Richardson). <i> Sea-perch; Pohuiakaroa </i>. Secured in rocky localities among blue cod. Large numbers common in Christchurch Markets during early summer months. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (not so common in the north). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SEAPERCH (POHUIAKAROA) JOCK STUART, HIGHLANDER, FIVEFINGER, SOLDIERFISH or SCRODDIE <i> Helicolenus papillosus </i> (Bloch and Schneider) The seaperch is a fish well known to all fishermen both sports and commercial fishermen alike, who give it quite a number of vernacular names, ... <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,344</i>)

>  <i> Helicolenus percoides </i> (Richardson). SEA-PERCH; POHUIAKAROA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,53,1971 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Bibliography of N.Z. Fishes]

>  47. <i> Scorpaena percoides </i> Richardson. Sea Perch. Maori: Pohuiakaroa <i>(1929,TrNZI,61149</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  Their Maori name is Pohui-akaroa, and their scientific name Sebastes percoldes [sic]; but no fisherman could use such a name as that, so we call them "Stuarts," because that is the sort of colours they generally wear... <i>(Apr 23, 1902,Otago Witness,56</i>)

### pohuihui
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pohuehue](per-pop.md#pohuehue))

### pohute
 
 <i>n.</i> pohutukawa

>  <b>MASON</b> [LOMAS] ... bends to avoid the hanging branch of the pohutukawa. Tell Johnny his first job's to cut back the pohute. <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,85</i>)

### pohutukawa
 
 <i>n.</i> [<i>Metrosideros excelsa</i>]

>  Ten Pounds Reward .. will be paid for such information as will convict the person or persons who within the last month have been cutting and removing the roots or branches or both of the pohutukawa from my land near the Watchman. <i>(Mar 9, 1858,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  In a narrow valley a little distance from the hut a path led along a steep bank and descended abruptly by a drop of about ten feet through a small opening in a thick mat of the foliage of stunted pohutukawa. <i>(May 22, 1930,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  <b>CARMAN</b> Pohutukawa are our remarkable trees - the symbol of New Zealand, as the oak is the emblem of England. Like the oak, the pohutukawa has a short, stout gnarled trunk that becomes beautifully gnarled with time, the wide-spreading branches forming a most stately and memorable tree... The name "pohutukawa" comes from "hutukawa" (a head-dress of red feathers) and "po" which has meany meanings, including night or underworld. Governor Grey interpreted this as meaning "the tree with red adornment near the sea", but "po" could equally be a reference to the traditional tree at Cape Reinga where the spirits of the dead descend via the pohutukawa tree to a secret cave, the entrance to Te Reinga, the spirit or underworld [sic]. A small, scrubby version still clings to the cliff edge at the leaping-off place of the spirits. In Maori, to say someone "has slid down the pohutukawa root" is a lyrical way of saying he has joined the ancestors. <i>(Jan 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  Pohutukawas are overrunning protected scrubland in Western Cape, South Africa... <i>(Nov 3, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Pohutukawa N.Z Christmas tree - Metosideras[sic] excelsior <i>(1989,Chosen Place,104</i>)

>  Pohutukawa honey from Barrier was so sought after, claims Les, that it found its way to King George's table. <i>(Jan 1991,NZ Geographic,113</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> His farmhouse was on a headland, sheltered by unkempt pohutukawas, the pakeha's Christmas tree. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,149</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ... the fact remains that in some localities [the non-epiphytic rata <i>M. robusta</i> [contrast <i>M. florida</i>]] are frequent enough to attract the special attention of the bushman, who calls this form the `inland pohutukawa', a designation he also bestows upon symmetrical specimens of true pohutukawa sometimes found in the forest. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/269</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk NZ Pittosporads)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i> Metrosideros excelsa </i> (formerly <i> M. tomentosa </i>) POHUTUKAWA [old name, 2x ref, no citations]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen) [omitted from 1940 ed] nf W3 f.]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen) [omitted from 1940 ed] nf W3 f.]

### Pohutukawa Festival
 
 <i>n.</i> a celebration of summer flowering of pohutukawa

>  Coromandel's Pohutukawa Festival runs Nov 25-Dec 11, celebrating the summer flower that enhances Coromandel's coastal beauty. <i>(Nov 1, 2005,NZ Herald,E8</i>)

### poi
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib]

>  The rhythmic properties of the Maori language and the poi rhythms are the root, they're in all the songs. <i>(Oct 18, 2002,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Now, also, are sedulously practised, haka and poi swinging; but these do not irk; oh, dear, no! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,89, 91</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [The Maoris] sang grandly, especially a very fine poi song. <i>(Mar 12, 1915,New Zealander's Diary,30,1922 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> ... the rhythm of their dancing and the beauty of their poi girls. <i>(1943,Brown Man's Burden,88,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> And the poi tunes and melodies [Maoris] use in canoes. [fn] poi = Maori dance <i>(1958,Spinster,47</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... the poi women began their dance to slow chanting ... the tiny pois danced against arms and shoulders and breasts ... <i>(1935,Little Country, The,147,1937 ed.</i>)

>  [Photo caption] Some of the Poi Girls who performed at the Maori Carnival in the Choral Hall. <i>(Jul 2, 1902,Otago Witness,48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [an informal game]

>  Among the amusements was the "poi" game, in which rows of girls stood up and juggled poi balls to the rhythmic accompaniments of a song and graceful movements of the body. <i>(Apr 5, 1899,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Another game is with one ball (poi) suspended from a string. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/57</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Amid her damsels, scarlet-crowned/ With <i>kowhai</i>-flowers, a lively ring/ Playing at `poi' sent flying round/ [157] The ornamented ball o'erwound ... <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/156</i>)

>  The Maori game of <i>poi</i> (ball) has been brought before the public lately in connection with some utterances of the great Maori prophet Te Whiti, and here for the first time we saw the girls playing <i>poi</i>. <i>(Aug 22, 1885,Otago Witness,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [Any of various poi dances or songs using a poi or pois on strings]

>  [Photo caption] Canoe Poi by Maori Women. <i>(Aug 26, 1908,Otago Witness,45</i>)

>  The poi, a ball dance by Titokowaru's young women was a novelty. <i>(Dec 31, 1884,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  The performers were attired in Native costume, and opened the programme with a haka of welcome; then followed the poi Waero (the most ancient poi), most gracefully given by the champion prize poi performers. <i>(Sep 11, 1914,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Among other dances was the <i>poi</i>, danced by Maori maidens, who ... manipulated a couple of balls, ... one in each hand. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,155</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The open marae where the sun beats down relentlessly is now deserted. <i>Afterwards there were pois and there were hakas, ... and there was to be a korero on politics and the land question ... </i> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,13,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Mum taught them the short, long and double poi [fn] <i>poi</i> - dance performed to music with a light ball attached to a string. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,32</i>)

>  After a stirring poi performed by Putiki Maori Club, Prince Edward said he was delighted to be be back in New Zealand ... <i>(Jul 16, 1986,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> MIRI. Oh yes! I'm going to do the long poi! <i>(1969,Awatea,24,1978 ed.</i>)

>  After the single poi had finished, the tots dropped out and the others gave the more difficult and elaborate double poi dance, which was performed with the precision of clockwork. <i>(Mar 29, 1900,Evening Post,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a light ball on a string swung to a rhythmic beat in Maori songs and dances. [Maori]; [aka poi ball, poi-ball, poe]

>  In all, the royal visitors to-day received 40 flax mats, 20 feather mats (many of them made from rare kiwi feathers), six greenstone meres, three clubs or taiahas of carved whalebone, a large model of a canoe, and many feather kits and poi balls. <i>(Jun 26, 1901,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  The poi is a small hollow ball, of plaited flax, and is swung from the wrist in movements very similar to those of modern club-swinging. <i>(Mar 29, 1900,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  We recorded real poi - Mina had to poi her arms off! <i>(Oct 18, 2002,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  <b>Nicholas</b> They made Mr. Marsden [318] a present of a ball called a poe, with which the ladies amuse themselves by throwing it repeatedly backward and forward; it is ... made of their cloth or canvas, stuffed with the down of the bull-rush, having a long string appended to it, ... <i>(1871,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/317</i>)

>  At the conclusion of this troupe's performance, which was arrived at by the whole making a curious sidling movement towards the entrance of the ring, and then with a simultaneous shout rushing tumultuously away, Titikowaru was seen outside the chequered ring of humanity which bounded the amphitheatre, at the head of the damsels who were to bring the performance to a close by executing the haka of the poi or ball. <i>(Dec 30, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  In this poi half of the dancers dress in plain, light-coloured "korowai", the other half in the "kahu kiwi," the sombre yet valuable kiwi feathered mat. [9] The word "poi" means a to and fro motion. <i>(1918,Link, The,8</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|oi,<i> s</i>. A round ball with which children play. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,194</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Running, climbing, swimming, wrestling, flying kites, and tossing the <i>poi</i>, a ball about the size of a good cricket-ball, are most of the games of native origin. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i> poi</i>, a light ball made from raupo blades, with a short string attached to it which is swung and twisted rhythmically, in the manner of Indian clubs, to the accompaniment of a song; the so-called poi-dance, which is less a dance than a whirling of the balls and a swaying of the body in time with the song. There is a long-poi, in which the string is perhaps a yard long. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> The<i> poi</i> is an egg-shaped thing, made of the pith from a swamp plant covered with a woven cover of <i>raupo,</i> somewhat like the leaves of the cattail. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,18</i>)

>  <i>poi</i>: small stuffed ball; ball game. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) poi: a light ball of flax swung by a string, used in Maori dances. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Whirling poi was easy, but flipping it so that it slapped first on the top of the wrist, then underneath, was the hard part. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,201</i>)

>  <i>poi n</i>, pl <i>poi</i> or <i>pois</i> [Maori]: a small ball which is made typically of flax, grass, or rushes, to which a string of varying length is attached, and which is swung rhythmically by Maori performers in various dances and songs <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1749</i>)

>  <i> poi n</i>. light ball on the end of a string used by Maori woman at ceremonial dances [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> POI Flax ball on end of flax string <i>(1967,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> poi: a ball of flax swinging on a flax thong, swung in unison by girl performers. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <i>poi ... n</i>. light ball of raupo reed on end of string used by Maori women in a graceful dance [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>poi n. N.Z.</i> a ball of woven New Zealand flax swung rhythmically by Maori women while performing <i>poi dances.</i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,874</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 580; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 373]]

>  We understand that after the close of the performance at the Theatre to-night, Maori mats, carved sticks, poi balls, Maori baskets, kiwi feather mats, &c, will be on sale, and those remaining after the performance may effect purchases. <i>(Oct 2, 1895,Colonist, The,2</i>)

>  [Photo caption] The girl with the poi balls in her hand is a noted poi dancer, and leads the whole party at the different dances. <i>(May 30, 1906,Otago Witness,41</i>)

### poi ball
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka poi]

>  The poi balls commence to spin, the deft hands twirling them move up and down, sideways, backwards and forwards, hovering over the shoulders, now over and across the knee, the whirling balls appearing to surround, as with a network of gossamer, the bodies of the dancers as they sway from side to side, lifting alternate feet and throwing one across, gently forward with a lilting motion, giving the general effect of a waltz step. <i>(1918,Link, The,8</i>)

>  The old men often amused themselves with ... encouraging the younger ones ...in playing with the <i>poi</i>-ball. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/3/27</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  The arts and crafts section comprised tukutuku, ... plaiting (rourou [fn A small basket used for cooked food.], rough kits and floor mats), taniko work and making poi balls. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/212</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,13, 67</i>)

>  The poi balls fell to rest. <i>(Oct 12, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,94</i>)

### poi bird
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of poe (the tui)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> The most remarkable bird is one to which Cook's people gave the name of mocking-bird, from the extraordinary variety of its notes. There is also another which is called by the English the poe, or poi bird, from a little tuft of white curled feathers which it has under its throat, and which seemed to them to resemble certain white flowers worn as ornaments by the people of otaheite, and known there [188] by a similar name. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,187</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  POI DANCE AT THE OPENING OF THE MAORI HOUSE AT PORIRUA, WELLINGTON. <i>(Jun 5, 1901,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The women go through a pretty and curious performance called the `poi' dance. Its chief feature is the excellent time that they keep with their `pois,' or inflated bladders, held by strings and thrown about with the utmost precision to their weird, droning notes. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,107</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,29</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>poi</i>, a light ball made from raupo blades, with a short string attached to it which is swung and twisted rhythmically, in the manner of Indian clubs, to the accompaniment of a song; the so-called poi-dance, which is less a dance than a whirling of the balls and a swaying of the body in time with the song. There is a long-poi, in which the string is perhaps a yard long. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Be sure that when a poi dance is suggested, and while the maidens are displaying their most captivating attitudes, one of them ... glides outside [to a tryst]. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,91</i>)

>  They also did poi dances and sang songs in Maori. <i>(Oct 28, 1924,Otago Witness,72</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

>  [Photo caption] The girl with the poi balls in her hand is a noted poi dancer, and leads the whole party at the different dances. <i>(May 30, 1906,Otago Witness,41</i>)

### poi party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>2/51</b> After a night spent in the usual declaration by the ope [ed fn: Visiting group] that it wished to return the next day and to have ashes deposited in their last resting-place before departure and the usual pupuri [ed fn: Invitation to remain] by the tangata whenua the casket was borne to the vault. Three Taranaki poi parties, six persons representing three Hahi [ed fn: Churches], a gathering representative of the Crown, Parliament (all three parties in the Lower House and the Acting-Leader of the Upper), the Press and Pakeha public and all sections of Maoridom, gathered together all the elements that constitute the New Zealand community of to-day, ... <i>(Sep 20, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,1987</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

### poik
 
 <i>n.</i> ? poet

### point
 
 <i>n.</i> [shearing]

>  <b>PERRY</b> The wool should be lustrous, soft to the touch, free from kemp, with a blocky tip, and well spread on the back, belly, and points. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,44</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> The sheep they shore were not so well woolled on the belly and carried less wool on the points than they do now, so that fewer short blows were required to take off the trimmings; ... <i>(1938,High Country,84</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Fribby</i>, ... the yolky locks round the points taken of by the ... roller from a decently skirted fleece. <i>(1948,Early Canterbury Runs,379,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The Corriedale ... grows more wool than hair on the hocks, thereby making it imperative that they be shorn trimmed to the feet. These points or socks do not have a tendency to lift or rise off the skin. <i>Ibid.</i> 137 The Romney is a well-woolled sheep, covered right down on to the points. At the right time of the year these points lift or rise off the skin allowing the comb and the handpiece to enter easily and the blows to finish out on the points without poking. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,132</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> POINTS. - The points or extremities of the fleece, i.e., the flanks, shanks, neck and head-pieces.] <i>(1899,Clip of Wool,66</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> They made Mr. Marsden [318] a present of a ball called a <i>poe</i>, with which the ladies amuse themselves by throwing it repeatedly backward and forward; it is ... made of their cloth or canvas, stuffed with the down of the bull-rush, having a long string appended to it, ... <i>(1871,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/317</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [shearing]

>  <b>GORDON</b> After <i>plucking</i>, a <i>trimmer </i>cuts <i>points</i> off the top of the brisket. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The first beast nears the top with torn neck and dragging head. A quick glance to make sure the points are not open, a firm grip of the foreleg and I am swung across the floor until I can get it under control and drag the dead weight on to the second tackle. Change the points and manhandle the second beast on to the first tackle ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,12</i>)

>  <b>bow</b> The Corriedale ... grows more wool than hair on the hocks, thereby making it imperative that they be shorn trimmed to the feet. These points or socks do not have a tendency to lift or rise off the skin.] <i>(1956,Wool Away!,12/132</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to impose upon dishonestly or dishonourably

>  A certain section of bookmakers make a practice of `pointing' during the progress of a race by roaring out a short price about a horse they say is in front is another prad altogether, and in luring the public to invest on it they are doing nothing short of obtaining money under false pretences. <i>(Oct 29, 1910,NZ Truth,279/2/5</i>)

>  <i>Point</i>.- Loaf on (your mates); let them do the hardest of the work. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>To point</i>, to impose upon [shared with Australia] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [drug measure]

>  The <i>Listener</i> spoke to a number of "casual" users of Pure who say they buy it by the "point" (10 points = one gram) for around $100 to $120. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim says definitions in British dictionaries relate <i>point</i> to the seacoast (headland, cape, promontory - "a high point jutting out into the sea"). NSOD gives as <i>chiefly US</i> "a tapering extremity of land etc.. constituting a geographical feature". This echoes the common use of point in NZ in <i>inland</i> placenames e.g. Arthur's Point, Awakino Point, Pleasant Point, Titoki Point, Ti-tree Point." I have not noticed any inland "Points" in England or in a cursory skim through British gazetteers. This use of the word may be worth followng up.]

>  <b>ADKIN</b> "Paki's Point" is the name given to the tongue of land within an acute meander of the Manawatu River immediately above the confluence of the Oroua River. <i>(1986,Horowhenua,284</i>) [NOTES: check page and edition; 1st edition 1948]



 
 <i>n.</i> a measure of rainfall [ca1939 Havelock school]

>  A TERRIFIC storm raged at Inverwell on the afternoon of 11th inst., during which, in 15 minutes, 67 points of rain were recorded, but probably much more fell, as the rain was driving fiercely. <i>(Jan 2, 1889,Te Aroha News,3</i>)

>  In 45 minutes just after ten there were 88 points of rain, and in an hour and a quarter an inch and a quarter fell. <i>(Jan 5, 1892,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  Wellington registered 68 points of rain and Wanganui 10 points. <i>(Jun 29, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (chiefly U.S.), such a feature on a river; the tapering extremity of any woodland reaching down into a prairie or other treeless area; any tapering extremity of land, or of rocks, woods, etc., constituting a special feature of this. 1637 in <i>Amer. Speech</i> (1940) XV.297/2 Easterly butting



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hock of a sheep or the wool that grows on it.

### pointer
 
 <i>n.</i> sc in the Southern Cross

>  Here in our southern skies we have ... [146] Crux Australis, ... and Centaurus, with the two neighbouring first-class stars in the legs of the Centaur, commonly called the `Pointers'. ... The constellation Crux Australis, or the Southern Cross ... forming with the two `Pointers' such a glorious sight on a starlight night, has ever been an object of universal attraction. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/145</i>)

>  <b>GREY</b> When I looked out of my tent window I could see the Southern Cross and the Pointers that pointed to it. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,38</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> The Southern Cross and the Pointers are clear in the sky. <i>(1936,Politician, The,71,1987 ed.</i>)

>  At about 7 p.m. the "pointers" put about east and west, but observation shows that the revolution of the earth on its axis makes the Southern Cross and the pointers appear to revolve in a circle. <i>(Jun 28, 1905,Otago Witness,79</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> one of the pair of animals next to the pole in a bullock team

>  <i>pointers n.</i> pl. in a bullock team, the pair ahead of the pole pair. in a team. See quotation. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  Usually the third pair of bullocks from the back were called the `point' bullocks or pointers. ... strongest in the team ... to pull hard in chain when rounding corners. <i>(1915,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [Radio Talk 3: TS][c1914] 1951 H.S. McCARROLL The Days of the Kauri Bushmen]

>  <i>pointer n.</i> one of two bullocks next to the pole in a team. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>pointer n.</i> one of two bullocks next to the pole in a team. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>C.H. EDEN</b> `Twelve bullocks is the usual number in a team, the two polers and the leaders being steady old stagers; the pair next to the pole are called the `pointers,' and are also required ti be pretty steady, the remainder being called the `body bullocks,' and it is not necessary to be so particular about their being thoroughly broken in.' <i>(1872,My Wife and I in Queensland,36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a schemer, idler, bludger (See [cf. point v](per-pop.md#point))

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> I believe he is a bit of a pointer because he asked me what billet I was coming to and I wouldn't be surprised if he came along any minute. <i>(1935,Slang Today,288</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 288]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> I suppose at that time [c1860s goldrush] I would be more interested in grabbing my `dumps' out of the ring at school, when some quick eye had noticed that a young pointer had tried to get away with placing a stinkie or a chalkie into play with the rest of his marbles. The `Grab your dumps' was the roar, as we all rushed in for our own, and anybody else's one could get. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,22</i>)

### poka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The most common species are the poka (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) and the katote (<i>Hemitelia smithii</i>); the stem and fronds of the latter are sometimes used as food for cows. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/217</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>MONRO</b> A tree, which I have not seen in this part of the country [Molyneux], is also sufficiently abundant there. Its leaf is like the rata, but its stem is clean and cylindrical, of a white colour, a good deal fissured. The native name of it is pokaka, and with the bark of it a black dye is made, which is equal to the dye of the hinau. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson <i>Examiner </i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 249]

### pokaka
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Elaeocarpus hookerianus</i>, a tree like the hinau, but smaller. [ORIGIN: Maori, aka bokaka, bokaka]

>  The native name of it is pokaka, and with the bark of it a black dye is made, which is equal to the dye of the hinau. <i>(Aug 24, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,99</i>)

>  FOR SALE - Right to cut 200 acres good bush White Pine and Bokaka, 3/4 mile from railway [s]iding; 2 to 3 years work. <i>(Aug 1, 1904,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> [In Otago] I sketched a tree called Pakaka from the bark of which the natives procure a beautiful Black dye like that of hinau. <i>(1845,Journal,178</i>)

>  The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara. ... That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound: ... <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Elaeocarpus <i> Hookerianus, </i> Raoul. ... Nat. name, `Hinau', <i>Raoul</i>; `Pokaka', and `Mahimahi', <i>Colenso.</i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> [?S.I form] Bocarro, or Bokako, <i> Hector. Elaeocarpus Hookerianus</i>. <i>Ibid.</i> 767 Pokaka, <i> Col[enso]. Elaeocarpus dentatus</i> and <i>Hookerianus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  <b>McINDOE</b> Timbers most in demand are totara, black and white pine, red pine, goi -, bokaka (Eleocarpus dentatus) ... <i>(1878,Sketch of Otago,29</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen)]

>  Amongst smaller trees and shrubs the most prominent are the horoeka(<i>Panax crassifolia</i>), and its congeners <i> Panax simplex</i> and <i> P. edgerleyi,</i> both exhibiting such excessive luxuriance as frequently to require an examination of the fruit before their identification can be determined; the rautawhiri (<i> Pittosporum tenuifolium,</i> var. <i> colensoi</i>), the kotukutuku (<i> Fuchsia excorticata</i>), the mako (<i> Aristotelia racemosa</i>), the mapau (<i> Myrsine australis</i>), the pokako[sic] (<i> Eloeocarpus hookerianus</i>), also <i> Carpodetus serratus, Myrsine divaricata</i>, both of which are abundant. The remarkable `weeping tree', <i> Myrsine montana</i>, with its crowded recurved branches and small foliage, affords a picturesque effect <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  [Native name] Pokaka .. [Settlers' name] Pokaka .. [Family] Tiliaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> ELOEOCARPUS HOOKERIANUS (<i> Raoul.) ... The `POKAKO'[sic].- This species is indigenous to the hilly portions of the Northern, and common in the Middle Island. ... [66] Its native name is `Pokako', or `Mahi-mahi', but it is very commonly called `Hinau' by the settlers. In the same manner the `Hinau' in the Northern Island is sometimes called `Pokako'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,65</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> <i>(1897,Counterfeit Seal</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen)]

>  Besides the broadleaf (<i> Griselinia littoralis</i>), fuchsia (<i> F. excorticata</i>) hinahina <i>(Melicytus ramiflorus</i>), and other widely distributed trees, an occasional pokaka (<i> Elaeocarpus Hookerianus</i>) and pennantia (<i> Pl corymbosa</i>) were to be found. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/280</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Elaeocarpus Hookerianus (The Pokaka)</i>. A smaller species, called Pokaka by the Maoris. The flowers do not open out so widely as those of the Hinau. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,261</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Underscrub dense .... Matipo, Ini-ini, konini, Pokaka ... <i>Ibid..</i> (1954) 242 The under-scrub was very dense, Matipo, Ini-Ini, Konini, Pokaka, Mick-a-Mick, Ohau, Ribbonwood, Ngaio, Lemonwood and others, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,231,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i> po[o]ka[a]ka[a]</i>, a tree (Elaeocarpus Hookerianus): <i>E. dentatus</i>, also so called, but more usually known as hinau. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Herrick stated (<i> in litt</i>., 1964) that moose fed on pokaka (<i> Eleaeocarpus hookerianus</i>) [in Fiordland Sounds]...Apparently Pokaka is not a very common tree in that district. <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,214</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> At the season when bokakas dropped their berries the wild pigs made a migration from tree to tree, and a `Captain Cooker' was not despised [in Southland c1900]. [fn] Bokaka : Pokaka. <i>(1953,Chaslands,82</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Elaeocarpus hookerianus</i> POOKAAKAA ... Tree with very pale, corky bark. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,72</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 1958,NZ Timber Journal,62/1</i>)

>  59 [Lithograph] of pokaka foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,17</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> POKAKO.- (<i>Elaeocarpus hookerianus</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,22</i>)

>  <i>Elaeocarpus Hookerianus </i> <i>(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>(1864,Handbook NZ Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Elaeocarpus dentatus </i> <i>(1890,Descriptive & Priced List</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Pokaka .. Elaeocarpus Hookerianus .. Timber white and tough; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  Cedar, or pahautea, pokaka, and hinau are not unfrequent, while most of the beeches are plentiful, especially in the southern parts of the district. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,119</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i> Elaeocarpus Hookerianus] Pokaka</i>. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,86</i>)

>  Pokako (Elaeocarpus Hookerianus). A large round-headed tree, near Dunedin; common also on the west coast. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 Pokako. Elaeocarpus Hookerianus, Raoul. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/34</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]
