### Pokarekare Ana
 
 <i>n.</i> a popular Maori song

>  The documentary <i>Pokarekare Ana: A Maori Love Song</i>.. on the origins of the song, surrounds the contributions made by Sir Apirana Ngata and Paraire Tomoana with a misleading element of mystery... For a number of reasons, "Pokarekare" is unlikely tobeen the original work of either of these men. (1) "Pokarekare" was published in sheet music form in 1926 (twice), in 1927, 1936 and 1940 prior to Paraire Tomoana's death in 1946. The two 1926 versions are said to have been arranged by "Hemi Piripata" and "Alfred Hill": respectively. the 1927 version is said to have been arranged by "P H Tomoana and Archie Don". The 1936 version is said to have been "collected and sung by Ernest McKinley" and the 1940 version is again said to have ben arranged by "Hemi Piripata". in short, no published version of the song, including the 1919 booklet entry, gave any credit to either Apirana Ngata or to P H Tomoana for composing the song in their lifetimes... The most likely conclusion would seem to be that what has affectionately become "New Zealand's unofficial anthem" originated in the far north of New Zealand circa 1900 and was adapted. modified and developed in its journey through the country by a number of gifted Maori composers of the day. <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>) [NOTES: David Smith (Mandeville North, Kaiapoi)]

>  ...Tomoana explains the song's origin: "7. Pakarekare. These ditties emanated in the North of Auckland and were popularised in Narrowneck Camp, and eventually drifted to ... the East Cape. There they took the present form ... and acquired close association with the last drafts of single men from the East Cape and Wairoa districts ... the 19th and 20th Maori Reinforcements." <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  In 1978 I went with a Hamilton Teachers College kapa haka student group on a tour of Aboriginal settlements in Australia's Northern Territory... The group refused to include "Pokarekare Ana" in their repertoire because in their eyes the song had become a parody of the original, with "squashed banana" lyrics... At the Adelaide Festival, Rolf Harris asked us to accompany him in his latest hit, "Pokarekare Ana."... Anyone from Dame Kiri Te Kanawa down can sing "Pokarekare". It adds to the popular culture of New Zealand. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  On hearing "Pokarekare" for the first time George Bernard Shaw immediately identified the music as a Swiss cradle song. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  ...all too often what happens is someone gets sentimental and the song somehow stumbles on to become a mumbled medley involving "Pokarekareana". The full lyrics to which are "Pokarekareana Something Something Roto-rua". <i>(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### poke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sullivan said to me `Did you see his poke?' meaning the bulk of his pocket. <i>(1866,Maungatapu Murders,72</i>)

>  <b>BURGESS</b> ... Kelly observed after he had passed, `Did you take notice what a poke he had?' - meaning thereby the bulk of his pocket. <i>(1866,Confessions,117,1983 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (for sweets) [cf. pokeful ca1920 Les Souness (Southland and Dunedin) A twisted paper spill for lollies with top folded over. ca1935-1940. A. Campbell - Dunedin] (See [cf. pokeful](per-pop.md#pokeful))

>  <b>LEE</b> `Hullo, Milly.' `Hullo.' `Have a lollie?' he had a poke of sweets. Milly had one. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,131,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> We were not encouraged to buy lollies [on Stewart Island c1901] if we had a penny, although it was tempting when other children seemed to have pokes of lollies so often. (A `poke' was a cone-shaped twist of newspaper.) <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,153</i>)

>  <b>HARWOOD</b> A penny could [in early 20C.] buy a full `paper poke' - that popular lolly bag made of a triangle of paper. <i>(1982,Heritage Trail,58</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Even when a poke of amphetamines was found hidden behind the switchboard, he blandly claimed it must have been there when he took over the bach. <i>(1983,Murder to Burn,13</i>)

>  <b>BLAIR</b> I'll find the poke boss. / Truly it is no hollow bag. <i>(1934,By Pacific Waters</i>) [NOTES: Mrs Douglas Blair [`Heather in the Fern']]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The average Romney in winter and early spring does not shear well: in many of them at these times the wool seems almost glued on to the skin, so that it is common to see shearers poking the wool off, taking five or six minutes a sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,136</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Our Annual Meetings were held in the hall. ... Then there was supper. There was always a number of children who were usually given a `Poke' - a sandwich, a bun, a piece of cake, and an apple, and a mug of tea. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,51</i>)

### poke borax
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [borak](../B#borak),[borax](bla-bri.md#borax))

### poke out
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [of stock] to stray beyond a boundary or fence. (See [poking](per-pop.md#poking))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Anyway, in the spring these sheep used to poke out onto the lower terraces adjoining the bush. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,154</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. if any sheep have poked out beyond the front facings he can signal the top men. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,211</i>)

### poke the borax
 
 <i>phr.</i> to tease or ridicule (See [borax](bla-bri.md#borax))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Poking the borax - Hungry-gutted bastards - What a bunch of scrubbers - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,163</i>)

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> I was only poking the borax. You jokers just can't take it. <i>(1984,Ratatui,189</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Coasters never miss a chance to poke the borax [159] at the Cathedral City. <i>(1985,G'day Country,158</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>borax </i>in phr. <i>poke the borax</i> to ridicule; most popular spelling here of Aboriginal word `borak' aka `borac' and `borack', perhaps because radio's Aunt Daisy was a great promoter of borax (no relation) powder as a household cleaner. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> You wouldn't be poking the borax, would you. <i>Ibid.</i> 57 ... go on, poke the borax. <i>(1944,Troop Target,34</i>)

>  Those bastards [sc pongo M.P.s] are too bloody fond of poking the borax at a bloke just back from the blue. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,209</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Now you're poking borax at me, Mother! Making me out to be a romancer ... Aren't I a farmer?' <i>Ibid.</i> 195 He knew I was `poking borax' at him, but he couldn't see how to resent it. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,xiii</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Taffy was always poking the borax at the queers and I didn't see the joke at first. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,224</i>)

### poked
 
 <i>adj.</i> annoyed?; exhausted

>  "When you're poked, you're poked. It doesn't matter how much money you're paid." <i>(Nov 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> He had told the boys he'd be out at the lake by nine, it was no good getting up on the skis if you felt poked. <i>(1978,Boy, The Bridge, The River, The,115</i>)

### pokeful
 
 <i>n.</i> a bagful (See [poke n](per-pop.md#poke))

>  <b>LEE</b> I would sneak pokefuls of brown sugar. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,67,1949 ed.</i>)

### pokeka
 
 <i>n.</i> a rain cape

>  In the 1920s, a tattered rain cape, or pokeka, thatched with layer on layer of tussock grass, was recovered from a cave on Mt Benger, above Roxburgh. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> The principal sorts of cloak or mantle at present made by the Maoris from their native flax are - 1. The `Korowai,' or shaggy mantle. 2. The `Kaitaka,' or fine mantle; and 3. The `Pokeha,'[check transcription] or rain cloak. Of these, the variety of flax plant called the `Koura' yields the best fibre for the `Korowai'. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,78</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In those old days [c1840s Maoris] were very skilful at making `bogek' (pokeka in standard, or northern, Maori - it means raincloaks). They seemed to pleat the flax in layers, and all round they left little tails in rows hanging over each layer. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,12</i>)

>  The high class women ... stayed at home and made mats - piupiu from the fibre of flax; pokeka, rain mats from dried flax; ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,252,1988 ed.</i>)

>  Five examples of the korowai mat .. and two pokeka or pake mats, made from long undressed strips of flax skin, are shown. <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

### pokekha
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukeko](pop-pyo.md#pukeko))

### pokekker
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukeko](pop-pyo.md#pukeko))

### poker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> [The boundary-keeper] would know jolly well up which gullies and spurs the fidgety old pokers (shepherds' term for restless sheep that liked to poke away and take others with them) would try to escape. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,8</i>)

### pokeweed
 
 <i>n.</i> inkweed

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Some [128][plants] which are conspicuous in the Auckland Province - the pokeweed (<i>Phytolacea octandra</i>), for instance - are absent farther south. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,127</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> INKWEED (<i>Phytolacea octandra</i>) is also called poke weed, red ink plant, and pheasant weed. It is found on waste land and new pastures from Wangannui northwards, but is commonest in Auckland. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,70</i>)

>  <i>pokeweed</i> [s] <i>Phytolacca americana</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,60</i>)

### pokie
 
 <i>n.</i> poker machine

>  The pokie room at McGinty's is the liveliest spot on Huntly's main street which hums all day with young women pushing jeans and sullen teenage boys in baggy jeans and beanies. <i>(Oct 6, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  [Hdg] Pokies - downright, flat-out good fun...Almost every second Saturday, [she] spends a few hours down at the Featherston Tavern, slotting 20 cent pieces into pokie machines. <i>(Oct 22, 1999,Dominion,9</i>)

>  She was also told she would not get her final week's wages till the pokie keys were handed in. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Dominion,3</i>)

>  For years, racing's leaders have argued the salvation of the beleaguered industry lies in providing more pokies on track. <i>(Apr 23, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B13</i>)

>  Operation of `pokies', or gambling machines, is to be licensed ... Dr Bassett, announced today <i>(Nov 25, 1987,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  "Yeah," I replied, sliding round on the barstool after a round of pokies and a few rounds of bourbon and coke. <i>(Oct 15, 2003,Capital Times,17</i>)

### pokie bar
 
 <i>n.</i> a bar with gaming machines

>  There is also no legal mechanism, to veto the siting of a pokie bar in a neighbourhood that doesn't want one, as Newtown residents discovered two years ago. <i>(Feb 6, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  Pokie bars or "gaming shops" - where pokie machines, not selling alcohol or food, are the main reason for business - will be banned. <i>(Sep 18, 2002,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### poking
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farm stock] breaking through fences into the next paddock.

>  Dairy stock, as we all know to our sorrow, are poking brutes. To replace a broken post, tighten a slack wire or partly re-batten is not a big job. <i>(Jan 23, 1941,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  I suggested that for a start we could bowl over the half-dozen poking hoggets which had been annoying me immensely by forever getting into the next paddock or the next turnip break ... <i>(Sep 1961,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

### pokokatea
 
 <i>n.</i> ?if genuine, a mis-spelling of popokatea, itself a mispelling of POPOKOTEA q.v.

### pokopoko
 
 <i>n.</i> Williams nf: ? a mis-hearing of <i>poroka</i> the maorification of English<i> frog </i>

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> In this district I found the rare New Zealand frog (<i>pokopoko</i>) both in the creeks and deep down in the mines. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,80,1933 ed.</i>)

### pokopokonui-a-hura
 
 <i>n.</i> clematis

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Clematis<i> paviflora</i>, A.Cunn. ... Nat. name, `Poko-Poko nui-ha-ura'. [sic, but check] <i>(1853,Flora NZ,7</i>)

>  Pokopokonuihaura. Clematis parviflora, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/38</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> Pokonuiahuru [Maori name] .. Small Clematis [Settlers' name] .. Clematis parviflora .. Climber <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

### pokotiwha
 
 <i>n.</i> crested penguin

>  <b>BULLER</b> EUDYPTES CHRYSOCOMUS {PACHYRHYNCHUS}. (CRESTED PENGUIN. <i>Native names</i>.- Tawaki and Pokotiwha. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/287</i>)

### pokoto
 
 <i>n.</i> a small fish (Taupo)

>  Pokoto are a small fish said by some to come down from Taupo to spawn in a lake called Wangapi they [sic] are caught and dried in great numbers and as well as dried Eels are much esteemed. <i>(Apr 9, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,344,1966 ed.</i>)

### pol
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a politician [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Spare a thought now for the battered old National Party pols. There was a time when anyone of cabinet rank in the National Party would be put out to graze armed with at least one company director's job in their portfolio. <i>(Sep 18, 1987,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

>  A pastime, particularly an adventurous one, does much to keep the top pols in touch. <i>(May 6, 1988,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

### polach
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pollock n](per-pop.md#pollock))

### pole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> POLE: The musterer carries a long walking stick - a straight pole, perhaps one inch thick and shoulder high. He uses it for vaulting creeks and to aid him in keeping his balance when sidling steep country. <i>(1949,High Country,196,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to steal

>  T'other day in a Lambton Quay pub, he, with some friends, was drinking, and the pimp thought he saw the barman `poleing', and reported him to the boss, who subsequently sacked the bar-tender, who informs `Truth' that he did not thieve, and was asked to stop on at his job, but very rightly refused. <i>(Sep 12, 1908,NZ Truth,169/1/3</i>) [NOTES: [?vbl n]]

>  ... What does `polled' mean? That's a new word to me? ... It means `shook' them; stole them, you know. <i>(Mar 12, 1910,NZ Truth,246/7/6</i>)

>  [In local criminal slang]. . . `to clout,' `to pole,' `to fend off' are to steal; ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang,]

>  <b>LEE</b> Did you ever hear of a man called Arthur Beaumont who poled three hundred thousand pounds? <i>(1964,Shiner Slattery,140</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pole</i>. 2. To steal: New Zealand c.: since ca. 1930. R.G.C. McNab, in <i>The Press</i> (Christchurch, N.Z.), April 2, 1938. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1139</i>)

>  <i>pole v. </i>of which past tense only is used, <i>poled</i>, stolen. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pole</i>to steal, c.1930, Australia later, but earlier Australian meaning to scrounge. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a branch of silver poplar or willow that will take root when placed in slipped or eroded soil areas. (See [pole-planting](per-pop.md#pole-planting))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. I gained some insight into the practice of planting "poles" for stabilisation purposes. These poles are straight lengths of willow or silver poplar up to ten feet in length and up to two inches in diameter which are simply sharpened and pushed a couple of feet or so into the ground. The planting of poles is common practice in the Gisborne districts .. along the roadsides one sees nursery blocks devoted entirely to producing these poles ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,235</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The severed 'pole' is planted in a dam site or in ground that requires stabilising, and quickly shoots up to become a tree. Four years later, the original tree is cut again, the naturally branching occurring immediately above the first cut yielding some four to five 'poles' which in their turn produce fresh trees. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,11</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> For some years I decided to plant 1000 poles [of poplar] annually ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,97</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW & WILLIAMS</b> If too many poplar poles are planted in one year there is a chance they will not receive the care they need. At an average cost of $10 per pole it is an expensive practice if they die. <i>(1998,Information and Communication Needs,22</i>)

### pole bullock
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [poler n](per-pop.md#poler))

>  I noticed before I came up to him that the near-hand pole bullock of the team was inclined to kick, and I warned Mr M'Master's man to beware of it. <i>(Nov 23, 1865,North Otago Times,1</i>)

>  When we turned into Butcher's Lane, deceased rushed in front of me, close to the bullocks, when the near side pole bullock kicked him on the legs and threw him down. <i>(Apr 18, 1877,North Otago Times,2</i>)

### pole chimney
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Next day I built a pole chimney round the fireplace and lined it with flat stones from beside the river. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,16</i>)

### pole house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAHY</b> It was what was called a pole-house,built on a platform which hung out overthe slope on great poles, driven deepinto the rocks and stones of thepeninsula. <i>(1992,Underrunners,16</i>)

>  The Gregory House system of pole construction houses provides an effective and economical answer to many building problems. "Polehouses" are already being built on sections that would be considered far too steep for conventional construction... Polehouses completely eliminate the need for extensive site preparation and foundations. <i>(Nov 20, 1976,North & South,Suppl. "The House" 6</i>)

### pole on
 
 <i>v.</i> to impose on, sponge on

>  Many times during its career `Truth' has had to use the scalpel on the hidebound quacks who wax fat and wealthy by poling on the credulity of the people. <i>(May 14, 1910,NZ Truth,255/5/8</i>)

>  <i>poler n</i>. bullock yoked to the pole, who leaves most of the hard work to the leaders, whence pole on, <i>v.int.</i> to impose on; <i>poler</i>. n. any one who sponges on his mates. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>pole ... 2 v.i. NZ & Austral.</i> impose or sponge on person. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,581</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### pole position
 
 <i>n.</i> the number-one stand in a shearing shed

>  The man with the highest tally takes the number-one stand - the "pole position" next morning. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/84</i>)

### pole strap
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> POLE STRAP: Heavy leathers from end of coach pole upward to horse collars. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 188]

### pole yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a primitive stock yard made from poles of manuka or strong saplings.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> When the handling and the mouthing and the riding was all done, in an old manuka pole yard by the creek ... <i>(1975,More From the High Country,38</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> We'll build a yard to hold him [a bull], a small pole yard will do. <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,46</i>)

### pole-planting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the ''planting'' and subsequent rooting of stakes of silver poplar and willow on steep hillsides to prevent soil loss and erosion.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. pole-planting is now an accepted off-season job for shearers and other seasonal workers. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,235</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> However, a feature of the station's management has been a regular practice of pole-planting which is doing much to stabilize the country. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,242</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW & WILLIAMS</b> The benefits of pole planting in order of priority are soil erosion, shelter, shade, fodder, aesthetics and possibly, timber. <i>(1998,Information and Communication Needs,21</i>)

>  <b>WRC</b> The impact of the long drought is still being felt in some locations with pole planting being held in some drier areas. <i>(2000,Wairarapa Schemes,1</i>)

### polecat
 
 <i>n.</i> a collector of ceramic insulators

>  <b>TASKER</b> Those who seek them out [sc. cermic insulators] are known in the hobby as 'polecats'; the activity itself is referred to as 'polecatting'. <i>(1989,Old New Zealand Bottles & Bygones,120 ed.</i>)

### poled
 
 <i>adj.</i> stolen

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Poled</i>. ppl.adj. Stolen: New Zealanders': C. 20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,645</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Underworld slang includes] <i>poled </i>for stolen. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>) [NOTES: (So P:)]

### poler
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse or bullock working beside the pole of a dray or wagon.

>  The horses were so dead beat that first one and then the other poler gave in. <i>(Apr 8, 1878,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  No damage was done to the waggon [sic] but one of the coach horses (the off poler) broke its shoulder which necessitated it being shot. <i>(Mar 29, 1902,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>MACKAY</b> Squat, powerful bullocks - the 'polers' - were at the back of the team to take the most strain. <i>(1991,Working the Kauri,38</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i>-po'lers n. pl. in a bullock team, the pair at the pole.-pith'ing pole n. an implement used in slaughtering cattle, with a sharp blade for piercing the spinal cord [pole and pith] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,7/104</i>) [NOTES: [see LEADER for cit.] 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year vii 104-105]

>  He is a new chum only landed that day. He was getting on the dray to ride just behind the off polar and of course got cicked. <i>(Dec 16, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,114,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The polers, that is the bullocks attached to the pole of the dray and accustomed to bear the weight of the dray-load on their necks, are always the quietest. <i>(1871,Boy Colonists,234</i>)

>  <b>J.G. WILSON</b> Willie brought [the dray] up soon after I got there and tried ... to yoke the two polers ... <i>(May 13, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May 13 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 190]

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> ... he could [with a whip] ... tickle the ear of the leader, or flip a fly from off the rump of a poler. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,9</i>)

>  I had 3 generations of white bulls, all of whom learned this trick in their third year, when I used them in the bullock team as `polers' ... <i>(1900,TrNZI,32/280</i>) [NOTES: (White)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> POLERS: Last pair in bush team of bullocks two abreast. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 188]

>  Stolid old `poler' ... <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/476</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> That blanked poler, Strawberry <i>(1916,Wowser, The,22</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> poler, pole-bullock. Usy. pl. The two bullocks which work on the pole of a dray or wagon. <i>(1933,First Year in Canterbury Settlement</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> The poler's yoke had a ring in the middle, fixed by means of two pins to the end of the pole. <i>(1938,High Country,47</i>)

>  Polers, Pole-bullocks.- <i>The two bullocks which work on the</i> p.<i> of a dray or </i>waggon. The <i>leaders</i> are two front ones, and the intermediates are the<i> body bs. </i> <i>(1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: ACLAND Glossary]

>  <b>AYSON</b> The pins would fall out and the polers and yokes would get free of the dray pole to stroll along the track until arrested by the Major's stentorian `Whoa.' <i>(1937,Thomas,20</i>)

>  <i>poler n</i>. bullock yoked to the pole, who leaves most of the hard work to the leaders, whence pole on, <i>v.int.</i> to impose on;<i> poler</i>. n. any one who sponges on his mates. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>poler n</i>. bullock yoked to the pole, which leaves most of the hard work to the leaders, whence <i>pole on v.i</i>. to impose [o]n; <i>poler. n</i>. any one who sponges on his mates. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Each team comprised five horses - three leaders and two polers. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,12</i>)

>  <i>poler n. NZ & Austral.</i> horse, bullock, etc. harnassed alongside the pole (and leaving most of the pulling to others); <i>sl</i>. sponger, idler. [POLE 1] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,581</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Stumbling down the hill, the trusty polers hold the dray. <i>(Jun 7, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 4</i>)

>  The eight-year-olds Dan and Blucher arethe polers, those nearest the dray. <i>(Jul 9, 1973,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  The driver promptly pulled up, but the unfortunate poler was in a curious predicament, being wholly underneath the brake, from where he was soon extricated, but it was found he was cut about considerably. <i>(Jun 4, 1901,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bludger (cf. pole on v.)

>  <i>poler n</i>. bullock yoked to the pole, who leaves most of the hard work to the leaders, whencepole on, <i>v.int.</i> to impose on; <i>poler</i>. n. any one who sponges on his mates. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) one of a pair of bullocks in a bullock team [working on one side of the pole of a dray as 'nearside poler' on the left or 'offside poler' on the right.]

>  For Sale .. four pairs working bullocks, leaders, and polers. <i>(May 3, 1862,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  I do not recollect the words "have you two good poler bullocks for sale." <i>(Sep 22, 1882,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  "Boss, you're the dandiest bullocky in the West. You've got the polers in the lead, an' you got 'em here without bustin' anything. You're top dog with bullocks, all right." <i>(Jan 5, 1924,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  FOR SALE, A TEAM of Four or Six FIRST-CLASS WORKING BULLOCKS, Polers and Leaders. <i>(Jul 2, 1859,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> .. we had a little misunderstanding with the bullocks; the leaders .. tied themselves into an inextricable knot with the polers. <i>(1890,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,105</i>) [NOTES: (1863)]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The polers .. are always the quietest. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,234</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> The poler's yoke had a ring in the middle, fixed by means of two pins to the end of the pole. <i>(1938,High Country: the evolution of a NZ Sheep Station,47</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [coach horse working next the coach behind the leaders or other horses in the team]

>  The polers then shied, and took the coach slightly over the slope of the road, and there being altogether four persons on the box seat, Morse was forced from his position and thrown on the road, losing the reins at the same time. <i>(Oct 21, 1885,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  He was up in a moment and seized the leaders' heads, and called on one of the members of the Company to hold the polers. <i>(Nov 13, 1891,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> The `polers' pulled back. <i>(1929,Bonzer Jones,97</i>)

>  On Monday last, at 6 o'clock, Cobb's coach ... left Dunedin for Dunstan all right, but ater running twenty miles, one of the fore wheels collapsed. ... Upon [the driver's] arrival at the Union Hotel with his polers, he was rushed with enquiries. ...Seating himself on the axle in a comical position, he was `Joe'd' by a chorus of lookers-on. Replying that those who had nothing better to do than `Joe' him were a set of `tarnation fools', he drove off at top speed. <i>(Jul 25, 1863,Otago Colonist</i>) [NOTES: 1863 July 25 Otago Colonist in 1931 Lovell-Smith Old Coaching Days 35]

>  The leaders reached the shore, and the polers had just got their footing when the coach turned over. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,169,1978 ed.</i>)

### poley
 
 <i>n.</i> a hornless cattlebeast

>  A new feature among the cattle is the Norfolk red poley recently imported by Mr. Heaton Rhodes. <i>(Nov 11, 1898,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [The bullocks'] names were Nimble and Nobby, both poleys, and Bluey and Yellowburn. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,154</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The Poleys seemed to be deficient in body. <i>Ibid.</i> 135 In the nineties we tried breeding polled shorthorns from freaks which occurred at intervals amongst the horned beasts, but soon gave it up, for the poleys seemed to be deficient in the body. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,119,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> By-the-bye the red poley was killed just after calving. <i>(1884,In Memoriam,65</i>)

>  <b>HASELDEN</b> The cow had nothing wrong with her at all, but was a poley, and ... many cattle were polies. <i>(1900,Winter's Night, A</i>)

>  He swore that the beast killed by the prisoner belonged to him, and that it was a poley, about seven years old. <i>(May 3, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... some poley Devons, and a few long-horned Devon and Hereford working bullocks have also been received. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,169</i>)

>  ... poley bullock. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,140</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,173</i>)

>  I say, Mum, will you be so kind / As to 'low me to damn that 'ere Poley. <i>(1875,Colonial Songster,100</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (of a cattle beast) hornless

>  Notice. Running on my farm, One Red Poly Heifer, branded W on rump. Owner can have same on paying expenses. <i>(Jul 30, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  [F]or Sale by Public Auction .. 1 excellent poley cow in milk, to calve in about three months. <i>(Jun 27, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,65</i>)

>  IMPOUNDED at Auckland, the following Cattle .. Red Poley Heifer, brand off rump W, off ribs S .. White Poley Ox, with red spots, brand near shoulder R, near hip R ... <i>(Feb 27, 1850,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  One yellow poley Steer, with white spots, 18 months, slit in back part of off ear, no brand visible ... <i>(Aug 26, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  IMPOUNDED - December 15th, by George Queale, Fern Bush .. 1 black poley steer, branded M M on rump; 1 black poley bull, no brand, 2-year old .. 2 brindled poley cows, one with calf, branded M M ... <i>(Dec 19, 1882,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  In Polled Angus cattle an improvement is also noticeable, and it is evident that more attention is being paid to the welfare of the stock. A number of dairymen hold the black poley in much higher estimation than any other cattle. <i>(Nov 24, 1904,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <b>S D PARNELL</b> Bought a poly cow <i>(1844,Farm Diary and Notebook</i>)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Yearling Poley heifer <i>(1846,Journal,36</i>)

>  Impounded at Te Aro .. 1 black and white poley cow, brand mark S on rump. <i>(Feb 9, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  Sales by Auction .. One superior cow - in milk, One poley cow and calf, in do. <i>(Nov 1, 1845,New Zealander, The,1</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

### police-court
 
 <i>v.</i> [1910 cit. refers to (invalid) noun]

>  There is no `Police Court' in New Zealand, although the term is frequently used. The point was referred to by Mr. Salmond, Solicitor-General, in the Court of Appeal at Wellington recently. He said that a `Magistrate's Court' is known to the law, but not a police court. `A Justice of the Peace has no court,' he added. <i>(Apr 30, 1910,NZ Truth,23/4/8</i>)

>  The result was that Ah Sue, or Ah Chow, or whatever his name is was police-courted along with his good, kind, accommodating lady. [2nd example of this coinage]. <i>(May 4, 1907,NZ Truth,98/6/2</i>)

>  He was police-courted over the firing business, and was let go with a fine of half a sov., the pistol being confiscated. <i>(Jul 21, 1906,NZ Truth</i>)

### policeman
 
 <i>n.</i> a timber reinforcement

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> POLICEMAN : Stout sapling fitted on roadside to safeguard water table. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an inmate informant or renegade

In this sense British prison slang since early 20th century. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,73</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (historical)

 <i>n.</i>

### Political Reform League
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In the eighteen-nineties, ... a group of National Associations, which had been established by business men, was quietly promoting conservative views and conservative candidates. By 1904 these bodies, united and rechristened the Political Reform League, had replaced the remnants of the old Conservative Party. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,203</i>)

 <i>phr.</i>

>  "If the public is told often enough misleading, even occasionally malign, interpretations of why things have gone wrong in our society and why other things have gone right, a new collective myth will be created that can become a breeding ground for complacency and, finally, political sclerosis." <i>(Nov 28, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>) [NOTES: Tim Groser, National MP maiden speech.]

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (historical) an entry tax demanded from Chinese immigrants

>  With visions of the colony being overtaken by the "Yellow Peril" or "Mongolian Horde", the government, in 1881, passed a law making Chinese migrants pay a poll tax of GBP10 before they could enter New Zealand... The situation improved with the abolition of the poll tax in 1944. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/55</i>)

### pollard
 
 <i>n.</i>

### pollie/polly
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a politician [also poli (pl. polis) in 6.1992 citation] (See [polie](per-pop.md#polie))

>  There is no way the pollies can butcher the growth profile over the next year or two. <i>(Nov 28, 1999,Sunday Star Times,E2</i>)

>  The anti-smoking pollies have bigger plans. They were considering suing the cigarette makers for what their product costs the community. <i>(Mar 19, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  Fish officials ask pollies to whitewash their remarks. <i>(Nov 21, 1997,Independent (Auckland),5</i>)

>  At last we crossed to the panel consisting of two pollies without a perch - John Banks and Nandor Tanczos and former National Party head Michelle Boag heavily swathed in a blue scarf that gave her the appearance of a delegate from the subcontinent. <i>(Sep 24, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  A local pollie was invited to launch the venture ... but when he looked up `politician' in the handsome tome ... he had to be persuaded not to stalk off the platform. <i>(Aug 18, 1989,National Business Review,W17</i>) [NOTES: (Margaret Clark)]

>  I silently wonder whether word had raced round Wellington that Bennett's questions were about as piercing as those children's arrows tipped with red rubber suckers, and that the pollies spotted an easy chance to boost their image. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  Some pollies offered their support ... providing whistleblowers first put their cases to their bosses. <i>(Mar 12, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  They say that a polly has to be a tub-thumper on, say, family values, while having a series lof affairs, before qualifying for attention. <i>(Jun 25, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  The polis' restaurant, Bellamy's, is further away than before and many MPs now eschew the place. <i>(Jun 1992,Cuisine,101</i>)

>  The intestinal state of established pollies is irrelevant. <i>(Dec 5, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),95</i>)

### polling booth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We have polling places and polling booths, although the term polling booth is often used loosely to encompass also both polling places and what the Electoral Act calls 'inner compartments' where the voter marks the ballot paper. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### polling place
 
 <i>n.</i> a location, e.g. a school, where votes may be cast at an election [Jim Cameron writes : See Electoral Act 1993 s. 155. The British equivalent is <i>polling station</i>>. Popularly a polling place is often referred to as a <i>polling booth</i>, which has the distinct legal meaning of a room within a polling place. For each polling booth the Returning Officer for the electorate appoints a <i>deputy returning officer (DRO)</i>. The corresponding perrson in Britain appears to be known as a <i>presiding officer</i>.]

### pollock
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our<i> hakes, tench, bream, snapper, haddock, elephant-fish, pollock, salmon, gurnards, pipe-fish, parrot-fish, leather-jackets, cole-fish, John Dorys, sword-fish, cod;</i> various kinds of <i>skate</i> and <i>cat-fish, sting-ray</i> and <i>dog-fish.</i> <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/322</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Polack mentions `cod-fish', bearing the native name of `Wapuka,' but we do not know the fish he alludes to. The `polach' he speaks of are, perhaps, the young of the <i>Percis colias</i>, the adult of which are known to the settlers as the `cole-fish'. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/222</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/222]

 <i>n.</i>

### Pom
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of Pommie/Pommy q.v. [see also whingeing Pom, ten-pound Pom]

>  To talk of slogans like "Punch a Pom a Day" is absurd. <i>(Mar 9, 1974,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Expect boatloads of Poms to arrive from the UK to experience a virtual England. <i>(Jan 9, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  But even the Poms have lost their sense of humour. <i>(Dec 6, 2011,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> A schedule of changes followed and some wag had lonG ago added with Day-Glo aerosol POMS ON BICYCLES FREE. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,146</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> The chap next to me leaned over. `Great value, these drivers. Aussie drivers are never like that.' He was a Pom. Smoked lens glasses, bush shirt, shorts, tramping boots. `You mean they don't crack funnies?' `Menace is the word <i>(1985,G'day Country,169</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> `I'll go up North to Auckland ... save up money and go to England, where the poms are. ... My grandad was one of them and he was a real beaut. ... He was a boozer and loved the trots, went every Saturday.' <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,54,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> His sentences had a `Kiddy' [Kidderminster], not a Kiwi cadence. Some people thought that he had always remained a `Pom' at heart. <i>(1976,Nash,318</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> ... And a Pom ... HUGH. I'm not a Pom ... I'm Welsh. JIM. Same bloody difference as far as I'm concerned. <i>(1977,Glasgow Constitutional,48</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>pom, pommie</i>: A ruddy (BLOODY? op.cit.) ENGLISHMAN. ... <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,78</i>)

>  <i>pom/pommy </i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> TINY: A Pom? JACKO: Yeah. A homey. <i>(1985,Shuriken,23</i>)

>  We all roared at Billy T. James' Irish joke. Could we have more please - about coconuts, dallies, poms and horis? Chows too, if possible. <i>(Jun 15, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),a8</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... Poms in the 1960s were particularly bad at making mock of our quarter-acre section, ... our State Housing ..., and above all our expression: `She'll be right'. (In a way, this is an Antipodean equivalent of the beloved English: `Mustn't grumble'.) <i>(1980,New Zealanders, The (Henderson)</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He was a big matelot, though not a Pom, it was easy to tell he was a Pig Islander. <i>(1946,That Summer,92</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Suppose it's because Andy's missis is a Pom [that she sends the shearers' lunch in an embroidered table-cloth]. She'll learn though. Look at Wally's Ma - she got over her Pommy ways. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,110,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Pom: Englishman. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>Pom</i>: Suspected Englishman. The confirmed case is referred to in the clinical term of Pongo. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> I wonder why I used the word `Brambles'. I'm disturbed by it, because really only the Poms call them `brambles'! We say `blackberries'!  <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Well, Pommy pay in the army was just a joke to a New Zealander. <i>(1972,Man of England Now,26</i>)

>  They met a boy they knew, Ricky Hatch, who was joined by another boy, `Pommie' Paul. The four went on the boys' motor-cycles to the `25' clubhouse in Kent Terrace. <i>(May 8, 1968,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>HUTT</b> [A New Zealander] may be amused at [immigrants'] `pommy' headgear. <i>(1948,Your NZ - Your Future,40</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> `Couldn't say about that mate. You'll have to ask your Pommy friend.' <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,230</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> We never really talked the same language anyhow. `I don't know,' he said and his accent seemed to have gone a bit pommier than usual, or what he thought was pommy, only that was another thing that meeting a lot of pommies had made some of us wise to. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,124</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> They all thought Dad was a pommy holy roller and its was a bit of a dag, a way of getting rid of him. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,95</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an English person; British person, especially one who has immigrated. [from a play on the pomegranate (so Australian National Dictionary), formerly an Australian name for a British immigrant through Australian pommy grant, early New Zealand jimmy grant]

>  The Pommies was pretty tough. <i>(Mar 8, 1943,NZEF Times,II/89 : 5</i>)

>  One of these was a Greek lady who interrupted the verbal flow of a Jack tar who was describing our Pacific paradise (and doing it pretty well, too - for a Pommy). <i>(Sep 6, 1943,NZEF Times,III/115 : 5</i>)

>  <b>BEAN</b> `Never met him, matey, but he is all right, you bet. A Pommy* can't go wrong out there if he isn't too lazy to work.' ... `Oh, well, yer see, mate, we don't call the like of 'im `Pommies' because we dislike 'em, but just as a matter of description. [fn] *Pommy - short for pomegranate, and used as a nickname for immigrants. <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,31</i>) [NOTES: 1916 N.Ash `Anzac Dialogues' in 1916 Bean The Anzac Book 31]

>  I was amused... There is no obscurity about the origin of "Pommy". The origin is the obvious one - "Emigrant". The emigrants themselves corrupted this to "Pomegranate". From 1914 to 1925 Ifrequently heard and used the term "pomegranate" to describe a new arrival. Over recent years this has been abbreviated to "Pommy". I am, etc...Thick-skinned Pommy. <i>(Nov 18, 1950,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The term is of Australian origin, of a period long ago when the military and other authorities of the time called the men of the penal settlement, "Prisoners of Mother England," which was shortenedto P.O.M.E. and later made "Pommie". The New Zealand term was "Homie" until superseded by the Australian adjective. And even now "Pommie" is being superseded by the term "Pongo". <i>(Nov 21, 1950,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>Davin</b> We never really talked the same language anyhow. `I don't know,' he said and his accent seemed to have gone a bit pommier than usual, or what he thought was pommy, only that was another thing that meeting a lot of pommies had made some of us wise to. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,124</i>)

>  <b>THE MIXER</b> He'll corner a bloke often that's "shickered", / Who looks like a "pommie" or a "yob". <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,69</i>)

>  (Corresp. col) Sir, - Your correspondents are incorrect regarding the origin of the term `Pommie', meaning English immigrant. The term is of Australian origin, of a period long ago when the military and other authorities of the time called the men of the penal settlements, `Prisoners of Mother England', which was shortened to P.O.M.E. and later made `Pommie'. The New Zealand term was `Homie' until superseded by the Australian adjective. And even now Pommie is being superseded by the term `Pongo'. The term certainly was derisive in origin, and as to whether it is still derisive depends entirely upon the individual addressed as such. The writer is of the fourth generation (N.Z. with Austr. connections). Winther Pedersen. <i>(1951,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: ? Jan?]

>  I was amused by your reply to `T.P.P.' There is no obscurity about the origin of `Pommy'. The origin is the obvious one - `Emigrant'. The emigrants themselves corrupted this to `pomegranate'. From 1914 to 1925 I frequently heard and used `pomegranate' to describe a new arrival. Over recent years this has become abbreviated to `Pommy'. Thick-Skinned Pommy. <i>(1951,Evening Post</i>)

>  A Tasmanian gave the origin definitely as being `pomegranate', the colour of that fruit being similar to the high colour of the complexions of the English, and very noticeable against the sun-dried appearance of the Aussie complexion. This has been acceptable to many knowing the Aussies' susceptibility to using phrases such as `plates of meat' for feet and `trouble for strife' for wife. Pommy to many has become a most objectionable word owing to the way it has been used with other words. Only the most ignorant use of such terms, not thinking that their forefathers came from that same brave country. If my comrades of the Homeland, newly arrived, who use the nom-de-plume, or refer to themselves as `Pommies', read this they will now understand why it is objectionable even when used in a friendly manner. British and Proud of it. <i>(1951,Evening Post</i>)

>  Sir, - Fifty years ago a shipload of passengers arrived in Australia. Coming through the tropics, they got badly burned and their faces were scarlet. The word `Pommie' was derived from a well-known Australian fruit at the time, the scarlet pomegranate. So the Australians named the new arrivals `Pommies' (just fun), and `Pommie' is still used occasionally. -E.M.W. <i>(Dec 31, 1951,Christchurch Star-Sun,2</i>)

>  Sir, -A correspondent wishes to know the origin of the word `Pommie.' The famous war correspondent, the late Ashmad Barlett, in his book, `Despatches from the Dardanelles,' written at the time of the campaign, says that the term `Pommie' was applied by veteran troops of the campaign to newly arrived troops from Britain. Their fresh pink complexions were likened to the fruit pomegranate, in contrast to the bronzed features of the veteran troops. They were referred to as `Pommies.' The term, therefore, was not applied by Dominion troops in particular to those from Britain, but by veteran troops, both British and Dominion, to new arrivals. -KIWI. <i>(Jan 2, 1958,Christchurch Star-Sun,2</i>)

>  Sir, - In reply to O.J. Davis and `Very Interested' relating to the origin of the word `Pommie.' The word apparently came into being during the 1914-18 War and is derived from the French word pommes, meaning, I believe, potatoes. During the war the front line troops of the British Army were very seriously short of food, except for potatoes, on which they subsisted for many weeks. Hence on the fields at Flanders this peculiar and, I might say, very controversial word came into being. I am sure that any British World War I veteran could affirm this explanation.-W.J.BRACKS. <i>(Jan 4, 1958,Christchurch Star-Sun,2</i>)

>  Sir, - It is `POM-E,' not `Pommie.' These four letters originated in the old sailing ship days. Migrants leaving the British Isles were issued with a permit of migration certificate which was headed, `Permit of migration-Ireland,' `Permit of migration-England,' depending on the part of the British Isles one was from. The first letters in these four words `Permit of migration-England' are `POM-E,' spelt now, as pronounced then, `Pommie.' -SAILS. <i>(Jan 6, 1958,Christchurch Star-Sun,2</i>)

>  Sir, - The red coat of the soldier gave rise to the sailors' nickname for him of `Pommy,' a corruption of the French `pomme.' This derivation is far more likely than `Prisoner of Mother England.' The derogatory nickname would be more likely to stick to the guards than to the convicts. If my explanation is incorrect am I entitled to call our trans-Tasman cousins (and others) `Pommies?' - I am, etc., A PENSIONER OF MOTHER ENGLAND. <i>(Feb 23, 1962,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Sir, - Unfortunately previous correspondents have been confused by the spelling of `Pommy.' I understand the correct spelling to be P.O.M.M.I.E., this being derived from the initial letters of the phrase, `People of Many Magnificient Immortal Epics.' This derivation was given in the Australian R.S.L. magazine several years ago. - I am, etc, DRONGATUS PONGORUM. <i>(1962,Evening Post</i>)

>  EVERY now and then some one puts forward a new theory on why Englishmen are occasionally referred to in these southern latitudes as `Pommies'. The following note from Mr. G.T.M., of the coastal motor vessel Squall, at Wellington, may clear things up: `I first visited Aussie in 1911-Western Australia, Victoria, New South Wales, and Queensland. Immigrants were variously termed-Immygrant', `Jimmy grants', and up among the bananalanders, quite often `Pommy grants' (pomegranates). Very soon this became `Pommy'; and this, I think, would be the true derivation.' <i>(Sep 3, 1964,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>GOLDIE BROWN</b> : Naval Slang for Portsmouth is Pompey. Where are you going to - Back to Pompey. Hence Pommy? <i>(1983,Letter</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> We got into this hullabaloo with some poor damned immigrants. What system, they were brought out under, I don't know, but they were 'Homeys' - the word 'Pommy' wasn't in use then. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,78</i>)

>  Inferior English residents, called"Homies" more often than "Pommies", spokein a variety of non-U accents andoccupied a variety of non-U jobs. <i>(Aug 1994,Quote Unquote,16</i>)

>  I have never felt any hostility towards any"homie" or "pommie" until recently. <i>(Apr 8, 1953,NZ Times,19</i>)

>  Has anybody heard of `Pommy-ism' and, if so, what is it. Says `The Soldier' (Sydney) ... The name `Pommy' is strongly resented by immigrants after they have been resident in Australia a few weeks, yet they are entirely to blame if the name sticks. Some of the newcomers will not drop their `pommy-ism' and they assume a silly air of `superiority', even going to the extent of trying to keep ex-Imperial sevice men in a sort of private organisaion. ... What is written of Australia might well apply with R.S.A. substituted for R.S.S.I.L. <i>(Oct 11, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  He'll comer a bloke off that's `shickered', / Who looks like a `pommie' or a `yob' <i>(1927,Transport Workers' Song Book,69</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Why don't you dump Janet and Laura and get a couple of Pommies? They'll do it [sc. housework] for much less money and work longer hours. <i>(1929,Riven,158</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `[New Zealanders] like the English, provided they are the right type; the educated young man gets on well. They don't like the `Pommie' as they call him, God knows why, and no one can tell you where the name came from, but its more common in Australia than in New Zealand'. `What is a Pommie?' `Blest if I know; it's hard to define, but usually the short, shrunken industrial worker, the man with the broad accent, from Yorkshire or Lancashire. The stunted product of sweated labour, from mills factories and mines. I've heard him referred to as a Pommie, but never the upstanding educated young fellow. They seem to welcome him; he is like themselves'. <i>(1938,Guest of Life, A,141</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Mr Massey, however, gave his name to the army boot. `Homie' and `Pommy' are, I believe Australian [120] words, and so is `cocky', in the sense of small farmer, a term that is coming into general use, and some day will be recognised even in the schools. <i>(1939,First With the Sun,119</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [for discussion] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,23</i>)

>  <i>Pommie</i> - English person. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> It is the characteristic of Kiwis that they will approach a `Pommie' and say, `You know, you Pongoes are a hopeless lot'. <i>(1944,Troop Target,159</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> And even the poor old Pommies will find things tough in bashed-about over-crowded England. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,143</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>pommie</i>: Englishman <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> New Zealanders themselves may have been subconsciously aware of this difference of approach. They never quite knew where they had Mr. Nash. >  <i>pommy n</i>. an English newcomer to Australia [contraction of pomegranate, possibly by rhyming play on word immigrant, as extended to jimmygrant]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> [The immigrants] were `Homeys' - the word `Pommy' wasn't in use then [early 1930s depression], and [79] we called them `Homeys', and this was used in a derogatory manner ... as if it was something contemptible. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,78</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... ### Pommieland
 
 <i>n.</i> Britain, England [also Pommie-land]

>  Two women, a cook and a nurse, were gaoled for three months in Wallasey, England, recently for "communicating" with German prisoners. The two "tarts" used to sneak out and "communicate" with the 'orrid 'Uns under cover of darkness. Food is certainly not the only shortage in Pommie-land nowadays, as far as the womenfolk are concerned, at any rate. Nor, it is fairly evident, are all the ministering angels as noble-hearted as was Nurse Cavell. <i>(Dec 28, 1918,NZ Truth,706/6</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> A fellow that's come all the way from Pommieland to have a quick look at Kiwi-Country, ... at least ought to have an overcoat to put on himself. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,22</i>)

### Pommified
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Noel Virtue is a pommified Kiwi who wandered off on his OE in 1967 and only came back for brief time. <i>(Feb 19, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

### Pommising
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RUSSELL</b> ... one took out an undergraduate degree in New Zealand, and went on to Oxford, where one took on the Schools, the undergraduate examination, over again. This was held, I assume, to be a kind of correction, a kind of deepening, a kind (I suspect) of what some might call Pommising. <i>(1986,Medieval English Religious and Ethical Literature,2</i>)

### Pommy-ism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Has anybody heard of `Pommy-ism' and, if so, what is it. Says `The Soldier' (Sydney) ... The name `Pommy' is strongly resented by immigrants after they have been resident in Australia a few weeks, yet they are entirely to blame if the name sticks. Some of the newcomers will not drop their `pommy-ism' and they assume a silly air of `superiority', even going to the extent of trying to keep ex-Imperial sevice men in a sort of private organisaion. ... What is written of Australia might well apply with R.S.A. substituted for R.S.S.I.L. <i>(Oct 11, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

### pompano
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>TREVALLY </i>(a) Scientific name:<i> Caranx lutescens</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Trevally; New Zealand Pompano. (c) Other common names: Hoppers (fish in uncleaned state). (d) Principal localities where taken: [Largely confined to northern waters and south to Cook Strait]. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,48</i>)

### pond
 
 <i>n.</i> the Tasman Sea
