### Poneke
 
 <i>n.</i> Wellington; Maori version of Port Nick, the familiar shortening of Port Nicholson, the old name for Wellington, in use as in Ngati Poneke, the Maori people of Wellington, the urban Wellington `tribe'; and in the names of some Wellington organisationsl [Maori Poneke[o-macron]]

### ponga
 
 <i>n.</i> the New Zealand silver tree-fern <i>Cyathea dealbata</i>, (silver(y), white) treefern (or its trunk) (also other <i>Cyathea</i> spp.) [ORIGIN: Maori, sometimes called bunga or bungie and often misspelt punga; Various spellings (once<i> ponja</i>) representing various pronunciations among English speakers, dividing roughly into a chiefly North Island form<i> punga</i> (almost a standard spelling) and an older (possibly obsolescent 1984) South Island form <i>bunger, bungie</i>.]

>  Subsequently we built our houses; the sides we built of ponga (a species of fern tree) and the roofs we thatched with flax. <i>(Feb 4, 1868,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The butt of the staff is charred, and lined in the ground all round with ponga (the stem of the fern tree), which never rots. <i>(Jun 11, 1860,Taranaki Herald,1</i>)

>  In the distance is seen the volcanic hills of this country .. while in the fore ground the graceful pongo (tree fern) casts a shadow over the solitary kiwi. <i>(Nov 5, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  " .. The .. gum of the ponga (Cyatahea medullaris) is a vermifuge ... " <i>(Dec 28, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... I got a Man who went & fetched me a Fern-tree with root and all, he called it <i>Ma</i>[macron]<i>a</i>[macron,grave]<i>gu</i>, & as I pointed to a large ferntree near me, with leaves whose underside is white, he shewed me the difference & called the latter <i>Po</i>[grave]<i>nga</i>. This Tree is full of tender pulp, ... and I believe this Fern is nearly related to the <i>Sago</i>-tree, which is likewise a Fern. <i>(Nov 23, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,3/426,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|onga,<i> s</i>. A pithy wood so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,194</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>Pongo</i>, and <i>Wou, (achroma petandria Zelandica)</i> are varieties of the corktree, when cut down an adhesive juice exudes in some quantity, and are of much service to the natives in their fishing nets. The fronds are five feet long, virent, circumference of trunk one and a half foot, covered with chaffy scales. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/399</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> ... the ponga. <i>(1842,Journey in NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Ponga fern is perhaps the male tree, as it grows frequently near the Korau and is like it in leaf, with the exception of growing taller and the stalk bare, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,109,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The <i>ponga</i>, one of the species of tree-fern, is very beautiful at this season of the year; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/75</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cyathea <i>dealbata</i>, Sw. ... Nat. name, `Ponga', <i>Col</i>. <i>(1855,Flora NZ,II Flowerless Plants 7</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the ponga (a fern-tree) <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,115</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> [PONJA] <i>Cyathea Cunninghamii </i> <i>(1864,Vegetation of the Chatham Islands</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ponja,[sic] of Chatham Island. <i>Cyathea Cunninghamii. </i> ... Ponga, <i>Col[enso]. Cyathea dealbata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>) [NOTES: [?PONJA err. or local pron]]

>  Ponga. Cyathea dealbata, Swartz <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/51</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... the fronds of different tree ferns were used, particularly of the Ponga <i>(Cyathea dealbata). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/36</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  ... the Ponga Flat ... owes its name to the large grove of Black Tree-ferns with which it was formerly covered. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/90</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  [The nest was] lined with hairy substance off the fronds of the <i>punga. </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/86</i>)

>  [The nest was] lined with hairy substance off the fronds of the <i>punga. </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/86</i>)

>  Hydrangeas provide incongruous splashes of colour at spots where two sisters once set up a tea shop in a ponga grove and where Hatrick's houseboat once provided a floating hotel for the steady stream of tourists in the 1920s and 1930s. <i>(Feb 10, 2004,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

>  Her cynical views on life, love and Maori culture - "every frigging ponga tree has a hundred owners" - lighten the sense of foreboding that shrouds the play. <i>(Aug 2, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> Ponga was tough to chop too. <i>(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),44</i>)

>  Now a shrine stands there, a carved ponga with a blue baseball cap perched on top. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  A green man came and turned our pongas into palm trees. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  the ponga fronds quiver in the wind / and the incandescent white horses / roll in, roll in ... <i>(Dec 6, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  Ponga is the classical New Zealand silver fern, so named because the stalks and undersides of its leaves are pale and bright. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  It is ironic that we revere the ponga, a tree fern peculiar to New Zealand, but one whose ancestors reached here scarcely seven million years ago. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,304/38</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> <i>C. dealbata</i> (whitish). "Ponga. " Silver King." <i>(1921,NZ Ferns,86</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> The "bunga" of the bushman, used for all manner of purposes - fences, verandah posts, even for making roads. <i>(1921,NZ Ferns,88</i>)

>  There is the danger of a pakeha supervisor with his pakeha standard imposing on a people just out of raupo and ponga shacks a type of dwelling far above their requirements. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/26,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <i>ponga</i>, a tree-fern (Cyathes dealbata), silver tree-fern; very often corrupted to bunger. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>ponga</i>: tree fern. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Then there were the magnificent tree-ferns, one kind growing some forty feet high, called punga by the natives; another sort ... they call mamaku; and yet another giant fern, the pura ... [with] a bulbous-looking root, like Brobdingnagian dahlias. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,198</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tuakura (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) and the Ponga (<i>Cyathea dealbata</i>) are the two principal varieties of fern-tree. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/203</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[yathea] dealbata, Swartz.</i> ... Trunk 10-30 ft. high ... Fronds ... green or yellow-green above, pure-white beneath from a coating of deciduous powder. ... <i>Ponga; Silver Tree-fern. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,948</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> [Tara-ao] and his people were hungry and cut down ponga and cooked and ate them. <i>(1888,Ancient History of the Maori,4/191</i>)

>  <i>ponga n</i> -s [Maori] : a tree fern (<i>Cyathea dealbata</i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1763</i>)

>  <b>JEFFREY</b> `The ferns by the camellias are tree-ferns. Do you know what the Maori name is? Ponga! They're natives, of course.' <i>(1964,Mairangi,13</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PONGA Tree fern, <i>Cyathea dealbata</i>. <i>(1967,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  <i>ponga</i> [s] <i>Cyathea dealbata</i> (Forst.f.) Swartz <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,60</i>)

>  <i>ponga n</i>. a tall New Zealand tree fern with large leathery leaves. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,881</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 584; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 375]]

>  There were very big kanuka trees nearby, and many small trees and shrubs on our section, including ponga, which Dad called `king ferns'. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair `At the Point')]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> In the ponga or silver tree fern (<i>Cyathea dealbata</i>) the leaf bases decay more gradually and do not form well defined scars. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,86</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,123, 232</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a berry

>  <b>YATE</b> It [the huia] resides in deep long grass: it food is worms and insects, with a small berry called <i>ponga</i>. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [ponga fence](per-pop.md#ponga-fence))

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... we came to his [sc Maori's] homestead - a ponga whare with a bark roof, stained with smoke. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,123</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> A tree-fern, punga stump hollowed ... hit him under the chin, and he sprawled in the mud. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,173</i>)

>  We legislate to protect our forests and birds: we raid the bush for punga fronds and lycopodium to decorate dance-halls, we knock down pigeons with stones and forestall protest with a sneer. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/209</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

>  <b>LEVY</b> [Table 1] Black punga <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,88,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN & DAVIES</b> <i>Cyathea dealbata</i> silver ponga <i>(1925,NZ Native Trees</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>silver punga [=] ponga</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,70</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  BRICKMAKERS', near Punga Flat. Five men's ground, taken up in March 1868. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,83</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Then there were the magnificent tree-ferns, one kind growing some forty feet high, called punga by the natives; another sort ... they call mamaku; and yet another giant fern, the pura ... [with] a bulbous-looking root, like Brobdingnagian dahlias. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,198</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> The Survey Department graded a zigzag track up the side to the top, fixing in punga steps. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,139</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Punga, n</i>. the trunk of the tree-fern that is known as <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>, the `black fern' of the settlers. It has an edible pith. <i>(1898,Austral English,371</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,115</i>)

>  <i>punga n</i>. a tree-fern, the pith of which is eaten. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> And there was a vague and somewhat disturbing unfamiliarity about the trees, the fantastic, prehistoric looking <i>punga</i> tree fern, for instance, that the mist also softened. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,4</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> punga: `ponga' is the Maori name for the silver tree-fern, often written `punga' and applied to all tree-ferns. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>R GILBERD</b> <i>bungers </i>(punga to you) make the safest jigger boards. <i>(1950,Letter</i>) [NOTES: ca1950 R. Gilberd (Okaihau, Northland) letter]

>  [Corresp. col.] Sir, - It is to be regretted that the artist who labelled his picture `Punga with Parasites,' did not write `Tree fern with Parasites,' as punga does not mean tree fern, or anything like it. Bushmen usually called a pukatea, buckyteer; a kowhai, goey; and a kaponga, [variant of ponga] a bunger whence comes the word punga, both a noun and a verb in Maori. The kaponga or brown tree fern is not nearly so handsome as the mamaku, or black tree fern [Cyathea medullaris], the latter often being a chief's prerogative for the white heart in the middle of the young leaves, which was baked and eaten. <i>(Aug 12, 1957,Dominion</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He wheeled his cycle off the road ... and hid it behind a ragged cluster of pungas. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,92,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Love Story)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... to herald the approach of visitors through aisles of motionless <i>pungas</i> ... <i>(1960,Maori Girl,38</i>)

>  <i>punga n.</i> New Zealand tree-fern, with edible pith [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <i>punga [=] mamaku: ponga</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,61</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Hilltop behind hilltop/ A mile of green pungas <i>Ibid.</i> 633 <i>punga</i> tree-fern [glossary] <i>(1979,Collected Poems,539,1972 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [B.'s usual spg]]

>  <i>punga n</i>. a variant spelling of <i>ponga</i>. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,923</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 612; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 393]]

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> punga <i>tree-fern </i> <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> punga <i>tree-fern </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[note punga also = lump, swelling]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>punga</i> penis, by association with the tree fern, Maori `ponga'. <i>- how's your punga? </i>a male student 1960s salutation; eg `G'day, Bosh, me old cobber. How's ya punga?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [[a1927] Ver.= Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) bunga ca1935 Marlborough punga, pungie, punga-punga; bunga, bungy; bungie-bung.]

>  Bungies: A native tree fern in New Zealand that has nice soft fronds. Absolutely one of the best arse wiping materials that nature provides. ..Sometimes it is called Punga. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,277</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> <i>Cyathea dealbata </i>bunga-bunga <i>(1900,Our Stolen Summer</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> .... building a hotel of `bungies' <i>(1914,Digger Stories,70</i>) [NOTES: (West Coast)]

>  The construction ... was that of most bush bunks; the main supports consisting of a section of `bungi' laid on the ground at each end. These supported wine-berry poles ... <i>(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,41</i>)

>  Some people speak and write of them as `tree ferns' ... this is of course loose and misleading. There are fern-trees on my section up to fifty feet in height, and at least one of them - the wheki variety, is a hard-bitten fellow ... The unobservant are apt to lump all fern-trees together, as pongas, or, in the vernacular, `bungers'. <i>(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> bunga <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> (Nelson) Graceful punga, or `bungy' as it is called in local parlance. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,27</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> [Otago] Bluey made a kennel for him of bungy ferns and manuka scrub, ... <i>(1970,Tat,86</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... he had bought a complete smithy outfit, built himself a workshop of ponga, and fitted it up in every detail like mine! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,266</i>)



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Dicksonia fibrosa </i>

>  [PUNGA] <i>(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### ponga and daub
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [of early small houses] constructed of ponga trunks and fronds, mixed with sod or clay. (See [wattle and daub](../W#wattle-and-daub))

>  <b>ALLEN</b> The boys built a ponga and daub house in this section as a home for them [parents] in their old age. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,67</i>)

### ponga fence
 
 <i>n.</i> a fence of ponga trunks (used for fences, frames, garden or summerhouses; for corduroying tracks, etc.) [also punga fence]

>  <b>WHITE</b> Round two sides a ponga fence is put (Cyatha dealbata) <i>(1874,Te Rou,179</i>)

>  Made a cow bale in the morning, worked at the punga fence in the afternoon, damp and nasty. <i>(Nov 4, 1882,Chudleigh Diary,314,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> Perhaps it pine for the other lilies, the ponga fence and the salt-laden sea breezes. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,30</i>)

>  punga-fence <i>(Sep 11, 1968,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10/1</i>)

### pongelo
 
 <i>n.</i> Pongelo beer

>  Defendant, who has had the pub. since April last, denied that he served drunken people with pongelow. <i>(1908,NZ Truth,182/6/4</i>)

### Pongo
 
 <i>adj.</i> British, Pommie [Pongolia, a fanciful and derogatory name for the British Isles. Pongolian, adjective.]

>  Those guys in D company might not understand if we talked to them with a Pongo accent. <i>(Dec 18, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/182 : 4</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> "You can call me 'chum', lad," he added, breaking into a broad Pongo dialect. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,130</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Like that pongo cobber of yours, the home. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,165</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> We strolled up the main <i>strada</i> in the evening ... and the Pongo red-cap stopped us to demand where our hats were. Bloody cheek said Mick, as if we're in their damned Pongo army ... The poor old Pongos in their hairy battle-dress, on skimpy rations and poor pay, the poor old Pongos with their white skins, calling their sergeants sir and saluting right left and centre all over the place. And nattering in incomprehensible dialects and county idiom. <i>Ibid.</i> 95 A real Pongo outlook because he'd only been out a few years when the war came and he went into our army. He didn't belong. He had that Pommie accent and that Pommie outlook. It's in the book he'd say. Do it as per the book. <i>Ibid.</i> 219 `Bloody Reginald. What a name to give a kid. A real sissy Pongo name.' ... `This Pongo business annoys me. You haven't even got it right. Pongo's the naval word for soldiers or marines. Out here they use it as just another form of Pommie.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,93</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `You may think I'm a bit of a drongo,' said Ivan surprisingly. `Letting that big Pommy bastard horn in on me and Mollie. ... If I turfed that Pongo bastard out of the house she'd go with him.' <i>(1962,Horse,35,1985 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 35]



 
 <i>n.</i> a Pommy (soldier)

>  I had to tell him that I was company runner and couldn't stand about all day talking to mere Pongos. <i>(Jan 4, 1943,NZEF Times,II/80 : 6</i>)

>  It's about the young pongo of Bari.. / Who tried to commit hari-kari. <i>(May 22, 1944,NZEF Times,III/152 : 4</i>)

>  At breakfast someone quoted the "unknown Pongo," to the effect that there's lashins o' slashin' groob. <i>(Sep 6, 1943,NZEF Times,III/115 : 5</i>)

>  <b>GRANT</b> I think that.. perhaps we ought to pickup a pongo or two, just to show we aren'tracially biased, ha ha. <i>(1979,I Rode with the Epigrams,23</i>)

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> He felt rattled and puzzled, ... like some poor bloody pongo pinned down under an artillery barrage ... <i>(1984,Ratatui,197</i>)

>  A big bronzed Pongo came in. <i>(Sep 7, 1942,2NZEF Times,5</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> An elderly quiet-spoken Pongo ... <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,148</i>)

>  <i>Pongo</i>: Anyone unfortunate enough not to be a New Zealander - generally a Tommy. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East</i>)

>  <i>Pongo</i>: New Zealand for Tommy. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> A trio of Jutes, a gaggle of pongos on leave <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,8</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> It is characteristic of Kiwis that they will approach a `Pommie' and say, `You know, you Pongoes are a hopeless lot.' <i>(1944,Troop Target,159</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> <i>Pongo</i>: (Kiwi-ism) U.K. Soldiers or Tommies. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> As one Pongo the audience rose, and dealt with the personnel of that theatre. <i>(1944,Desert Daze,9</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> Don't like these shells falling round, but its worse for the Pongos [English troops in a holed landing craft], and them still seasick. <i>(1963,Bedside Book,77</i>) [NOTES: [WWII, and D-Day]]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... some of the nearby Pongos cried: `E-e-e-e! What a Fred Karno's Army!' <i>(1978,Soldier Country,112</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a British soldier; occas. derog. for a base-wallah, =bludger, =red arse (perhaps, from the military figurative connection between (field-officers red tabs etc developing) RED-ARSE with play ape's arse, and PONGO `ape'.)

>  Talking to mere Pongos. <i>(Jan 11, 1943,2NZEF Times,81/5</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> The [supply] vehicles were obviously driven by `base-wallahs' - `pongoes' is the expression. <i>Ibid.</i> 151 Hear about the Pongo up in the blue for the first time. <i>(1944,Troop Target,127</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Pommy [ca1950 (R.Barraclough) An Englishman; disparagingly.]

>  Even people who speak English with an accent are watched, like yanks and `pongos'. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/220</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers') 220]

>  <b>WILSON</b> You can't do this, yer bloody Pongo. <i>Ibid.</i> 33 `How long does a bloke have to be in this country before people stop calling you a Pongo?' <i>(1957,Strip Jack Naked,25</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `A real Pommie eh? Imagine that.' `Yes, we're all Pommies here.' `A real bloody Pongo.' `This Pongo business annoys me. You haven't even got it right. Pongo's the naval word for soldiers or marines. Out here they use it as just another form of Pommie.' `Oh well, it's all the same to me.' ... `They're all right really. The Pommies, I mean. The Homeys, the Chooms, the Pongos.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,219</i>)

>  [Correspondent writing on the word POMMIE signs himself] Drongatus Pongorum. <i>(Feb 23, 1962,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <i>pongo n</i>. in N.Z. an Englishman; in Australia, flying squirrel [Abo]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  I keep forgetting. Forgive me, old man, I <i>am</i> a foolish pongo. <i>(1978,Listener Short Stories,2/19</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Young in 1978 Listener Short Stories Vol 2 19]

>  Mortimer looked a real nong. `This here is Mortimer,' said Fred. `He's a pongo, but he can't help it.' <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,147</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Grant `NZ Short Story']

>  ... others I had no idea were pure New Zild <i>(jacksy, pongo). </i> <i>(Jan 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>) [NOTES: (Deverson)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pongo</i> British person; originally used by British sailors to describe marine or soldier they were required to join forces with in WWI, probably not unaware that the word meant `monkey', though derivation credited to forage cap resembling that worn by the dog Pongo in a Punch and Judy show; brought back by Anzacs as name for their British counterpart. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)

### Pongoland
 
 <i>n.</i> The British Isles, more specifically England.

>  I, for example, was born in what Jane Bowron calls Pongoland. She uses the term with a mocking hatred that I always find terribly helpful and amusing. <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Dominion Post,B8</i>)

### Pongolia
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Britain, esp. England [cf. Pomgolia]

>  ... and indeed even the barbarian Pongolians to whom he normally sells the bulk of his produce have said that they will soon wish to buy their foodstuffs elsewhere. <i>(Sep 1967,Comment,32/12</i>) [NOTES: [Satirical piece]]

>  <b>McGill</b> 'As I was saying, poor show. Fellow came out here to learn his cricket, goes and plays for Pongolia. Not on what?' <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,130</i>)

>  During his month-long mission to Pongolia, Trev made a bird of talking to shopkeepers on their experience of the British version of GST. <i>(Jun 7, 1985,Contact (Wgtn),5</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Pongolia</i> Britain, modelled on Mongolia, promoted successfully by WWII soldier Les Cleveland, who told author he regretted `Pongloid' never caught on in the same way. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)

>  In Pongolia they do not understand these things. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

### Pongolian
 
 <i>n.</i> a British citizen [(coined ca1960)]

>  Ms Coney, she said, fawning all over her, had done more for women's rights than "you Pomgolian" stuck down in Canterbury. <i>(Jun 26, 2003,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  Pongolians understand men who wear their wives' knickers under their business suits, and their frocks at night. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

### Ponies, the
 
 <i>n.</i> the Ponsonby football club

>  <b>MACRAE</b> In manner Dunc was yer down-to-earth Kiwi joker. ... great man for his piss-ups; great man for the `Ponies' (Ponsonby football club) ... <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,47</i>)

### ponja
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [ponga](per-pop.md#ponga))

### ponk
 
 <i>n.</i> a stink, a pong [ca1938 (espec. children's)]

>  <i>pong, ponk n</i>. offensive smell [poss. from Maori <i>puhonga</i>, stinking]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> I suspect also that when we speak of an offensive stench as a <i>ponk</i> we are coupling the Maori <i>puhonga</i>, stinking, offensive, P: rather rare variant <i>pong</i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Ponk</i>. As a New Zealand usage, however, it must be modified in the light of B., 1941: I suspect ... that when we speak of an offensive stench as a <i>ponk</i> we are coupling the Maori <i>puhonga</i>, stinking, offensive, with the earlier English use of <i>pong</i>. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1140</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>ponk</i> stink; possibly from Maori `puhonga', stinking or offensive, and English word `pong', which has the rare form `ponk'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)

### pono
 
 <i>n.</i> the truth [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> `We tidied up, you can ask the others. Gary, Riki, ... even Neri ... pono, it's a fact'. <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,128</i>)

### Ponsonby gumboots
 
 <i>n.</i> knee-high leather boots

>  In a weird coincidence almost every TV personality present was wearing knee high leather boots. Are they considered appropriate footwear for gardening in the Auckland inner city area? Is it true they are now called Ponsonby gumboots? <i>(Nov 26, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

### Ponsonby handshake
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport)

>  The cyclist said he put his head down for a moment and then looked up as the man jumped out on to the road and struck him hard in the face. "I wasn't sure if it was closed fist or a forearm. In rugby days we would have called it a Ponsonby handshake. " <i>(Mar 11, 2008,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

### pontoon
 
 <i>n.</i> built-in hollow space to float under a deck of river punts.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> PONTOONS: Built-in hollow space to float under a deck of river punts. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961]

### pony
 
 <i>n.</i> a small glass [of beer] (See [also pony beer](per-pop.md#pony-beer),[pony shandy](per-pop.md#pony-shandy))

>  The normal method of address is "twoponies", "two mediums", "two longs", or"two handles." <i>(Jun 27, 1951,NZ Truth,18</i>)

>  <b>MACAIRE</b> Well then, a `pony'. (This is the smallest of glasses but the publican gets sixpence all the same.) <i>(1911,Disease and the Remedy, The,90</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Into the bar he'd go, up that worn wooden step dustcoat an' all, `for a pony', a puzzling expression until the meaning came that this was a slender glass of beer. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,129</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pony</i> smallest glass of beer served in pubs in imperial measure times, usually 5 ounce here and Sydney, 4 ounce in other places in Australia; originally American for small glass of liquor. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a one pound or occas. (?) a five pound note [20 pounds ca1930 Wellington (so a barber), and Blenheim (G.O'Connor)]

>  <b>CHURCH</b> Done for a pony .... Make it a fiver. <i>(1916,Tonks,148</i>)

>  Rumor [sic] has it that before leaving Wellington this youthful nobleman `tapped' a Minister of the Crown for a pony, and soon after his arrival in the northern city he suddenly became short of the `needful'. <i>(Feb 11, 1911,NZ Truth,294/7/1</i>)

### pony beer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mine's a pony beer. <i>(Dec 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 43</i>)

>  Whether.. the customer.. should pay.. a similar price for what is known as a "pony" beer, is a matter for the experts. <i>(1947,NZ Parliamentary Debates,278 : 709</i>)

>  A witness at the Christchurch Supreme Court the other day mentioned that he drank a pony beer on a certain occasion. `Pony beer,' repeated Judge Denniston, `a technical term, I presume.' Smiles. <i>(Nov 24, 1906,NZ Truth,75/1/3</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Between races he drank pony beers, watery in taste, at the bar behind the stand. <i>(1985,Horse,82,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> While you rubbed your belly and drank one pony beer. <i>(1975,Collected Poems,375,1966 ed.</i>)

### pony clubber
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Of course, not all pony clubbers make it to international arenas, but that was never the original intention. <i>(2006,AA Directions,Autumn / 20</i>)

### pony paddock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On the rugged hill farms and muddy roads the horse was the best means of transport and the pony paddock on the hill by the school often holding 30 school ponies. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock adjacent to primary school where children kept ponies or horses.

>  As a convenience to those attending social functions at Kapuni, the horse paddock in the school grounds has been thrown open. <i>(Jun 1, 1910,Hawera Star,6</i>)

>  <b>ELLMERS</b> We walked towards the building across a soggy paddock which he said was the horse paddock. I was told that all of the children except the Cleggs rode to school. <i>(1993,Schoolhouse Under the Sky,14</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> The horse spent the day in a horse paddock next to the school. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,52</i>)

>  In times past rural schools had paddocks for the ponies students rode to school. Now kids travel via horsepower in the shape of the school bus, so some schools are using the pony paddocks as a way to make extra money. <i>(Nov 29, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

>  Schools turn to pony paddocks for fundraising. <i>(Dec 5, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard,13</i>)

>  On the rugged hill farms and muddy roads the horse was the best means of transport and the pony paddock on the hill by the school often holding 30 school ponies. [horse paddock] <i>(Aug 24, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### pony shandy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PICARD</b> I will ... go and have a [31] pony shandy at Warren's <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,30</i>)

### poo pot
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a portable pot for the collection of faecal waste, for walkers and climbers in National Parks etc.

>  The Conservation Department has this season introduced so-called "poo pots" at Aoraki-Mt Cook National Park to help climbers dispose of their waste. <i>(Dec 20, 2004,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  Godley Hut is in good condition but De la Beche Hut is to have its near-full long-drop toilet removed completely, and a trial system of encouraging poo pot use to handle the small number of users the hut receives. <i>(Mar 2005,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.alpineclub.org.nz/documents/sections/auckland/05Mar.pdf]

>  Each pot comes with a brochure of instructions on how to use your poo pot, cornstarch bag liners and hand sanitizer. "Just as many people carry a pooper scooper when walking the dog, they can now carry their own poo pot when walking themselves. It's no longer acceptable to just poo anywhere." <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,19</i>)

### poo-ree-dee
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [puriri](pop-pyo.md#puriri))

### poodle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Slang. Pet dog of any breed. (C, 1895) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [shepherding] a non-collie dog.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> poodle: shepherd's term for anything which is not a collie. Use of such a term can raise eyebrows, and perhaps a little blood when used within earshot of the local hunt. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,63</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Oddly enough, shepherds never seem to call rabbiting dogs poodles, but they draw the line at referring to them as a team. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,65</i>)

### poof
 
 <i>n.</i> a gay person; someone soft or unused to hard work

>  I'd probably call him a poof - it'd put him off! <i>(Nov 9, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),2</i>)

>  I had even less claim to be on a road-gang than the varsity poofs they usually had to work with over the summer. <i>(Dec 30, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  ...I know what a supporter" is and it isn't some foul-mouthed oaf calling everyone a poof and demanding his money back if his team only wins by eight points. <i>(Nov 21, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B10</i>)

### poofie
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of poofter [spelt pouffey below]

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> ... we went and looked at five places which met all these requirements [for a cheap family house] and ended up in Remuera Road among the high-life pouffeys, paying heaven knows what tiny rent by today's standards. <i>(1974,Sugarbag Years,52</i>)

### poofler
 
 <i>n.</i> >?variant of POOFTER [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 17 POOFLERS [a useless person]]

### pooftah
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of poofter

>  There were cries of pooftah, a bit of pushing and shoving and suddenly it was handbags at 40 paces. <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

### poofter
 
 <i>n.</i> a male homosexual; a pretentious person; also as a general term of abuse [also pufter]

>  <i>Dynasty</i> broke a few boundaries, making the Carrington heir a poofta... But he was a token poofta, as it turns out, and wasn't actually allowed to be seen touching another man. <i>(Oct 7, 2002,Dominion Post,B11</i>)

>  <b>McGEE</b> Use your bloody feet, you pack of poofters. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,21</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> The poofters at Hautu made no secret of the fact that they were available. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,127</i>)

>  Accused then replied, "You pufter, you tried to bring a charge of --- against ---," and at the same time threw off his coat and rushed out, picked up an axe, and made a blow at the witness's head, but the latter warded it off. <i>(May 11, 1915,Grey River Argus,8</i>)

>  As [the judges] ascended the steps of Parliament the crowd broke into a good-natured chant of "Poofters! Poofters!". <i>(Feb 23, 2002,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  "I never stood up in the pulpit and said, 'I hope you realise your minister's a poofter,'" he says. <i>(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  <b>CHEVASSE</b> `I don't like my boss,' he said. `He's a stuck-up poofter, and he makes mistakes. <i>(1990,Integrity,40</i>)

>  "<i>Anybody</i> could be mistaken for a poofter now that sartorial pointers had gone." <i>(Nov 20, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### pooh-pooh
 
 <i>n.</i> a rifle; a big gun (See [also pu/poo](../A#also-pu/poo))

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>poohpooh</i> rifle or big gun, WWI; probably Maori `pu', a piped instrument and a gun. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pooh-pooh</i>. A rifle; a big gun: New Zealand soldiers' (rare): in G.W. Cf. <i>poop</i>, v., 4. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,648</i>)

### pook
 
 ([pu:k]) <i>n.</i> a pukeko [ORIGIN: abbreviation, [pu:k] ca1957 p.c. R. Mason, heard at Wildlife course from K. Miers]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PUKEKO <i>Porphyrio melanotus Other names</i>: Swamp-hen, Pukaki, Pook. ... A variety of sounds emerges from a raupo-swamp which holds a colony of Pukekos: a drawn-out, almost booming <i>poo-koo-koo-koo ... </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,108</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> ... one or two pukeko are shot for every gun during the [duck-] shooting season. I recall a friend returning one early May morning with a disgusted report: `Only a pook'. <i>(1982,Four Seasons</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori [used by Marist Maori Missionaries up to c1940s ? f. Puku -->1987 June 14 F.McKay p.c.]

### pool
 
 <i>v.</i> to involve

>  <i>pool v.</i> in Australia, involve, incriminate, trap; in N.Z. meaning as in U.K. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a housing pool

>  The Minister claims that State servants who are allocated houses from the pool are receiving special treatment. <i>(Sep 1950,Public Service Journal,47</i>)

### pool house
 
 <i>n.</i> a state house allocated for state servants on transfer ca1945 at least [I think pool housing probably arose from the committee mentioned in the Jan. 1947 cit; however, the only use of the term "pool" I found in 1947 and 1949 PSJs was the second which could be the first printed surfacing of an underground term. The December 1946 issue has numerous "wanted to swap state house" advertisements indicating the need existed.]

>  Mr Tuohy suggested certain lines ofapproach - .. to increase quickly thenumber of pool houses available not onlyin the main centres. <i>(Dec 1954,Public Service Journal,9</i>)

>  The Committee [dealing with housing forPublic Servants] respectfully submits..(2) That immediate steps.. be taken toimplement the proposal that houses shouldbe purchased on the open market fortenancy by public servants.. (4) That alimited scheme of transit housing forpublic servants be instituted as quicklyas possible in Wellington and Auckland. <i>(Jan 1947,Public Service Journal,10</i>)

>  After four month's operation, the HousingBureau located in the office of thePublic Service Commission, reports thatthe housing needs of one hundred andthirty-six public servants on transfer..have been satisfied... Every time apublic servant on transfer gives up atenancy which then passes to someoneother than a public servant, the marginbetween the number of houses occupied bypublic servants and the number of marriedofficers is increased. Active steps needto be taken to avoid this loss from the"pool." <i>(Jul 1947,Public Service Journal,244</i>)

>  The Cabinet Sub-Committee.. had madecertain recommendations to Cabinet...These provided for the setting up of apool of houses for State servants whowere subject to transfer... It did notseem that there was any provisions [sic]for the transfer to the State housingpool of a nucleus consisting of houses atpresent occupied by public servants. <i>(Sep 1950,Public Service Journal,32</i>)

### pooling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `Pooling' is much the same as binning except that a whole clip is group-classed with other clips whereas at present binning applies mainly to the oddments only. Only a few brokers pool wool for their clients, since in most cases interlotting and binning are considered sufficient devices to handle most small lines. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,712</i>)

### poon
 
 <i>n.</i> a loner, an idiot

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>poon</i> loner, idiot; possibly Aboriginal, possible link English public school word meaning to be unsteady, from Latin `ponere', to place. NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,86</i>)

### poop bag
 
 <i>n.</i> a bag for collecting dog excreta

>  ...staff on their regular street rounds would be on the look-out for owners walking registered dogs on a leash and carrying an official poop bag or plastic bag to pick up dog droppings. <i>(Nov 4, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### poor cow
 
 <i>n.</i> (compassionate use)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The poor cow,' say his cobbers. `What rotten luck. Wouldn't it rock you.' <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,120</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I saved a joker from drowning once ... Pulled the poor cow out. Then he ... stepped on a bloody mine. Stonkered the poor bastard properly. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,201</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> ... I filled a kettle and put it on the gas. Some flies were sleeping on the wall over the stove, so I gave them a heck of a scare with the gas gun. I kept pulling the trigger and all the sparks shot out and scared the daylights out of the poor cows; ... <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,49</i>)

### poor doer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal that fails to thrive in good conditions.

>  It is remarkable how quickly buyers shy off particular lines of cattle because they are proven poor doers. <i>(Sep 10, 1970,NZ Farmer,53</i>)

>  Such data will objectively detect 'poor doers' and 'shy feeders.' <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,5</i>)

>  Poor doers often have significant number of coccidia in the faeces. <i>(Jun 1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

### Poor Jenny
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> ...Tom says I am a sheeler for playing Poor Jenny ... My spelling cannot have improved much with the years for I had to look up the dictionary to find whether one spelt the word `sheila' or `sheilagh'. Of course `Poor Jenny' or `Poor Sally', as it is perhaps better known, was and no doubt still is a girl's game. As I had three doting sisters I cheerfully joined their games when my boy friends were not looking. Remember the words of the song? Poor Sally is a-weeping, a-weeping, a-weeping, Poor Sally is a-weeping, On a bright summer's day. On the carpet she shall kneel While the grass grows in the field - Stand up, stand up, upon your feet, And choose the one you love so sweet. Then followed `the moment of truth' and with girl and boy standing demurely in the middle of the ring of children, the circle danced around them singing: Now you're married I wish you joy, First a girl and then a boy. I forget the final words, if any. Usually this game was followed by `Ring-a-ring a rosie, Pop down a posie.' Strange, though, how the love-making aspect would come into many of these old games. I must have been a pronounced `sheeler' about this time because I also remember joining in the singing of I sent a letter to my love And on the way I dropped it, Some of you have picked it up And put it in your pocket. Dropped it - dropped it! And so on. A year or two later at the mellowing age of 10 years I abhorred all these `sissy' games though I still had a hankering to play `Oranges and Lemons', even at the great age of 12. <i>(Jun 27, 1903,Diary</i>) [NOTES: (1903 June 27) Pat Lawlor Wellington Diary in 1977 Aug. 27 Dominion 6 June 27 1903]

### Poor Knights gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a medium sized gecko restricted to the Poor Knights Islands group. [<i>Hoplodactylus "Poor Knights gecko"</i> Unusual in that some eat fish regurgitated by seabirds.]

>  Poor Knights gecko (<i>Hoplodactylus</i> sp.) is restricted to those islands off Northland. It eats fish waste from seabirds. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/18</i>)

### Poor Knights lily
 
 <i>n.</i> a flax-like flowering plant. [from Poor Knights Islands] Xeronema callistemon

>  The poor Knights lily is a member of the larger lily order, but the flower spikes look more like a scarlet bottlebrush, with leaves like a bearded iris. It was first found in 1924, and plant material which was collected first flowered in 1932 in cultivation. From the first flowering, seed was gathered, but none of the seedlings flowered until 1954. <i>(Feb 4, 1984,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  The Poor Knights Lily ...was discovered in 1924 by a scientific party visiting the Poor Knights Islands 25 km off the east coast of Northland. <i>(Nov 1985,Forest & Bird,35</i>)

>  The [cliff-hanging] plants chosen were the poor knights lily (Xeronema callistemon) and ringaringa, also called the New Zealand rock lily (Arthropodium cirrhatum). <i>(May 10, 1973,Dominion,5/1</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> ... the Poor Knights lily, a flax-like plant which grew in great clumps and had sharp-edged points [on Poor Knights Island]. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,40</i>)

>  The Poor Knights lily, Xeronema <i>callistemon</i>, is an outstanding plant with large fans of long straight leaves.. and long, scarlet heads that look like long, large blooms of the Australian bottlebrush. <i>(Mar 31, 2000,Dominion,18</i>)

>  There's nothing like a blinding flash of inspiration and it came in the spectacular form of the native Xeronema callistemon, Poor Knight's Lily. <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Dominion Post,F14</i>)

>  She chose clumps of sword-like Poor Knights lilies, <i>Xeronema callistemon</i>, instead... Poor Kinghts lilies need full sun and excellent drainage and eventually - after seven to 10 years - produce spectacular, flaming-red bottlebrush flowers, too. <i>(Jul 4, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),47</i>)

>  Poor Knights Lily, Xeronoma callistemon, makes few demands except sublime patience. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe that requires assistance during lambing.

### poor man's cocaine
 
 <i>n.</i> speed

Known as "poor man's cocaine" - it's half the price with similar effects - it is often used by people in their late teens and early 20s who mix in the nightclub scene. <i>(Sep 12, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

### poor man's curtains
 
 <i>n.</i> cobwebbed windows

>  I asked a friend about poorman's orange" and she told me that, in her youth, cobwebbed windows were called "poor man's curtains". <i>(Sep 25, 2001,Letter</i>)

### poor man's diggings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The field proved to be one of the richest ever discovered in the colony, and at the same time it was essentially a "poor man's" diggings, as most of the gold was picked up out of the crevices in the rocks. <i>(Sep 29, 1896,Star, The,4</i>)

>  I fully believe it will, as a `poor man's' diggings, prove second to none ... <i>Ibid.</i> 26 The Lindis has been known for some time .... but being isolated, and a considerable distance apart from the settled districts, it has not received <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,19</i>)

>  They all described [the claims] as a `good poor man's diggings'; meaning thereby that anybody could be sure of earning a living from them, but few would realise large sums. <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/85/3</i>) [NOTES: [West Coast]]

>  <b>MAY</b> The term `poor man's diggings' was used by contemporaries to describe the West Coast goldfields. This term referred not to their richness or otherwise but to the fact that gold could be readily obtained by any `poor man' with limited means. `Gold is the great friend of the masses', ran a line in the chorus of a diggers' song. <i>(May 1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,240,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> It was known as a `poor man's diggings' because the gold was fine and dispersed over a wide area. Frost and drought alternated to rob the races of water, and there were also disputes with landowners over the polluted water that the fields produced downriver. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,84</i>)

>  <b>COCKBURN-HOOD</b> Should it turn out what the Australians call a `poor man's diggings ', there is likely to be some trouble with the native owners of the soil [at Coromandel]. much attention. It is what is generally known as a `poor man's diggings'. <i>(1863,Notes of a Cruise,5</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> The latter place ... having rich alluvial, or poor men's diggings, was a very bank with an ever-ready balance in favour of the clever and the muscular. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,265</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> A `poor man's goldfield' they called the whole Buller area, not because of a shortage of gold but because a man with a minimum of equipment could at least make wages. <i>(1976,Difficult Country,34</i>)

### poor man's orange
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also New Zealand grapefruit](new-no.md#new-zealand-grapefruit))

>  Don't forget the Poor Man's Orange makes the best Marmalade. <i>(Jul 28, 1904,Wanganui Herald,1</i>)

>  20 Grafted Orange Trees, assorted, viz., - The Poor Man's Orange, St Michael's, Smith's Thin Skin, Mandarin, Pride of Sydney Market. <i>(Jun 12, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Where climate and soil conditions are favourable, every garden should have two or three lemons and a Poor Man's Orange. <i>(1921,Brett's Gardening Guide,187</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  Poor Man's Orange, like the second-grade fruit from which it takes its name, is a bitter story but it's not depressing. <i>(Oct 21, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  Where climate and soil conditions are favourable every garden should have two or three lemons and a Poor Man's orange. <i>(1921,Brett's Gardening Guide,187</i>)

>  These are all of this variety known as "The Poor Man's Orange." They grow to a large size, are hardy, coarse in texture, harsh in flavour, and possessed of a degree of bitterness which many do not like. <i>(Aug 20, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

>  <b>CRICHTON</b> Poor Man's Orange. - A very hardy,vigorous, and prolific variety. <i>(1893,Australasian Fruit Culturalist, The,II/207</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The fruit formerly known as <i>poor man's orange</i> has now become <i>New Zealand grapefruit. </i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,87</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>... poor man's orange</i> (New Zealand grapefruit: a once standard term now abandoned for advertising reasons), ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,116</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  N.Z. Grapefruit Wrongly Named [heading] The export potential of New Zealand grapefruit could be enhanced if it was renamed, delegates to the annual conference of the New Zealand Citrus and Sub-tropical Council have been told. ... New Zealand grapefruit are not, in fact grapefruit at all. They are a type of orangelo, and probably more accurately described by the nickname `poor man's orange.' <i>(Jul 1, 1978,Otago Daily Times,7</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> We had three lemon trees and a poorman's orange and two nectarines as well as roses and shrubs and annuals. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,30</i>)

### poor man's weather glass
 
 <i>n.</i> scarlet pimpernel [in ODE unlabelled]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SCARLET PIMPERNEL (<i>Anagallis arvensis</i>), sometimes called poor man's weather glass, is found in cultivated fields and waste places throughout the islands. It is annual with trailing or slightly ascending stems about a foot long. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,132,1967 ed.</i>)

>  The blossoms .. do not expand except during sunshine. In this they resemble such well-known flowers as the Star of Bethlehem .. and Pimpernel, which from its habit of closing its corolla as soon as the sun is overcast is sometimes known as the Poor Man's Weather Glass. <i>(Jan 23, 1901,Otago Witness,71</i>)

### poor man's whitebait
 
 <i>n.</i> strips of shredded skate wing

>  <b>BROWN</b> Rachel stripped the last of the skin from the steamed skate's wing ... When fried with beaten, seasoned eggs, it was almost identical in taste and appearance to whitebait. Skittleback : poor man's whitebait, but as good as the real thing. <i>(1968,Weaver's Apprentice, The,43</i>)

### poor men's diggings
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [poor man's diggings](per-pop.md#poor-mans-diggings))

>  To facilitate the creation of poor men's diggings, the Government might issue instructions to resident magistrates to provide <i>bona fide</i> applicants .. with miner's rights and such tools as are necessary, together with free rations for a few months, and an extended claim as a reward in case of the discovery of a poor man's gold field capable of carrying a population of 2000 men. <i>(Mar 20, 1880,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  .. steady workers with a small capital are wanted here to develope [sic] properly the various auriferous resources; the "poor men's diggings" are pretty well expended ... <i>(May 21, 1864,Otago Witness,5</i>)

### Poor Old Tom
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast</i>: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland</i>: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay</i>: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; <i>Canterbury</i>: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago</i>: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### poor pretences
 
 <i>n.</i> the grass Poa pratensis [folk etymology]

>  I saw some man sowing grass seed the other day on one of the public roads, and to satisfy an innate curiosity I asked him what it was. "It's what they call 'poor pretences,'" he said, "and bedad, sir, it's a very good name, for it is that same, for divil a one of it iver comes up that ivir I sees!" <i>(Apr 27, 1880,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> In the early 'eighties occurred two dry seasons during which grass fires were run over the hot western and northern faces of certain portions on eastern Tutira; on the blackened ground then and once again seedlings of brome-grass (<i>Bromus mollis</i>) appeared in so great profusion that other grasses were temporarily submerged in hillsides of waving hay. [284] `Poor Pretences' was then the name by which this brome-grass was known on the east coast : it may be worth putting on record a suggestion as to how the plant came by such a curious designation. It was a `poor pretence' compared with ryegrass, cock's-foot, and white clover, the mixture sown then almost as a religious duty on Hawke's Bay runs, good and bad alike. So much for the sense; as regards the sound, `poor pretences' can, I think, only be a corruption of <i>Poa pratensis</i>; the Latin name of the one plant done into English has been fitted to another. The reader will recollect how this grass - more widely known as goose-grass - was sown wholesale over the trough of the run. <i>(1926,Tutira,283</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a children's game (See [Poor Jenny](per-pop.md#poor-jenny))

### poor show
 
 <i>phr.</i> usual NZ equivalent of <i>bad show</i> (cf P.) and <i>put up a show</i> (cf P. orig. mily: 1915). Used occas. to express disapproval, but mainly in the sense of `a poor showing, display; state of affairs; behaviour'. Almost now a c.p. [? omit]

>  `You can do what you b- well like about it', [the accused said to the Magistrate. ... Later he] quietly pleaded guilty. `It was a poor show wasn't it, eh?' Mr Inglish [S.M.] said <i>(Mar 22, 1963,Evening Post,10</i>)

### poorman
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of orange tree

>  There is the famous strain of poorman called "Morrison's Seedless." ... Some citrus experts.. believe that when the ideal sweet orange for the New Zealand climate is found, it will be a development of the poorman <i>(Apr 27, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,79</i>)

### poorman orange
 
 <i>n.</i> a small grapefruit used for marmalade; New Zealand grapefruit, marmalade orange, a smallish, bitter grapefruit (correctly poor man's orange q.v.) [also spelt poorman's orange] (See [also New Zealand grapefruit](new-no.md#new-zealand-grapefruit))

>  The varieties grown are the Seville and Poor-man, both marmalade oranges. <i>(Oct 23, 1890,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  I have a twelve-year-old orange tree (Poorman) which fails to make thenecessary fruiting growth. <i>(Jul 1, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,34</i>)

>  <b>MORTIMER</b> .. the poorman orange trees .. <i>(2000,From a Distance: The Waima River Years,49</i>)

>  At Kerikeri .. infection occurred roughly in the following order: Ponderosa lemon .. grapefruit (Marsh's Seedless) .. New Zealand grapefruit or Poorman Orange (Pommelo X Sour Orange). <i>Ibid.</i> 20/115A .. Poorman orange [fn] Known in New Zealand as New Zealand grapefruit. For further information, see <i>N.Z. Jr. Agric.</i>, vol. 55, p.225. <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,20/56A</i>)

>  This development [sc. the popularity of grapefruit] has a particular interest for New Zealand in that a type of grapefruit has been evolved from citrus introduced by Sir George grey and named by him the Poorman orange. New Zealand grapefuit as known to-day is a selected strain known generally as Morrison's Seedless, after the late Mr. H. Morrison, of Warkworth, who sought to improve the original Poorman orange, which was at one time considered suitable only for the manufacture of marmalade. <i>(1937,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,18/409</i>)

>  One of his more successful efforts was the isolation of the New Zealand grapefruit, which he called "poorman orange" for its heavy fruiting and which became widely known throughout the northern North Island. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/103</i>)

>  Grown extensively, too, are New Zealand Poorman oranges and grapefruit and Wheeny grapefruit, a variety imported from Australia, similar to the Californian grapefruit in size, inside appearance, and flower ... <i>(1948,Our Own Country,42</i>)

>  Mr. E. Boult, Whangarei, --- Oranges: Silette, Parramatta, Poor Man, also a branch of Poor Man orange. <i>(Aug 6, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

>  [Governor] Grey introduced and named the Poorman's Orange, known now as the Morrison Grapefruit; he gave budwood of his Orange to Mr Edward Morrison of Warkworth, who developed the present Seedless strain. <i>(Nov 1985,NZ Gardener,56</i>)

>  Poorman oranges sold at 5s 6d and 7s 6d a case, and were difficult to quit. <i>(Jul 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,52</i>)

>  The designation, "Poorman orange," was obviously a misnomer which, in itself, would discourage potential purchasers. <i>(Feb 9, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,78</i>)

>  One variety on which, mutations frequently occur is the poorman orange, or New Zealand grapefruit. <i>(Apr 27, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,79</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Kerikeri [with] its groves of sweet orange, grapefruit ... and what is known as the Poorman Orange, which is much in demand for marmalade making. <i>(1946,Pastoral NZ,81</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>) [NOTES: I. Davey (Whangarei)]

>  `What's that you brought for us?' `Poorman's oranges.' .. I hate grapefruit. <i>(1986,New Outlook,21/48</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Macrae (The Kingsland Kid) in New Outlook 21/48]

 <i>n.</i>

### poozle
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Finally, beware of the big purple poozle person (to poozle is to snaffle; to take).Things do go missing if left lying around - that includes tools, resources, equipment, materials and artwork. Put things away, be conscious of what you're using and where it comes from. If something disappears whilst you're using it, let the tutor know. And don't poozle other people's stuff without asking, or the original owner might forget to look after you and may break some furniture in their effort to get at you. <i>(Aug 2004,tlc creativity Newsletter,No. 25/1</i>) [NOTES: http://www.tlc.ac.nz/newsletters/25_aug_04.htm]

>  When it turns translucent, add the mushrooms, cover the pan with a lid, you've poozled off a saucepan, lower the heat right down and allow the mixture to very gently stew while you cook the risotto. <i>(Aug 24, 2002,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Leo would spend his sober periods worrying about Jill's poozles - a variety of antics with great sentimental and artistic value. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,57</i>)

>  Peter was fighting to save old houses/ once he felt so strong/ he was shouting about the old people/ who had lived in Grafton so long/ for instance himself/ he had built his own room/ out of poozled pieces of glass/ <i>(Nov 15, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),69</i>) [NOTES: (Helen Rutherford)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>poozling</i> scavenging in abandoned buildings before the demolisher gets there, old bottles favoured, but anything of antique significance or value. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> ...as usual, when the pressure went on, I couldn't find my start, of course - poozle a construction from the best first line in New Zealand literature - Ronald Hugh Morrieson, <i>The Scarecrow</i>, out of 1963. 'The same week our fowls were stolen, Daphne Moran had her throat cut.' Right. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,263</i>)

### poozler
 
 <i>n.</i> fossicker

>  Antique Alley is poozler heaven on Dominion Road. <i>(May 2004,Metro,64</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> My old friend Kae Miller used to drive a gaily decorated van with "A Pride of Poozlers" inscribed on the side. My son Jonathan, who has driven that van, believes it was around in the early 1770. I am sure it was some time in the 70s. I have the impression, perhaps wrongly, that Kae invented the words poozle, poozling and poozler. Jonathan has the same belief but neither of us is sure if it is correct. She certainly used these words. Kae (single name Katrine Hursthouse), was a dedicated environmentalist,long before it was fashionable. Poozling was a part of her environmentalism. She poozled (I think she would have resisted the word "scavenge") diligently in the old Wilton tip and claimed to have rescued valuable antiques. <i>(Oct 1, 2004,Letter</i>)

### poozling
 
 <i>n.</i> scavenging for (especially old or antique) collector's items [e.g. `their bottle collection came from poozling under old houses'. ca1974 p.c. P.B.O., et al. in Wellington and Christchurch -- a fanciful word (used also in Sydney now)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>poozling</i>: Going through abandoned houses scheduled for demolition and removing the (usually antique) fittings that strike your fancy. Until recently this was a socially acceptable practice (although not strictly legal), however, as demolition contractors catch on to the value of these fittings legal and moral pressure is exerted to discourage the practice. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,79</i>)

>  <b>TREWBY</b> poozling: scavenging among cargoes for 'loose' goods, also, scavenging in about-to-be demolioshed buildings for anything of value <i>(2004,Beachcomber: a New Zealand & Pacific Miscellany</i>)

### pop
 
 <i>n.</i> a shot (from a gun)

>  <b>STEPHENS</b> Many is the sly pop made on these generally useless animals [sc. dogs]. <i>(1844,Journal,331</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> attempt; a go, a `shot', espec. in express: give it a pop, have a pop at [ca1935 G'is a pop at it]

>  <b>MARSH</b> How about having a pop at Mr Liversidge [sc a go at questioning him.] <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,131</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pop</i>, phr. <i>have a pop at</i> a fight; NZA. <i>- to go off pop</i> to start speaking angrily. <i>- not a fair pop</i> not a fair chance; eg `I'd like to have a pop at the little basket, but I apologise for going off pop at you. It's not a fair pop you having to be involved.' c. 1925. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)
